# Cheeky one-paragraph summaries, one per day's reading.
#
# Format, tab-separated:   BOOK CH:FIRST-LAST<TAB>summary
#
# The verse range must be exactly one of the day splits the plan makes at its
# default settings (5 min x 180 wpm). ./build-summaries.py validates that and
# refuses to build otherwise, so a boundary can never quietly drift out of step
# with the pacing code.
#
# A chapter that splits mid-narrative ends every part but the last on episodic
# TV continuation text ("Next time on ..."). A chapter that splits mid-poem
# does not: a psalm has no cliffhanger.

EST 1:1-22	Ahasuerus rules 127 provinces and spends six months showing off his things, then throws a week-long open bar for the entire capital. Vashti hosts her own party for the women. On day seven the king, thoroughly marinated, sends seven eunuchs to fetch her in the crown so the nobles can admire her looks. She declines. The king's legal team panics that wives everywhere will now get ideas, so an unrepealable law goes out in every language of the empire: Vashti is fired, and every man is officially the boss of his own house. Nothing says secure like legislating it.
EST 2:1-23	The king sobers up, remembers he is now single, and his staff propose an empire-wide talent search for a replacement. Every eligible young woman gets rounded up into the harem for a twelve-month spa protocol, six months of myrrh and six of perfume, in exchange for exactly one night. Mordecai, an exile raising his orphaned cousin Esther, tells her to keep quiet about being Jewish and paces outside the compound daily like a nervous parent. Esther asks the eunuch in charge what to wear and wears that, which turns out to be the winning move. Crown, feast, tax holiday. Then Mordecai overhears two doorkeepers plotting to kill the king, passes it up the chain, the plotters are hanged, someone writes it in the royal diary, and nobody thanks him.
EST 3:1-15	Haman gets promoted and everyone at the gate has to bow. Mordecai won't. The other staff nag him about it daily until he tells them he's Jewish, at which point they run and tell Haman, because of course they do. Haman decides murdering one man is beneath a person of his standing and goes for the entire ethnic group. He rolls dice to pick the date, lands on eleven months out, and sells the king a vague story about a people with inconvenient laws, plus ten thousand talents of silver. The king hands over his signet ring without one follow-up question. Couriers ride out with the order and the two of them sit down for a drink, while the city of Susa is left thoroughly perplexed.
EST 4:1-17	Mordecai tears his clothes, puts on sackcloth and ashes, and wails through the middle of town, stopping at the gate because sackcloth is not permitted past it. Esther hears and sends him a change of clothes, which he declines. So they conduct the entire crisis by courier, with Hathach relaying: here is the decree, here is the exact bribe figure, go beg the king. Esther points out that walking in uninvited is a capital offence and the king hasn't sent for her in a month. Mordecai's reply is not comforting: you won't survive this either, rescue will come from somewhere regardless, and who knows, maybe this is the entire point of you. Esther agrees, orders three days of no food and no water for everybody, and signs off with "if I perish, I perish".
EST 5:1-14	Day three, Esther puts on the royal outfit and goes to stand in the one courtyard where standing is a capital offence. The king holds out the scepter and offers her half the kingdom, the sort of thing men say around beautiful women and later regret. She asks for a dinner party. With Haman. At dinner he offers half the kingdom again, and she asks for another dinner tomorrow. Haman floats home delighted, spots Mordecai still not standing up for him, and arrives to brag to his family about his money, his children, his promotions and his exclusive invitations, before admitting none of it counts while one man sits at a gate. His wife suggests a seventy-five foot gallows and an early word with the king. Haman loves it and starts building.
EST 6:1-14	King can't sleep, has the royal minutes read to him as a bedtime story, and discovers he never thanked Mordecai for saving his life. Haman turns up early to ask permission to hang the guy and gets asked instead what should be done for a man the king really likes. He assumes this is obviously about himself and describes the most extravagant parade imaginable. King says great, do all of that, for Mordecai. Haman spends his day leading the horse of the man he built a gallows for, then goes home to be told by his own wife that he is finished.
EST 7:1-10	Dinner, round two. Esther finally names the plot and the man behind it, and the king storms out to the garden to have feelings about it. Haman throws himself on Esther's couch to beg, the king walks back in, sees a man on his wife's furniture, and that settles it. A helpful servant mentions there's a seventy-five foot gallows already standing in the yard. Haman gets to try it out.
EST 8:1-17	Esther inherits Haman's estate, Mordecai gets the signet ring, and everyone discovers Persian law has no undo button. So instead of cancelling the genocide they write a second decree letting the targets fight back. Mordecai walks out in royal blue and white, Susa throws a party, and a great many locals decide this is a fine week to become Jewish.
EST 9:1-17	The appointed day arrives and goes very badly for everyone who showed up planning a massacre: five hundred dead in Susa, Haman's ten sons among them, and nobody touches the loot, which the text is very keen you notice. The king asks Esther whether she wants anything else, which by now is a dangerous question, and she asks for one more day. Seventy-five thousand later they stop and have lunch. Next time on Esther: the paperwork.
EST 9:18-32	Mordecai writes it all down and invents a holiday. Purim, named after the dice Haman rolled to pick the date, observed with feasting, presents, and food for the poor. Ratified by letter, confirmed by a second letter, and entered into the book, because nothing says "we survived" like getting it properly minuted.
EST 10:1-3	The king celebrates by raising taxes. Mordecai ends up second in the empire and genuinely well liked, and the narrator points you at the official chronicles for the details, which is ancient for "see the extended edition".
GEN 1:1-31	God starts with a formless nothing and a wind on the water, says "light" and gets light, then spends six days separating things from other things and filling the gaps: sky from sea, land from sea, plants, sun and moon, and "he also made the stars" dropped in like an afterthought. Then fish, birds, livestock, and finally humans, made in the divine image and immediately handed the deed to everything that moves. The whole planet is put on a plant-based diet. God reviews the work and files it as very good, which is the last performance review anyone passes for a while.
GEN 2:1-25	God knocks off on day seven and makes the day off holy. Then the camera pulls in for the close-up: a man shaped out of dirt and started with mouth-to-nose, a garden planted in the east, and a travel guide's detour about four rivers and where the good gold is. One tree is off-limits, penalty death, noted and moved past. God decides solitude is the first thing that isn't good, parades every animal by to be named on the off chance one works out, and none do. So: anaesthetic, one rib, and a woman. The man's reaction is the oldest poem on record. Nobody is wearing anything and nobody minds.
GEN 3:1-24	A talking snake opens with "did God really say", which is how every bad idea starts, and works the fine print until the fruit looks nutritious, attractive and educational all at once. They eat, discover they are naked, and invent clothing out of shrubbery. God comes walking in the cool of the day and asks where they are, which is not a geography question. The man blames the woman and, in the same sentence, God for supplying her. The woman blames the snake. The snake wisely says nothing. Everyone is cursed, God makes them proper leather clothes anyway, and they are evicted past an angel with a spinning flaming sword.
GEN 4:1-26	Two brothers, two offerings, one accepted. Cain takes the feedback badly, God warns him that sin is crouching at the door like a bad dog, and Cain invites his brother for a walk in the field regardless. Asked where Abel is, he tries "am I my brother's keeper", history's first alibi and possibly its first sarcasm. He is exiled, complains the sentence is too harsh, and is given a protective mark anyway. His descendants invent tents, music and metalwork, and one of them writes his two wives a song about killing a young man for bruising him, so the family is doing great. Meanwhile Adam and Eve have Seth, and people start saying God's name out loud.
GEN 5:1-32	A genealogy, and a punishing one: ten generations from Adam to Noah, each entry the same three facts, had a son at X, lived another Y, died. Everyone is impossibly old. Two names break the rhythm. Enoch walks with God for three hundred years and then simply isn't there anymore, no death recorded, the sentence just stops. And Methuselah, at nine hundred sixty-nine, takes the record and does absolutely nothing else with it. Lamech names his son Noah hoping he'll be a relief from all the hard work, which is one way of putting what happens next.
GEN 6:1-22	Divine beings marry human women, giants turn up, and God caps the human lifespan at a hundred and twenty, all inside four verses with no further explanation offered, ever. Humanity's every thought is continually evil, God is sorry he made any of it, and calls for a reset. Noah is the exception. He gets blueprints: gopher wood, three decks, tarred inside and out, four hundred and fifty feet long, one door, one window, and a passenger manifest of his family plus two of everything that breathes. Noah does not say a single word in this chapter. He just builds it.
GEN 7:1-24	Boarding call: seven pairs of the clean animals, two of everything else, seven days' notice, and the rain arrives exactly on schedule. The deep bursts open from below while the sky opens from above, which is day two of creation being undone from both directions at once. God shuts the door personally, a detail worth noticing. Forty days of rain, water over the mountaintops, and everything breathing outside that boat is gone. The flood sits there for a hundred and fifty days.
GEN 8:1-22	God remembers Noah, which the text mentions in a way that suggests it was briefly in doubt, and sends a wind. The water drops, the boat parks on Ararat, and Noah opens the world's first weather bureau: a raven that just flaps around usefully to nobody, a dove that comes back with nothing, a dove that comes back with an olive leaf, and a dove that decides it has better places to be. Everyone disembarks. Noah's first act on dry land is to barbecue a selection of the animals he has just spent a year saving, and God, enjoying the smell, promises never again, seedtime and harvest, cold and heat, on the record.
GEN 9:1-29	Reboot terms: be fruitful, animals are now afraid of you and also on the menu, though not the blood, and killing people is now a capital matter, since people are made in God's image. Then the rainbow, hung in the sky as a knot in the divine handkerchief so God will remember his own promise, which is a striking way to put it. Then Noah, the one righteous man on earth, plants a vineyard, gets comprehensively drunk, and passes out naked in his tent. Ham sees and tells his brothers, who reverse in with a blanket held up between them. Noah wakes up and curses Ham's son, who wasn't even there. Then lives another three hundred fifty years.
GEN 10:1-32	The table of nations: seventy-odd names sorted under Japheth, Ham and Shem, functioning as a map of the known world with every people filed under whoever they came from. Most of them you will never hear from again. Exactly one gets a story: Nimrod, a mighty hunter before Yahweh, who founds Babel and Nineveh and ends up as a proverb. Everyone else is a country wearing a person's name.
GEN 11:1-32	Everyone speaks one language and decides to build a tower to the sky to make a name for themselves, specifically so they won't get scattered. God comes down to have a look, a small joke at the tower's expense, and scatters them by making everybody unintelligible to everybody else. Then another genealogy, Shem down to Terah, with the lifespans quietly collapsing from five hundred years to two hundred. It lands on Abram and his wife Sarai, who has no children, which is the sentence the next thirteen chapters are about. Terah packs the family for Canaan, gets as far as Haran, and stops there permanently.
GEN 12:1-20	God tells a seventy-five-year-old to leave his country, his relatives and his father's house for a destination to be disclosed later, and promises to make him a great nation, which is bold given his wife can't have children. Abram goes. Then a famine sends him to Egypt, where he looks at his beautiful wife and calculates that "she's my sister" will keep him both alive and comfortable. It does both. Sarai is taken into Pharaoh's house, Abram collects livestock and servants out of the deal, and God has to plague an entirely innocent Pharaoh to sort it out. Pharaoh's telling-off of the great patriarch is completely justified, and Abram, notably, has no reply.
GEN 13:1-18	Abram comes back from Egypt considerably richer than he left, and he and Lot now have too many animals to share a postcode. Rather than fight, Abram offers his nephew first pick of the entire land, which is either enormous generosity or enormous confidence. Lot looks at the well-watered Jordan plain and takes it, and the narrator immediately adds "this was before Yahweh destroyed Sodom and Gomorrah", which is the literary equivalent of ominous music. Lot pitches his tent toward Sodom. Abram, left with the leftovers, is told to look in every direction, because all of it is his, and his descendants will be as countable as dust.
GEN 14:1-24	A four-kings-versus-five-kings war breaks out over unpaid tribute, the losing side falls into tar pits while fleeing, and the winners carry off Sodom's goods and, incidentally, Lot. Abram hears, arms three hundred and eighteen men born in his own household, runs the raiders down, attacks at night, and gets everything back. On the way home a mysterious priest-king called Melchizedek turns up out of nowhere with bread and wine, blesses him, collects a tenth, and vanishes from the story entirely. The king of Sodom offers Abram the plunder and gets refused down to the last thread and sandal strap, because Abram would rather stay poor than let anyone claim credit for making him rich.
GEN 15:1-21	God shows up in a vision offering himself as a shield and a great reward, and Abram, who has been extremely patient, finally says the quiet part: I have no children and my household manager is going to inherit everything. So God takes him outside and tells him to count the stars. He believes, and it is credited to him as righteousness, a single sentence the rest of the Bible will not stop quoting. Then he asks for proof, and gets a genuinely strange one: animals cut in half, birds of prey to be shooed off, a terrifying darkness, four hundred years of foreign slavery foretold, and a smoking firepot with a flaming torch walking the aisle between the pieces alone.
GEN 16:1-16	Ten childless years in, Sarai proposes a workaround: use my slave. Abram agrees without comment. Hagar gets pregnant and starts looking at Sarai differently, Sarai blames Abram for the plan she suggested, and Abram, at his least impressive, says do whatever you like with her and leaves the room. Hagar runs into the desert, where an angel finds her at a spring, tells her to go back, and promises her more descendants than anyone can count, plus a son who will be a wild donkey of a man permanently at odds with everybody. Hagar responds by naming God, the only person in the Bible to do so, and the name she picks is "the God who sees me".
GEN 17:1-27	Abraham is ninety-nine when God turns up to renew the deal and hand out new names: Abram becomes Abraham, Sarai becomes Sarah, and the sign of the covenant is going to be surgical. Every male, permanently, from eight days old, including staff. When told he'll have a son at a hundred by a ninety-year-old, Abraham falls on his face laughing, then tries to redirect the whole promise onto Ishmael. God says no, it's Isaac, this time next year. Ishmael gets his own blessing as a consolation. Then Abraham circumcises every male in the household, himself at ninety-nine and Ishmael at thirteen, all on the same afternoon.
GEN 18:1-33	Three men appear at Abraham's tent in the midday heat and he mounts a full state banquet in about ten minutes. Over lunch one of them mentions that Sarah will have a son next year. Sarah, eavesdropping from the tent door, laughs at the mechanics of the thing, then denies laughing, and is told, gently but firmly, "yes you did". Then the visitors look toward Sodom, and God, thinking out loud about whether to tell Abraham anything, tells him. What follows is the great haggle: fifty righteous people, then forty-five, forty, thirty, twenty, and finally ten, with Abraham apologising more elaborately at every step and God agreeing every single time.
GEN 19:1-17	Two angels reach Sodom and Lot insists they stay with him rather than sleep in the square, which turns out to be well judged. Every man in the city surrounds the house demanding to rape them. Lot steps outside, shuts the door behind him, and offers his two virgin daughters instead, a sentence nobody has ever managed to make better. The crowd is unimpressed, turns on Lot for playing judge, and is about to break the door down when the angels haul him inside and blind the lot of them. Told to collect his family and run, Lot's sons-in-law think he's joking, and Lot himself dawdles until the angels physically drag four people out by the hand. Don't look back, they say. Next time on Genesis: somebody looks back.
GEN 19:18-38	Told to run to the mountains, Lot negotiates. He'd prefer a nearby town, it's only a little one, and he gets his way, which is how Zoar gets its name. Then sulfur and fire, the plain flattened, and Lot's wife turns around and becomes a pillar of salt in the middle of a sentence. Abraham gets up early to look and sees smoke rising like a furnace. Then Lot, having argued for Zoar, gets frightened of Zoar and moves into a cave, where his two daughters conclude the human race is over and get him drunk two nights running to keep the family line going. The resulting sons are named Moab and Ben-Ammi, and the narrator points out, twice, exactly which neighbouring nations came from that cave.
GEN 20:1-18	Abraham moves to Gerar and runs the sister routine again, unchanged, on a new king, having apparently learned nothing in Egypt. Abimelech takes Sarah, then God turns up in his dream with the opener "you are a dead man". Abimelech, who hasn't touched her, argues his case and God concedes it entirely, admitting he's the one who kept him away. Abimelech then delivers the second righteous pagan lecture of Abraham's career, and Abraham's defence is that she is technically his half-sister and he assumed nobody here feared God. He walks away with sheep, cattle, servants and a thousand pieces of silver, then prays for Abimelech's household, which God had quietly rendered infertile the whole time.
GEN 21:1-34	Isaac is finally born, Abraham is a hundred, and Sarah says God has given her a laugh that everyone will share. Then she sees Ishmael being a teenager at the weaning party and demands both him and his mother be thrown out. Abraham is distressed until God tells him to do what Sarah says. So Hagar is sent into the desert with bread and one skin of water, and when it runs out she puts the boy under a bush and sits a bowshot away so she won't have to watch him die. God hears the boy, opens her eyes, and there's a well right there. Then a change of subject: Abimelech turns up to sign a non-aggression pact, they argue over a stolen well, and seven ewe lambs settle it at Beersheba.
GEN 22:1-24	God tells Abraham to take his son, his only son, whom he loves, and offer him as a burnt offering. Abraham gets up early and saddles the donkey, which is either faith or shock. Three days of walking, and the worst conversation in the book happens on the way up: Isaac notices they have fire and wood but no lamb, and Abraham says God will provide one. He builds the altar, ties up his son, and has the knife in the air before an angel shouts twice to stop him. There is a ram caught in a thicket. He names the place "Yahweh Will Provide", collects an oath and a blessing, and goes home to Beersheba. The chapter then closes with a family update from back home, mainly so you'll notice the name Rebekah.
GEN 23:1-20	Sarah dies at a hundred and twenty-seven, and Abraham, who owns none of the land he was promised, has to buy a grave in it. What follows is a masterclass in Middle Eastern negotiation conducted entirely in compliments: the Hittites insist he take the best tomb they have, Ephron insists on giving him the field for free, Abraham insists on paying full price, Ephron mentions in passing that the land is worth four hundred shekels of silver, what's four hundred shekels between friends, and Abraham weighs it out on the spot. Everything is deeded publicly at the city gate. The first square foot of the promised land the family actually owns is a grave.
GEN 24:1-32	Abraham, now very old, makes his senior servant swear an oath with his hand in an anatomically memorable position: find Isaac a wife from the old country, and whatever happens, do not take my son back there. The servant loads ten camels and goes. At the well outside Nahor he invents a test, the woman who offers to water the camels as well is the one, which is a large ask given a thirsty camel drinks like a bathtub. He hasn't finished praying before Rebekah arrives, waters every camel, and turns out to be from exactly the right family. He produces the gold jewellery immediately. Her brother Laban comes sprinting out to be hospitable the moment he sees how much gold there is, a detail worth filing away. Next time on Genesis: the servant makes a speech.
GEN 24:33-67	Dinner is served and the servant refuses to eat until he has told the entire story, which he then does, at length, repeating the last thirty verses back to the family almost word for word, because the narrator wants you to hear it twice. Laban and Bethuel concede that God has clearly arranged this and there's nothing left to discuss. More gifts appear. The family asks for ten days, the servant says no, so for the first time in the chapter somebody asks Rebekah, and she says "I will go". She rides out, spots a man walking in a field at evening, gets off the camel and veils herself, and that is Isaac. He marries her and loves her, and is comforted after his mother's death.
1JN 1:1-10	John opens without a greeting, without his name and without an address, just a pile of clauses about something heard, seen and handled from the beginning. The message is that God is light with no darkness in him at all. What follows is a set of if-statements that keep closing the same door: claim fellowship while walking in darkness and you are lying, claim to have no sin and you are deceiving yourself, claim you have never sinned and you have made God a liar. The one door left open is confession, and he says God is faithful enough to handle it.
1JN 2:1-29	The point of writing, he says, is that you would not sin, but if you do there is a Counselor. The test of knowing God is keeping his commandments, and the commandment is both old and new at once, which he does not treat as a contradiction. Anyone who hates his brother is walking in the dark and does not know where he is going. There is a poetic aside addressed to children, fathers and young men in turn. Do not love the world, it is passing. It is the final hour, antichrists have already appeared, and the giveaway is that they left: they went out from us, never having belonged.
1JN 3:1-24	Look at the love, he says, that we should be called children of God, and then he admits nobody knows yet what that turns into. Sin is lawlessness. The children of God and the children of the devil are told apart by two things only: doing what is right, and loving your brother. Cain is the counterexample, and John asks why he killed him and then answers his own question. Hating your brother is filed under murder. Then the practical version: seeing someone in need, having the means, and shutting your heart is where the theory gets tested. Love in deed, not in vocabulary.
1JN 4:1-21	Do not believe every spirit, test them, and the test is specific: does it confess that Jesus Christ has come in the flesh. Then the chapter becomes the one everybody quotes. God is love, and the evidence offered is not a feeling but a sending. Nobody has ever seen God, so the visible proof is people loving each other. Perfect love throws fear out, because fear has to do with punishment. And the closing argument is blunt: saying you love God while hating your brother is a lie, since you can see your brother and you cannot see God.
1JN 5:1-21	Believing and loving are welded together here: love the Father and you love his children by definition. His commandments, he adds, are not burdensome, which is the kind of claim that gets tested. Faith is what overcomes the world. Three witnesses agree, the Spirit and the water and the blood, and God's testimony outranks anyone's. The purpose statement arrives near the end: this was written so you may know you have eternal life. Then a genuinely puzzling passage about a sin leading to death, and then a last line, keep yourselves from idols, with no follow-up whatsoever.
1PE 1:1-25	Peter writes to people scattered across five provinces and calls them foreigners, which sets the tone for the entire letter. There is an inheritance kept in reserve for them, and meanwhile grief in various trials, which he compares to fire testing gold, on the grounds that gold perishes and faith does not. The prophets, he says, were researching all this for somebody else's benefit, and angels are craning their necks to see it. So prepare your minds for action, be holy, and remember what you were bought with, which was not silver. Then Isaiah: all flesh is grass, the flower falls, the word stands.
1PE 2:1-25	Drop the malice and the gossip and want the pure milk of the word the way a newborn does. Then a building metaphor: Christ is the living stone the builders rejected, and the readers are living stones stacked into a house, staffed by themselves as the priesthood. Same stone, two outcomes: a cornerstone for some, a thing to trip over for others. They are a chosen race and a holy nation, and also resident foreigners, so behave well enough that the accusations do not stick. Submit to the emperor and his governors. Servants are told to endure even harsh masters, with Christ as the example: cursed, he did not curse back.
1PE 3:1-22	Household advice: wives who might win an unbelieving husband without a word being said, and a preference for a gentle spirit over braided hair and gold. Husbands get one verse with a penalty attached: honor your wife as a joint heir of grace, or your prayers are obstructed. Everyone else is told not to return insult for insult. Then the line the letter is remembered for: always be ready to give an answer for the hope in you, and give it with humility. Suffering for doing good beats suffering for doing wrong. Then the strange part, Christ preaching to spirits in prison, Noah's eight, and baptism.
1PE 4:1-19	You have spent enough time doing what the Gentiles wanted, he says, and lists it in some detail before moving along. Your old friends now find it strange that you will not join in, and take it personally. The end of all things is near, so be sober enough to pray, be earnest in love, and be hospitable without grumbling about it, which is the most realistic instruction in the chapter. Do not be astonished at the fiery trial. Do not suffer as a murderer, a thief, or a meddler in other men's business, a list where one item is not like the others. Judgment starts at home.
1PE 5:1-14	A word to elders from a fellow elder: shepherd the flock because you want to, not for dishonest gain, and do not lord it over anyone. The younger defer, and everybody puts humility on like clothing, since God resists the proud. Throw your worries at him, because he cares. Stay sober and watchful: the devil is prowling like a roaring lion looking for someone to eat, and the comfort offered is that believers everywhere are getting the same treatment. God restores you after a little suffering. Then the greetings, from Silvanus, from the church in Babylon, and from Mark, and a kiss.
2PE 1:1-21	Peter starts with a ladder: faith, then moral excellence, then knowledge, self-control, perseverance, godliness, brotherly affection, love. Lacking them makes you shortsighted and forgetful that you were ever cleaned up. He then explains, in advance, why he keeps repeating himself: he knows he is putting off this tent shortly, and wants it to stick after he has gone. And no, he says, we were not passing on cunningly devised fables. We heard the voice on the holy mountain ourselves. Prophecy is not somebody's private invention, so treat it as a lamp in a dark place until the day dawns.
2PE 2:1-22	An entire chapter of invective against false teachers, and Peter is clearly enjoying himself. Three precedents get stacked up: angels dropped into Tartarus, the flood that spared Noah and seven others, and Sodom and Gomorrah reduced to ash with Lot pulled out worn down. The conclusion is that God knows how to rescue and how to hold the rest for judgment. Then the abuse proper. They are unreasoning animals, they revel in daylight, they follow Balaam, who was corrected by his own donkey. They are wells without water. They promise liberty while being owned themselves. It ends with a dog and its vomit.
2PE 3:1-18	Mockers will show up asking where this promised coming has got to, since everything carries on as it always has. Peter's answer is that they are willfully forgetting the flood. The delay is not slowness, it is patience: one day is a thousand years and the reverse, and he does not want anyone to perish. The day will arrive like a thief, with the elements dissolving in heat, followed by new heavens and a new earth. Then a footnote about Paul, whose letters contain some things that are hard to understand and get twisted, which is a remarkable admission to find in a letter.
BEL 1:1-20	Cyrus takes the kingdom and wants to know why Daniel will not worship Bel, given how much the idol eats: twelve great measures of flour, forty sheep and six firkins of wine a day. Daniel laughs and says it is clay within and brass without and has never eaten anything. Seventy priests stake their lives on it, the king seals the temple with his own signet, and Daniel quietly has the floor strewn with ashes first. That night the priests come in through the trapdoor as usual and eat the lot. Next time on Bel: somebody looks at the floor.
BEL 1:21-42	Footprints in the ash, of men and women and children. The priests show the king their private entrance, he has them killed, and Daniel demolishes Bel and his temple. The king then produces a dragon that indisputably eats and drinks, and Daniel offers to kill it without sword or staff, using boiled lumps of pitch, fat and hair. It bursts. Babylon riots and the king caves, and Daniel spends six days in a den of seven lions deliberately kept hungry. An angel carries Habakkuk over by the hair with dinner. On the seventh day the accusers go in instead.
GEN 25:1-34	Abraham remarries, fathers six more sons, gives everything to Isaac and sends the others east with parting gifts, then dies at a hundred and seventy-five. Isaac and Ishmael bury him together, which is the last time this family agrees on anything. Ishmael's twelve princes are listed and he dies at a hundred and thirty-seven. Rebekah's twins fight before they are born, and the younger arrives gripping the elder's heel. Esau, back from the field and dramatic about starvation, trades his birthright for lentil stew and goes on his way, having eaten.
GEN 26:1-35	Famine, and Isaac heads to Gerar with his father's old routine: she is my sister. It survives right up until Abimelech looks out of a window and sees him being affectionate with his sister. Isaac reaps a hundredfold and gets rich enough that the Philistines fill in his wells out of spite, so he digs them again with his father's names, argues over two more, and finally reaches Rehoboth, which nobody contests. Then Abimelech arrives wanting a treaty with the man he expelled. Esau marries two Hittite women, to nobody's delight.
GEN 27:1-26	Isaac is old and blind and wants venison before he blesses his firstborn. Rebekah, listening at the tent wall, has the whole plot assembled before Esau is out of sight: two goats cooked to taste, Esau's clothes on Jacob, goat skins on his hands and neck. Jacob's only objection is that he might get caught, and his mother volunteers to take the curse. He walks in, claims to be Esau, and credits God with how fast the hunting went. Isaac notes the voice is wrong and the hands are right, and goes with the hands. Next time on Genesis: the other son comes home with dinner.
GEN 27:27-46	The kiss, the smell of the borrowed clothes, and the blessing goes out: dew, grain, wine, and lordship over his brothers. Esau walks in with the real venison about a minute later. Isaac trembles violently and confirms the blessing anyway, because it has already left the building. Esau cries bitterly and points out that his brother's name has now been earned twice over, birthright and blessing. He gets a leftover blessing about swords and yokes, and starts planning a funeral followed by a murder. Rebekah sends Jacob to Haran for a few days and tells Isaac it is really about the local wives.
GEN 28:1-22	Isaac sends Jacob to Paddan Aram to marry inside the family. Esau, working out at last that Canaanite wives are the problem, fixes it by adding a third wife from Ishmael's line. Jacob camps overnight with a rock for a pillow and dreams a stairway with traffic on it and God at the top repeating the family promise. He wakes up frightened, decides the place is the gate of heaven, names it Bethel and stands the pillow up as a monument. His vow is impressively conditional: food, clothing and a safe return home, and then God gets a tenth.
GEN 29:1-35	Jacob reaches a well, rolls the stone off single-handed, kisses Rachel and bursts into tears. He offers Laban seven years of labour for her, and they pass like a few days, which is the last generous thing said about this arrangement. At the wedding Laban substitutes Leah, and Jacob only discovers it in the morning: the man who impersonated his brother is out-conned by his uncle, who explains that the younger simply does not go first here. Another seven years. Leah, unloved, bears Reuben, Simeon, Levi and Judah, each name a small complaint or a hope.
GEN 30:1-25	Rachel, childless, demands children of Jacob, who points out that he is not God. So the sons arrive by proxy: Bilhah bears Dan and Naphtali, Zilpah bears Gad and Asher, and every name is a scoreline in a contest between two sisters. Reuben brings his mother mandrakes from the field, and Rachel buys them with a night of her own husband, who learns of the transaction as he comes in from work. Leah gets Issachar, Zebulun and Dinah out of it. Then God remembers Rachel, Joseph is born, and Jacob announces he would like to go home. Next time on Genesis: a discussion about wages.
GEN 30:26-43	Laban, who has divined that his prosperity is entirely borrowed, does not want Jacob leaving and asks him to name his price. Jacob asks only for the speckled, spotted and black animals, which Laban agrees to and then immediately removes from the flock and parks three days away. Jacob answers with peeled poplar rods propped in the watering troughs, a breeding theory the text reports without comment and which works flawlessly. He arranges matters so the strong stock comes out his and the feeble stays Laban's, and ends up with flocks, servants, camels and donkeys.
GEN 31:1-29	Laban's sons start saying out loud that Jacob has taken everything, and Laban's face stops being friendly. Jacob calls his wives out to the field for a business meeting and makes his case: ten changes of wages, a dream, the God of Bethel. Rachel and Leah agree, on the grounds that their father sold them and spent the money. So they go, Rachel quietly taking his household gods with her, and Jacob leaves without mentioning it. Laban finds out three days later, chases them for seven, and is warned in a dream to say nothing either way. He opens by complaining about the farewell party he never got to throw. Next time on Genesis: somebody searches the luggage.
GEN 31:30-55	Laban's real grievance surfaces: someone has taken his gods. Jacob, who has no idea, cheerfully offers the death penalty for whoever has them, and Laban ransacks every tent while Rachel sits on the idols in a camel saddle and apologises for not standing up. Nothing is found, so Jacob lets twenty years out at once: fourteen for the daughters, six for the flock, ten changes of wages, every loss he covered, the drought and the frost and the lost sleep. Laban's counterargument is that all of it is his anyway. They pile up stones, name the heap in two languages, and part.
GEN 32:1-32	Angels meet Jacob and he names the place God's army, then hears that Esau is coming with four hundred men and immediately splits his camp in half on the logic that one half might survive. He prays, then hedges the prayer with an enormous staggered gift of goats, ewes, camels, cows and donkeys, sent in separate waves so Esau meets the apology several times before he meets the man. Alone at the ford, Jacob wrestles someone until dawn, refuses to let go without a blessing, and limps into the sunrise with a strained hip and a new name.
GEN 33:1-20	After all that preparation, Esau runs up, hugs him, and they both cry, which is the least expected outcome available. Jacob has still arranged his household in ascending order of how much he would mind losing them, with Rachel and Joseph safely last, and bows seven times on the way in. Esau tries to refuse the gift and is talked into taking it, then offers company for the road. Jacob pleads the pace of children and nursing livestock, promises to follow him to Seir, and promptly goes the other way, to Succoth and then Shechem, where he buys a field and builds an altar.
GEN 34:1-31	Shechem rapes Dinah and then asks his father to arrange the marriage, as though that settles it. Jacob says nothing until his sons come in from the fields. They answer with deceit: we will intermarry, but every male in your city must be circumcised first. Hamor sells the deal to his townsmen as a business opportunity and they agree. On the third day, while the men are still in pain, Simeon and Levi kill every one of them and take Dinah out of Shechem's house, and the rest of the brothers plunder the city. Jacob's only stated objection is that it has made him look bad.
GEN 35:1-29	God tells Jacob to go back to Bethel, so he collects the household's foreign gods and earrings and buries them under a tree before travelling. The altar goes up, and God renames him Israel again, in case the first time did not take. Then the losses come one after another: Deborah, Rebekah's nurse, and then Rachel, who dies in childbirth naming the boy Benoni before his father renames him Benjamin. Reuben sleeps with his father's concubine, and the narrator records only that Israel heard of it before moving straight to the list of twelve sons. Isaac dies at a hundred and eighty.
GEN 36:1-43	This is the Edom chapter, and it is a list. Esau's three wives, his five sons, and then the chiefs, who are largely the same names again with a title attached. The Horites of Seir get a roster of their own, including Anah, remembered forever for finding hot springs while pasturing his father's donkeys. Then eight kings of Edom, each recorded dying so the next can reign, with their home cities. Then eleven more chiefs. The only actual news is at the top: Esau moved away because two brothers with that much livestock could not share a country.
GEN 37:1-36	Joseph is seventeen, brings his father a bad report about his brothers, and is dressed in a way that settles any question about who the favourite is. He also tells them both of his dreams, in which they bow to him, and repeats the second to his father as well. They see him coming across the field and decide the dreamer is best discussed in a pit. Reuben talks them down to abandonment, Judah improves the plan into a sale, and Joseph goes to Egypt for twenty pieces of silver. The coat is dipped in goat blood, and Jacob supplies the rest of the story himself.
GEN 38:1-30	Judah leaves his brothers, marries, and has three sons. Two of them die on the page, and rather than risk the third he sends Tamar home to be a widow indefinitely. So Tamar veils herself, sits by the road to Timnah, and takes his signet, his cord and his staff as security for a payment that never arrives. Three months later Judah hears she is pregnant and orders her burned, until she sends his own belongings back and asks him to identify them. He concedes that she is more righteous than he is. Twins arrive, and the one who reaches out first is not the one born first.
GEN 39:1-23	Potiphar buys Joseph and eventually stops supervising anything except his own dinner, because everything Joseph touches prospers. The narrator then mentions, immediately before the next scene, that Joseph is well built and handsome. His master's wife makes the same request daily and is refused daily, on grounds of trust and of God. She finally gets hold of his garment and he runs out of the house leaving the evidence in her hand, which is the second time in his life a piece of clothing testifies falsely against him. He lands in prison, where the warden promptly stops supervising anything too.
GEN 40:1-23	Pharaoh's cup bearer and baker offend him and land in the same prison, where Joseph is assigned to look after them. Both dream on the same night, and neither has anyone to explain it. The vine with three branches means three days and a restored job, and Joseph asks, reasonably, to be mentioned upstairs. The baker, encouraged by how well that went, offers his three baskets and the birds, and is told plainly that it also means three days, in a much worse way. Pharaoh's birthday proves both readings exactly right. The cup bearer goes back to work and forgets him.
GEN 41:1-30	Two years later Pharaoh dreams of seven fat cattle eaten by seven thin ones, then seven good heads of grain swallowed by seven withered ones, and every professional in Egypt draws a blank. The cup bearer chooses this moment to remember his faults. Joseph is hauled out of the dungeon, shaved and re-dressed, and opens by saying the skill is not his. Pharaoh retells both dreams at greater length, with a personal note that he has never seen cattle that ugly. Joseph's reading is that the two dreams are one: seven years of plenty, then seven of famine. Next time on Genesis: the interpretation keeps going.
GEN 41:31-57	Joseph finishes interpreting and then, entirely unasked, adds a national policy: find a discreet and wise man, appoint overseers, take a fifth of everything for seven years. Pharaoh looks around the room for such a man and hires the consultant. Signet ring, fine linen, gold chain, the second chariot, an Egyptian name and a priest's daughter, all in one afternoon, and Joseph is thirty. The grain piles up until he stops counting it, two sons are born, and when the famine arrives Pharaoh's only instruction to a starving country is to go and ask Joseph.
GEN 42:1-21	The famine reaches Canaan and Jacob asks his sons why they are standing about looking at one another. Ten go to Egypt, Benjamin kept home in case of accidents, and they bow to the governor without recognising him. Joseph knows them at once, speaks roughly, and accuses them of espionage, which conveniently extracts a full family inventory: twelve brothers, the youngest at home, and one who is no more. He holds them three days, then proposes terms involving a hostage. They begin telling one another that this is about their brother. Next time on Genesis: the sacks get opened.
GEN 42:22-38	Reuben takes the opportunity to say he told them so. They still do not know that Joseph understands every word, since he has been using an interpreter, and he steps out to weep before coming back to bind Simeon in front of them. Then he has their money secretly returned in their sacks, which turns a mercy into a terror the moment one of them finds it at the lodging place. At home all the bundles turn up at once. Jacob counts his losses out loud, and Reuben offers his own two sons as a guarantee, a proposal so bad his father does not address it.
GEN 43:1-34	The food runs out and Jacob tells his sons to go and buy a little more, as though nothing else were attached to the request. Judah lays out the terms and puts himself up as collateral, and Israel gives in, sending balm, honey, spices, nuts and double the money in case the first lot was an oversight. In Egypt they are brought into Joseph's house and assume they are being arrested, but the steward tells them their God put treasure in their sacks and produces Simeon. Joseph asks after his father, sees Benjamin, and has to leave the room. Then he seats them in exact birth order, which unsettles everyone.
GEN 44:1-34	Joseph has his silver cup planted in Benjamin's sack, along with everyone's money again, then sends the steward after them with an accusation. The brothers are so certain of their innocence that they volunteer a death sentence, which the steward moderates to enslaving whoever holds it. The sacks are searched oldest to youngest, which stretches the moment out on purpose, and the cup is in the youngest. Judah, who once sold a brother for silver, makes a long speech about a father whose life is bound up in this boy's, and offers to be the slave in his place.
GEN 45:1-28	Joseph clears the room and gives up. He weeps loudly enough for Pharaoh's household to hear, announces who he is, and asks whether his father is alive, which his brothers are too terrified to answer. He does the theology for them: you sold me, but God sent me, and five years of famine are still to come. Pharaoh is delighted and sends wagons. Joseph loads them with provisions and changes of clothing, gives Benjamin five times everything, and sends them off with the only instruction that matters: do not quarrel on the road. Jacob believes none of it until he sees the wagons.
GEN 46:1-34	Israel stops at Beersheba to sacrifice and is told not to fear Egypt, in a promise that ends on the detail that Joseph will close his eyes. Then the census, sorted by mother: thirty-three, sixteen, fourteen and seven, seventy souls in all. Judah is sent ahead to find the road into Goshen. Joseph drives out in his chariot and weeps on his father's neck a good while, and Jacob says he can die now. Then Joseph coaches the family for their audience: say livestock, because Egyptians cannot abide shepherds, which is precisely how Goshen becomes theirs.
GEN 47:1-17	Joseph presents five of his brothers to Pharaoh, who asks their occupation, gets the rehearsed answer, and hands over the best of the land plus a job offer for anyone competent. Then Jacob is brought in and blesses Pharaoh, twice, and when asked his age gives one hundred and thirty and calls the years few and evil, which is a remarkable thing to tell a head of state. Meanwhile the famine bites. Joseph gathers every coin in Egypt and Canaan into Pharaoh's house, and when the money is gone he begins accepting livestock instead. Next time on Genesis: the livestock runs out.
GEN 47:18-31	Year two of the money-free economy: the Egyptians come back with nothing left but their bodies and their fields, and propose the sale themselves. Joseph buys the whole country for Pharaoh, moves the population into the cities, and exempts only the priests, who were already on a royal allowance. Everyone receives seed and a permanent fifth to Pharaoh, and thanks him for saving their lives. Israel meanwhile settles in Goshen and multiplies. Jacob lives seventeen years in Egypt, reaching a hundred and forty-seven, and makes Joseph swear an oath not to bury him there.
GEN 48:1-22	Jacob is dying and strengthens himself enough to sit up for the occasion. He adopts Joseph's two Egyptian-born sons outright, so Ephraim and Manasseh now count like Reuben and Simeon, then asks who the boys standing there are, because his eyes have gone. Joseph carefully arranges them so the firstborn falls under the right hand. Jacob crosses his arms anyway. Joseph tries to correct the mistake and gets I know, my son, I know, which in this family is the sound of the younger brother winning again, exactly as it did when Jacob was the one holding a heel.
GEN 49:1-33	Jacob gathers his twelve sons and delivers twelve verdicts dressed as blessings. Reuben loses his standing over his father's bed. Simeon and Levi are cursed for their anger and told they will be scattered. Judah gets the lion and the scepter, Zebulun the harbours, Issachar the flattering description of a strong donkey lying down under a load, Dan a snake biting a horse's heels, Benjamin a ravenous wolf, and Joseph the longest and warmest passage by a distance. Then he gives exact burial instructions, names everyone already in the cave, draws his feet into the bed, and dies.
GEN 50:1-26	Joseph has his father embalmed, which takes forty days, and Egypt mourns for seventy. The convoy that goes up to Canaan carries chariots, horsemen and every elder in Egypt, and the Canaanites watching it name the place after the Egyptians. Once Jacob is buried, the brothers panic and send Joseph a message reporting a deathbed instruction from their father to forgive them. Joseph weeps, tells them he is not in the place of God, and says they meant evil while God meant good. He dies at a hundred and ten, having made them swear about his bones, and is put in a coffin in Egypt.
JAS 1:1-27	James opens with count it all joy about trials, which is a great deal to ask in verse two. Ask God for wisdom, but ask without doubting, because a doubter is a wave and will get nothing. The rich fade like a flower in a scorching wind. Nobody gets to blame God for their own temptation: desire conceives, sin is born, death follows, a genealogy nobody wants. Quick to hear, slow to speak, slow to anger. A hearer who is not a doer is a man who checks the mirror and instantly forgets his own face. Real religion: widows, orphans, staying unstained.
JAS 2:1-26	Picture it: the man with the gold ring gets the good seat, the man in filthy clothes gets a spot by the footstool, and James asks what exactly that makes you. God chose the poor to be rich in faith, and it is the rich who drag you into court. Break the law at one point and you have broken the whole thing. Then the famous argument: telling a cold, hungry person to be warmed and filled while handing them nothing is what faith without works looks like. Demons believe God is one, and they shudder. Abraham acted, Rahab acted. A body without breath is a corpse.
JAS 3:1-18	A warning to aspiring teachers that they will be judged more strictly, followed by three pictures of a tiny thing steering a large one: a bit in a horse's mouth, a rudder on a ship in a gale, and a spark in a forest. The tongue is the spark. Humanity has tamed every kind of animal and cannot tame this one item. The same mouth blesses God and curses people made in his image, which James finds absurd on the face of it, like a spring running fresh and bitter at once. True wisdom is gentle and merciful; the jealous, ambitious kind he calls demonic.
JAS 4:1-17	Where do the fights come from? From wanting things. You do not have because you do not ask, and when you do ask you ask so you can spend it on yourself. Friendship with the world is declared open hostility toward God, which leaves very little middle ground. Resist the devil, draw near to God, and, unusually for a passage of advice, turn your laughter into mourning. Stop judging one another, since there is one lawgiver and it is not you. And stop saying you will spend a year in that city turning a profit: you are a vapor, and you do not know tomorrow.
JAS 5:1-20	It opens with the rich told to weep and howl, their gold corroded and the corrosion set to eat their flesh, and the withheld wages of their harvesters shouting into God's ears. Then a turn: be patient, like a farmer who cannot hurry the rain, because the judge is already standing at the door. Job is named for endurance. Do not swear oaths, let yes mean yes. Suffering, pray; cheerful, sing; sick, call the elders for oil and prayer. Elijah, he notes, was a man with a nature exactly like ours, and it did not rain for three and a half years.
JDT 1:1-16	Nebuchadnezzar turns up here as king of the Assyrians reigning in Nineveh, which is not where history left him, and the narrator does not blink. Four verses go on the cubit-by-cubit dimensions of Arphaxad's walls at Ecbatana. He summons the entire west to his war, and every nation refuses and sends the messengers back in disgrace, because he seemed to them like one man. He wins anyway, runs Arphaxad through with darts in the mountains of Ragau, and goes home to banquet for a hundred and twenty days.
JDT 2:1-28	Eighteenth year, first month, twenty-second day: the king holds a meeting and formally decrees the destruction of everyone who ignored him. Holofernes gets a hundred and twenty thousand foot and twelve thousand horse, plus a standing order that the west should prepare earth and water. The logistics are itemised, camels and mules and sheep and gold out of the king's house, and the army goes out described as locusts. Then a travelogue of arson: Put and Lud destroyed, Midian's tents burned, Damascus fired at wheat harvest. The coast is terrified, correctly.
JDT 3:1-10	The coastal cities send messengers with a surrender so total it is nearly a poem: our houses, our fields, our flocks, our sheepfolds, our cities and the people in them all lie before you, use them as you please. Holofernes accepts, garrisons the high cities, conscripts their best men, and is welcomed with garlands and dances and timbrels. Then he cuts down their sacred groves, since the assignment is to abolish every god so that all nations call on Nebuchadnezzar alone. He camps a month near Esdraelon gathering baggage.
JDT 4:1-15	Judea hears what happened to everyone else's temples and reacts as a nation newly back from exile with a barely reconsecrated sanctuary would. They occupy the hilltops, fortify the villages and store the freshly reaped harvest. Joakim the high priest writes to Bethulia to hold the ascents, which are narrow enough for two men at most. Then everyone puts on sackcloth: men, women, children, hired hands, resident foreigners and, in a detail worth pausing over, the cattle. Sackcloth on the altar too, ashes on every head, and the Lord hears them.
JDT 5:1-24	Holofernes wants to know who these hill people are and why they alone have not come out to meet him. Achior the Ammonite gives him the honest answer, which turns out to be a summary of most of the Old Testament: Chaldean ancestry, Egypt, plagues, a dried sea, conquest, exile and return. His military conclusion follows from it. Find out whether they have sinned against their God, and if they have not, my lord should simply pass by. The tent immediately starts saying he should be killed for it.
JDT 6:1-21	Holofernes answers with the question the book is built around, who is God but Nebuchadnezzar, and promises mountains drunk with blood. Rather than kill Achior he sentences him to being right: tied up and dumped at the foot of Bethulia to die with the people he defended. Israelite slingers drive the escort off, the townspeople untie him and bring him in, and he repeats to the assembly exactly what was said in the war council. They fall down and pray, praise him exceedingly, and give him dinner.
JDT 7:1-17	The army moves on Bethulia, now a hundred and seventy thousand foot and twelve thousand horse, and fills the valley. The defenders look at it and say these men will lick up the face of all the earth. Holofernes rides out to survey the ascents and, more usefully, the springs. His allies then advise against fighting at all: seize the water, sit still, and thirst will hand over the city without costing one man. He likes it. Five thousand Assyrians join the Ammonites at the springs. Next time on Judith: the cisterns run dry.
JDT 7:18-32	The siege becomes arithmetic. Thirty-four days, cisterns emptied, drink rationed by measure, children discouraged, women and young men fainting in the streets and the gateways. The whole population turns on Ozias and the elders with a case that is hard to answer: you should have made peace, slavery is survivable, watching our children die is not. Ozias asks for five more days on the grounds that God will not forsake them utterly, and promises to open the gates if nothing comes. Then he sends everyone back to the walls.
JDT 8:1-17	Enter Judith, with a sixteen-name genealogy, a husband who died of sunstroke standing over the harvest crew, three years and four months of widowhood, a tent on her own roof, and a fasting schedule with careful exemptions for sabbaths, new moons and feast days. She is rich, beautiful, and nobody has a bad word for her. Hearing about the five-day ultimatum, she summons the elders and tells them that putting God on a deadline is not faith but a test, and one they cannot pass. Next time on Judith: she is not finished.
JDT 8:18-36	She keeps going. No one in this generation worships idols, unlike the fathers who fell before their enemies for it. If Bethulia falls the sanctuary falls with it, and the blood will be required of them. And this is testing, the kind God does to the people he keeps close, not vengeance. Ozias concedes every word, then explains that the people were thirsty and made him swear. So Judith stops arguing: stand at the gate tonight, I am going out with my maid, and do not ask what I intend.
JDT 9:1-14	Judith prays face down in ashes and sackcloth, timed to the hour the evening incense goes up in Jerusalem. She opens on her ancestor Simeon and the sword given to avenge a violated sister, which tells you where this is going, then works through God's foreknowledge, the Assyrian faith in horse and shield and sling, and their intention to defile the sanctuary. The requests are specific: break their strength, strike the prince with his servant, and bring down their stateliness by the hand of a woman. She asks that her deceit be the weapon.
JDT 10:1-23	The transformation is inventoried item by item: sackcloth off, washed, anointed, hair braided, tiara, the good clothes from her married life, sandals, chains, bracelets, rings, earrings, all of it, expressly to deceive the eyes of every man who sees her. Her maid packs food she can lawfully eat. The elders at the gate are struck dumb. The Assyrian patrol takes her, swallows her story about betraying the hill country, and escorts her in with a hundred men while the camp turns out to stare. Holofernes rises from a bed under a jewelled canopy.
JDT 11:1-23	Holofernes opens gallantly by explaining that the war is Israel's own fault for slighting him. Judith then delivers a masterpiece with a false bottom: she flatters Nebuchadnezzar, flatters the general, endorses Achior's theology as true, and reports that the starving city is about to eat the consecrated food and forfeit its protection. She offers to pray in the valley each night and tell him the moment they sin. Every sentence is technically defensible and the whole thing is a lie. The room is enchanted, and he promises her a house in the palace.
JDT 12:1-20	Offered the general's own meat and wine, Judith declines and eats what she brought, which quietly establishes the routine that lets her leave camp every night to bathe and pray unchallenged. On the fourth day Holofernes throws a private party for his household staff and sends the eunuch Bagoas to fetch her, on the reasoning that letting such a woman go untouched would make them a laughing stock. She agrees at once, calling it her joy to the day of her death. He is delighted, and drinks more wine than on any day since he was born.
JDT 13:1-20	Everyone clears out and Holofernes is unconscious. Judith takes his own scimitar off the bedrail, prays for strength, takes him by the hair and strikes his neck twice. The head goes into the food bag, the canopy comes down as evidence, and the two women walk out through the camp on the nightly prayer walk nobody questions any more. At Bethulia's gate she wakes the town, holds it up, and states exactly what happened and what did not: he never touched her. Ozias blesses her above all the women on earth.
JDT 14:1-19	Judith issues the tactical orders: hang the head on the battlement, arm at dawn, march down as if to attack, and do not actually attack. The enemy will run to Holofernes' tent, find nothing, and panic. First she has Achior brought in. He sees the head in a man's hand, faints, and on being revived converts and joins Israel. At sunrise the plan runs exactly as written. Bagoas knocks discreetly, assuming his lord is occupied, then goes in and finds the body on the threshold with the head gone.
JDT 15:1-13	The panic works. The camp empties in every direction, every fighting man in Bethulia comes down after them, and Jerusalem, the hill country, Gilead and Galilee join in and chase them past Damascus. Plundering the camp takes thirty days and enriches everyone exceedingly. Joakim the high priest comes up from Jerusalem specifically to see Judith and call her the exaltation of Jerusalem and the great glory of Israel. They give her Holofernes' entire tent and furniture, which she loads onto a mule, and she leads the women of Israel in a dance.
JDT 16:1-25	Judith's song makes the point plainly. Asshur came down from the north with ten thousands, and no young man, no son of the Titans and no tall giant brought their champion down: the daughter of Merari did, with her face. Her sandal ravished his eye, and the scimitar concluded matters. Then thanksgiving in Jerusalem, three months of feasting, and Holofernes' canopy dedicated to the Lord. She goes home, refuses every suitor for the rest of her life, frees her maid, and dies at a hundred and five. Nobody troubles Israel again for a long time.
LEV 1:1-17	The book opens with God speaking from a tent about how to burn a bull correctly. The animal must be male and without defect, the offerer lays a hand on its head, and then everything is specified: flay it, joint it, wash the legs and innards, stack the wood just so. The poor bring birds, whose heads are wrung off and whose crops are thrown east onto the ash heap. Three times the phrase 'a pleasant aroma to Yahweh' arrives like a chorus.
LEV 2:1-16	Now the grain offering, which is fine flour, oil and frankincense, and comes in oven, griddle and pan variants for reasons never explained. The priest burns a token handful and keeps the entire rest, an arrangement worth noticing. Yeast and honey are banned from the fire; salt is compulsory, called the salt of the covenant, and required with every offering you bring. First fruits go in as roasted grain still in the ear.
LEV 3:1-17	Peace offerings, and here for once the animal may be male or female, which counts as flexibility. What matters is the fat: the fat over the innards, the two kidneys, the cover on the liver, and with a sheep the entire tail cut close to the backbone. The same anatomical list is recited three times, once for cattle, once for sheep, once for goats. It closes with a permanent rule: no fat, no blood, ever.
LEV 4:1-19	Sin here is defined as the accidental kind, the rule you broke without noticing. If the anointed priest is the one who did it, the price is a bull: blood dipped on a finger and flicked seven times toward the veil, dabbed on the horns of the incense altar, poured out at the base, and the entire carcass hauled outside the camp to the ash dump and burned. If the whole assembly sinned without realising, the elders lay hands on a second bull and it goes the same way. Next time on Leviticus: that bull, still.
LEV 4:20-35	The scale slides by rank. A ruler who sins unwittingly owes a male goat; an ordinary person owes a female goat, or a female lamb instead. The procedure barely changes, blood on the horns of the altar with a finger, the rest poured at the base, the fat burned like a peace offering. The sentence that closes each case is the one that matters: he will be forgiven. Said four times, in case anyone was reading only for the anatomy.
LEV 5:1-19	A list of ways to be guilty without meaning to: hearing the call to testify and staying quiet, touching a carcass and forgetting, swearing something rash and remembering later. Confession comes first, then the offering, which scales down honestly with what you have. A lamb, or two pigeons, or if you cannot manage birds, a tenth of an ephah of plain flour with no oil and no frankincense on it, because it is a sin offering and not a treat. Mishandle holy things and it is a ram plus restitution plus a fifth.
LEV 6:1-30	Fraud is filed as a sin against God rather than as paperwork: return what you took, add a fifth, and do it on the day you are found guilty. Then housekeeping. The altar fire must never go out, which is stated three separate times. The priest changes clothes to carry the ashes outside the camp. Priests eat the leftovers of everyone else's grain offering, but a priest's own offering is burned entirely and never eaten. An earthen pot that boiled a sin offering is smashed; a bronze one is scoured.
LEV 7:1-19	Trespass offerings first, then a note on priestly income: the priest who handles your burnt offering keeps the hide, and whoever bakes the grain offering keeps the baked thing. Peace offerings come with an expiry date. A thanksgiving sacrifice must be eaten the day it is offered; a vow or freewill offering gets two days, and anything left on the third day is burned. Eat it on day three anyway and the whole sacrifice is void, retroactively. Next time on Leviticus: who is allowed to eat any of it.
LEV 7:20-38	Eat sacrificial meat while unclean and you are cut off from your people, which is the strongest sanction available and gets used twice on one page. Fat and blood are permanently off the menu, though fat from an animal that died on its own may be used for anything except eating. Then the priests' portion is fixed by gesture: the breast is waved, the right thigh is heaved, and both belong to Aaron's family forever. A closing line files the entire set of laws under Sinai.
LEV 8:1-21	After seven chapters of instruction, someone finally does something. Moses assembles the whole congregation to watch him dress his brother: coat, sash, robe, ephod, breastplate with the Urim and Thummim inside it, turban with a gold plate on the front. Then oil on the tabernacle, seven sprinkles on the altar, oil poured over Aaron's head. A bull dies for sin and a ram is burned whole, and the refrain 'as Yahweh commanded Moses' lands at the end of nearly every step. Next time on Leviticus: there is a second ram.
LEV 8:22-36	The second ram introduces the strangest gesture in the book: blood dabbed on the right earlobe, the right thumb and the right big toe, first of Aaron, then of each son. Cakes and fat and the right thigh are loaded into their hands and waved. Moses keeps the breast for himself, which the text notes and does not defend. Then the closing instruction: seven days and nights at the door of the tent, do not go out, so that you do not die.
LEV 9:1-24	Day eight, and Aaron works the altar himself for the first time, beginning with a sin offering on his own account before he can do anything for anyone else. Calf, ram, goat, bull, grain, in that order, all of it exactly as prescribed. Then he lifts his hands and blesses the people, Moses goes in with him and they come out together, and the glory appears. Fire comes out from Yahweh and takes the offering off the altar. The people shout and fall on their faces. Remember that fire.
LEV 10:1-20	Nadab and Abihu offer fire God did not ask for, and the fire that came out yesterday to accept the offering comes out again and kills them. Moses gives his brother a theological explanation. Aaron says nothing. The bodies are carried out of the camp in their coats, and their father and remaining brothers are forbidden to mourn. Rules follow: no wine before entering the tent, and the priests are to teach the difference between holy and common. At the end Moses finds a goat burned rather than eaten and is angry, and Aaron asks quietly whether eating it today would have pleased anyone. Moses accepts that.
LEV 11:1-28	The dietary rules run on a two-part test, chews the cud and has a split hoof, and the memorable part is the near misses: camel, cony and hare all chew but fail on feet, and the pig has the feet but does not chew. Water creatures need fins and scales. The forbidden bird list runs to twenty-odd species and files the bat among them. Insects are out unless they hop, which lets locusts, katydids, crickets and grasshoppers through. Touch a carcass and you are unclean until evening. Next time on Leviticus: the lizards.
LEV 11:29-47	A roll call of eight small unclean creatures, mostly lizards, then a surprisingly careful set of rules about where one might land after it dies. Onto a wooden bowl: soak it. Into a clay pot: smash the pot, and whatever was inside it. Onto an oven: break the oven. Into a spring or cistern: the water stays clean. Onto dry seed: fine. Onto seed you had already watered: not fine. The chapter then stops listing and gives a reason instead: be holy, because I am holy.
LEV 12:1-8	Eight verses on purification after childbirth. A son costs seven days of uncleanness and thirty-three more of purification, with circumcision on the eighth day in the middle of it; a daughter doubles both numbers to fourteen and sixty-six, and the text offers no explanation for the doubling. At the end she brings a lamb and a bird, or two birds if she cannot afford the lamb. The concession is the one this book keeps making: the rule holds, the price does not have to.
LEV 13:1-30	The priest becomes a dermatologist. The criteria are specific: has the hair in the patch turned white, does the spot look deeper than the surrounding skin, has it spread since last week. When nothing is certain, shut the person away seven days and look again, then seven days more. The strangest ruling comes early, since a man covered head to foot and entirely white is declared clean, and one patch of raw flesh reverses it. Burns, boils and an itch in the beard each get their own protocol. Next time on Leviticus: it gets into the laundry.
LEV 13:31-59	More diagnostics, with a shave involved: everything comes off except the patch itself. Two rulings are pure reassurance, since a dull white rash is harmless and going bald is explicitly clean, stated twice. Then the chapter turns hard. Anyone genuinely infected wears torn clothes, leaves his hair loose, covers his lip, calls out 'Unclean, unclean' and lives alone outside the camp. The remaining verses transfer the whole procedure to a piece of cloth: quarantine the garment seven days, wash it, tear out the spot, burn it if the colour returns.
LEV 14:1-25	The healed person is met outside the camp, and the rite is strange and rather beautiful: two birds, one killed over fresh water in a clay pot, the other dipped in its blood along with cedar, scarlet yarn and hyssop, then released into open country. He shaves, washes, waits seven days, then shaves everything again including his eyebrows. On the eighth day, blood on the right ear, thumb and toe, then oil on the same three places and the rest poured on his head. A cheaper version is provided. Next time on Leviticus: that oil, again, slower.
LEV 14:26-57	The budget cleansing is written out again in full, ear and thumb and toe and oil, on the principle that being poor should not buy an abbreviated ceremony. Then mildew in a house, which God says he will personally put there. The priest orders the house emptied before he inspects it, so its contents are not condemned along with it. Seven days shut up, then pull the affected stones, scrape the walls, replaster. If it returns, the house comes down. If it clears, the house gets the two-bird rite and one bird goes free over the city.
LEV 15:1-33	A full chapter on bodily discharges, worked out with the same unhurried thoroughness as everything else: the bed, the chair, the saddle, anyone who touches any of them, and anyone the affected person spits on. Clay vessels are broken, wooden ones rinsed. Men and women get parallel treatment, seven days and then two birds in each case, and ordinary marital sex simply means both of them bathe and wait until evening. The stated reason arrives at the end and is not disgust: keep them separate so they do not die by defiling the tabernacle.
LEV 16:1-17	This chapter is dated: after the death of Aaron's two sons. The high priest may not walk into the Most Holy Place whenever he likes. He bathes, wears plain linen rather than gold, and brings a bull for his own sins before he touches anyone else's. Two goats are set before God and lots are cast, one for Yahweh and one for the scapegoat. He carries in coals and incense so the smoke covers the mercy seat and he does not die, then sprinkles blood with his finger seven times. No one else may be inside. Next time on Leviticus: the other goat.
LEV 16:18-34	The live goat carries the whole weight of it. Aaron lays both hands on its head, confesses every iniquity of Israel over it, and a man appointed in advance leads it out into the wilderness, to a solitary land, and releases it. Everyone involved washes afterward, including the man who took the goat and the man who burns the carcasses outside the camp. The date is fixed and permanent: seventh month, tenth day, no work of any kind, for the native-born and the foreigner alike, once a year.
LEV 17:1-16	Every animal slaughtered has to be brought to the tent door, and killing one out in the open field counts as shedding blood, which is a startling way to describe dinner. The stated purpose is to stop people sacrificing to the goat idols they have apparently been sacrificing to. Blood is forbidden four separate ways, with the reason stated plainly: the life of the flesh is in the blood, and the blood is what makes atonement. Hunted game must be drained and the blood covered with dust. Eat something that died on its own and you wash and wait until evening.
LEV 18:1-30	The chapter opens by naming two places, the Egypt behind them and the Canaan ahead, and telling Israel to live like neither. Then a long list of relatives no one may approach, worked through methodically by degree: mother, father's wife, sister, granddaughter, aunt by blood and aunt by marriage, daughter-in-law, sister-in-law. Child sacrifice to Molech sits on the same list, and so does adultery. The closing image is deliberately physical: the land grew sick of the people living there before and vomited them out, and it can do that again.
LEV 19:1-37	The most quotable chapter in the book, and the most miscellaneous. Leave the corners of your field unharvested for the poor. Pay a hired worker before nightfall. Do not curse the deaf or put a stumbling block in front of the blind, since neither of them can catch you at it. Judge without favouring the poor or the great. Love your neighbour as yourself, and sixteen verses later, love the foreigner as yourself, on the ground that you were foreigners in Egypt. Filed in among all this: no mixed seed, no mixed cloth, no tattoos, no trimming the beard's edge, and honest weights.
LEV 20:1-27	Chapter eighteen listed the offences; this one attaches the penalties, and the escalation is the point. Giving a child to Molech means stoning, and if the community hides its eyes, God says he will deal with the community. Cursing a parent, adultery and several degrees of incest carry death. Some cases get a lesser sentence instead, being cut off, or dying childless, which is a real punishment in a book this concerned with descent. It ends where eighteen ended, with the land ready to vomit, and with the call to be holy.
LEV 21:1-24	Rules for priests, tightening as the rank rises. An ordinary priest may attend the funeral of a parent, child or sibling, but no one further out. The high priest may not go near a dead body at all, including his mother's and his father's. Then a list of physical defects barring a descendant of Aaron from the altar, running from blindness and lameness through a flat nose to scabs. The chapter is careful about one thing: he is disqualified from serving, not from eating. He still gets the holy bread. He simply may not approach the veil.
LEV 22:1-33	Two halves, both about not treating holy things casually. First, who may eat the priests' portion: not a guest, not a hired hand, not a daughter who married outside, but yes to a slave the priest bought and to a daughter come home widowed or divorced without a child. Then the animals, which must be flawless, with a list of disqualifying flaws exhaustive down to warts and running sores. Two rules at the end are almost tender: a newborn animal stays with its mother seven days, and no animal may be killed on the same day as its young.
LEV 23:1-21	The festival calendar, and it opens with the Sabbath, which is to say the first appointed feast is the one that happens every week. Passover on the fourteenth of the first month, unleavened bread from the fifteenth for seven days. Then the sheaf of first fruits, waved, before anyone may eat any of the new harvest. Count seven Sabbaths from that day, fifty days, and bring two loaves, specified as baked with yeast, in a book that has spent chapters keeping yeast off the altar. Next time on Leviticus: the autumn ones.
LEV 23:22-44	The gleaning law about leaving the corners of the field reappears, dropped straight into the middle of the calendar, as though the festivals do not count without it. Then the autumn cluster: trumpets on the first of the seventh month, the Day of Atonement on the tenth with no work and self-denial, and on the fifteenth the feast of tents. That last comes with instructions to take palm branches and willows from the brook, rejoice for seven days, and sleep in temporary shelters, so the next generation learns what their ancestors lived in.
LEV 24:1-23	It begins with housekeeping: olive oil for a lamp that never goes out, twelve loaves in two rows of six on the gold table, replaced every Sabbath. Then a fight breaks out in the camp and a man blasphemes the Name. He is held in custody until God's will can be asked, which stands out in a book of fixed penalties. His mother is named, Shelomith daughter of Dibri of Dan; his father was Egyptian. Everyone who heard him lays hands on his head and he is stoned. The verdict widens into eye for eye, and one law for foreigner and native-born alike.
