LEV 25:1-29	The land itself gets a Sabbath: sow six years, leave the seventh alone, and whatever comes up on its own is open to the servants, the foreigners, the livestock and the wild animals equally. Count seven of those cycles and the fiftieth year is jubilee, announced by trumpet on the Day of Atonement, when everyone returns to their own property and their own family. Property is therefore priced in remaining harvests rather than acres. Someone asks what they will eat in year seven and is told year six will carry three. Next time on Leviticus: what happens to a house, and to a person.
LEV 25:30-55	A city house sold and not bought back within a year stays sold forever, while a village house counts as farmland and comes back at jubilee. Levite property is always redeemable. Then the poor: hold your brother up, lend him nothing at interest, sell him nothing at a profit. An Israelite who sells himself is a hired worker and not a slave, and goes free at jubilee with his children. Foreign slaves are inheritable property, which the text says flatly. Redeeming an Israelite sold to a resident foreigner is priced by the years left.
LEV 26:1-25	The covenant terms, laid out in two columns. Obey and the rains come on time, the threshing runs into the grape harvest and the grape harvest into sowing, five of you chase a hundred, and God says he will walk among you. Refuse and it escalates in rounds, each opening with 'if you still will not listen' and each adding seven times more: fever, enemies eating your crop, fleeing when nobody pursues, a sky like iron and soil like brass, wild animals, then sword and pestilence. Next time on Leviticus: it gets worse before it turns.
LEV 26:26-46	The curses reach their worst here, and the images are precise rather than rhetorical: ten women baking in a single oven and handing bread back by weight, cities in ruins, people scattered among the nations and so frightened that the sound of a driven leaf sends them running. Cannibalism is named plainly. One strange consolation runs underneath it: the emptied land finally gets the Sabbaths it was never given. Then the chapter turns. If they confess, God remembers Jacob, Isaac, Abraham and the land, and says he will not reject them utterly even there.
LEV 27:1-17	The book ends on an appendix about vows, and mostly about the price list. A person promised to God has a fixed valuation by age and sex, from fifty shekels for a man in his prime down to three for an infant girl, and anyone who cannot pay is assessed by the priest at what he can. Animals may not be swapped, and an attempted upgrade forfeits both animals. Houses are appraised, and a field is valued by how much barley seed it takes to sow. Next time on Leviticus: the paperwork, concluded.
LEV 27:18-34	More valuations, all prorated to the jubilee, all redeemable at the assessed price plus a fifth. A firstborn animal cannot be dedicated, because it already belongs to God, which quietly closes a loophole. Then the tithe of the herd, counted by whatever passes under the shepherd's rod, with the owner forbidden to inspect the tenth animal or to trade it for a better one. So the book that opened with a bull at the tent door ends with a shepherd counting sheep and being told not to cheat.
MAN 1:1-15	Fifteen verses of one man's apology, front-loaded with everything he can say about God before he has to say anything about himself: heaven and earth made, the sea bound by a word of command, the deep shut up and sealed. Then the pivot. Repentance was not appointed for Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, who did not need it, but for me. His sins outnumber the sand of the sea, he is bowed down under iron bands, he set up abominations. He bows the knee of his heart, says he has sinned twice over, and asks not to be destroyed with his iniquities.
MRK 1:1-45	Mark skips the nativity entirely and opens at a run. John arrives in camel hair on a locust diet, announces someone stronger, and baptizes him; the sky tears open and says so out loud. Forty days in the wilderness get one sentence. Four fishermen abandon their nets immediately, a word Mark will be using constantly. Demons recognize him and are told to be quiet. A cleansed leper is told to say nothing and immediately tells everyone, which ends Jesus walking into towns.
MRK 2:1-28	The house is so full that four men take the roof apart and lower their friend through the hole, which Jesus reads as faith rather than vandalism. He forgives sins first, which the scribes silently object to, and heals the man largely to settle the argument. Then he recruits a tax collector, eats with the wrong crowd, declines to make his disciples fast, and observes that nobody patches old cloth with new. On the Sabbath his disciples snack, and he cites David doing worse.
MRK 3:1-35	A man with a withered hand becomes a test case, and Jesus answers with a question nobody will touch, then heals him while angry. The Pharisees respond by teaming up with the Herodians to kill him, which is quick work for chapter three. He keeps a boat on standby to avoid being crushed, and appoints twelve, including two he nicknames Sons of Thunder and one the narrator flags on the spot as the betrayer. His family arrives to collect him on grounds of insanity. The scribes go with demon possession. He points out that Satan evicting Satan is bad business.
MRK 4:1-41	He teaches from a boat because the crowd will not give him room, and the sower parable does what parables do: everyone hears it, nobody asks. Privately he explains that the seed is the word and the soils are people, then adds that nothing hidden stays hidden. Two more seed parables follow, including the mustard seed that starts smallest and ends big enough for birds. That evening the boat crosses in a storm and he sleeps through it, is woken with a complaint rather than a request, and tells the weather to be quiet.
MRK 5:1-43	A man living in the tombs, who has snapped every chain anyone ever put on him, meets Jesus, and the demons introduce themselves as Legion. They negotiate a transfer into two thousand pigs, which promptly drown. The town weighs a healed man against its livestock and asks Jesus to leave. On the other shore a synagogue ruler begs for his dying daughter, and the trip is interrupted by a woman who has spent twelve years and all her money on doctors and is healed by touching his coat. Word arrives that the girl is dead. He raises her, then says feed her.
MRK 6:1-27	The hometown looks at him and can only see the carpenter whose sisters they know, so he does almost nothing there and marvels at their unbelief. He sends the twelve out in pairs with a staff and nothing else, and they preach and cast out demons. Then Herod hears the reports and concludes, unhelpfully, that John the Baptizer is back from the dead. Mark rewinds: Herodias wanted John dead, Herod protected him because he liked listening to him, and then a birthday, a dance and a public oath handed her exactly what she wanted. Next time on Mark: the platter arrives.
MRK 6:28-56	The head is delivered and John's disciples bury the body. The apostles come back full of reports, so Jesus proposes a rest, which the crowd defeats by running around the lake on foot and arriving first. Five loaves and two fish feed five thousand men and leave twelve baskets. He sends the disciples ahead, goes up to pray, then walks out to their struggling boat and intends to pass by, which is not a detail anyone expects. They take him for a ghost. Mark's verdict on all this amazement: they had not understood about the loaves.
MRK 7:1-37	The Pharisees open on hand washing, and Mark stops to explain the custom for readers who would not know it. Jesus escalates from washing to Corban, the loophole letting a man dedicate to God the support he owed his parents, and calls the whole thing voiding God's word with tradition. Nothing going in defiles a person; the problem is what comes out, followed by a list. Then a Syrophoenician woman out-argues him about dogs and crumbs and wins, and a deaf man with a speech impediment gets fingers, spit and one word: Ephphatha.
MRK 8:1-38	Four thousand this time, seven loaves, seven baskets left. The Pharisees ask for a sign, and he sighs deeply and refuses. In the boat he warns about the yeast of the Pharisees and of Herod, and the disciples conclude he is annoyed about the forgotten lunch, which prompts the sharpest quiz in the gospel: how many baskets, and how many, and do you still not understand. A blind man at Bethsaida needs two attempts and reports seeing men like walking trees. Peter says Christ, is told to keep it quiet, then rebukes Jesus about dying and gets called Satan.
MRK 9:1-26	Six days later three of them go up a mountain and watch him change, with Moses and Elijah alongside. Peter, whom Mark excuses on the grounds that he had no idea what to say, proposes building tents. A cloud settles the matter. On the way down they argue about Elijah and are told to keep quiet about all of it. At the bottom the other nine have failed to help a boy with a mute spirit and the scribes are enjoying it. The father says if you can do anything, and Jesus catches the if. The father's reply is the best line in the chapter: I believe, help my unbelief. Next time on Mark: the boy looks dead.
MRK 9:27-50	He takes the boy's hand and stands him up, then privately tells the disciples that this kind takes prayer. He predicts his death again; they do not understand and are too afraid to ask, and then spend the walk arguing about which of them is greatest. He sits down, tells them first means last, and puts a child in the middle. John reports having stopped an outsider casting out demons, clearly expecting praise, and is told not to. Then millstones, and the hand, the foot and the eye, and a closing note about salt, which after all that is nearly a relief.
MRK 10:1-29	Pharisees open with divorce as a test, and he takes it back past Moses to the beginning: two become one, so nobody separates them. Children are brought to him and the disciples try to manage the diary; he is indignant about it. Then a man runs up, kneels, asks about eternal life, has kept everything since childhood, and is looked at with love and told to sell the lot. He leaves sad, because he owns a great deal. Camels and needles follow, and the disciples ask who could possibly be saved. Peter notes that they, at least, have left everything. Next time on Mark: what that gets you.
MRK 10:30-52	A hundred times over, he says, houses and family and land, and then adds with persecutions, which is not how anyone else would have pitched it. Walking up to Jerusalem he spells out the mocking, the spitting, the flogging and the killing. James and John respond by asking for the two best seats. The other ten are furious, presumably for not asking first. He explains that greatness here runs the other way and that he came to serve. At Jericho a blind beggar shouts, is told to be quiet, shouts louder, throws off his cloak, and follows him down the road.
MRK 11:1-33	Two disciples are sent to untie a donkey with a cover story ready. Coats and branches go down, hosannas go up, and then he arrives at the temple, looks around at everything, notes that it is late, and leaves. Next morning he curses a fig tree that was never going to have figs in that season, then clears the temple and quotes a house of prayer at people running a market inside it. The tree is dead by morning, which Peter thinks worth mentioning. When the chief priests ask who authorized any of this, he asks them one question about John and they discover they cannot answer it in public.
MRK 12:1-23	A parable about a vineyard whose tenants beat the servants, kill several more, and finally kill the owner's son on the theory that this gets them the inheritance. The chief priests correctly identify themselves as the tenants and would arrest him if the crowd were not there. Next the Pharisees and Herodians arrive with compliments and a tax question built to have no safe answer; he asks whose face is on the coin. Then the Sadducees, who do not believe in resurrection, propose a woman widowed seven times over by seven brothers. Next time on Mark: whose wife is she.
MRK 12:24-44	He tells the Sadducees they are mistaken about both the Scriptures and God, since the raised do not marry and God calls himself the God of Abraham in the present tense. A scribe asks which command is greatest, gets love God and love your neighbor, agrees enthusiastically, and is told he is not far from the kingdom. After that nobody dares ask anything. He raises a puzzle about David calling his own son Lord, warns about scribes who like long robes and devour widows' houses, then sits down opposite the treasury and watches a widow put in two small coins.
MRK 13:1-37	A disciple admires the stonework, and Jesus tells him not one stone will be left on another, which effectively ends the tour. On the Mount of Olives four of them ask when, and get an answer that mostly declines to say when. Wars, earthquakes, famines: beginnings, not the end. They will be handed over, and should not rehearse a defense. When the abomination stands where it should not, run without stopping for a coat. False christs, a darkened sun, the Son of Man in clouds. As for the day and hour, not even the Son knows, so the instruction is simply to watch.
MRK 14:1-34	Two days before Passover the chief priests look for a quiet way to take him. At Bethany a woman pours a jar of costly nard over his head and is scolded for the waste; he defends her, saying she has anointed his body for burial in advance. Judas goes to the priests, who are glad and offer money. The upper room is found exactly as described. At the table he says one of the twelve will hand him over, then gives them bread and a cup. They sing a hymn and walk out to the Mount of Olives, where Peter swears he will never deny him. At Gethsemane he says his soul is sorrowful to the point of death. Next time on Mark: he prays alone.
MRK 14:35-72	He falls on the ground and prays that the hour might pass, and then says not what I desire but what you desire. Three times he comes back and finds them asleep. Judas arrives with an armed crowd and identifies him with a kiss. Someone cuts off a servant's ear. Everyone runs, including a young man who leaves his linen cloth behind and flees naked. At the high priest's house the witnesses contradict one another, so the high priest asks him directly, and he answers directly. They condemn him, spit on him and strike him. Out in the courtyard Peter denies him three times, the rooster crows a second time, and he weeps.
MRK 15:1-47	Pilate asks if he is the King of the Jews, gets three words, and then nothing at all, which surprises him. Offered a choice, the crowd asks for Barabbas, who had killed during the insurrection. Pilate, wanting to please them, hands Jesus over. Soldiers dress him in purple, crown him with thorns, salute him and beat him. Simon of Cyrene is pulled out of the crowd to carry the cross. He refuses the wine mixed with myrrh. From the sixth hour darkness covers the land until the ninth, when he cries out that God has forsaken him, and dies. The temple veil tears from top to bottom. A centurion says what nobody else has said. Joseph of Arimathaea asks Pilate for the body.
MRK 16:1-20	Three women come at sunrise with spices and one unsolved logistical problem: the stone. It is already rolled back. A young man in white tells them he has risen, and to tell the disciples, and Peter by name. They run and say nothing to anyone, because they are afraid, which is a strange place to end a gospel. What follows records the appearances anyway: to Mary Magdalene, who is disbelieved, to two walking into the country, who are also disbelieved, and finally to the eleven at dinner, who get told off about it. Then the commission, the signs, and the ascension.
PS2 1:1-7	Seven verses of David giving his own account of himself. Youngest brother, sent out to the sheep, made his own instrument and tuned the lute himself. Who would tell the Lord about it? The Lord hears it himself, as it turns out. So an angel fetches him from the flock and anoints him with the anointing oil, passing over brothers who were taller and better looking. Then the whole Goliath business compressed into two lines: the Philistine cursed him by his idols, and he drew the man's own sword and beheaded him, taking the reproach off Israel.
PSA 1:1-6	The book opens with a posture chart: don't walk with the wicked, don't stand with sinners, don't sit with scoffers, a slide into bad company measured by how comfortable you get. The alternative is a man who chews on the law day and night and grows like a tree planted by water, fruit on schedule, leaf intact. The wicked get one image and it is chaff, which the wind handles without effort.
PSA 2:1-12	Nations rage, kings hold a summit, and the agenda item is throwing off God's ropes. The response from heaven is laughter, which is a rougher answer than anger would be. Then the decree: a king installed on Zion, told to ask for the nations as an inheritance and issued an iron rod for the pottery. It closes by advising those same kings to be wise, serve with fear, and take refuge before the fuse burns down.
PSA 3:1-8	The complaint is a headcount: enemies multiplying, and a chorus insisting God has no help for him. The rebuttal is a man who lay down, slept, and woke up, offered as evidence of protection and, in fairness, hard to argue with. Ten thousand surrounding him do not change the arithmetic. The prayer for the enemies is specific: cheekbones struck, teeth broken.
PSA 4:1-8	A night prayer, filed under grievance. He asks how long his reputation will be someone else's entertainment, tells the offenders to lie on their beds and think about it quietly, and notes that plenty of people are asking who will show them anything good at all. His own answer is unfashionable: more gladness than a bumper harvest of grain and new wine. Then he goes to sleep, which is the point.
PSA 5:1-12	Morning shift. He lays out his requests and then watches, like a man who expects a reply. What follows is an inventory of things God cannot stand: arrogance, lies, bloodshed, flattery. Their throat is an open tomb, which is not a remark about their breath. He asks that their own schemes trip them, and that everyone taking refuge get to shout about it. He is going to the temple either way.
PSA 6:1-10	He asks not to be corrected while God is angry, which is a reasonable request in any relationship. Then the case for staying alive: nobody thanks you from Sheol, so healing him is arguably in God's own interest. He floods his bed nightly and his eyes are wearing out with grief. In the last three verses the tone flips completely. God has heard, and the workers of iniquity can leave.
PSA 7:1-17	He offers to be trampled into the dust by his enemy if he actually did the thing he is accused of, which is either innocence or excellent bluffing. Then he asks God to convene court and rule on his integrity. God turns up as a judge with a standing daily grievance, a sharpened sword and arrows already lit. The wicked man's downfall is self-inflicted: pregnant with mischief, he digs a pit and falls into it.
PSA 8:1-9	A man looks up at the moon and stars, calls them finger-work, and asks the obvious follow-up: why would anything that big pay attention to us. The answer is not modest. Humans come out a little lower than the angels, crowned, and handed the management of sheep, cattle, wild animals, birds and everything swimming through the sea. The psalm opens and closes on the same line, which is how you know it means it.
PSA 9:1-20	Thanksgiving with receipts: enemies routed, nations rebuked, their names blotted out, whole cities now remembered by nobody. The throne is described as permanently occupied and permanently in session. Then the turn to himself, asking to be lifted up from the gates of death. The nations, he notes, sank into a pit of their own construction. His closing request is small and devastating: let them find out they are only men.
PSA 10:1-18	It opens by accusing God of standing too far off, and it is not polite about it. Most of the psalm is a portrait of the predator: prosperous, sneering, ambushing from the outskirts, dragging the helpless off in a net while assuring himself God has forgotten and will never look. The psalmist wants the arm broken, specifically. The ending insists God is king anyway and does hear, which reads like an argument he is slowly winning against himself.
PSA 11:1-7	Somebody advises him to flee to the mountains like a bird, since the archers are already stringing up in the dark and the foundations are going. He declines. His answer is that God is still in his temple and still on the throne, and more to the point still watching, eyes examining everybody. The forecast for the violent involves coals, fire, sulfur and a scorching wind, served as the contents of their cup.
PSA 12:1-8	An emergency call about a shortage of honest people. Everybody flatters, everybody keeps a second heart in reserve, and the boast is that their lips belong to them and nobody is lord over them. God interrupts in the middle of the psalm to say he is getting up, because of the groaning of the needy. His words get weighed against theirs: silver refined seven times against pure surface. The vile, meanwhile, are being promoted.
PSA 13:1-6	Four how longs in two verses, which is the sound of someone who has stopped pacing himself. Forgotten forever, face hidden, sorrow every day, enemy on top. He asks for light in his eyes before he sleeps the other kind of sleep. Then, with no explanation offered and nothing visibly changed, he ends on trust and a song.
PSA 14:1-7	The fool's position is stated in four words, and the rest is God leaning over the balcony of heaven to see whether anyone down there understands anything. The audit result: nobody, not one. The wicked eat his people the way you eat bread, casually and without thinking about it. Then a flicker of dread on their side, and a closing wish for rescue to come out of Zion.
PSA 15:1-5	A question about temple admission, answered as a character reference rather than a ritual. Speak truth internally, do not slander, do not harm a friend, honor the right people, and keep an oath after it has turned out badly for you. No lending at interest, no taking money to convict the innocent. Anyone who lives like that, the psalm says, will never be shaken.
PSA 16:1-11	Other gods get declined with a detail that stays with you: he will not pour their drink offerings of blood or even take their names on his lips. What he has instead is described in property terms, a portion, a cup, boundary lines that happened to fall in a pleasant part of the map. He gets counselled by his own heart at night. The last verses are pure confidence: not left in Sheol, shown the path of life, joy at capacity.
PSA 17:1-15	The case goes in for audit. He notes that God already tested him overnight and found nothing, then asks to be kept as the apple of an eye and hidden under wings. The opposition is a lion crouching in secret. Sharper still are the men whose whole portion is in this life: full bellies, well-provided sons, wealth stored for the grandchildren. His closing line quietly wants something else, to wake up and see God's face.
PSA 18:1-50	Fifty verses and it never lowers the volume. He calls out of distress and the answer arrives as weather: earth shaking, smoke out of God's nostrils, a cherub used as transport, hailstones and lightning, the sea floor laid bare. The rescue itself takes one line, drawn out of many waters. Then a long stretch of self-defence about clean hands, a claim that God matches whatever you bring him, and a victory lap of leaping walls, bending a bronze bow, and grinding enemies into street mud. He credits the greatness to God's gentleness, which after all that is a strange and lovely note.
PSA 19:1-14	Two halves, welded. First the sky preaches without saying anything, in a language with no words that everyone somehow hears, and the sun comes out of its tent like a bridegroom with somewhere to be. Then the law, rated in six adjectives and priced above fine gold and honeycomb. The ending goes personal and slightly nervous: forgive the errors I cannot even see, and keep the deliberate ones off me. Then the famous request about the words of his mouth.
PSA 20:1-9	A blessing said over a king before a battle: may God answer you, send help from the sanctuary, remember your offerings, hand you what your heart is after. Then the banners go up. The famous line arrives late and does the work of the whole psalm, some trusting in chariots, some in horses, and this lot trusting a name. The scoreboard is given in advance. They fall, we stand upright.
PSA 21:1-13	The thank-you note for the previous psalm's prayer. The king asked for life and received length of days forever, plus a gold crown he did not pay for. Then the tone turns and the second half is what happens to the plotters: a furnace, fire, their descendants cut off, and a drawn bow aimed at the face. It lands finally on a request that God be exalted, which after all that is a heavy note to finish on.
PSA 22:1-31	It begins with the question that has no answer inside it: my God, my God, why have you forsaken me. He cries day and night and gets nothing back. He calls himself a worm, and describes being mocked by people who tell him to let God rescue him if God is so fond of him. Then the body: poured out like water, bones out of joint, heart melting like wax, tongue stuck to the roof of his mouth. Dogs surround him, his hands and feet are pierced, and they divide his clothes by lot while he watches. Only near the end does it turn, insisting God did not hide his face after all, and that the ends of the earth will remember.
PSA 23:1-6	The most famous six verses in the book, and they earn it by staying small. A shepherd, green grass, quiet water, a soul put back together. Then the valley with death's shadow over it, walked through rather than avoided, with a rod and a staff for company. The picture changes at the end into a table laid in full view of his enemies, oil on his head, and a cup that will not stop overflowing.
PSA 24:1-10	First a deed of ownership covering the entire planet and everyone standing on it. Then the entry requirements for the holy hill, and they concern hands and heart rather than paperwork. The second half is written to be shouted at a gate: lift up your heads, doors, the King of glory is coming in. Someone on the other side asks who that is, twice, and gets told twice, louder. Yahweh of Armies.
PSA 25:1-22	A prayer that keeps alternating between asking for instruction and asking for forgiveness, as though the two were one request. He wants his youthful sins forgotten and his current one pardoned on the grounds that it is great, which is an unusual defence and possibly a good one. Between the pleas he drifts into calm statements about God teaching the humble. He is desolate, netted and cruelly hated, and the last line widens out to cover all of Israel.
PSA 26:1-12	He invites God to examine, test and try him, which takes a certain confidence. The evidence is largely a list of chairs he has not sat in: not with the deceitful, not with hypocrites, not with the assembly of evildoers. He washes his hands in innocence and walks around the altar. What he genuinely dreads is being filed with the bribe-takers when the sorting happens, and he says outright that he loves the house.
PSA 27:1-14	Half of this is unshakeable and half is frightened, and the psalm makes no apology for the seam. In the first part an army could camp around him and he would be fine. In the second he is begging God not to hide, not to abandon him, and admitting his own father and mother might. In the middle sits the one thing he actually asks for, which is not victory but a permanent address in God's house. It ends telling itself to wait, twice.
PSA 28:1-9	The opening worry is not that God will refuse but that God will say nothing, which he rates as equivalent to being in the pit already. He asks specifically not to be dragged off with the men who speak peace to their neighbors with mischief already loaded. Their sentence is to be paid exactly what their hands built. Then the psalm flips into thanks mid-stream, because the petition was heard, and closes asking for the whole nation to be shepherded.
PSA 29:1-11	A thunderstorm, tracked from the waters inland, and the word voice lands seven times. It splinters the cedars of Lebanon, makes whole mountains skip like calves, throws lightning, shakes the wilderness of Kadesh and strips the forests bare. Inside the temple the only appropriate response is one word, repeated. Then the last verse does something unexpected: after all that noise, what God hands his people is strength and peace.
PSA 30:1-12	A recovery song with an embarrassing confession in the middle. When things were going well he announced he would never be moved, and then God hid his face and he came apart immediately. His prayer from the bottom is frank negotiation, asking what profit there is in his destruction, since dust makes a poor choir. The famous line sits in verse five, weeping for the night and joy in the morning. It ends with the sackcloth off and dancing.
PSA 31:1-24	Two images do most of the work here and both are about being discarded: forgotten like a dead man, and broken pottery. His neighbors find him contemptible and people who see him on the street run. He commends his spirit into God's hand in verse five, a line later borrowed. Then it turns. His times are in God's hand, there is goodness laid up in storage, and there is a shelter specifically from the strife of tongues. He admits he panicked and said he was cut off, and admits God heard him anyway.
PSA 32:1-11	The physical cost of not confessing, described like an illness: bones wasting, a hand pressing down day and night, strength drying out in the heat of summer. Then he says it out loud and the whole thing lifts in a single verse. What follows is advice offered gently right up until it isn't, since do not be like the horse or the mule, animals with no understanding that need a bit and bridle, is not gentle at all.
PSA 33:1-22	Sheet music first: a new song, a ten-string harp, played skillfully and with a shout. The theology is creation by speech, heavens made with a word and their armies with a breath. Then a run of demolition. God cancels the counsel of nations, no king is saved by the size of his army, no strong man by his strength, and a horse is flatly called a vain thing for safety. What is left is an eye on those who hope, and a soul waiting.
PSA 34:1-22	Praise for a dozen verses, then it turns into a teaching session without warning. The testimony first: he asked, he was answered, and the people who looked to God went away radiant. Taste and see, he says, which treats God as something to try rather than something to agree with. Then the children get sat down for the syllabus: keep your tongue off evil, depart from it, do good, seek peace and pursue it. The quietest line is the best, that God is near the broken-hearted.
PSA 35:1-28	He asks God to pick up an actual shield and spear and take the field in person, and then to lean over and tell his soul it is safe. His grievance has a sharp centre: when these people were sick he wore sackcloth, fasted, and mourned as though for his own mother, and when he went down they gathered to watch and tore at him without stopping. The word Aha appears twice, which is somehow worse than a threat. He wants their hidden net to catch them.
PSA 36:1-12	The first four verses are a diagnosis: the wicked man flatters himself so thoroughly that he can no longer detect his own sin, and does his plotting lying in bed. Then the psalm changes register entirely and goes vertical, kindness reaching the heavens, righteousness like mountains, judgments like a great deep, and animals covered by the same care as people. There is a river to drink from and a spring of life. It closes with one small request, keep the foot of pride off me.
PSA 37:1-40	Forty verses aimed at one specific temptation, envying people who are getting away with it. The counsel repeats on purpose: do not fret, trust, delight, commit, rest, wait, stop being angry. The argument underneath is entirely about time, which is why God laughs, having already seen the day coming. Best of all is an old man's eyewitness note about a wicked man spreading like a green tree in native soil, who went back to look and found nobody there.
PSA 38:1-22	A full medical report submitted as a confession. No soundness anywhere, wounds gone rotten, a burning waist, a throbbing heart, the light of his eyes gone, and he traces all of it to his own foolishness. Friends and relatives have arranged themselves at a distance. His strategy against the accusers is to go entirely deaf and mute, which is either dignity or exhaustion. The last verses shorten into three requests: don't forsake me, don't be far, hurry.
PSA 39:1-13	He decides to say nothing at all in front of the wicked, holds it until the silence heats up like a fire, and then says something far bleaker than whatever he was suppressing. Life measures out in hand widths, a man stands as a breath, a walking shadow heaping up wealth without knowing who gathers it. He calls himself a stranger and a foreigner, as all his fathers were. The last line asks God to look away long enough for him to recover before he exists no more.
PSA 40:1-17	Rescue first: a horrible pit, miry clay, feet set on a rock and a new song issued. Then the theological turn, that what God wanted was not sacrifice and offering but an opened ear, and the answer given is simply here I have come. He is careful to record that he did not seal his lips about it. Then, having testified at length, he admits his iniquities outnumber the hairs on his head and asks God to hurry, which undercuts the confidence in exactly the right way.
PSA 41:1-13	The promise up front is that God will sit at the bedside of anyone who considers the poor. Then it gets specific about the psalmist's own sickroom. Visitors turn up, speak falsehood to his face, and go straight out to tell everyone. The whisper campaign has already concluded he will not rise again. Worst of all, the familiar friend who ate bread at his table has lifted his heel. His prayer to be raised so he can repay them is honest rather than admirable. The amens close Book One.
PSA 42:1-11	Homesickness written as thirst. He remembers going with the crowd into God's house, leading them, singing, and now his food is his own tears while people keep asking him where his God has got to. He is somewhere north, near the Jordan and Hermon and the hill Mizar, which is not where he wants to be. Deep calls to deep and the waves go over him. Twice he stops to ask his own soul why it is in despair, and tells it to hope.
PSA 43:1-5	The same poem continuing, same refrain, but the request has sharpened into a legal one: plead my cause against an ungodly nation and a set of deceitful men. He wants God's light and truth sent out ahead of him as guides, specifically to lead him back to the holy hill and the altar, where the harp is waiting. Then he asks his soul the same question a third time and gives it the same answer.
PSA 44:1-26	Grandfathers speak first: God drove out nations and planted his own people, and no, not by their own sword. Then the word but arrives in verse nine and the rest is a complaint made under oath. Sold for nothing, scattered, made a byword and a shaking of the head, regarded as sheep for the slaughter, and they insist they have not forgotten him or been false to the covenant. The last verses shake God awake and ask why he is sleeping. No answer comes.
PSA 45:1-17	A wedding song, and the poet tells you up front that his tongue is a skillful writer's pen, which is the least modest opening in the collection. The groom gets a sword strapped to his thigh, sharp arrows, an eternal throne and garments smelling of myrrh, aloes and cassia. The bride is told to forget her own people and her father's house, offered as advice and landing as a cost. She arrives in gold and embroidery with her companions behind her. Sons are promised as princes.
PSA 46:1-11	The scenario is the worst one available: the earth changing, mountains shaken into the sea, the waters roaring. What is promised is not that it will not happen but that they will not be afraid when it does. In the middle of all that there is a river and a city, quietly glad. God ends wars by breaking the equipment, bows snapped, spears shattered, chariots burned. The famous command to be still is addressed to the raging nations, not to anyone anxious.
PSA 47:1-9	Applause is demanded from every nation, and then the occasion is given: a coronation, God going up to the throne with a shout and the sound of a trumpet. Sing praises appears four times inside a verse and a half, which is not an accident. The final verse gathers the princes of the peoples in with the people of the God of Abraham, a larger claim than the volume lets you notice.
PSA 48:1-14	A city guide with a military anecdote in the middle. Enemy kings assemble, pass by together to look at Zion, take one look and hurry off, their panic compared to labour pains. Then the instruction that shapes the psalm: walk around the city, count the towers, mark the bulwarks, consider the palaces, and tell the next generation what you counted. The tour is not really about architecture. The last line is about a guide who goes all the way.
PSA 49:1-20	A wisdom riddle set to a harp and addressed to rich and poor together, on the theme that money does not work where it matters. Nobody can redeem a brother from death, the ransom being permanently out of range. Wise men and fools die at the same rate and leave it all to others. The rich name their lands after themselves, which is treated as touching rather than impressive. The bleakest image is Death as a shepherd. The line about the animals is used twice.
PSA 50:1-23	God convenes a court, summons heaven and earth as witnesses, and then makes clear the charge is not that the sacrifices stopped. They did not. The complaint is the assumption behind them: he owns every animal of the forest and the livestock on a thousand hills, and if he were hungry he would not mention it. Then he turns on people who recite his statutes while consenting with thieves and slandering their own mother's son. The worst line is the quietest: I kept silent, and you thought I was like you.
S3Y 1:1-29	Three men are walking about inside a furnace praising God, and the narrative treats this as the setting rather than the event. Azarias prays: you are righteous, we sinned, you handed us to the most wicked king in the world, and we have no prince, no prophet, no altar and nothing left to offer, so take a contrite heart in place of ten thousand fat lambs. Meanwhile the servants keep feeding the fire with naphtha and pitch, the flame goes up forty-nine cubits and kills the Chaldeans standing near it, and an angel turns the inside into a damp whistling wind.
S3Y 1:30-68	The song is a roll call of creation and it leaves nothing out. Heavens, angels, the waters above the sky, sun and moon and stars, showers and dew, winds, fire and heat, nights and days, light and darkness, frost, snow, lightning, clouds, mountains, growing things, seas and rivers, springs, whales, birds, cattle, people, Israel, priests, and finally the three of them by name, thanking God for pulling them out of the fire. Every line ends the same way. It is a hymn built entirely out of repetition, sung by men standing in a furnace.
SUS 1:1-33	Susanna is devout, well taught, and married to the most honourable man in Babylon, whose garden is where the local legal business happens. Two elders appointed judges that year become obsessed with her, each hiding it from the other until they collide doubling back to the same garden and admit it. They lie in wait, and when she sends her maids for oil and shuts the doors, they corner her: consent, or we testify that we caught you with a young man. She refuses and cries out. So do they. Next time on Susanna: the judges get to speak first.
SUS 1:34-64	The two elders lay their hands on her head and give evidence: a young man, too strong to hold, who got the doors open and escaped. The assembly believes them because they are elders and judges, and condemns her. Susanna prays aloud to the God who knows things before they happen. Daniel interrupts the execution to point out that nobody examined the witnesses, then examines them himself, separately, with one question each. Under what tree? A mastick, says the first. A holm, says the second. The pair receive the sentence they had intended for her.
TOB 1:1-22	Tobit opens by listing his ancestors back five generations, then explains that he alone of the tribe of Naphtali kept going up to Jerusalem while everyone else sacrificed to a heifer. Deported to Nineveh, he refuses Gentile food, is somehow promoted to royal purveyor, and leaves ten talents of silver on deposit with Gabael in Media. Then he takes up burying the king's victims by night, which is illegal, and loses everything he owns. Sennacherib is murdered by two of his sons, and Tobit's nephew, who now runs the kingdom's accounts, gets him recalled.
TOB 2:1-14	Restored and comfortable again, Tobit sits down to a Pentecost dinner and sends Tobias out to find a poor man to share it with. Tobias comes back reporting a strangled corpse dumped in the marketplace, so Tobit leaves his food untouched, buries the man after dark, and gets mocked by the neighbours for it. He sleeps by the courtyard wall, sparrows drop warm dung into his open eyes, and he goes blind. His wife takes in weaving and is given a goat. He accuses her of stealing it, and she asks him where all his righteousness got him.
TOB 3:1-17	Blind and mocked, Tobit prays to die. The same day, in another country, Sarah prays exactly the same prayer: seven husbands, and a demon named Asmodaeus killed every one of them on the wedding night, and now her father's maidservants blame her for it. She considers hanging herself, decides it would shame her father, and asks for death instead. Both prayers are heard at the same moment, and Raphael is dispatched to scale the films off Tobit's eyes, bind Asmodaeus, and marry Sarah to Tobias. Neither of them is told a thing.
TOB 4:1-21	Having just asked for death, Tobit remembers that nobody knows about the money, so he calls Tobias in for a farewell speech that runs to twenty verses. Bury me, honour your mother, give alms, marry inside the family, pay your workers the same day, avoid drunkenness, take advice from anyone wise, and do to no one what you yourself hate. Almsgiving comes round roughly five times. The actual point, ten talents of silver on deposit with Gabael at Rages, arrives at verse twenty.
TOB 5:1-22	Tobias points out the flaw in the plan: he has never met Gabael. Told to hire a guide, he goes out and finds Raphael, who is an angel, and does not notice. Tobit interviews the applicant about his tribe and family, is satisfied, settles on a drachma a day plus a bonus if both come home safe, and sends them off praying that God's angel go with them. The dog comes too. Anna weeps that no amount of silver is worth her son, and Tobit assures her a good angel is on the job, which is more accurate than he knows.
TOB 6:1-17	At the Tigris a fish comes at Tobias, the angel says grab it, and they roast and eat it, keeping the heart, liver and gall. Asked what those are for, Raphael explains: burn the heart and liver and any demon flees, smear the gall and blindness clears. He then mentions that tonight they lodge with Raguel, whose only daughter Sarah is legally Tobias's to marry. Tobias raises the small matter of seven dead husbands. Raphael tells him to bring the fish parts. Tobias hears all of this and falls in love on the spot.
TOB 7:1-18	Sarah answers the door. Raguel notices the family resemblance, learns that this is Tobit's son, kisses him, then weeps when he hears Tobit has gone blind. Dinner is served, and Tobias refuses to touch it until the marriage is agreed. Raguel tries eat, drink and be merry first, then admits that seven previous husbands all died in the night, then hands his daughter over anyway, writes the contract and seals it. Edna prepares the other chamber, cries with her daughter, and tells her twice to be comforted.
TOB 8:1-21	In the bridal chamber Tobias burns the fish heart and liver on the incense, the demon catches the smell and flees to the uppermost parts of Egypt, and the angel binds him there. Then the couple get up and pray before anything else. Outside, Raguel is digging a grave on the assumption that he will need it, and sends a maidservant at first light to check. The report comes back: both alive, both asleep. He blesses God, has the grave quietly filled in, and throws a fourteen-day wedding feast that Tobias is sworn not to leave.
TOB 9:1-6	Sworn to stay at his own wedding, Tobias sends Raphael to run the errand the entire journey was for: take a servant and two camels, collect the silver from Gabael, and bring him back for the feast. The reason given is that his father counts the days and will grieve. Raphael goes, produces the handwriting, receives the bags with their seals unbroken, and is back by morning with Gabael. The money that has driven the whole plot changes hands offstage, in six verses.
TOB 10:1-12	Back in Nineveh, Tobit is counting days and working through explanations: detained, perhaps, or Gabael has died. Anna skips to the conclusion that her son is dead, stops eating by day, and walks out to the road every morning to watch. When the fourteen days end Tobias asks to leave. Raguel offers to send word to his father instead, is refused, and hands over Sarah, half his goods, servants, cattle and money. Edna's farewell is half blessing and half warning: she is my daughter, do not cause her grief.
TOB 11:1-19	Raphael proposes that the two of them run ahead of the caravan to prepare the house, and adds: bring the gall. Anna, still watching the road, sees her son coming and announces that she is ready to die. Tobit gets up, stumbles at the door, and his son catches him and rubs the gall on his eyes. They sting, he rubs, the white films scale away, and he sees. He walks out to the city gate to meet his daughter-in-law, blessing God in front of neighbours who cannot believe it. Seven more days of feasting.
TOB 12:1-22	Tobit tells Tobias to pay the guide well, and they agree on half of everything they brought back. The guide declines it, delivers a short sermon on prayer, fasting and almsgiving, quotes a line of his own back at them, and then explains that he carried their prayers before the Holy One and stood beside Tobit at every burial. He is Raphael, one of the seven. He adds that he never actually ate or drank, they only saw a vision. Both fall on their faces. Told to write it down, they look up and he is gone.
TOB 13:1-18	Tobit writes a hymn, and its argument is that the scourging and the mercy are the same hand: he leads down to the grave and brings up again. Praise God in front of the nations, he says, precisely because you were scattered among them. Turn back with your whole heart and he turns back. Then he reaches Jerusalem and loses all control of the vocabulary: sapphires, emeralds, walls and towers of pure gold, streets paved with beryl and stones of Ophir, and every street of it saying hallelujah.
TOB 14:1-15	Tobit went blind at fifty-eight and got his sight back eight years later. Very old now, he calls in Tobias and six grandsons for closing instructions: leave Nineveh, because Jonah was right about it, bury me and your mother properly, and remember what Aman did to Achiacharus and how that ended for Aman. He dies mid-speech at a hundred and fifty-eight. Tobias moves to Ecbatana, buries his in-laws magnificently, inherits twice over, and dies at a hundred and twenty-seven, having first heard that Nineveh has fallen.
JOS 1:1-18	Moses is dead, so Yahweh's first act is a pep talk: be strong and courageous, repeated so many times it starts to sound like a warning. Joshua orders three days of rations and reminds Reuben, Gad and half of Manasseh that they promised to fight for the rest before going home. The people answer with total obedience, on penalty of death for anyone who doesn't fall in line.
JOS 2:1-24	Two spies scout Jericho and end up at Rahab's, a prostitute whose house doubles as an intelligence safehouse. She hides them under flax on the roof, lies smoothly to the king's men, and cashes in her risk for a guarantee: spare my family when you take the city. They agree, on condition of a scarlet cord in her window, and she gets them out the wall by rope.
JOS 3:1-17	The ark leads the way to the Jordan, which is at flood stage, not exactly cooperative conditions for a river crossing. The moment the priests' feet touch the water it piles up in a heap upstream at Adam, and the whole nation walks across on dry ground while the priests stand in the riverbed holding the line.
JOS 4:1-24	Twelve men haul twelve stones out of the exact spot where the priests stood in the Jordan, set up as a monument so future kids will ask what they mean and get the whole crossing story. Joshua sets up a matching twelve inside the riverbed itself. Forty thousand armed men cross over, the water resumes flowing, and Israel camps at Gilgal.
JOS 5:1-15	With the enemy kings' morale already gone, Joshua's first order of business is mass circumcision of an entire generation born in the wilderness, using flint knives, at a hill now named for the occasion. Passover follows, the manna stops the day they eat the land's produce, and Joshua meets an armed stranger who turns out to outrank him: take off your shoes, this ground is holy.
JOS 6:1-27	Seven days of marching around Jericho, silent except for trumpets, seven laps on the seventh day, then a shout and the wall comes down. Israel kills every person and animal in the city except Rahab's household, and burns everything but the metal, which goes into the treasury. Joshua curses whoever rebuilds it, a curse specific enough to be unsettling.
JOS 7:1-26	One man, Achan, keeps loot he was told to destroy, and Israel loses thirty-six men in an otherwise routine attack on Ai as a result. Joshua tears his clothes and asks God why they even crossed the Jordan. The lot lands on Achan by process of elimination; he confesses to a robe, silver, and gold buried in his tent. He and his family and everything he owns are stoned and burned in the valley named for the trouble.
JOS 8:1-18	Round two at Ai, this time with a plan: thirty thousand men hide in ambush while Joshua leads the rest in a staged retreat to pull the defenders out of the city. It works exactly as drawn up, down to Joshua's javelin as the signal to spring the trap. Next time on Joshua: the trap closes.
JOS 8:19-35	The ambush takes the empty city and torches it; caught between two forces, the men of Ai are wiped out, twelve thousand dead in total, men and women both. The king is hanged, then his body is dumped at the gate under a heap of stones. Joshua builds an altar, writes the law on stone, and reads all of it aloud to the whole assembly, women and children included.
JOS 9:1-27	The Gibeonites fake a long journey with stale bread and cracked wineskins to talk Israel into a protection treaty, and it works because nobody thinks to ask God first. Three days later the truth comes out and it's too late; the oath holds. Israel's compromise is to let them live as permanent woodcutters and water carriers instead of killing them outright.
JOS 10:1-23	Five kings besiege Gibeon for allying with Israel, so Joshua marches all night to rescue them. Yahweh throws hailstones that kill more men than the Israelite swords do, and the day stretches long enough at Joshua's word for the rout to finish. The five kings are found hiding in a cave and sealed in with stones while the battle continues. Next time on Joshua: the cave gets opened.
JOS 10:24-43	The five trapped kings are brought out, killed, and hanged, their bodies sealed back in the cave. Joshua then moves south through Makkedah, Libnah, Lachish, Eglon, Hebron and Debir, one after another, and the pattern repeats at every city: total destruction, no survivors. The chapter states plainly that he left no one remaining, as commanded.
JOS 11:1-23	A northern coalition under Jabin of Hazor gathers at the waters of Merom with horses and chariots, and Israel routs them, hamstringing the horses and burning the chariots as instructed. Hazor alone is burned, since it had been the head of those kingdoms; the rest of the cities are left standing. The land finally has rest from war.
JOS 12:1-24	A tally of the defeated: Sihon and Og, taken care of under Moses east of the Jordan, followed by a bare list of thirty-one kings Joshua defeated on the west side, Jericho and Ai included. No narrative, just names and a final count.
JOS 13:1-33	Joshua is old, and Yahweh points out there's still a long list of unconquered territory, the Philistines and Geshurites among it, before turning to bookkeeping: what Moses already assigned to Reuben, Gad and half of Manasseh east of the Jordan, restated in border by border detail. The Levites get no land, since the offerings are their inheritance instead.
JOS 14:1-15	Land division west of the Jordan starts, and Caleb goes first, unprompted. At eighty-five he reminds Joshua he was forty when he scouted this land and still believed it, unlike his fellow spies, and asks for the hill country of Hebron, Anakim and all. Joshua gives it to him. Nobody argues with a man that stubborn.
JOS 15:1-14	Judah's border is traced in exhausting detail, south to east to north to west, mostly place names that mean nothing without a map. Buried in the middle of it: Caleb collects on his promise and drives the three sons of Anak out of Hebron himself. Next time on Joshua: Caleb offers his daughter as a prize.
JOS 15:15-63	Caleb offers his daughter Achsah to whoever takes Kiriath Sepher; his nephew Othniel does, and marries her. Achsah then asks her father for something more useful than a husband: springs of water to go with the dry southern land, and gets both the upper and lower springs. The rest of the chapter is a long roll call of Judah's towns by region, closing on the note that Jerusalem's original residents were never actually driven out.
JOS 16:1-10	The border of Ephraim and Manasseh, Joseph's two tribes, gets traced the same way Judah's did, town by boundary marker. One detail breaks the pattern: the Canaanites in Gezer are never driven out, just put to forced labor, which becomes a recurring theme.
JOS 17:1-18	Manasseh's territory is divided, and Zelophehad's five daughters, named individually, collect the inheritance Moses promised them since their father had no sons. Several towns' Canaanite residents are again left in place as forced labor rather than driven out. Joseph's tribe complains about getting only one allotment; Joshua tells them to go clear forest land themselves if they're so numerous, chariots of iron or not.
JOS 18:1-28	The tabernacle moves to Shiloh, and Joshua calls out the seven tribes still dragging their feet on claiming land. He sends surveyors to map what's left into seven portions, then casts lots. Benjamin draws first, with a border description followed by a list of twenty-six cities, Jerusalem among them, though it's still Jebusite at this point.
JOS 19:1-51	The remaining six lots get assigned: Simeon tucked inside Judah's oversized territory, then Zebulun, Issachar, Asher, and Naphtali by list of towns. Dan struggles to hold its assigned land and ends up conquering Leshem instead, renaming it Dan after their ancestor. Joshua himself finally gets an inheritance, the town he asked for, and the whole distribution is declared finished.
JOS 20:1-9	Six cities of refuge are designated, three on each side of the Jordan, where someone who kills accidentally can flee the victim's avenging relative and get a fair hearing instead of a knife in the back. He stays there until the current high priest dies, then goes home.
JOS 21:1-20	The Levites, who own no territory, are given forty-eight cities carved out of everyone else's land instead. Aaron's descendants get thirteen of them from Judah, Simeon and Benjamin, Hebron among them, though its farmland had already gone to Caleb personally rather than the priests. Next time on Joshua: the remaining forty-eight, and a closing line worth the wait.
JOS 21:21-45	The rest of the Levite cities are handed out to the remaining priestly families, forty-eight total, three of them doubling as cities of refuge. The chapter closes by stepping back: Yahweh gave Israel everything he promised, all of it, and not one word of it failed.
JOS 22:1-18	Joshua sends the eastern tribes home with a blessing and a share of the plunder, their duty fulfilled. On the way, they build a massive altar by the Jordan, and the rest of Israel, hearing about it secondhand, assumes rebellion and mobilizes for war, sending Phinehas to confront them before anyone strikes first. Next time on Joshua: the altar turns out not to be an altar.
JOS 22:19-34	The eastern tribes explain themselves: the altar isn't for sacrifice, it's a replica, a witness so future generations won't tell their children they have no share in Yahweh. Phinehas and the delegation accept the explanation, war is called off, and the altar gets named accordingly: Witness.
JOS 23:1-16	Old and near the end, Joshua gathers Israel's leaders for a farewell speech that's mostly warning: don't marry into the remaining nations, don't mention their gods, or the very blessings Yahweh delivered will flip into curses just as completely. He's seen the promises kept in full; he's equally sure about what happens if they don't hold up their end.
JOS 24:1-16	At Shechem, Joshua recites the whole national story in Yahweh's own first person voice, Abraham through the conquest, then puts the choice plainly: serve the old gods or serve Yahweh, but choose. His own answer is the famous one, as for me and my house, we will serve Yahweh. The people answer that they will too. Next time on Joshua: Joshua tells them they cannot do it.
JOS 24:17-33	The people confirm their choice, and Joshua, oddly, pushes back: you can't actually serve Yahweh, he's holy and jealous and won't forgive a casual betrayal. They insist anyway. A covenant is made, a witness stone set up, and Joshua dies at a hundred and ten, buried in the hill country he was given. Joseph's bones, carried out of Egypt generations earlier, are finally buried too.
RUT 1:1-22	Famine sends Elimelech's family to Moab, where both sons marry local women before Elimelech and then both sons die, leaving three widows. Naomi heads home and urges her daughters-in-law to stay behind; Orpah does, but Ruth refuses with the line that gets read at weddings, your people will be my people. They arrive in Bethlehem at harvest time, and Naomi asks to be called Mara, bitter, instead of her own name.
RUT 2:1-23	Ruth goes gleaning to feed them both and happens, by no plan of her own, into the field of Boaz, a relative of her dead father-in-law. He notices her, offers protection and extra grain left behind on purpose, and she comes home with far more than a normal day's gleaning. Naomi recognizes the name immediately: Boaz is family, a close enough relative to matter.
RUT 3:1-18	Naomi sends Ruth to the threshing floor at night with a plan: wait until Boaz falls asleep, then uncover his feet and lie down there, a request for him to take on the role of family protector. He wakes startled, agrees in principle, but discloses there's a nearer relative with first right of refusal. He sends her home before dawn, discreetly, with six measures of barley as a message to Naomi.
RUT 4:1-22	Boaz takes the matter to the city gate and offers the nearer kinsman first right to redeem Elimelech's land. The man agrees until he learns Ruth comes with it and it would complicate his own inheritance; he backs out, formalized with the old shoe-removing custom, and Boaz takes both the land and Ruth. Their son Obed becomes David's grandfather, and the book closes with the genealogy connecting Perez to David.
NAM 1:1-15	Nahum opens on Yahweh as a jealous avenger and spends the whole chapter describing what happens to mountains and seas when he arrives angry. The target is Nineveh, and the comfort in it is aimed entirely at the people Nineveh has been standing on.
1KI 1:1-27	The book opens with a king who cannot get warm, which tells you everything about who is actually in charge. Adonijah, next in line by age, does exactly what Absalom did, chariots, horsemen, fifty runners, and the narrator adds the crucial detail that his father had never once asked him why he was doing anything. The guest list at his coronation barbecue is the giveaway: everyone except the prophet, the general's rival, and the brother. Nathan's counter-move is choreography, sending Bathsheba in first with the oath and following her in to confirm it. Neither of them argues policy. They argue that she and her son are about to be executed. Next time on 1 Kings: the old man wakes up.
1KI 1:28-53	David, given a clear question, answers it in one sentence and then organises a coronation with the precision of a man who has run a revolt: my mule, that spring, those two clerics, one trumpet. The mule matters, since it is a public loan of the king's own status. Meanwhile Adonijah's party is still eating when the noise arrives, and Joab, who has heard a lot of trumpets, asks what it means. The messenger's report is a slow, thorough, devastating recitation of everything that has already happened, ending with the detail that David bowed on his bed. The guests do not say goodbye to each other. Adonijah ends up clinging to the altar bargaining for an oath, and gets a conditional one and an instruction to go home.
1KI 2:1-23	David's deathbed speech has two parts, and they do not match. The first is a fine exhortation to keep the law of Moses. The second is a hit list: Joab, who shed blood in peacetime, and Shimei, whom David personally swore not to kill and who is therefore to be killed by someone else, with the reasoning helpfully supplied that Solomon is a wise man and will work out how. Barzillai's sons get the one generous item. Then Adonijah, who was let off once, asks for the woman who shared his father's bed, which is a claim on the throne wearing a wedding invitation, and asks Solomon's mother to deliver it. Solomon's reply, ask the kingdom for him too, is not rhetorical. Next time on 1 Kings: the list gets worked through.
1KI 2:24-46	The list is settled in one chapter with an efficiency that is meant to be admired and is mostly chilling. Adonijah dies the same day. Abiathar is spared for old service and exiled, which the narrator notes closes an account opened against Eli's house four hundred years earlier. Joab does what Adonijah did and grabs the altar, and this time the answer is that the altar is not a loophole; he says he will die there and is obliged. Shimei is placed under house arrest with one rule and keeps it for three years, until two runaway slaves make him forget, and the oath he swore closes over him. The chapter's last line is the point of all of it: the kingdom was established in Solomon's hand.
1KI 3:1-28	A dynastic marriage to Egypt, and a note that both the country and the new king are still sacrificing on hilltops because the temple does not exist yet, which the book files as a qualification rather than an accusation. At Gibeon God offers a blank cheque and Solomon spends it on an understanding heart, describing himself as a child who does not know how to go out or come in, which is either humility or extremely good instincts about what to ask for. He gets the wisdom, plus the wealth and honour he did not request. The demonstration follows immediately, and it works because it is a trap rather than a judgment: nobody who wants the child alive will accept half of one. All Israel draws the correct conclusion and becomes afraid of him.
1KI 4:1-34	An administrative chapter, and the interesting thing about it is the plumbing. Twelve officers, one month each, feeding a court that gets through thirty cors of flour, ten stall-fed cattle, twenty pasture cattle and a hundred sheep a day before you count the gazelles. Two of the twelve are married to Solomon's daughters, which is how you make sure the supply chain stays loyal. Everyone is sitting under their own vine and fig tree, and there are forty thousand stalls of chariot horses to be fed too. Then the wisdom: three thousand proverbs, a thousand and five songs, and a curriculum stretching from Lebanese cedars to the hyssop in the wall and on to fish. Foreign kings send delegations. Everything here is enormous, and every item of it is being paid for by somebody.
1KI 5:1-18	A trade negotiation conducted entirely in compliments. Solomon explains that his father was too busy fighting to build, that he himself has peace, and that no Israelite can cut timber like a Sidonian, which is flattery and also true. Hiram is delighted, blesses Israel's God with genuine warmth, and proposes floating the cedar down the coast in rafts, and the price is food: twenty thousand cors of wheat and twenty of pure oil a year, forever. Then the labour figures, which the book states without comment and which are the real cost of the building: thirty thousand men conscripted in monthly shifts, seventy thousand carriers, eighty thousand men cutting stone in the mountains, and three thousand three hundred foremen keeping them at it. The man in charge of the forced labour is named, and he will be remembered.
1KI 6:1-18	The temple gets a precise date and precise dimensions, sixty by twenty by thirty, and if the numbers slide past you the book does not mind, because the detail it wants you to keep is the silence: every stone was dressed at the quarry so that no hammer, axe or iron tool was heard on the site while it went up. Three storeys of side rooms, each a cubit wider than the one below, sit on ledges so that nothing pierces the wall. God interrupts the construction report with a conditional, keep my commandments and I will live among my people, which is a reminder that the building is not the point. Then cedar over everything, carved with buds and flowers, until no stone is visible anywhere. Next time on 1 Kings: they start gilding it.
1KI 6:19-38	This is the gold section, and the word appears in almost every verse: gold walls, gold altar, gold floor, gold chains across the doorway, gold on the carvings and the doors and the hinges. Two olive-wood cherubim ten cubits tall stand in the inner room with their wings spanning the whole width, wingtip to wall on either side and touching in the middle, so the ark sits under a ceiling of wings. Everything not gold is carved: cherubim, palm trees, open flowers, inside and outside. And then the flat closing accountancy, founded in Ziv, finished in Bul, seven years, which for a building of this description is a remarkably short time and a remarkably plain sentence.
1KI 7:1-26	Note the arithmetic the book is doing without saying anything: seven years on God's house, thirteen on Solomon's. His complex includes a hall so full of cedar columns it is called the House of the Forest of Lebanon, a hall of pillars, a courtroom, his residence and a separate house for his Egyptian wife. Then the craftsman arrives, Hiram of Tyre, the son of an Israelite widow, and the chapter's tone changes completely into loving technical description. Two hollow bronze pillars eighteen cubits high with lilies and four hundred pomegranates between them, standing at the porch with names of their own, Jachin and Boaz. A bronze sea holding two thousand baths, resting on twelve bronze oxen facing outward. Next time on 1 Kings: ten wheeled basins.
1KI 7:27-51	Ten bronze stands on ten bronze chariot wheels, cast to one measure and one pattern, panelled with lions and oxen and cherubim, each carrying a basin holding forty baths. Somebody designed mobile washing units for a building nobody was going to move, and the text describes their axles with genuine affection. Then the inventory: pillars, capitals, networks, four hundred pomegranates, ten stands, ten basins, one sea, twelve oxen, pots, shovels, basins, all cast in clay moulds down in the Jordan plain and hauled uphill. The line that gives the game away is that Solomon left the bronze unweighed because there was too much of it to weigh. After that the gold: altar, table, lampstands, tongs, snuffers, spoons, fire pans, and the door hinges, because at this budget even the hinges are gold.
1KI 8:1-24	The ark comes up to the temple with a procession, uncountable sacrifices, and a detail the writer cannot resist: the carrying poles are long enough to be seen poking out from the inner room, and they are still there. Also noted, with no comment at all, is that the ark contains nothing but the two stone tablets, which is a very quiet sentence in a very expensive building. Then the cloud arrives and the priests have to stop working, because you cannot minister inside the glory of God. Solomon's opening remark is the strangest and best line of the chapter, that God said he would live in thick darkness, so here is a house. The rest is dynastic history, David wanted to build, his son did. Next time on 1 Kings: the prayer itself.
1KI 8:25-44	The prayer's foundation is a concession no builder wants to make: heaven cannot contain you, so obviously this cannot either. Having said that, he asks for the one thing that matters, that God watch this address and listen. Then it becomes a list of contingencies, and the list is what makes it great, because it assumes failure throughout. Perjury, military defeat, drought, famine, plague, blight, mildew, locusts, siege, and every man knowing the plague of his own heart, which is the finest phrase in it. Each case ends the same way, hear, and forgive. And then the request nobody would expect from a national temple, that the foreigner who comes from far away because he has heard of God's name be answered too, so that everyone learns it. Next time on 1 Kings: he plans for exile.
1KI 8:45-66	The prayer ends by planning for the worst thing that can happen, which is the thing that eventually happens: deportation. His premise is that there is no man who does not sin, so the question is not whether but what follows, and his answer is that repentance works from anywhere, prayed toward a place rather than in it. Then he stands up, blesses the crowd, and says not one word of the promise has failed, which is true so far. The accountancy afterwards is preposterous and probably meant to be: twenty-two thousand cattle, a hundred and twenty thousand sheep, and the bronze altar too small to hold the offering. Fourteen days of feasting, and everyone goes home glad, which is the last unambiguously happy sentence in the book.
1KI 9:1-28	God's second appearance is the same promise as the first with the conditional made explicit and the failure case spelled out in detail: the house cast away, the nation a proverb, and passers-by hissing at the ruin and asking what happened. That paragraph is the whole rest of Kings in advance. The chapter then turns to accounts, and they are less flattering. Hiram gets twenty Galilean towns for twenty years of timber and gold, inspects them, dislikes them, and names the district something rude. The building programme is itemised along with the forced labour that raised it, Canaanites conscripted permanently, Israelites exempt, five hundred and fifty foremen. And a fleet on the Red Sea crewed by Tyrian sailors, bringing back four hundred and twenty talents of gold from Ophir.
1KI 10:1-29	A visiting monarch arrives to test the famous wisdom with hard questions, and the text is more interested in the tour: the table, the seating plan, the uniforms of the waiting staff, the stairs up to the temple. She runs out of breath, which is the polite version of what happens. Then the inventory, and the reader is meant to notice it piling up: gold shields stacked in a cedar hall, an ivory throne with fourteen lions on it, gold cups because silver had stopped being worth counting, and a triennial shipment including apes and peacocks. The annual gold intake is given as six hundred and sixty-six talents, a number nobody has ever found innocent. And the last item is the one the law of the king specifically forbade: chariots, twelve thousand horsemen, and a bulk horse-trading business with Egypt.
1KI 11:1-23	Seven hundred wives and three hundred concubines, and the chapter's charge is not the arithmetic but the shrines, since Solomon builds hilltop sanctuaries for Chemosh and Molech within sight of the temple he built for Yahweh. The verdict is delivered with an aggravating factor attached, that God had appeared to him twice, in person, about exactly this. The sentence is the kingdom torn away, deferred to his son's reign and softened by one tribe, both concessions made for David rather than for him. Then the adversaries arrive, and their backstories are grim: Hadad escaped Joab's six-month campaign to kill every male in Edom as a small child and grew up in Pharaoh's household, and asks to leave the moment he hears David and Joab are dead. Next time on 1 Kings: a prophet ruins a good coat.
1KI 11:24-43	Two adversaries and one employee. Rezon sets up in Damascus and spends a career hating Israel. Jeroboam is the more dangerous, and the reason is buried in his job title: Solomon promoted him because he was industrious and put him in charge of the forced labour of the northern tribes, which means the man who knows exactly how much the building programme cost is now the man with a following. Ahijah's prophecy is delivered as performance art, a brand new coat torn into twelve pieces in an empty field, ten of them handed over. Solomon's response to a divine announcement is an assassination attempt, which tells you where he has ended up. Jeroboam waits it out in Egypt. Forty years, and the obituary is one line.
1KI 12:1-17	The kingdom is lost in a single meeting. The north's request is entirely reasonable and clearly costed: your father worked us too hard, ease it and we are yours. Rehoboam asks for three days, consults the men who actually ran the country, and receives the best political advice in scripture, be their servant today and you will have them forever. He then asks his friends, who have run nothing, and adopts their line about his little finger and his father's waist, whips and scorpions, which sounds magnificent in a room and catastrophic in a square. He delivers it word for word. The reply from Israel is the old rebel slogan from Sheba's revolt, and this time nobody puts it down. Next time on 1 Kings: the man in charge of forced labour is sent out to calm the crowd.
1KI 12:18-33	Rehoboam's response to a general strike is to send the minister for forced labour, who is stoned to death, after which the king discovers his chariot can go quite fast. Judah alone stays. The army musters and is stood down by a prophet on the grounds that the split is God's doing, which they accept, and it is the last sensible thing anyone does here. Jeroboam's problem is genuine, since a pilgrimage route to a rival capital is a security threat, and his solution creates the sin the rest of the book will measure every northern king against: two gold calves, one at each end of the country, a priesthood recruited from anyone available, and a festival moved one month so it does not clash with the real one.
1KI 13:1-17	A prophet from Judah interrupts a royal sacrifice by talking to the furniture, and names the king who will one day desecrate it, three centuries early. Jeroboam points at him and his arm locks in that position, which is the sort of thing that ends an argument, and the altar splits on cue. The king then asks the man who just cursed him to pray for his hand, and it works, and then offers him dinner, which is either hospitality or an attempt at ownership. The refusal is specific: no bread, no water, and go home by another road. He obeys all three. And then an old prophet in Bethel hears about the whole business from his sons, saddles a donkey, and rides out after him, which is not a good sign. Next time on 1 Kings: the lie.
1KI 13:18-34	The old prophet's line is that he is a prophet too and an angel gave him a countermanding order, and the narrator states flatly that he lied, without ever explaining why he did it. The man of God accepts a second-hand revelation over the one he received himself, and that is the whole error. The sentence is delivered by the liar, at his own table, which he must have found unpleasant. The death is strange and precise: a lion that kills him and then stands guard, eating neither the body nor the donkey, until witnesses arrive. The old prophet buries him in his own grave, mourns him as a brother, and asks to be buried beside him, which reads like both grief and insurance. Jeroboam, given every possible sign, changes nothing.
1KI 14:1-14	Jeroboam sends his wife in disguise to the prophet who made him king, which tells you he knows exactly what the answer will be and hopes anonymity will improve it. Ahijah is blind, which is presumably the plan, and God briefs him in advance, so the first thing she hears is her own name. What follows is the template every northern dynasty gets: I raised you up, you were not David, you made images and put me behind your back, and now the whole house goes like sweepings, with dogs and birds handling the disposal. The one mercy is the child, who will die before she gets home, will be the only member of the family to get a grave, and is the only one with some good found in him. Next time on 1 Kings: the door.
1KI 14:15-31	The sentence widens from one family to a whole nation: Israel shaken like a reed in water, uprooted, scattered beyond the Euphrates. Then the domestic detail that makes it land, the child dying exactly as his mother crosses her own threshold, mourned by everyone. Jeroboam gets twenty-two years and a footnote directing you elsewhere for the rest. The camera then turns south, where Rehoboam is no better, with shrines on every hill and under every green tree, and Shishak arrives in year five and simply removes the treasury, including Solomon's gold shields. Rehoboam has bronze replicas made for the door guard to carry in and out, which is the perfect image for the whole reign, and for the whole book from here: the same ceremony, in cheaper metal, with everyone pretending not to notice.
1KI 15:1-19	Two Judean kings, one paragraph each, and the accounting is explicit. Abijam is bad and survives anyway because of a promise made to his great-grandfather, and the narrator takes the opportunity to sum up David as faultless except in the matter of Uriah the Hittite, which is a remarkable amount of weight for one subordinate clause to carry. Asa is good, and his reforms are itemised, including deposing his own grandmother for her Asherah pole and burning it in the valley. The one blemish recorded is that he leaves the hill shrines standing, which becomes the standard qualification for every decent king in the book. Then his foreign policy: bribing Syria with the temple treasury to attack Israel from behind, which works and sets a precedent nobody should want. Next time on 1 Kings: Damascus takes the money.
