1KI 15:20-34	Damascus takes the money and burns its way through the north, and Baasha drops his blockade. Asa then does the thing that makes him a competent king rather than merely a pious one, conscripting the whole country to carry Ramah away stone by stone and rebuild it as two fortresses of his own. His epitaph includes diseased feet, which is the sort of detail the chronicler keeps for no obvious reason and which makes the whole book feel real. Meanwhile the north begins its cycle: Nadab lasts two years before an officer murders him at a siege, and the officer's first act as king is to exterminate the previous family, which the text notes fulfils prophecy while conspicuously declining to call it righteous. Baasha then does exactly what Jeroboam did.
1KI 16:1-17	The prophet Jehu delivers Baasha the same speech Ahijah gave Jeroboam, almost word for word, including the dogs and the birds, which is the point: the north keeps producing the same king under different names. Then the machinery accelerates. Elah gets two years and is murdered by his own chariot commander while drunk at a courtier's house, and Zimri's first official act is exterminating the family, exactly as Baasha had done to Jeroboam's. Zimri then holds the throne for seven days, which the text states with a straight face. The army, away at a siege, hears the news and appoints its own general on the spot, and the general marches home and puts the capital under siege. Nobody in this chapter dies of old age. Next time on 1 Kings: seven days is up.
1KI 16:18-34	Zimri's exit is to lock himself in the palace and burn it down around himself, and even he gets the standard verdict for walking in the way of Jeroboam, over a reign of one week. A civil war follows and Omri wins it, then does the one durable thing anyone does in this stretch: buys a hill for two talents of silver and builds Samaria on it, which will be the northern capital until the Assyrians take it. His verdict is worse than everyone before him, which lasts exactly one paragraph, because Ahab arrives and takes the record, marrying a Sidonian princess and building Baal a temple in the new capital. The last verse is a builder losing two sons to a curse in Joshua, dropped in as a hint of how this reign is going to go.
1KI 17:1-24	Elijah arrives with no introduction, no genealogy and no warning, announces a drought, and vanishes for three years. He is fed by ravens, which is not a detail anybody would invent, until the drought he announced dries up his own water supply, which is the joke the text does not make. Then he is sent to a widow in Sidon, Jezebel's own country, who is down to one meal for herself and her son, and asked to be served first. She does it, and the flour and oil hold. Then her son dies anyway, and her accusation is furious and fair. Elijah's prayer is equally blunt, asking God whether he has brought disaster on the woman sheltering him. The child lives, and her closing line is that now, finally, she knows.
1KI 18:1-24	The best character in this chapter is Obadiah, who runs Ahab's household while secretly keeping a hundred prophets alive in two caves, and whose reaction to meeting Elijah is professional terror: the Spirit will move you, Ahab will find nothing, and I will be the one who dies. The king's opening line, is that you, troubler of Israel, gets the only possible answer, which is no, it is you. Then the challenge, and its terms are calculated: their choice of bull, their turn first, four hundred and fifty to one, and no fire laid by anybody. The question to the crowd is the sharpest line in the book, how long will you hop between two opinions, and the crowd's response is to say nothing at all. Next time on 1 Kings: they hop for a whole day.
1KI 18:25-46	Baal gets a full working day and the home advantage, and produces nothing. Elijah's heckling at noon is the funniest thing in the Old Testament and is also a serious theological argument: perhaps he is thinking, or away, or travelling, or asleep. The self-cutting that follows is not funny and the text does not treat it as such. Elijah's own preparation is designed to remove every excuse, twelve stones, a trench, and twelve jars of water poured over the wood, and his prayer is short and asks for one thing, that the people know. Then fire takes the offering, the wood, the stones, the dust and the standing water. What follows the crowd's confession is a mass execution at the Kishon, and then a very small cloud, and a prophet outrunning a chariot.
1SA 1:1-28	A household with two wives, one fertile and cruel about it, one childless and adored, and an annual pilgrimage that reopens the wound every year. Elkanah's contribution to his wife's grief is asking whether he is not worth ten sons, which is not the comfort he thinks it is. Hannah prays without making a sound, and Eli's professional assessment of a woman praying in his temple is that she is drunk. She corrects him and he blesses her, which is the extent of his usefulness. She bears Samuel, and then does the astonishing thing: having finally got the son, she weans him and hands him over permanently, with three bulls and a bottle of wine, and goes home.
1SA 2:1-20	Hannah's song is not a thank-you note, it is a manifesto: bows broken, the well fed hiring themselves out for bread, the barren bearing seven, the poor moved from the dunghill to the throne. It ends by mentioning a king, several chapters before Israel has one. Then the camera pans to Eli's sons, whose priesthood consists of a servant with a three-pronged fork stabbing at the pot before God's portion is burned, and demanding the meat raw, with menaces. Against that the narrator keeps cutting back to a small boy in a linen ephod and the robe his mother sews a size larger every year. Next time on 1 Samuel: someone finally says it to Eli's face.
1SA 2:21-36	Eli's approach to his sons sleeping with the women at the Tent's door is a mild "why do you do such things", with the narrator noting they were past listening anyway. Then a man of God arrives and does the job properly: an itemised indictment ending in the withdrawal of a promise, no old men left in the family line, descendants dying young, and a dated receipt attached, both sons dead on the same day. The replacement is already in the building, wearing a small ephod. The last image is Eli's heirs queueing for a job in someone else's priesthood in exchange for bread.
1SA 3:1-21	Visions are rare, Eli's eyesight is going, and the first word from God in years arrives in the form of a child mistaking it for his boss calling him, three times. Eli, to his credit, works out what is happening and coaches the boy on how to answer. What God then says is a message about Eli, delivered to Eli's apprentice, which is the most awkward possible arrangement. Samuel would rather not repeat it; Eli insists, hears his family's ruin, and answers only that God should do what seems good to him. Samuel's career begins, and the narrator notes that none of his words fell to the ground.
1SA 4:1-22	Down four thousand men, Israel's plan is to fetch the ark, on the theory that God travels and can be deployed. The shout is enormous, the Philistines are briefly terrified, then decide to fight like men, and the second battle is worse than the first: thirty thousand dead, the ark captured, both of Eli's sons killed on the same day exactly as promised. The messenger reaches Shiloh and Eli, ninety-eight and blind, takes the casualty list without falling; it is the word about the ark that kills him. His daughter-in-law dies in childbirth and names the boy Ichabod, which is the whole chapter in one word.
1SA 5:1-12	The Philistines install their trophy in Dagon's temple, next to Dagon, which is either piety or gloating. Morning one, Dagon is face down in front of the ark. They stand him up. Morning two, he is face down again and has lost his head and both hands, leaving a torso and a threshold nobody in Ashdod will tread on ever again. Then the tumors start. Ashdod convenes a summit and the resolution is to make the ark Gath's problem; Gath's population promptly gets the same disease and forwards it to Ekron, which sees it coming and starts screaming before it arrives. The lords finally agree to send it home. A captured god that ruins every city it is stationed in is not a trophy, and it takes them three cities to notice.
1SA 6:1-21	Seven months in, the Philistines consult their own religious professionals, who advise sending the ark back with compensation: five golden tumors and five golden mice, one per lord, plus a pointed reference to how well hardening your heart worked out for Pharaoh. The test they design is neat. Two cows that have never pulled anything, calves locked up at home, and nobody steering. The cows walk the highway to Beth Shemesh in a straight line, lowing for their calves the whole way, while five lords follow to see whether their theology holds. Beth Shemesh is delighted, until the men who look inside the ark are struck down in numbers the text puts absurdly high, and the town's response is to ask somebody else to take it.
1SA 7:1-17	The ark sits in a private house on a hill at Kiriath Jearim for twenty years, which is what a national emergency looks like once everyone has adjusted to it. Samuel's terms are simple and unpopular: get rid of the other gods first, then talk about deliverance. They comply, assemble at Mizpah, and the Philistines read a religious gathering as a muster and attack, which under the circumstances is fair. Samuel is halfway through a lamb when God thunders, and the battle is over before Israel does much beyond the pursuit. He plants a stone and names it Help Up To Now, which is an unusually honest name for a monument. The border is restored and Samuel spends the rest of his life on a four-town circuit.
1SA 8:1-22	Samuel's succession plan is his sons, and his sons take bribes, which is precisely the failure Eli's sons demonstrated a few chapters ago in a book that notices such things. The elders ask for a king, phrased as wanting to be like all the nations, and God's reading is that the rejection is his, not Samuel's, and that they have been doing this since Egypt. He tells Samuel to grant it, with full disclosure. The disclosure is a job description written as a bill of costs: your sons, your daughters, your best fields, a tenth of everything, your servants, your donkeys, and no answer when you complain. The people hear the whole itemised list and say yes anyway, because they want someone to fight their battles for them.
1SA 9:1-13	Israel's first king is introduced by two facts: he is very tall and he cannot find his father's donkeys. Three regions of fruitless searching later, Saul's instinct is to give up in case his father starts worrying about him rather than the livestock, which is a nice piece of characterisation. It is the unnamed servant who knows there is a seer in town, and who has the quarter shekel to pay him, and who talks his master into going. Even the local girls at the well know the seer's schedule better than Saul does. He is looking for donkeys and about to be handed a kingdom. Next time on 1 Samuel: the seer has been expecting him for a day.
1SA 9:14-27	Samuel had a day's notice, so the meeting only looks like a coincidence. Saul walks up to the most famous prophet in Israel and asks him for directions to his own house. Samuel answers by naming what Saul has not told him, the donkeys, and then implies rather grandly that everything desirable in Israel is his, at which Saul takes refuge in his tribe's insignificance. Then a banquet for thirty at which the reserved portion has been kept back for a guest who did not know he was invited. The kingdom is not announced. It is hinted, seated, fed, and finally arranged for privately at dawn once the servant has been sent up the road.
1SA 10:1-27	The anointing comes with three verifiable predictions, which is Samuel showing his work: two men with news, three men with bread, and a procession of prophets whose music sweeps Saul up so completely that his neighbours coin a proverb about it. God gives him another heart, and the first thing he does with it is not mention the kingdom to his uncle. The public selection at Mizpah, meanwhile, opens with Samuel telling the electorate that wanting a king is a rejection of God, then narrows by lot to a man who is hiding in the luggage and has to be fetched. He is a head taller than everyone, so the crowd cheers. A few say nothing and bring no gift, and he lets it pass.
1SA 11:1-15	Nahash's peace terms are the right eye of every man in Jabesh Gilead, offered specifically as a humiliation to all Israel, and he is confident enough to grant them a week to look for help. Saul hears the news while walking home behind his oxen, which is where the newly crowned king apparently still is. Then the Spirit, the anger, and a piece of grim political theatre: the oxen chopped up and posted around the country with a clear implication attached. Three hundred and thirty thousand men turn up. The battle is over by lunchtime, and Saul's first act as a proven king is to refuse to execute the men who doubted him, which is the best decision he ever makes.
1SA 12:1-25	Samuel's retirement speech opens with an audit of himself, which he passes, having invited anyone present to name a single ox he ever took. Then the history lesson, in which every crisis follows the same shape and every rescue comes without a king, arriving at the awkward observation that they asked for one while God was already holding the post. He offers them terms rather than a curse: obey, and both they and the king are fine. The proof is meteorological. He calls for thunder and rain in the middle of wheat harvest, which in that climate is a threat and a demonstration at once, and the crowd immediately asks him to pray that they not die. He agrees, and says refusing to pray for them would itself be a sin.
1SA 13:1-23	The war starts because Jonathan attacks a garrison and Saul blows the trumpet as though he had planned it. Against thirty thousand chariots the national response is to hide in caves, thickets, rocks, tombs and pits, an unusually thorough list. Saul waits the appointed seven days, watches his army leak away, and performs the sacrifice himself on day seven, and Samuel walks in on the last word of it, which is either terrible luck or a test. The verdict is immediate and permanent: the kingdom will not continue, and a man after God's own heart has already been sought. Then the detail that explains the whole campaign. Israel has no blacksmiths, by Philistine policy, and two men in the entire army own a sword.
1SA 14:1-26	Jonathan's plan is two men against a garrison, with a decision procedure attached: if the enemy says wait, they wait; if the enemy says come up, God has handed them over. The Philistines, choosing the second option purely to be insulting, invite them up. Twenty men die in the space of half a furrow, and the panic spreads outward until the ground itself is shaking. Saul, meanwhile, is under a pomegranate tree taking a roll call to work out who started the battle, then sending for the ark, then cancelling the request because the noise is getting better without it. The victory is general, and Saul crowns the day by forbidding his exhausted army to eat until evening. Next time on 1 Samuel: honey.
1SA 14:27-52	Jonathan, having missed the oath entirely, eats honey off the end of his stick, brightens visibly, and delivers the verdict on his father's leadership in one line: he has troubled the land, and a fed army would have won bigger. He is right. The famished troops eventually fall on the plunder and eat it raw, at which point Saul discovers a scruple and a stone. God then declines to answer his question about a night attack, so Saul goes hunting for the sin, swearing to kill even his son, and the lots oblige. He would have done it, too. The army stops him, which tells you where the loyalty is. The chapter closes with a competent summary of his wars, and a note that he collected strong men.
1SA 15:1-35	The order is total destruction of Amalek, and Saul carries out most of it, which turns out to be the problem. He keeps the king and the good livestock, destroys everything worthless, and reports full compliance to a prophet who can hear sheep. Then the excuses, in order: the people did it, and it was for sacrifice anyway, to your God. Samuel's reply is the line the whole book turns on, that obedience outranks sacrifice, and rebellion is witchcraft. Saul finally admits he was afraid of his own army, and grabs the prophet's robe hard enough to tear it, which Samuel immediately reads as a visual aid. Note the order of Saul's requests: pardon first, then, when that fails, please honour me in front of the elders. Samuel does the execution Saul skipped and goes home.
1SA 16:1-23	God's instruction is to stop mourning and start anointing; Samuel's first response is that Saul will kill him, so the trip is disguised as a sacrifice, and the elders of Bethlehem come out shaking anyway. Samuel then repeats Israel's original mistake by admiring the tallest son present, and is corrected on the spot: appearance is a human metric. Seven brothers file past to nothing. The chosen one is not even invited, being out with the sheep. The second half is quietly devastating: the Spirit leaves Saul, an evil spirit arrives, and the cure his staff find is a harpist. So the boy who was anointed to replace him is hired to soothe him, and Saul loves him greatly.
1SA 17:1-31	Two armies on two hills doing nothing for forty days while one enormous man in an inventory's worth of bronze proposes to settle the war by single combat, and nobody accepts. Into this walks a delivery boy with grain, bread and ten cheeses for his brothers, whose first act on hearing the challenge is not fear but market research: what exactly does the man who kills him get? He asks it twice, which suggests he liked the answer. His eldest brother, who has been standing in that valley for six weeks not volunteering, accuses him of coming to watch the battle out of pride. The quotes reach the king, as such quotes do. Next time on 1 Samuel: the interview.
1SA 17:32-58	David's job application consists of two references, a lion and a bear, and the argument that a giant who has defied the living God is functionally another predator. Saul, who has no better offers, lends him the royal armour, and David takes it straight back off on the grounds that he has not tested it, which is the most professional line in the chapter. Five stones, one sling, and an exchange of speeches in which Goliath promises to feed him to birds and David promises the same to the whole Philistine army. Then it is over in one throw, followed by the sword and the head. The strangest detail comes last: Saul, who has employed this boy as his own armour bearer, asks Abner who he is.
1SA 18:1-30	Jonathan, the crown prince, hands the newcomer his robe, sword, bow and belt, which is either friendship or an abdication in mime. David is promoted and popular, and then the women invent the worst possible chant: Saul his thousands, David his ten thousands. Saul does the arithmetic out loud and concludes only the kingdom is left. From there the decline is steady and visible. Two spears thrown at a man playing music for him. A promotion designed to get him killed in the field. A daughter promised and quietly married off to somebody else. Then a bride price of a hundred Philistine foreskins, intended as a death sentence, which David pays at twice the asking rate. Saul gets a son-in-law he is afraid of and stays his enemy for life.
1SA 19:1-24	Saul's plan to have David killed is undone by his own family, twice in one chapter. Jonathan talks him into an oath, which holds until the next spear. Then Michal lowers her husband out of a window and dresses the bed with an idol and a wig of goats' hair, and tells the assassins he is unwell, then tells her father David threatened her, which is a good lie efficiently deployed. The chapter ends strangely and rather beautifully: three waves of arresting officers arrive at Naioth and start prophesying instead, and when Saul goes himself, the Spirit takes him too and leaves him stripped on the ground for a day and a night. The proverb from chapter ten comes back, and it means something much sadder the second time.
1SA 20:1-20	David wants to know what he is guilty of; Jonathan's defence is that his father tells him everything, which is exactly the kind of thing a son believes right up until it stops being true. They design a test around an empty chair at the new moon dinner, with a cover story about a family sacrifice in Bethlehem, and agree that Saul's tone will be the verdict. David also offers Jonathan the option of killing him personally, which is a lot to put on a friend. Jonathan's own request is stranger and sadder: keep faith with my family when your enemies are all destroyed, which is his way of saying he knows how this ends. Next time on 1 Samuel: three arrows.
1SA 20:21-42	The code is set up first, arrows on this side or beyond, because Jonathan already suspects which one he will need. Day one of the feast, Saul lets the empty seat pass as a purity problem. Day two he asks, gets the Bethlehem story, and the mask comes off entirely: an insult aimed at Jonathan's mother, a blunt statement that Jonathan will never inherit while David lives, an order to fetch him for execution, and, when Jonathan asks what David actually did, a spear thrown at his own son. That answers the question. Jonathan leaves hungry and furious, shoots the arrow beyond the boy, and says the words aloud to a child who has no idea what he is carrying. Then the goodbye, and David weeps the most.
1SA 21:1-15	David arrives at Nob with no escort and an explanation nobody asked for, which is always the sign of an improvised lie: secret royal business, cannot say more. What he actually wants is food, and the only food is the bread reserved for God, which the priest hands over after a brief exchange about ritual purity. Then he wants a weapon, and the sword available is Goliath's, wrapped behind the ephod like a museum piece, and David takes it with obvious relish. The chapter notes, quietly, that Doeg the Edomite was present, and then leaves that there. In Gath his hosts recognise him from the song, so the giant killer saves his life by drooling into his beard and scratching at the gate until the king dismisses him as surplus to a kingdom's madman requirements.
1SA 22:1-23	David's first command is assembled from everyone in the country who is broke, desperate or angry, four hundred of them, which is how insurgencies usually start. His parents are parked with the king of Moab. Saul, meanwhile, is under a tree with a spear, telling his Benjamite retainers that nobody feels sorry for him, and Doeg answers the invitation. Ahimelech's defence is perfectly good and completely irrelevant. Then the atrocity: Saul orders the priests killed, his own guards refuse, and Doeg kills eighty-five of them and then destroys Nob entirely, its people, its children and its animals. One son escapes. David says plainly that he saw Doeg there and knew, and that the deaths are on him, which is the only honest sentence anyone in the chapter says.
1SA 23:1-29	David asks God about Keilah, is told to go, then asks again because his men are frightened, and God repeats himself without complaint. He wins, and immediately the walled town he just saved becomes the trap: Saul is delighted that David has shut himself behind gates and bars. So David uses the ephod for two very practical questions, will Saul come and will Keilah hand me over, and gets yes and yes, which is a bleak thing to learn about people you have just rescued. He leaves. Jonathan makes one last visit to say out loud that David will be king and that Saul knows it. Then the Ziphites volunteer as informers, Saul closes in around a mountain, and a Philistine raid saves David by accident.
1SA 24:1-22	Three thousand men are hunting David, and their commander walks alone into the one cave in the region David is hiding at the back of, for the most private of reasons. His men read this as prophecy fulfilled. David's compromise is to commit vandalism on the royal wardrobe and then feel guilty about the vandalism. The speech from the hillside afterwards is a masterpiece of having it both ways: here is the hem, note that your life was in my hands, note also that I am a flea and a dead dog and beneath your notice. Saul weeps, concedes David is the better man, concedes he will be king, and then asks for the only thing that matters to him, that his family not be wiped out. He goes home. David goes back to the stronghold.
1SA 25:1-23	Samuel gets one verse. Then a story about a rich man's sheep. David's message to Nabal is polite protection-racket phrasing, all long life and peace and by the way your shepherds came to no harm while we were around, so anything you can spare. Nabal's reply is the most efficient insult in the book, asking who David even is and refusing to feed men who came from nowhere, and it costs him everything. Four hundred swords are on the road within a verse. Meanwhile a servant, correctly assessing his employer as a man nobody can talk to, goes to Abigail, who loads two hundred loaves, five dressed sheep and enough fruit to feed an army onto donkeys and rides out without mentioning it to her husband. Next time on 1 Samuel: she talks.
1SA 25:24-44	Abigail's speech is the best piece of negotiation in the book. She takes the blame she does not owe, dismisses her husband on etymological grounds, then flatters David by describing the king he is going to be and asking whether that man will want a massacre in his memoirs. She calls it what it is: bloodguilt and self-avenging. David agrees on the spot and admits he was hours away from killing everything male in the house. Nabal, oblivious, is throwing a party. She waits until he is sober, tells him, and something in him gives out; ten days later he is dead, and David's response is a blessing on God's efficiency, followed immediately by a marriage proposal. Two wives acquired in one paragraph, and Michal reassigned to someone else in the last line.
1SA 26:1-25	The Ziphites inform on David a second time, and this time David goes on the offensive, walking into a sleeping camp of three thousand with one companion. Abishai's offer is charmingly precise: one stroke, no second needed. David refuses again, on the same principle, and takes the spear and the water jug, which are worth more as evidence than a corpse. Then he stands on a hill and roasts Abner, the commander of the army, for failing to guard the king, which is the real humiliation of the chapter. Saul's confession is fuller this time, that he has played the fool and erred exceedingly, and it is worth exactly as much as the last one. They part with blessings, and neither man goes near the other again.
1SA 27:1-12	David's solution to being hunted by his own king is to defect to the enemy, and it works perfectly: Saul stops looking the moment he hears. He then asks Achish for a town of his own rather than a room in the capital, which is the request of a man who does not want his movements observed, and gets Ziklag. What follows is sixteen months of double bookkeeping. He raids the desert peoples, leaves no witnesses at all, and reports the raids to his patron as attacks on southern Judah, which is to say on his own people. The chapter's closing line is Achish congratulating himself on having acquired a servant for life, and it is the most thoroughly wrong assessment anyone makes in the book.
1SA 28:1-25	Saul, who expelled every medium in Israel, spends this chapter looking for one, having found God silent in all three approved channels. He goes in disguise, at night, and has to swear by the God who is not answering him that the woman will not be prosecuted under his own law. She is the professional here, and she is the only person who treats him kindly. Samuel arrives annoyed and adds nothing new, just confirmation: the kingdom is David's, the reason is Amalek, and tomorrow you will be with me. Saul falls flat on the floor, having not eaten in a day, and the witch of Endor kills her own fattened calf and bakes bread for the man who outlawed her. That detail is the whole chapter.
1SA 29:1-11	David marches to war against Israel in the Philistine rear guard, which is an impossible position that the Philistine high command solves for him. Their argument is excellent: the surest way for this man to buy his way back into Saul's favour is with our heads, and by the way, we know the song. Achish, who is fond of David and has never seen through him, gives him a glowing character reference and then dismisses him anyway. David's protest is a small masterpiece of ambiguity, asking why he may not fight the enemies of my lord the king, a phrase that could name either king. He rises early, as instructed, and rides away from the battle that will kill Saul.
1SA 30:1-15	David comes home to a burned town and no families, and the raiders have taken everyone alive, which is the only mercy in the chapter. His men weep until they physically cannot, and then start discussing whether to stone him, which is what leadership looks like on a bad day. He consults God properly this time, gets an unusually generous answer, pursue and you will recover all of it, and sets off. Two hundred of the six hundred cannot get across a stream. Then the pivot of the whole rescue turns up lying in a field: an Egyptian slave his Amalekite master abandoned when he fell ill three days earlier, who needs only bread, water and figs before he is willing to walk his old employers into an ambush. Next time on 1 Samuel: the party.
1SA 30:16-31	They find the Amalekites scattered across open ground, eating and drinking and dancing over their loot, which is the worst possible formation. The fighting runs from dusk to the next evening and only the four hundred with camels get away. Everything is recovered, everyone, nothing missing, which is exactly what he was promised. Then the real test: David's harder men propose the two hundred who collapsed at the brook get their families back and nothing else, and David overrules them with a principle rather than an order, that the man with the baggage shares equally with the man in the line, and it becomes law. He follows it up by posting plunder to a long list of Judean towns, which is generosity and also the most efficient election campaign in the book.
1SA 31:1-13	The battle is reported without a single line of comment. Israel breaks on Gilboa, Jonathan dies with two of his brothers, and Saul, wounded and cornered by archers, asks his armour bearer for the one favour left and is refused out of terror, so he does it himself, and the armour bearer follows him. Everything Samuel said the night before happens on schedule. The towns empty out, the Philistines move in, and Saul's head and armour go on tour as evidence. Then the last act belongs to Jabesh Gilead, the town he rescued in his very first week as king, whose men walk all night to take his body off a wall, and who fast seven days for him. It is the only kindness in the chapter, and it closes the circle.
2SA 1:1-27	A messenger arrives with the correct clothes, the correct dirt, and an improved version of events in which he personally finished Saul off at Saul's own request, plus the crown and bracelet as proof. He has misjudged his audience completely. David mourns, fasts, and then asks the one question that matters, why he was not afraid to kill Yahweh's anointed, and has him cut down on the spot. The lament that follows is the finest poetry in the book and mentions Saul's failings not once: don't tell Gath, let Gilboa go dry, they were swifter than eagles, and the line about Jonathan that everyone remembers. David has just gained a kingdom and is genuinely grieving, and the text lets both be true.
2SA 2:1-32	David asks where to go and is told Hebron, where Judah crowns him, and his first political act is a thank-you note to the town that buried his predecessor. Abner installs Saul's surviving son as a rival king, and the civil war opens with an atrocity dressed up as sport: twelve men each, let the young men play before us, twenty-four dead in one exchange. The pursuit turns personal when Asahel, who is fast and will not be talked out of it, is warned off twice by a man who plainly does not want to kill Joab's brother, and then killed with the wrong end of a spear. Abner's plea to stop is entirely reasonable and comes several hundred deaths late. Joab will remember all of it.
2SA 3:1-21	The war grinds on in one sentence, and the family expands in six. Then the alliance collapses over a question of etiquette: Ishbosheth accuses his own general of sleeping with his father's concubine, and Abner's reply is the sound of a man who has been holding a kingdom together and is done. He offers Israel to David that afternoon. David's only condition is the return of Michal, which is a claim on Saul's line as much as a marriage, and the chapter's saddest line belongs to Paltiel, who follows his wife up the road weeping until told to go home. Abner then lobbies the elders and Benjamin, dines at Hebron, and leaves in peace, which is the last thing he ever does. Next time on 2 Samuel: Joab gets back.
2SA 3:22-39	Joab comes home to find the man who killed his brother has been entertained and released, and his briefing to David is thin cover for what he has already decided to do. He recalls Abner from the road, takes him aside in the gateway as if to talk privately, and kills him there. David's response is a masterclass in managing a murder he did not order: a public curse on Joab's house, sackcloth, a walk behind the bier, tears at the grave, a lament good enough to quote, and a fast held until sundown so that everyone can see he is not celebrating. It works. The country believes him. And the last line is the honest one: he is king, and the sons of Zeruiah are stronger than he is.
2SA 4:1-12	Two of Ishbosheth's own officers walk into his house at noon, kill him asleep on his bed, remove his head and carry it overnight to Hebron, where they present it as a service rendered. They have not been paying attention. David has already executed one man for claiming to have finished off a king, and says so before he says anything else, then draws the obvious conclusion about murdering a defenceless man in his own bedroom. What happens to them is thorough and public. The head is buried in Abner's grave, which is David again being careful about appearances and probably also sincere. Tucked into the middle of the chapter, apparently at random, is the note that Jonathan has a surviving son who was dropped by his nurse at the age of five. Remember him.
2SA 5:1-25	The tribes arrive with a speech about bone and flesh and a reminder that David was doing the actual leading while Saul was king, which is tactful of them. He takes Jerusalem despite a Jebusite taunt about the blind and the lame keeping him out, a boast the text then repurposes into a proverb. Hiram of Tyre sends cedar and craftsmen, which is how a foreign king says he has assessed the situation. More wives, more sons, one named Solomon, filed without comment. Then two Philistine campaigns in the same valley, and the interesting part is that David asks before each: the first answer is go, the second is do not go, circle behind, and wait until you hear marching in the treetops.
2SA 6:1-23	Thirty thousand men, a new cart and a party, and then the oxen stumble, a man steadies the ark by hand, and he is dead. David's reaction is not piety but a mix of anger and fear, and he leaves the ark parked at a stranger's house for three months while he works out what to do. What changes his mind is hearing the stranger has been getting rich. Second attempt: sacrifices every six paces, and a king dancing in a linen ephod with everything he has, which his wife watches from a window with a great deal of thinking going on. Her line about the servants of his servants is genuinely cutting, and his reply, that he will be more undignified still, changes nothing except everything between them.
2SA 7:1-29	David, comfortable at last, notices that he has cedar and God has curtains, and proposes to fix it. Nathan says yes immediately and is corrected overnight, which is a useful lesson about prophets answering off the cuff. The reply is a gentle refusal wrapped around an enormous promise: God has never once asked for a building, and has been perfectly content in a tent since Egypt. You wanted to build me a house; I am going to build you one. The son will do the construction, the dynasty will outlast him, and discipline will come without the withdrawal that ended Saul. David then goes and sits down, which is an odd posture for prayer and exactly right here, and spends the rest of the chapter asking who he is and asking God to keep his word.
2SA 8:1-18	A chapter of conquests written like a ledger, and the entries are worth reading. Moab is defeated and then measured out with a rope, two lengths executed to one spared, which is the most chilling sentence in the book delivered in the flattest possible voice. Hadadezer loses seventeen hundred horsemen and every chariot horse but a hundred, hamstrung. Damascus sends help and loses twenty-two thousand men. A neighbouring king who happened to hate Hadadezer sends congratulations in the form of silver and gold, which David dedicates rather than banks. The refrain is repeated twice, that Yahweh gave victory wherever he went, and then the cabinet list arrives: army, records, priests, scribe, bodyguard, and the sons of the king in office.
2SA 9:1-13	David asks whether anyone is left of Saul's house he can be kind to, which is not a safe question for the person being looked for, since new kings usually ask it for the other reason. Ziba produces Jonathan's crippled son from a place called Lo Debar, and the man arrives on his face expecting the worst and calling himself a dead dog. What he gets is all his grandfather's land, a permanent place at the royal table, and a household staff of fifteen sons and twenty servants seconded to farm it. Ziba, who has just been demoted from managing an estate to managing it for someone else, agrees enthusiastically, and the book files his name away for later. The chapter ends by repeating that he was lame in both feet.
2SA 10:1-19	David sends a condolence delegation to the new Ammonite king, whose advisers decide the sympathy is reconnaissance. Hanun's response is a diplomatic insult with real teeth: half a beard each and their clothes cut off at the waist, and David quietly parks them in Jericho until they are presentable, which is the kindest line in the chapter. Then the bill for the joke arrives. Ammon hires thirty-three thousand mercenaries, Joab finds himself with enemies front and back, and his solution is to split the army in two, take the harder half himself, and tell Abishai they will each rescue the other. It works. The Syrians regroup with reinforcements from beyond the River and lose forty thousand horsemen and their commander, after which Hadadezer's vassals stop taking Ammonite contracts.
2SA 11:1-27	The chapter opens with the season when kings go out to war and the note that David stayed home, and everything follows from that sentence. He sees a woman from the roof, sends for her, and she is pregnant. The cover-up is where the horror is. Uriah is recalled and told three times, increasingly bluntly, to go home to his wife, and refuses each time on the grounds that the army is sleeping in a field, which is the most honourable thing anyone says in the book, and it kills him. He then carries his own death warrant to Joab. Joab, who understands his employer perfectly, briefs the messenger to lead with the casualty figures and bury Uriah's name at the end. David's reply is that the sword devours one as well as another.
2SA 12:1-16	Nathan does not accuse, he litigates. A rich man with flocks, a poor man with one lamb that ate from his plate and slept in his arms, a guest to feed, and the obvious theft. David, who has spent his life judging cases, delivers a furious verdict and a sentence, and then hears the four words. What follows is the indictment: I gave you all of this and would have given more, and you used a sword and a wife. The sentence is not death but consequence, and it is precisely shaped: the sword stays in his house, the rebellion comes from his own children, and what he did behind a curtain will be done to him in daylight. He confesses in five words, is forgiven, and is told the child will die. Next time on 2 Samuel: seven days.
2SA 12:17-31	For a week David lies on the ground and will not eat, and when the child dies his servants are too frightened to tell him, which suggests they expect a collapse. Instead he washes, dresses, worships, and asks for food, and explains himself with a clarity nobody around him has: while there was a chance I begged, and now there is none, and I will go to him rather than him coming back to me. Then Solomon is born, and God loves him. Meanwhile Joab, having done the actual siege work at Rabbah, invites the king to come and take the credit before the city gets named after a general, which is the most Joab sentence in the book. The chapter ends with forced labour, saws and brick kilns.
2SA 13:1-18	The consequences promised in the last chapter begin inside the family. Amnon wants his half-sister and makes himself ill over it, and his cousin Jonadab supplies the plan, which relies entirely on David doing as he is asked without asking why. Tamar is sent to cook for a man pretending to be sick, and the room is cleared. What she says is careful and clear: this is not done in Israel, think what it does to me, think what it makes you, ask the king for me properly. He does not listen. And the moment it is done, his desire turns to hatred and he has her put out of the house with the door bolted behind her. The narrator notes she was wearing the robe of a virgin king's daughter. Next time on 2 Samuel: her brother.
2SA 13:19-39	Tamar tears her robe and cries aloud in the street, which is the only voice raised in the whole affair; Absalom tells her to keep quiet, and she lives desolate in his house. David is very angry, and that is the entire extent of David's response, and that failure sets up everything that follows. Absalom says nothing to Amnon for two years, then throws a sheep shearing party and works his father into sending his brothers along. Amnon is killed drunk, the princes ride off in every direction on mules, and the first report to reach Jerusalem says all of them are dead. Jonadab, who supplied the original plan, reappears to explain calmly that only Amnon is dead and that everyone should have seen it coming. Absalom leaves for Geshur, and David mourns for three years.
2SA 14:1-17	Joab, reading his king correctly, hires a professional. The woman of Tekoa arrives in mourning with a case designed to look like a legal problem: two sons, a field, a killing, a family demanding the survivor's blood, and a widow about to lose her only remaining heir. David rules in her favour twice, each time more firmly, until she has a royal oath in hand, and then she springs it, asking why the king applies one rule to her son and another to his own banished one. Her argument about water spilled on the ground and God devising means to bring back the outcast is the best theology in the book, and it is a scripted speech, which does not make it less true. Next time on 2 Samuel: David asks who wrote it.
2SA 14:18-33	David spots the ghostwriter in one question and the woman admits it while managing to compliment him, which is why Joab hired her. Absalom is fetched home and then held at arm's length: back in the city, barred from the king's presence, for two more years. The character note the narrator chooses to insert here is that Absalom was the handsomest man in Israel and cut his hair annually because of the weight of it, five pounds of it, which will matter later. When Joab twice ignores his summons, Absalom's solution is arson on Joab's barley, which works immediately, and his message is a genuine grievance: why bring me back at all, let the king kill me or look at me. He gets the kiss and nothing resembling a conversation.
2SA 15:1-19	Absalom runs the most efficient campaign in scripture: a chariot, fifty runners, and a spot by the gate where he tells every litigant that his case is excellent and the crown has failed to provide a judge, followed by a personal embrace. Two years of that and the country is his. The coup itself is launched under cover of a religious vow at Hebron, with two hundred guests brought along who have no idea what they are attending, and the acquisition of David's own chief adviser. David's response is immediate flight, and his reason is worth noting: not to save himself but to spare the city a sword. He leaves ten concubines to keep the house, a detail the text will collect on shortly. Then he starts trying to send loyal men away. Next time on 2 Samuel: Ittai declines.
2SA 15:20-37	Ittai, a foreigner who arrived yesterday, gives the most loyal speech in the book, and David stops arguing. The whole country weeps at the Kidron. Then David starts making decisions that show he is a better general in flight than he was at rest: the ark goes back, because he will not use it as a talisman, and the priests go back with it as an intelligence network. He walks up the Mount of Olives barefoot with his head covered, weeping, and on hearing his best adviser has defected he prays a single sentence asking that the man's counsel be made foolish. Then Hushai arrives, and David immediately reroutes him into the palace as the answer to his own prayer. The last line puts Hushai in the city and Absalom in Jerusalem, in that order.
2SA 16:1-23	Two men meet David on the road. Ziba brings food and a story about his master hoping to get the crown back, and gets Mephibosheth's entire estate on his word alone, which will look worse later. Shimei throws stones and calls the king a man of blood, and Abishai offers to remove his head, and David's answer is the most interesting thing he says in the whole revolt: my own son wants me dead, so why not this man, and perhaps God will settle the account. Meanwhile Hushai reaches Absalom, greets him twice as king, and survives an awkward question with an answer that is technically true throughout. Ahithophel's first advice is to make the break with David public and irreversible, and it is carried out on the roof, where David once stood looking down.
2SA 17:1-29	Ahithophel's plan is correct and would have worked: twelve thousand men, tonight, kill the king only, bring the country home. Hushai's counter-plan is designed to sound better while wasting the one thing David needs, which is time, and he sells it with flattery, a bear robbed of her cubs, sand by the sea, and Absalom at the head of it all. Absalom takes the worse advice, and the narrator says plainly why. The warning goes out through the priests, two couriers get spotted, and a woman at Bahurim hides them in a well under a layer of grain, which is the second time an unnamed woman saves this dynasty. David crosses the Jordan by dawn. Ahithophel, who knows exactly what has just been decided, rides home, tidies his affairs, and hangs himself.
2SA 18:1-17	The army will not let David fight, on the grounds that he is worth ten thousand of them, which is both flattering and tactically true. He gives one order, loudly, in front of everyone, and it concerns his son. Then the forest of Ephraim, where the terrain kills more men than the fighting does. Absalom, whose hair the text made a point of weighing, is caught by the head in an oak while his mule walks on. The soldier who finds him refuses ten pieces of silver and quotes the king's order back at Joab, which is brave. Joab's reply is that he has no time for this, and then three darts. A pit and a heap of stones for the man who built himself a monument. Next time on 2 Samuel: somebody has to tell the father.
2SA 18:18-33	The chapter opens with the pillar Absalom built because he had no son to remember him, which the narrator places directly after burying him under rocks. Then a very good scene about who has to deliver the news. Joab will not send the priest's son, because the king's son is dead and the messenger's job is dangerous today, and sends a foreigner instead; Ahimaaz insists on running anyway and gets there first with nothing useful to say, having worked out on the road that he does not want to be the one. The Cushite answers the question by not answering it. Everything after that is one line repeated: my son, my son, and the wish to have died instead, from a king who has just won a war.
2SA 19:1-22	An army comes home from winning a war and has to sneak in, because the king is upstairs weeping for the man who tried to kill him. Joab's speech is brutal, accurate, and the only thing that works: you have humiliated the men who saved your family, and if you do not sit in that gate tonight you will be alone by morning. David sits in the gate. Then the politics, which he is very good at: a message to Judah asking why they are last, and a private offer to Amasa of Joab's job. Shimei, who threw stones two chapters ago, arrives first with a thousand men and an apology, and David declines to execute anyone on a day like this. Next time on 2 Samuel: the other claimants.
2SA 19:23-43	Mephibosheth arrives having not washed, shaved or changed his clothes since the king left, which is an alibi you can see, and says Ziba lied about him and stole his donkey. David, who gave Ziba the estate on Ziba's word alone, splits the difference rather than admit he was conned, and Mephibosheth's reply, let him take all of it, you are home, is the most graceful thing in the chapter. Then Barzillai, eighty years old, declines a place at court on the grounds that he can no longer taste his food or hear the singers and would rather be buried beside his parents, and sends a younger man instead. And then the reunion sours immediately into a quarrel between Israel and Judah about who owns the king, with Judah shouting loudest.
2SA 20:1-26	The kingdom lasts about one chapter. Sheba blows a trumpet, says the north has no share in David, and takes Israel with him. David's homecoming includes locking up the ten concubines Absalom violated, provided for and shut away for life, which the text records without comment. Amasa, newly promoted, misses his first deadline, and Joab settles the succession question himself, with a greeting, a hand on the beard, and a sword he had let fall from its sheath. The body is dragged out of the road because it is holding up the march. Then the siege of Abel, where another wise woman stops a war with a conversation: no city needs destroying, hand over one man. The head comes over the wall, Joab goes home, and the cabinet list closes the book.
2SA 21:1-22	Three years of famine are traced back to an old broken oath, and the Gibeonites, offered compensation, ask for seven of Saul's descendants instead. David hands them over, sparing the one he swore to protect, and the seven are killed at the beginning of the barley harvest. What the chapter is actually about is Rizpah, who takes sackcloth up onto the rock and keeps the birds and the animals off her sons' bodies for months, alone, until the rain comes. It is her vigil, not the execution, that moves David to collect the bones of Saul and Jonathan and bury the whole family properly, and only then is the land answered. The rest of the chapter is an appendix of Philistine giants, including one with twenty-four fingers and toes, and a note that David is now too old to fight.
2SA 22:1-51	A long psalm of thanks, and the striking thing is the weather. David calls, and God does not simply answer, he comes: the earth shaking, smoke out of his nostrils, darkness under his feet, a cherub used as transport, and the seabed exposed by his breath, all of it to pull one man out of deep water. The middle section is where a modern reader flinches, since David spends several verses on his clean hands and blameless conduct, which sits oddly beside the last ten chapters, though he is talking about the wars rather than the roof. The last third is a professional soldier's gratitude for entirely practical gifts: trained hands, a bronze bow, wide footing, ground that does not slide, and a promise extended past him to his descendants.
2SA 23:1-39	The last words first, and they are about good government: a just ruler is morning light after rain, and the wicked are thorns you cannot pick up bare-handed. Then the veterans' list, which is where the book keeps its affection. One man killed eight hundred at a sitting. One fought until his fingers locked around the hilt and had to be prised off. One defended a lentil field because nobody else would. Three broke an enemy line to fetch a drink for a homesick king, and he poured it on the ground rather than swallow what had cost that much. Benaiah's entry reads like a bar bet: two Moabites, a lion, in a pit, in the snow, and an Egyptian disarmed with a stick. The roll ends, quietly and deliberately, with Uriah the Hittite.
2SA 24:1-25	A census, which sounds harmless and is treated as a catastrophe, and even Joab, who is not squeamish, asks the king why he wants it. Nine months and twenty days of counting produce one million three hundred thousand swordsmen and an immediate attack of conscience. Gad brings three options, and David's reasoning for picking the plague is sound: God's mercy is larger than any man's. Seventy thousand die anyway, and his line about the sheep, what have they done, is the best thing he says in the book and comes far too late. The angel stops at a particular threshing floor, which is the point of the whole chapter, and when its owner offers the land free David refuses on the grounds that an offering that costs nothing is not one.
ACT 1:1-26	Luke opens volume two with a recap and a dedication, then a question that shows the disciples have learned very little: are you restoring the kingdom now. The answer is a firm no on timing and a very large yes on geography, Jerusalem to the ends of the earth, which is the table of contents for the rest of the book. Then he goes up, and two men in white have to point out that standing there looking at the sky is not the assignment. Back in the upper room, a list of names, prayer, and Peter's administrative proposal, complete with a genuinely gruesome footnote about what happened in Judas's field. The vacancy is filled by drawing lots, which is the last time the church makes a decision that way.
ACT 2:1-24	Wind, fire, and languages, and the detail Luke insists on is the audience: devout Jews from everywhere, each hearing his own dialect, with a list long enough to make the point. Two reactions are recorded, bafflement and the accusation of drunkenness, and Peter answers the second one first, on the grounds that it is nine in the morning. His argument is entirely a quotation from Joel until the last two verses, where it becomes very direct: the man you know about, whom you handed over and killed, God raised, because death was not able to keep hold of him. Next time on Acts: an argument about a tomb.
ACT 2:25-47	The sermon's core is a piece of close reading. David wrote about not being abandoned to the grave; David is demonstrably still in his tomb up the road; therefore he was talking about someone else. The conclusion is put as bluntly as possible, that the man they crucified has been made Lord and Christ. The crowd's reaction is a question rather than an argument, and the answer is short: repent, be baptized, receive the Spirit. Three thousand in a day. Then Luke's summary of what that community actually did, which is teaching, meals, prayer, and selling property to cover other people's needs, reported without any suggestion that it was remarkable.
ACT 3:1-26	A man carried daily to the same gate to beg asks for money and is told there is none, which is not the answer he was expecting, and then gets something else entirely. Luke keeps the physical detail: the ankles, the standing, and a grown man going into the temple jumping. The crowd gathers to look at Peter, which Peter shuts down immediately. His speech is unsparing about the arithmetic of the trial, that they asked for a murderer and refused the Holy One, and then, having said that, he calls it ignorance and offers them refreshing rather than punishment. The proof texts run from Moses through Samuel to Abraham, which is a lot of ground for a speech given in a portico.
ACT 4:1-37	They are arrested for teaching resurrection, which is the one doctrine the Sadducees cannot let pass, and spend the night in custody while the number of believers climbs to five thousand anyway. Asked for their authority, Peter names the name and adds the phrase whom you crucified, which is not a diplomatic choice. The council's difficulty is procedural rather than theological: the healed man is standing right there and cannot be argued with, so they issue a warning instead of a sentence. Peter and John's answer is that silence is not available to them. The prayer afterwards asks not for protection but for more boldness, which is either brave or reckless depending on the week. Then Luke's second note on shared property, and a Levite from Cyprus who sells a field, whose nickname will outlast his name.
ACT 5:1-21	Two people sell property, keep part of the money, and present the rest as the whole. Peter's point is that nobody asked them to sell it or hand it over, so the deception was unnecessary as well as dishonest. Ananias dies on the spot and is buried by young men who do not wait. Sapphira arrives three hours later without being told, is given one chance to answer differently, and does not, and Luke records the same outcome in the same flat sentences. Great fear follows, which he reports as a result rather than a problem. Then a strange detail: the sick laid out in the street on the chance that Peter's shadow will pass over them. The Sadducees jail the apostles, and an angel unlocks the doors overnight. Next time on Acts: an empty cell.
ACT 5:22-42	The best scene in the chapter is administrative. The officers report back that the prison is locked, the guards are at their posts, and there is nobody inside, and then somebody arrives to say the prisoners are teaching in the temple again. They are collected politely, because the crowd is a real risk. The high priest's complaint includes the phrase you intend to bring this man's blood on us, which is an admission. Peter's answer is short and Gamaliel's speech is the practical one: two previous movements, two dead leaders, nothing left of either, so wait and see, and if it is God you will lose. The council accepts the advice and has them flogged anyway, which is a compromise, and the apostles go away pleased.
ACT 6:1-15	The first internal complaint in the church is about catering, and it runs along a language line: Greek-speaking widows being missed in the daily distribution. The twelve's solution is to delegate it, with a sentence about not leaving the word to wait on tables that has been quoted approvingly and uneasily ever since. Seven men are appointed, all with Greek names, which suggests the complainants got the department. Two of them will matter enormously. Stephen, appointed to organise food, immediately starts working wonders and out-arguing several synagogues at once, which is not in the job description, and the response is a set of witnesses coached to say he spoke against the temple. Luke ends on the council staring at a face he describes as an angel's.
ACT 7:1-31	Asked a yes or no question, Stephen delivers the longest speech in Acts, and for a long while it is just history, told so agreeably that nobody in the room could object to it. Abraham, given a promise and not one square foot of land. Joseph, sold by his own brothers and made governor of Egypt. A new king who did not know Joseph. Moses, drawn out of the water and educated by the people who ordered the babies killed. The detail he lingers on is the rejection: Moses kills an Egyptian assuming his people will read it as deliverance, and they do not, and the next day one of them asks who made him a judge. Which is a pattern, and Stephen is building to it. Next time on Acts: the burning bush.
ACT 7:32-60	The history now has a thesis, and it is the same sentence twice: the man God sent was refused. Moses, refused, then made ruler and deliverer. The people, given living words, turned back toward Egypt and built a calf. The tabernacle, Joshua, David, Solomon, and then a quotation arguing that God was never housed in a building, which is the charge they arrested him for, now offered as his own argument. And then he stops narrating and starts accusing, in the second person: stiff-necked, always resisting, betrayers and murderers. What he says he sees, standing rather than seated, is the thing they cannot hear. They cover their ears and use stones. The coats are left with a young man Luke names, and Stephen dies asking that it not be counted against them.
ACT 8:1-40	The persecution scatters the church, which Luke reports as the mechanism by which it spreads, since the scattered ones preach on the way. Philip's Samaritan mission is a success, and the awkward part of it is Simon, a professional wonder-worker who believes, is baptized, and then tries to buy the ability wholesale, which earns the harshest sentence Peter delivers to anyone. His reply, asking them to pray it does not happen to him, is not quite repentance. Then the desert road, and a senior treasury official from Ethiopia reading Isaiah out loud and admitting he has no idea who it is about. Philip's answer takes one passage and one conversation, the baptism happens at the first water they pass, and then Philip is simply gone.
ECC 1:1-18	The Preacher opens by announcing that everything is vapour and then proves it with hydrology. The sun rises and hurries back to where it started. The wind goes round and comes back. Every river runs into a sea that never fills, and then runs into it again. The eye is never satisfied and the ear never full. Nothing is new, and worse, nobody will remember either. Then his credentials, which is the funniest part: he was king, he had more wisdom than anyone before him in Jerusalem, he applied himself thoroughly to the study of both wisdom and folly, and his finding is that the crooked cannot be straightened and that knowing more simply hurts more.
ECC 2:1-26	A funded research project with a sample size of one. He tries laughter, which accomplishes nothing, then wine, then construction on an imperial scale: houses, vineyards, gardens, parks, orchards, reservoirs, staff, herds, silver, gold, and singers with every instrument available. He denies himself nothing his eyes want and becomes the greatest man in Jerusalem's history. Then he looks at the whole portfolio and files it as wind. Wisdom does beat folly, he concedes, the way light beats darkness, and then ruins it by noting that the wise man and the fool arrive at the same event and are forgotten equally. What finally breaks him is inheritance: someone else gets it, and he may well be an idiot.
ECC 3:1-22	The famous poem, fourteen paired times, running from birth and death through killing and healing to war and peace, which is quoted at funerals and is considerably stranger in context. His gloss is the interesting part: God made everything beautiful in its time and put eternity in the human heart, which is precisely the problem, since it means wanting to see the whole design while being built to see a fragment. His practical advice is therefore small and repeated: rejoice, do good, eat, drink. Then he looks at the courts, finds wickedness sitting in the place of justice, and follows the thought all the way down to the observation that people and animals share one breath and one destination.
ECC 4:1-16	He goes looking at oppression, and the phrase he cannot let go of is that they had no comforter, which he says twice in one verse. His conclusion is bleak enough to be startling: the dead are better off, and the never born better still. Then he notices that achievement runs on envy of the neighbour, which is a sharper account of ambition than most economics. There is a small portrait of a rich man with no son and no brother, working without end, who stops long enough to ask who exactly this is for. And then the warmest passage in the book, about two being better than one, someone to pick you up, someone to keep you warm, and a cord of three strands.
ECC 5:1-20	Instructions for religion, and they amount to talking less. Go to listen. Keep your words few, on the strictly geographical grounds that God is in heaven and you are not. Do not make a vow you will be renegotiating later. On corruption he is worldly rather than outraged: do not be shocked when justice disappears in a district, because every official is being watched by a bigger official. Then the money section, which is the best writing here: the lover of silver is never satisfied, more goods only means more people eating them, and the labourer sleeps well while the rich man lies awake. You leave as naked as you arrived. Being able to enjoy what you have is itself a separate gift.
ECC 6:1-12	The specific horror in this chapter is not poverty but wealth you cannot use. A man with riches, honour and every desire met, who is then denied the capacity to enjoy any of it while a stranger eats it, which the Preacher calls an evil disease. He escalates ruthlessly: a hundred children, a long life, no satisfaction and no grave, and a stillborn child is better off, having at least got rest. Even two thousand years of it ends at the same address. All the work goes into the mouth and the appetite stays open. Better what your eyes actually see than what your desire wanders after. Nobody can tell you what happens next.
ECC 7:1-29	A chapter of deliberately unwelcome preferences. A good name beats perfume, the day of death beats the day of birth, and the funeral beats the party, on the grounds that the funeral is where the useful information is. A fool's laughter is described as thorns crackling under a pot, which is precise and unkind. He forbids nostalgia outright, since asking why the old days were better is not a wise question. Then the strangest advice in the book, delivered straight: do not be excessively righteous, and also do not be excessively wicked, since both shorten a life. And a piece of social wisdom worth keeping: do not listen to everything said about you, because you have said worse.
ECC 8:1-17	Court etiquette from a man who clearly worked near power. Keep the command, do not storm out, and remember that nobody gets to ask the king what he thinks he is doing. Underneath that runs the real subject, which is the absence of visible consequences: sentence against evil is not carried out quickly, so people simply decide to go ahead. He does not resolve it. He states both halves and calls it vapour, righteous men getting what the wicked deserve and wicked men getting what the righteous deserve. His response is the same one he always reaches, eat, drink, be glad, and then his flat concluding admission that nobody finds out the work of God, wise men included, however many sleepless nights they put in.
ECC 9:1-18	The great levelling chapter. One event comes to everybody, the good, the clean, the unclean, the man who sacrifices and the man who does not. A living dog beats a dead lion, because the living at least know what is coming. From that he draws, remarkably, a list of pleasures rather than a warning: eat your bread with joy, drink your wine, wear white, keep oil on your head, enjoy the wife you love, and throw your whole strength at whatever your hand finds, since Sheol has no projects. Then the line that has outlived every other in the book: the race is not to the swift nor the battle to the strong, and time and chance happen to them all.
ECC 10:1-20	Assorted observations, mostly about incompetence in high places. Dead flies ruining the perfumer's oil is the model: a small amount of folly outweighs a great deal of wisdom. The fool identifies himself to everyone simply by walking down the road. Then a run of workplace injuries, pits, walls, quarried stone, split wood, and a snake that bites before the charmer has started, all making the same point about consequences arriving mid-task. The blunt axe is the best of them, since more strength is what you use when you have not sharpened anything. Woe to a land whose king is a child. And a closing note on discretion: do not curse the king in your thoughts, because a bird may be listening.
ECC 11:1-10	The Preacher's advice for acting under uncertainty, and it is nearly optimistic. Cast the bread on the water. Spread it across seven ventures and then eight, because you do not know what is coming. Anyone who stands about watching the wind will not sow, and anyone studying the clouds will never reap, which is the whole case against waiting for conditions. He grounds it in honest ignorance: nobody knows how wind travels or how bones grow in a womb, so sow morning and evening and let the results sort themselves out. Light is sweet and the sun is a pleasure to see. Enjoy your youth, he tells the young man, and then, characteristically, mentions the judgment on the way out.
ECC 12:1-14	The book ends on the finest description of ageing anyone has written, and it works entirely by not naming what it describes. The keepers of the house tremble, the strong men bow, the grinders stop because there are too few left, the ones who look out of the windows go dark, doors shut in the street, a bird's voice is enough to wake you, and a grasshopper is a burden. Then the household breakages: silver cord severed, golden bowl broken, pitcher smashed at the spring, wheel broken at the well. Dust back to earth, spirit back to God. The epilogue is warm about the Preacher's craft, warns that books never stop being written, and closes on the only instruction left.
EXO 1:1-22	Seventy people came down to Egypt and the roster has since gotten away from everyone. A new king who never knew Joseph does the arithmetic, decides the guests are now a military problem, and applies the classic solution of unlimited free labour, which has the unfortunate property of making them multiply faster. Plan B is the midwives Shiphrah and Puah, who quietly decline and explain that Hebrew women are simply too quick for them. God rewards the lie with families of their own. Plan C is the river, and Pharaoh stops pretending this is about bricks.
EXO 2:1-25	A baby is hidden three months, then waterproofed and floated, which is how you obey the letter of an order to put your son in the Nile. Pharaoh's daughter adopts him, and by a small miracle of sisterly nerve his own mother is hired to nurse him. Grown up, Moses looks both ways before committing murder, which tells you he knew. It is known by morning. He runs to Midian, defends seven sisters at a well, acquires a wife and a son named Sojourner, and settles in. Meanwhile God hears, remembers, sees, and is concerned.
EXO 3:1-22	A bush burns and does not burn down, so Moses turns aside, which is apparently the whole audition. Shoes off. God has seen, heard and come down, and would now like Moses to do the actual walking. Moses asks who am I, and gets an answer about who God is instead. Pressed for a name to give the elders, he receives I AM WHO I AM, which settles nothing and everything. The briefing includes a spoiler: Pharaoh will say no, repeatedly, and the people will leave carrying Egypt's jewellery.
EXO 4:1-31	Moses raises objections in ascending order of honesty. They will not believe me, so he is handed three signs, including a rod that turns into a snake and makes him run from his own staff. I am not eloquent, and God points out who made mouths. Finally, out of excuses, Moses says please send someone else, and the divine patience visibly thins. Aaron is drafted as a mouthpiece. Then a strange, unexplained night at a lodging place where God moves to kill Moses and Zipporah settles it with a flint. The people, at least, believe.
EXO 5:1-23	Moses delivers the message and Pharaoh replies with the sentence the whole book exists to answer: who is Yahweh. He then diagnoses the problem as idleness, which is his read on people making bricks all day, and cancels the straw while keeping the quota. The foremen get beaten, appeal to Pharaoh, get told they are idle again, and come out to find Moses conveniently waiting. Everyone blames Moses. Moses blames God. Round one of the liberation has left everybody worse off, which the text does not soften.
EXO 6:1-30	God answers the complaint by restating everything more grandly, seven I wills in a row and a name to go with them. It is one of the great speeches, and its audience is too exhausted to hear it. Moses returns to his one reliable excuse about his lips. At which point the narrative stops dead for a family tree, gets through Reuben and Simeon at speed, then slows right down for Levi, because the real point is establishing exactly who these two brothers are. It ends where it paused, with Moses still objecting.
ISA 1:1-31	Yahweh subpoenas heaven and earth and opens with a comparison unfavourable to Israel: the ox knows its owner, you do not. Then a startling refusal of worship, the sacrifices and festivals rejected because the hands lifted in prayer have blood on them. Wash, seek justice, defend the widow, and scarlet sins can go white as snow. The faithful city is now a prostitute with watered wine and bribed princes, and God proposes to smelt her.
ISA 2:1-22	Two visions, back to back, and they do not agree about the mood. First the mountain of Yahweh's house lifted above the hills, nations queueing to be taught, swords becoming farm equipment. Then the present, full of silver, imported horses and gods people made with their own fingers. A day is coming against everything tall: cedars, oaks, towers, ships. People will bolt into caves and hand their gold idols to the moles and bats.
ISA 3:1-26	Jerusalem loses its supports one by one, and the list is thorough enough to include the enchanters. Government collapses to the point where owning a coat qualifies you to rule, and the man drafted declines. The elders are tried for grinding the faces of the poor. Then the women of Zion get an itemised inventory of their finery confiscated, down to the perfume bottles and nose rings, and every item is replaced by its opposite: rope for a belt, baldness for a hairstyle.
ISA 4:1-6	It opens on a demographic horror stated as an offer: seven women to one man, willing to feed and dress themselves if he will just lend his name. Then the register changes entirely. Zion's survivors are called holy, the filth washed off by a spirit of justice and burning, and God builds weather over Mount Zion: cloud and smoke by day, flaming fire by night, a canopy that turns out to be a sunshade and an umbrella.
ISA 5:1-30	Isaiah opens with a love song about a friend's vineyard, which is a trap. Best vines, watchtower, winepress, and the crop is wild grapes, so the hedge comes down and the clouds are ordered to stop. He looked for justice and got bloodshed. Six woes follow: property accumulators, dawn drinkers, people who call evil good, the professionally clever, and judges for hire. He whistles, and a nation arrives that never gets tired.
ISA 6:1-13	The year a king dies, Isaiah sees the real throne, the robe alone filling the temple, and seraphim shouting holy at each other hard enough to shake the doorposts. His first thought is that he is a man of unclean lips, which is a professional problem for a prophet, and it is solved by a live coal applied to the mouth. He volunteers before hearing the job description: preach to people guaranteed not to listen. He asks how long. The answer is until the land is empty.
ISA 7:1-25	Two kings march on Jerusalem and Ahaz's nerve goes. Isaiah turns up with a son named Remnant Will Return and calls the invaders two smoking stubs of torch. Offered a sign anywhere from the depths to the heights, Ahaz declines out of what he presents as piety, and gets one anyway: a child called Immanuel, and before he can tell right from wrong both threatening kingdoms will be empty. The relief is temporary. Assyria is whistled up like flies and shaves the country with a hired razor.
ISA 8:1-22	Isaiah writes Maher Shalal Hash Baz on a billboard-sized tablet before witnesses, then names his newborn son the same thing, which is one way to date a prophecy. Reject the quiet waters of Shiloah and you get the River instead, up to the neck. He is warned off joining everyone else in shouting conspiracy: God will be a sanctuary to some and a tripwire to others. And consulting mediums who chirp and mutter is not research. Those who try it find thick darkness.
ISA 9:1-21	The lights come up on Zebulun and Naphtali, the yoke snapped, the bloodied battle gear burned for fuel, and a child given titles that get steadily larger until they finish at Prince of Peace on David's throne forever. Then the north's judgment resumes, and Samaria's response to losing bricks is a promise to rebuild in cut stone. Enemies eat Israel from both sides, head and tail cut off in one day, and Manasseh and Ephraim turn on each other. The hand is still out.
ISA 10:1-34	A woe for legislators who draft laws specifically to make widows into plunder. Then the strangest argument in the book: Assyria is the rod of God's anger and has no idea, its king boasting about gathering the earth like eggs from a nest nobody was guarding. God asks whether an axe brags about the arm swinging it, and promises to reduce Assyria's forest to a remnant a child could tally. The invasion is narrated town by town, Michmash to Nob, and then the tall trees come down.
ISA 11:1-16	Out of a cut stump comes a shoot, equipped with wisdom, counsel, might and knowledge, and notably not with the habit of judging by appearances. He judges the poor fairly and kills the wicked with his breath, which is efficient. Then the famous zoo: wolf boarding with lamb, leopard with kid, lion on a straw diet, and a toddler as both shepherd and, apparently, snake handler. The remnant comes home from every direction, Ephraim and Judah stop needling each other, and the River is divided into seven streams shallow enough to walk in sandals.
ISA 12:1-6	Six verses of relief, which in this book counts as a holiday. The speaker thanks God for being angry and then stopping, calls him strength and song, and declares he will not be afraid, in that order. Water gets drawn from the wells of salvation, cheerfully. Then the song turns outward and gets loud: tell the peoples, make it known in all the earth, shout, because the Holy One of Israel is standing in the middle of town.
ISA 13:1-22	Babylon's turn. God musters an army from the far end of heaven and the day of Yahweh arrives with the lights off, sun, moon and stars all withheld, hands limp and faces like flame. He promises to make human beings rarer than the gold of Ophir. Then the sack is described without any softening: children killed in front of their parents, houses stripped, wives raped. The Medes arrive uninterested in bribes. And the glory of kingdoms ends as real estate for jackals, ostriches and wild goats, with wolves in the nice palaces.
