ISA 14:1-32	A funeral song for the king of Babylon, sung with enormous enjoyment. The whole earth goes quiet, and even the cedars of Lebanon are relieved that nobody is coming with an axe. Sheol wakes up specially to greet him, dead kings getting off their thrones to ask whether he has gone weak like the rest of them, and his royal bedding is maggots. He had said he would put his throne above the stars and be like the Most High; instead he is dumped unburied like a trodden corpse. God adds Assyria to the list, then tells Philistia not to throw a party over a broken rod, since the root is producing adders.
ISA 15:1-9	Moab is destroyed overnight, and the oracle is mostly a map of people crying in specific places. Ar and Kir gone before morning, weeping on the high places, every head shaved and every beard off, sackcloth in the streets and on the roofs, and the noise carrying from Heshbon to Jahaz. Even the soldiers are trembling. The prophet says his own heart cries out for Moab, which is not how these oracles usually go. Refugees haul their savings over the brook of willows, the water at Dimon runs with blood, and a lion is waiting for the survivors.
ISA 16:1-14	It opens as diplomacy: send lambs to Zion, and Judah is asked to be shade at noon, to hide the outcasts and not hand over the fugitive. Then the reason the request will fail, which is Moab's pride, whose boasts are described as nothing. So Moab mourns for Moab, and the specific loss named is the raisin cakes of Kir Hareseth. The vineyards get an unexpectedly tender lament, the prophet watering Heshbon with his tears, the harvest shout replaced by a battle shout, nobody treading the presses. Moab prays on the high place and it does not work. Three years, counted the way a contractor counts them.
ISA 17:1-14	Damascus is told, in the passive voice, that it will stop being a city. The oracle then quietly folds Israel in with it: Jacob's fatness goes lean, and what is left is gleanings, two or three olives at the top of the tree where the pole could not reach. On that day people will look at their Maker rather than at things their own fingers made, which is the whole argument of the book in one sentence. They planted exotic seedlings, forced them to bloom by morning, and got a harvest that ran away. The nations roar like the sea, get rebuked, and blow off as chaff: terror at bedtime, gone by breakfast.
ISA 18:1-7	An oracle for the land of rustling wings past the rivers of Ethiopia, which conducts its foreign policy in papyrus boats, addressed to a people described twice, in identical words, as tall and smooth and awesome from the beginning. Everyone is told to watch for the banner and listen for the trumpet, and then God announces that he intends to do nothing yet, watching quietly like heat shimmer. The pruning comes at the worst possible moment, just as the flower is becoming a grape, and the cuttings are left for birds and animals to summer and winter on. Later that same people brings a present to Mount Zion.
ISA 19:1-25	Egypt's oracle begins with God arriving by fast cloud and Egypt promptly fighting itself, brother against brother, city against city, then consulting mediums, which the text presents as the symptom rather than the cure. The Nile fails, and the whole economy fails with it: fishermen, flax workers, weavers, wage labourers. Pharaoh's advisers, the professional wise men of Zoan and Memphis, are described as stupid and staggering in their own vomit. Then, without warning, the ending: an altar to Yahweh inside Egypt, a highway to Assyria, and Egypt, Assyria and Israel blessed together, which is not where any of this looked like it was going.
ISA 20:1-6	The year Assyria takes Ashdod, God gives Isaiah an assignment consisting of taking his clothes off. He walks naked and barefoot for three years, which the text mentions once and then explains: Egyptian and Ethiopian captives will be marched away exactly like that, young and old, with buttocks uncovered. The sign is aimed at everyone in Judah who had quietly decided Egypt was the safe bet. The coastland's response is the correct one, a question: if that was our hope, what happens to us?
ISA 21:1-17	A vision arrives like a desert whirlwind and hits the prophet physically: labour pains, deafness, blindness, a heart that will not settle, the evening he had looked forward to turned to trembling. Somewhere a banquet is being laid, and into the middle of it comes the order to oil the shield. A watchman is stationed and stands there day and night until he reports horsemen in pairs, and the verdict is two words repeated: fallen, fallen is Babylon. Then Dumah, where a voice keeps asking what is left of the night and gets the least satisfying answer available, morning comes, and also night. Then Arabia, and one year on a contractor's calendar.
ISA 22:1-25	A city on its rooftops, cheering, with dead who did not die in battle and rulers who fled and were caught. The prophet asks not to be comforted. What follows is a study in competence pointed the wrong way: they audit the wall breaches, gather the pool water, demolish houses for masonry, build a reservoir, and never once look at the one who arranged the crisis. Called to sackcloth, they throw a dinner, on the grounds that tomorrow they die. Then Shebna, found chiselling himself a prestige tomb in the rock, gets balled up and thrown into a wide country, and Eliakim gets the robe, the belt and the key, nailed in a sure place, until the nail gives way under everything hung on it.
ISA 23:1-18	Tyre falls, and the oracle grieves in the language of shipping. The ships of Tarshish howl because there is no harbour to come home to, and news arrives secondhand from Kittim. The sea itself speaks up to say it never had children. Tyre was the market of nations, handing out crowns, its merchants princes, and Yahweh's stated purpose is precisely to stain the pride of all glory. Seventy years of being forgotten, then the trade resumes, described without euphemism as a forgotten prostitute walking the city with a harp trying to be remembered. The proceeds end up holy: not banked, just spent on food and clothes that last.
ISA 24:1-23	The earth gets turned upside down, and the striking thing is how evenly: priest and people, master and servant, creditor and debtor, all treated identically, which is not usually how any of them expect it to go. The covenant is broken, so the curse eats the land. Wine mourns, the harps stop, the drink goes bitter, the city shuts its doors. What is left is gleanings. Whoever runs from the noise falls in the pit, and whoever gets out of the pit finds the snare. The earth staggers like a drunk and sways like a hammock and does not get up. Sun and moon are put out of countenance by the light on Mount Zion.
ISA 25:1-12	Praise, and its grounds are that a fortified city is now a heap that will never be rebuilt, which tells you what kind of song this is. God is a shelter for the poor and a shade in heat, and he ends the noise of the ruthless the way a cloud ends the glare. Then the banquet: every people on this mountain, rich meat, well refined wine, and the covering that lies over all nations pulled off. Death is swallowed. Tears wiped off every face. Then Moab, in the last verses, is trodden into a dung pit doing a swimming stroke, which is a hard image to unsee.
ISA 26:1-21	A song about a strong city whose fortifications turn out to be salvation, sung while another city is being ground into dust under the feet of the poor, which is a pointed detail. Steadfast minds get perfect peace; the wicked get favour and learn nothing from it. Then the most candid line in the chapter, the nation admitting it was in labour and delivered wind, having accomplished no rescue at all. Against that, the dead are told to wake up and sing. The instruction that follows is domestic and strange: go to your rooms, shut the doors, wait a little, because the earth is about to stop hiding its dead.
ISA 27:1-13	The sword comes out for leviathan, twice named, and the sea dragon. Then the vineyard from chapter five gets a rewrite in a completely different mood: God as keeper, watering every moment, on guard night and day, insisting there is no wrath in him, though thorns will be burned unless they come and hold on to his strength instead. Forgiveness has a physical form here, and it is grinding your own altar stones to chalk. The abandoned city gets a calf browsing in it and women collecting the dead branches for firewood. Then the threshing runs from the Euphrates to Egypt, and the exiles are gathered one by one, which is a slow way to do it.
ISA 28:1-29	The drunks of Ephraim are wearing a crown made of a wilting flower, and it will be plucked like the first fig of the season, which is to say instantly and without ceremony. Priests and prophets are drunk on duty, getting visions wrong, and every table has vomit on it. They had mocked Isaiah as baby talk, precept on precept, line on line, here a little, there a little, so God adopts their mockery as his method. Jerusalem's rulers claim to have a signed agreement with death. God offers a cornerstone instead, plus a measuring line and a plumb line, and observes that the bed is too short and the blanket too narrow. It ends, unexpectedly, in agronomy: nobody threshes cumin with a cart.
ISA 29:1-24	Ariel is besieged until it whispers out of the dust like a medium, and then the besiegers turn to chaff in an instant. The armies attacking Zion are compared to a starving man dreaming of dinner and waking up hungry, which is a better insult than a longer one. The people stagger without having drunk anything, because a spirit of deep sleep has been poured out. The sealed-book joke is precise: they give it to the educated man, who cannot read it because it is sealed, then to the uneducated man, who simply cannot read. Worship is lips with the heart posted elsewhere. And the potter question lands: can the thing made inform its maker that he does not exist? Afterwards the deaf hear the book and the blind read it.
ISA 30:1-18	The complaint is procedural: they made an alliance and did not consult God. Ambassadors go to Zoan, treasure goes south on donkeys and camels through lion and viper country, and the recipient is a nation that cannot help. God gives Egypt a nickname, Rahab Who Sits Still. Isaiah is told to write it down permanently, because this is a people who instruct their prophets to prophesy something nicer. Their guilt is a bulge in a high wall, and when it goes it shatters so completely that not one shard is big enough to carry coals in. They were offered quietness and confidence and preferred fast horses, so their pursuers will simply be faster. And still God waits, in order to be gracious.
ISA 30:19-33	Jerusalem stops weeping and gets an answer while it is still crying out. The comforts are specific and mixed: still the bread of adversity, but teachers no longer hidden, and a voice behind you at every turn saying this is the way. The idols are stripped of their gold plating and thrown out as garbage. Then rain, wide pasture, winnowed feed for the plow animals, brooks on every hill, and a moon as bright as the sun with the sun turned up sevenfold. God arrives from far off in smoke, breath like a river up to the neck, sifting the nations with a sieve. And every blow of the rod on Assyria falls in time with tambourines and harps, which is a very particular way to describe a beating.
ISA 31:1-9	The argument against the Egyptian alliance is not moral, it is a category error, and the text says so plainly: Egyptians are men, not God, and their horses are flesh, not spirit. Stretch out one hand and the helper falls with the helped. The two images for God's defence of Zion sit oddly together and are both meant: a lion so unimpressed by a crowd of shouting shepherds that it will not stop eating, and a bird hovering over the nest. So come back, and throw out the silver and gold idols, described as sin your own hands made for you. Assyria will fall by a sword belonging to nobody, its princes losing their nerve at the sight of a flag.
ISA 32:1-20	A competent government is imagined, which in this book counts as a vision: a righteous king, just officials, and a man who functions as weather protection, running water and shade. Also a return of accurate vocabulary, in which fools stop being addressed as noble. Then the address turns to the comfortable women of Zion, told to strip and put on sackcloth because the vintage will simply not arrive. Thorns take the houses of joy, and the watchtower becomes a wild donkey pasture, which is where the chapter would end if the Spirit were not poured out from on high, at which point justice takes up residence in the desert.
ISA 33:1-24	It opens on the destroyer who has so far not been destroyed and the betrayer nobody has betrayed, with the observation that both are only a matter of scheduling. Plunder gets stripped the way locusts strip a field. Meanwhile the roads are empty, the treaty is broken and the peace envoys are crying, which is what diplomacy looks like when it has failed completely. The sinners in Zion ask who can live with everlasting fire and get an answer with no mysticism in it at all: someone who refuses bribes, waving the money off with his hand, and will not look at blood. Zion becomes a tent whose pegs are never pulled, and the plunder is carried off by the lame.
ISA 34:1-17	A universal summons, followed by the most extravagant violence in the book: mountains melting with blood, the sky rolled up like a scroll, the stars falling off like leaves. Then the sword, described as drunk, comes down on Edom, and the slaughter is deliberately staged as a sacrifice, complete with fat and kidneys. Edom's streams turn to pitch and burn without stopping. What moves in afterwards is a careful list of birds and animals, measured out with a line of confusion and a plumb line of emptiness, and the point of the list is the last verse: check the book, none of them is missing, and none is without her mate.
ISA 35:1-10	The desert blossoms, and is handed Lebanon's glory as a gift, which is generous. Weak hands and shaky knees get strengthened, and the fearful are told God is coming with vengeance, which is presented as good news for them specifically. Then blind eyes, deaf ears, a lame man leaping like a deer, a mute tongue singing, water in the sand and reeds growing where jackals used to lie down. The road through it has entry requirements and no predators, which is unusual for a highway. The ransomed come home singing, and sorrow and sighing do not merely stop, they run away.
ISA 36:1-22	Assyria takes every fortified city in Judah and then sends a spokesman to do the rest with a speech, delivered at the aqueduct in front of an audience on the wall. His argument is not bad: Egypt is a bruised reed that goes through your hand, and by the way, the God you trust is the one whose altars your own king demolished. He offers two thousand horses on the condition that Judah can produce riders. Asked politely to switch to Aramaic so the soldiers cannot follow, he turns and shouts in Hebrew, mentions dung and urine, and lists the gods that did not save Hamath, Arpad, Sepharvaim or Samaria. The officials say nothing, under orders, and go home with their clothes torn. Next time on Isaiah: someone finally takes it to the temple.
ISA 37:1-21	Hezekiah tears his clothes and goes to the temple, sending a delegation to Isaiah with a description of the crisis as a labour that has reached the birth with no strength to push. Isaiah's reply is short: do not be frightened of a speech, he will hear a rumour, go home and die there. Which begins immediately, as news of Tirhakah of Ethiopia pulls the Assyrian king away, and he switches from speeches to correspondence, listing the towns whose gods failed. Hezekiah's response to the letter is the best move in the chapter and also the most literal: he carries it up to the temple and unfolds it in front of God, then prays, pointing out that the gods Assyria burned were wood and stone. Next time on Isaiah: God answers in verse, and then in numbers.
ISA 37:22-38	God's reply to the letter is a poem, and it is merciless. Zion is pictured as a girl shaking her head at him. His own boasting gets quoted back verbatim, cedars, cypresses, wells, and drying up the rivers of Egypt with his feet, and then dismantled: the cities he flattened were grass growing on a roof, and the plan was made long before he arrived. God says he knows the man's sitting down and standing up, and will lead him home by the nose with a hook and a bridle. Judah gets two years of volunteer crops and then normal farming. And the siege that never happens is settled overnight: a hundred and eighty five thousand dead in camp, Sennacherib home to Nineveh, and murdered by two of his sons mid-prayer in his own god's temple.
ISA 38:1-22	Hezekiah gets a terminal diagnosis delivered as a prophecy, turns to face the wall, and makes the case for himself in the third person, weeping. It works: fifteen years, plus a sundial shadow that goes backwards ten steps as a receipt. Then his own written account of the illness, and it is genuinely good poetry: life rolled up like cloth and cut off the loom, bones broken as if by a lion, a voice reduced to a swallow's chatter and a dove's moan, eyes tired from looking up. His theological argument is a practical one, that Sheol cannot praise anybody. And after all of that, the medical treatment turns out to have been a fig poultice.
ISA 39:1-8	Babylon sends a get-well card and a present, and Hezekiah, flattered, gives the delegation the complete tour: treasury, silver, gold, spices, oil, armoury, everything, and the text stresses that he left nothing out. Isaiah's questions are the questions of a man who already knows: what did they say, where were they from, what did they see. All of it, says Hezekiah, twice. So all of it goes to Babylon, and your sons will be eunuchs there. Hezekiah's response is one of the more revealing lines in the book: the word of Yahweh is good, and anyway there will be peace in my time.
ISA 40:1-31	The whole book changes register here, and the first word is comfort, said twice. A highway gets graded through the desert, valleys up, mountains down. Then the crier is given his script and it is not encouraging, all flesh is grass, though the punchline is that the word outlasts the grass. God arrives as a conquering arm and, four verses later, as a shepherd carrying lambs, and both are meant. Then the interrogation: who measured the sea by hand, weighed the mountains on scales, and where were you when the advice was taken? Nations are a drop in a bucket. Idol manufacture gets described as manufacture. And the closing promise is aimed exactly at the exhausted: even young men fall over, but those who wait get wings.
ISA 41:1-29	Court is convened, the islands summoned, and the question is who raised up the conqueror from the east. The answer, as ever, is the first and the last. The nations respond to fear by making an idol, and the scene is affectionate and devastating: the carpenter encouraging the goldsmith, the soldering pronounced good, the finished god nailed down so it does not fall over. Israel is addressed as worm Jacob and then promoted to a brand new threshing sledge with teeth, which grinds mountains. The desert gets a full arboretum, cedar, acacia, myrtle, cypress, pine, planted so that people work out who did it. Then the gods are invited to predict something, anything, do good or evil, and the silence is the verdict.
ISA 42:1-25	The servant is introduced by everything he does not do: no shouting in the street, no snapping a bruised reed, no snuffing a wick that is barely alight. Just justice, without giving up. He is a covenant and a light, opening dungeons. Then God, who has been quiet a long time and says so, decides to make noise, and the simile he picks for himself is a woman in labour, gasping and panting. He leads the blind down roads they do not know. And then the sting: who is blind, but my servant? Eyes open and observing nothing, ears open and not listening. The people are looted and stuck in holes, and the question who handed them over has an uncomfortable answer. They were on fire and did not take it to heart.
ISA 43:1-28	The reassurance is specific about the terms: not that there will be no water or fire, but that neither will finish them. Egypt, Ethiopia and Seba go into the ledger as ransom. The court scene resumes, the nations invited to produce one accurate prediction and failing, so the witnesses called are a crowd of blind people with eyes and deaf people with ears, which is a pointed choice of jury. Then the famous instruction to stop remembering the old rescue because a new one is starting, endorsed by jackals and ostriches. The complaint that follows is almost domestic: I never asked much of you, and what you have actually brought me is your sins. He blots them out anyway, for his own sake, and then invites them to argue the case.
ISA 44:1-28	Water on dry ground, Spirit on the children, and people signing their names over to Yahweh. Then the centrepiece, which is a workshop tour. The blacksmith gets hungry and faint making his god. The carpenter uses a line, a pencil and compasses to produce a man-shaped object for indoor use. And the woodcutter plants a tree, lets the rain grow it, burns half for heat and bread and roast meat, announces that he is warm, and turns the offcut into something he begs to save him. The text asks the obvious question on his behalf and notes he never asks it himself. Sins blotted out like a cloud, mountains told to sing, and then, remarkably, a Persian king named as God's shepherd.
ISA 45:1-25	A foreign king is called anointed, held by the right hand, with bronze doors broken open ahead of him, and the text twice makes a point of saying he does not know the God doing it. All of it is for Jacob's sake. Then a claim most readers step around: I form light and create darkness, I make peace and create calamity. The pottery argument follows, in which the clay is invited to ask the potter what he thinks he is making, or a newborn to critique its parents. Cyrus frees the exiles without a fee. And the ending is enormous: look to me and be saved, all the ends of the earth, sworn by God on himself, every knee and every tongue.
ISA 46:1-13	The chapter opens on a logistics problem. Bel and Nebo, being statues, have to be evacuated by donkey, and they go into captivity along with everyone else because they could not even carry themselves. Set against that: Israel has been carried since the womb and will be carried to grey hair, which is the whole argument in one image. Then the manufacturing detail again, gold weighed out, a goldsmith hired, the finished god shouldered into position where it stays, unable to move or answer. God's counter-credential is that he declares the end from the beginning, and the bird of prey he is calling from the east is already on the way.
ISA 47:1-15	Babylon is addressed as a pampered daughter and told to get down in the dust, take up the millstones and wade through the rivers with her skirt lifted, which is a deliberately humiliating picture of a former empire doing labourer's work. The charge is specific: she was handed the exiles and showed them no mercy, including putting a heavy yoke on the elderly, while telling herself she would be a princess forever. Her catchphrase, I am, and there is no one besides me, is a straight parody of God's. Widowhood and childlessness both arrive in one day. And the astrologers and monthly forecasters are invited to step forward and save her, being stubble.
ISA 48:1-22	The addressees swear by God's name, though not truthfully, and consider themselves residents of the holy city. God's reason for announcing things in advance turns out to be pre-emptive: he knew they would credit their idols otherwise. He calls them iron-necked and brass-browed, treacherous from the womb, and says he is holding off for the sake of his own reputation, not theirs. Refined, but not as silver. Then a genuinely wistful line, the peace that would have been like a river, the descendants like grains of sand, in the subjunctive. Then out of Babylon, singing, with the split rock in the desert remembered on the way. And a last flat sentence with no comfort in it at all.
ISA 49:1-26	The servant introduces himself with two weapon metaphors, a sword mouth and a polished arrow kept in the quiver, and then immediately files a complaint: I have laboured for nothing. The response is to enlarge the job, on the grounds that Jacob alone was too small an assignment. Then Zion's line, he has forgotten me, and the reply, which is a nursing mother who cannot forget, plus a name engraved on the palms of the hands. The reversal that follows is domestic and enormous: the woman who thought she was childless has to ask where all these children came from, and the land is too small for them. It ends less gently, with the oppressors eating their own flesh.
ISA 50:1-11	God opens with paperwork: produce the divorce certificate, name the creditor I sold you to. There is none, which is the point. Then, is my arm too short? Followed by the credentials, drying up the sea and dressing the sky in sackcloth. The servant's own testimony is quieter and much harder: an ear woken every morning to be taught, a back given to the people beating it, cheeks given to the people pulling out the hair, no face hidden from the spitting, and a face set like flint. He invites his accusers to identify themselves and notes they will be eaten by moths. The last verse is aimed at the self-illuminated: enjoy the torches you lit, and lie down in sorrow.
ISA 51:1-23	The argument from precedent: look at the quarry you were cut from, look at Abraham and Sarah, who were one household before they were a nation. Heaven goes like smoke, the earth wears out like an old coat, and the people insulting you get eaten by moths, so the reproach of men is a poor thing to be afraid of. Then the prophet wakes God's arm up, reminding it about Rahab and the pierced monster, which is a bold thing to do. And then the cup. Jerusalem has drunk the cup of staggering dry, her children collapsed in the streets like antelope in a net, and the cup is taken out of her hand and handed to the people who told her to lie down so they could walk on her.
ISA 52:1-15	Three imperatives in a row, wake up, get dressed, sit up, and one that is easy to miss: untie the rope on your own neck. Sold for nothing, redeemed without money, which is a strange balance sheet and deliberately so. God's stated grievance is reputational, that his name is being blasphemed all day by the people who rule his people. Then the runner on the mountains, whose feet the text calls beautiful, and the watchmen singing. The departure is specifically unhurried: not in haste, not in flight, unlike the first exodus, with God at the front and also at the back. And the servant returns, exalted and very high, with a face marred beyond anyone's, silencing kings with something nobody had told them.
ISA 53:1-12	Nothing about him invites attention: a shoot out of dry ground, no beauty, no presence, someone people look away from. The turn in the chapter is a change of pronoun. The sickness was ours; the assumption that God had struck him was ours too, and wrong. He was pierced for our transgressions, and all of us had wandered off like sheep, each in his own direction. Then silence: no answer to the oppression, a lamb led away, a sheep quiet in front of the shearers. Buried with the wicked and with a rich man, having done no violence and told no lie. And the last verses turn it forward, an offering that sees offspring, sees light, is satisfied, and is counted among transgressors on purpose.
ISA 54:1-17	A woman who has never had a child is told to sing about her children, and then to do the practical thing and enlarge the tent, because the guest list has grown. The apology, if it can be called that, is carefully proportioned: forsaken for a small moment, gathered with great mercies, sworn on the same terms as the promise after the flood. The mountains may leave; the loving kindness will not. Then the rebuild, which is done entirely in gemstones, sapphire foundations, ruby pinnacles, jewelled gates. And the last argument is elegant: I made the blacksmith who makes the weapons, so no weapon made against you gets to work.
ISA 55:1-13	The invitation is aimed at people with no money, which is a strange thing to advertise at a market: come and buy wine and milk for nothing. The question that follows is the sharpest in the chapter, why you are paying for something that is not bread. Then the distance clause, my thoughts are not your thoughts, as high as the sky is over the ground, which is often quoted as comfort and is not obviously offered as any. The image for how the word works is rain and snow, which do not go back up empty. And the exit is a procession where the hills sing and the trees applaud, with cypress replacing thorn and myrtle replacing brier.
ISA 56:1-12	Two people who had every reason to think they were excluded get addressed by name of category: the foreigner who assumes he will be sorted out of the congregation, and the eunuch who calls himself a dry tree. Both are told otherwise, and the eunuch specifically gets a name in God's house better than sons and daughters, which is the exact thing he does not have. My house, says God, will be called a house of prayer for all peoples. Then the chapter turns and savages the leadership: watchmen who are blind, guard dogs that cannot bark, lying down, dreaming, greedy, and looking forward to tomorrow being just as good a drinking day as today.
ISA 57:1-21	It opens on something quiet and awful, the righteous dying with nobody paying attention, and reframes it as mercy, since they are being spared what is coming. Then the indictment, and it is the most graphic in the book: worship under every green tree, children killed in the ravines, drink offerings poured to the smooth stones of a wadi, a bed set up on a mountain and a memorial hidden behind the door. The most exhausting detail is that they wore themselves out on this road and never once said it was not working. Then, without transition, the high and lofty One who inhabits eternity announces that his other residence is with the contrite. Peace, peace, twice, for far and near. And the wicked get compared to water that cannot stop churning up mud.
ISA 58:1-14	The complaint is filed by the congregation: we fasted and you did not notice. The answer is that on the fast day they were pursuing business and driving their workers, and using the fast as a run-up to a fistfight. Is a head bowed like a reed really what was asked for? Then the redefinition, which is entirely practical and contains no ritual at all: undo the yoke straps, free the oppressed, feed the hungry, house the homeless poor, clothe the naked, and do not hide from your own relatives. Do that and God answers with here I am. Also drop the pointing finger. The reward vocabulary is agricultural, a watered garden and a spring that does not fail, and a job title, Repairer of the Breach.
ISA 59:1-21	The chapter opens by ruling out the two easy explanations: the arm is not short and the ear is not dull. What follows is the problem stated as biology. They hatch adders' eggs, so eating one kills you and crushing one produces a viper, and they weave spider webs, which will never be clothing no matter how much is woven. Then the confession, and it is unusually honest: we grope along the wall like blind men, we stumble at noon, we roar like bears and moan like doves, and we know exactly what our sins are. Truth has fallen in the street. God looks for an intercessor, finds nobody, is described as astonished, and dresses for battle himself, breastplate, helmet, and zeal as an overcoat.
ISA 60:1-22	The chapter is a logistics fantasy, and the details are the point. Camels from Midian, Ephah and Sheba, gold and frankincense, rams of Nebaioth, ships of Tarshish, Lebanon's timber, all converging on one city whose gates never close because the deliveries never stop. Foreigners rebuild the walls that foreigners knocked down. The upgrade schedule is stated in metals: gold for brass, silver for iron, and then peace appointed as governor, which is a strange thing to do with an abstract noun and works. Sun and moon become redundant. And the last promise is a scaling one, the small one becomes a strong nation, quickly, in its time.
ISA 61:1-11	The job description is all verbs, and they are all reversals: good news to the humble, bandages for broken hearts, liberty for captives, release for prisoners. The best line is a swap of one thing for another that rhymes with it, a garland for ashes, oil of joy for mourning, praise for heaviness. Then the ruins get rebuilt by the people who lived in them, and instead of shame they get double, which is the same arithmetic used earlier for the punishment. The speaker's own reaction is to describe getting dressed: salvation as clothes, righteousness as a robe, and the comparison chosen is a bridegroom and a bride, which is not a modest one.
ISA 62:1-12	The speaker refuses to shut up, and then instructs everyone else to refuse as well, giving God no rest until Jerusalem is a praise in the earth, which is a bold model of prayer. The renaming is the heart of it: not Forsaken, not Desolate, but Hephzibah and Beulah, delighted in and married. Then a very concrete oath, sworn on God's own right hand, about grain and new wine: the people who harvested it get to drink it, in the sanctuary courts, rather than watching an army carry it off. And a road crew is dispatched to clear the stones, which is what hope looks like when it has a job to do.
ISA 63:1-19	A figure arrives from Edom in red clothing and the question asked is the ordinary one, why are your clothes that colour. The answer is a winepress, trodden alone, and it is one of the most frightening images in the book precisely because it is domestic. Then the mood changes completely into a history recital: he called them his people, was afflicted in their affliction, carried them, and then they grieved his Spirit and he became their enemy. The chapter ends as a complaint addressed upward, and it is not polite: where is your zeal, why do you let us wander, you are our Father even if Abraham does not recognise us, and we have ended up looking like people you never ruled.
ISA 64:1-12	The request is for something violent, that God tear the sky open and come down hard enough to shake the mountains, on the grounds that he has done it before. What follows is one of the bleakest self-assessments anywhere: our righteousness is a filthy garment, we fade like leaves, and our own sins carry us off like wind. Nobody is even bothering to pray. Then the argument shifts to craftsmanship, we are the clay and you are the potter, which is a claim on the maker rather than an excuse. And the last line is a question left open, will you stay silent and punish us this hard, with no answer given in the chapter.
ISA 65:1-25	God complains about being found by people who were not looking and ignored by the ones who were supposed to be, while he stood there all day with his hands out. The offending practices are itemised, gardens, bricks, graves, pig's meat, and the sting is the last one, telling other people to keep back because you are holier. The reprieve arrives as a wine-grower's instinct, do not throw out the cluster, there is something in it. Then the parallel columns: my servants eat, you are hungry; my servants sing, you wail. And then the new heavens and new earth, where the arithmetic of a good life is that a hundred year old counts as a child, and nobody plants a vineyard for somebody else to drink.
ISA 66:1-24	It opens by dismantling the premise of temple building: heaven is the throne and earth the footstool, so what exactly would a house be for. The man God looks at is poor, contrite and trembling. Sacrifice offered by the self-satisfied is put on a level with murder and pig's blood, deliberately. Then a birth that skips the labour, a nation delivered in one day, followed by the tenderest image in the whole book, God comparing himself to a mother comforting a child. Then fire and chariots. The ending is genuinely strange: survivors sent out to Tarshish, Pul, Lud, Tubal, Javan and the far islands as missionaries, exiles brought home by mule and camel, and then a last verse on unquenched fire that the book simply stops on.
JDG 1:1-18	Joshua is dead and nobody is obviously in charge, so Israel asks who goes first and is told Judah. Judah recruits Simeon on a mutual-help basis and things go well: ten thousand at Bezek, and Adoni-Bezek relieved of his thumbs and big toes, which he concedes is exactly what he did to seventy kings collecting crumbs under his table. Othniel takes a city to win a wife, and Achsah, given dry land, sensibly asks for the water to go with it. Next time on Judges: the long list of what did not happen.
JDG 1:19-36	A conquest report that quietly becomes a register of exceptions. Yahweh is with Judah, though apparently not against iron chariots. Bethel falls because an informant is bought with kindness, and he promptly leaves to found a replacement Luz of his own. Then the tally: Manasseh did not, Ephraim did not, Zebulun did not, Asher did not, Naphtali did not, and Dan is shoved into the hills by the Amorites. The compromise everyone settles on is forced labour, which the book will remember later.
JDG 2:1-23	An angel turns up at Bochim to read out the terms Israel agreed to and did not keep, and the people cry so hard the place is renamed Weeping. Joshua is buried, his generation dies out, and the next one has never heard of any of it. What follows is the book's engine, stated in advance so nobody can be surprised: evil, oppression, groaning, a judge, peace, funeral, worse evil. The narrator notes they relapsed harder each round, which is not how learning is meant to work.
JDG 3:1-31	The leftover nations are officially retained as a test, and also so a generation with no combat experience can get some. Othniel handles Cushan Rishathaim in a verse and a half. Then Ehud, left-handed and therefore not frisked on the correct thigh, promises Eglon of Moab a message from God and delivers it into his stomach, where the fat closes over the handle. He locks the doors and walks out; the servants wait politely, assuming the king is otherwise engaged, until embarrassment forces them to fetch the key. Shamgar closes with six hundred Philistines and a stick for prodding cattle.
JDG 4:1-24	Nine hundred iron chariots, twenty years of it, and the person actually running Israel is Deborah, holding court under a tree. She relays orders to Barak, who agrees to command on the condition that she come along, and is told the credit will therefore belong to a woman. It does. Sisera loses everything, runs on foot, and accepts hospitality from Jael, who gives him milk instead of water, tucks him in, and then uses a tent peg. Barak arrives just in time to be shown the body.
JDG 5:1-31	The same battle again, this time as poetry and considerably more pointed. Deborah credits the volunteers, then works down the roll call naming exactly who stayed home: Reuben deliberating among the sheepfolds, Dan lingering with the ships, Asher at his beach, and Meroz cursed outright. The stars fight from their courses and the Kishon does much of the work. Jael gets the blessing and the tent peg gets four lines. It closes on Sisera's mother at the lattice, reassuring herself that plunder simply takes time to divide.
JDG 6:1-21	Midian arrives annually like locusts and eats everything, so Israel lives in caves. A prophet is sent, delivers a summary of the problem, and is not mentioned again. Then an angel finds Gideon threshing wheat in a winepress, which is where you do it if you are hiding, and hails him as a mighty man of valour, a greeting the situation does not support. Gideon replies with a complaint about God's absence and a note that his family is the least of the least, then asks for a sign and goes to cook a goat. Next time on Judges: the rock catches fire.
JDG 6:22-40	The offering goes up in flame, the angel vanishes, and Gideon's first reaction is that he has seen too much and will now die. Reassured, he demolishes his father's Baal altar, at night, with ten men, because doing it in daylight was unthinkable. The town wants him executed; his father points out that a god capable of defending himself should do so, which wins the argument and earns Gideon the name Let Baal Contend. Then, with the Spirit on him and an army assembled, he asks for the fleece test. Twice. In both directions.
JDG 7:1-12	Gideon finally has an army, and God's first note is that it is too big, on the grounds that a comfortable win would be misreported. Anyone frightened may leave, and twenty-two thousand promptly do, which is a candid poll. Ten thousand is still too many, so there is a drinking test, and the three hundred who lap the water like dogs are the ones chosen. Then God suggests a night reconnaissance, adding "but if you are afraid, take Purah," which tells you what God already knows. Below them the enemy lies in the valley like locusts, camels past counting. Next time on Judges: a loaf of bread with a prophecy in it.
JDG 7:13-25	Gideon arrives at the enemy camp in time to hear a soldier describe a nightmare about a barley loaf rolling downhill and squashing a tent, and his friend helpfully identifies the loaf as Gideon. Encouraged by being an item of baked goods, Gideon hands out trumpets, jars and torches, and at midnight three hundred men make the noise of an army. Midian's army obligingly kills itself and runs. Naphtali, Asher and Manasseh join the pursuit, Ephraim takes the river crossings, and the heads of Oreb and Zeeb are delivered to Gideon like receipts.
JDG 8:1-17	Ephraim's grievance is not the war but the invitation, and Gideon defuses it with pure flattery about their gleanings beating his vintage, which works instantly. Then, faint yet pursuing, he asks Succoth for bread and gets asked whether he has caught anyone yet. He promises to come back with thorns. Penuel gives the same answer and is promised the loss of its tower. He catches Zebah and Zalmunna at Karkor, then returns with a list of Succoth's seventy-seven elders and the noun "taught" doing appalling work. Penuel loses its tower and its men. Next time on Judges: the reason any of this was personal.
JDG 8:18-35	One question about Tabor reveals the whole campaign has been a blood feud: they killed his brothers. He orders his son to do the execution, the boy freezes, and the kings ask to be killed by a professional. Offered a crown, Gideon delivers a fine speech about Yahweh alone ruling, then asks for everyone's gold, receives seventeen hundred shekels of it, and makes an ephod that Israel worships almost immediately. Forty years of peace, seventy sons, many wives, and one son by a Shechem concubine named Abimelech, which the narrator mentions the way you mention a loaded gun on a table.
JDG 9:1-28	Abimelech's pitch to Shechem is that one king is more efficient than seventy brothers, and he funds the restructuring with temple money. Seventy brothers die on one stone; the youngest hides and survives, which is how anyone ever hears about it. Jotham climbs Gerizim and delivers the Bible's first political fable: the useful trees all decline the crown because they have jobs, so the office goes to a thornbush, which immediately threatens arson. Three years later God sends a spirit of mutual loathing, the roads become unsafe, and a newcomer named Gaal starts talking loudly in the vineyard. Next time on Judges: the fable's fire arrives.
JDG 9:29-57	Zebul plays both sides beautifully, encouraging Gaal in public and writing to Abimelech at night. When Gaal spots the ambush coming, Zebul tells him he is seeing shadows, and by the time the shadows have swords it is too late. Abimelech takes Shechem, kills it, and sows salt into the ground, which is a statement rather than a tactic. A thousand more die in the tower under branches he cut personally. Then at Thebez a woman leans over a wall with a millstone, and his last recorded concern is the wording of the report. The narrator says plainly that Jotham's curse has come due.
JDG 10:1-18	Two judges pass with almost no incident, which in this book counts as a golden age, though Jair's legacy is thirty sons on thirty donkeys owning thirty towns, so the accounting is at least memorable. Then Israel takes up the gods of Syria, Sidon, Moab, Ammon and Philistia, a portfolio approach, and spends eighteen years being oppressed by two of those suppliers at once. The cry for help gets an unusually sharp reply: God recites the whole rescue history and then declines, suggesting they try the gods they picked. They repent properly, remove the idols, and he cannot keep it up. Gilead's leadership vacancy is advertised at Mizpah.
JDG 11:1-20	Jephthah is the son of a prostitute, disinherited by his half-brothers and running a gang of outlaws in Tob, which makes him exactly the man Gilead remembers the moment a war starts. He points this out. They promise him the top job, in writing, before God, because a warlord with a grudge reads contracts carefully. Then he opens with diplomacy: a letter asking Ammon what its problem is. Ammon says Israel stole the land. Jephthah answers with a detailed itinerary of the exodus, complete with who refused passage and who attacked first, the ancient equivalent of citing sources. Next time on Judges: a promise nobody should make.
JDG 11:21-40	The legal brief ends well: keep what Chemosh gives you, Balak never disputed it, and three hundred years is a long time to sit on a claim. Ammon does not reply. Then the Spirit comes on Jephthah and he says the sentence that ruins him, promising to burn whatever walks out of his door first. He wins twenty cities. What walks out is his daughter, dancing, an only child. He tears his clothes and tells her he cannot unsay it, and the terrible thing is that she agrees. She asks for two months in the hills with her friends, comes back, and he keeps the vow. The narrator offers no comment and no rescue. Israel's daughters remember her four days a year.
JDG 12:1-15	Ephraim arrives late again, this time with a complaint and a threat to burn Jephthah's house down, and Jephthah, who is not Gideon and has no flattery in him, fights them instead. Gilead takes the river crossings and screens for accent: say Shibboleth. Forty-two thousand men die of a consonant. After that the book files three judges in a paragraph, and what survives of them is arithmetic: thirty sons and thirty daughters traded in and out for Ibzan, ten quiet years for Elon, and seventy relatives on seventy donkeys for Abdon.
JDG 13:1-25	Another forty years of Philistine rule, and the rescue plan begins with an angel visiting a childless woman in Zorah with a detailed prenatal regimen: no wine, no unclean food, and never cut the boy's hair. Manoah, who was not present, prays for a repeat performance and gets one, then asks his own question and receives the same answer given to his wife, verbatim. He offers dinner, is told to offer a sacrifice instead, asks for a name and is told the name is incomprehensible. The angel then leaves via the flame, at which point Manoah concludes they are about to die and his wife has to talk him down with sound theology. The boy is Samson.
JDG 14:1-20	Samson's first recorded act as a deliverer is demanding his parents arrange a marriage with a Philistine woman because she pleases him, and the narrator quietly notes God is using this. On the road he kills a lion with his hands and mentions it to nobody, then later scoops honey out of the corpse and feeds it to his parents, still mentioning nothing, which is a Nazirite handling a carcass and a family eating it blind. At the feast he sets a riddle only he could possibly answer, on a wager he cannot lose fairly. The groomsmen threaten his bride with fire, she cries for a week, and he talks. He settles the debt by killing thirty strangers in Ashkelon.
JDG 15:1-20	Samson turns up after the wedding as if nothing happened, holding a goat, and finds his wife has been reassigned and her younger sister offered as a substitute. His response is three hundred foxes tied in pairs with torches, which burns the harvest and the olive groves. The Philistines answer by burning his wife and her father alive, which the text reports without a word of comment, and the feud stops being funny. Judah's contribution to national liberation is sending three thousand men to arrest their own strongman. He lets them tie him up, then kills a thousand men with a jawbone, composes a couplet about it, and nearly dies of thirst until God opens a spring.
JDG 16:1-16	Samson's judgeship continues to consist mainly of his private life. In Gaza an ambush waits at the gate all night to kill him at dawn, and he leaves at midnight taking the gate with him, doors, posts and bar, uphill, toward Hebron. Then comes Delilah, and the Philistine lords with a very specific price. What follows is three rounds of the same conversation: he names a binding, she applies it, she shouts that the Philistines are on him while men wait in the next room, and he walks out of it. That he does not find this pattern suspicious the third time is the whole characterisation. She switches to asking how he can claim to love her, daily, until he is worn to nothing. Next time on Judges: he answers honestly.
JDG 16:17-31	He finally tells the truth, which is the one thing that could hurt him, and the money arrives before the razor does. He wakes up planning to shake himself free, and the narrator notes that Yahweh had gone, which he had not noticed. Then blinding, bronze fetters, and a mill in Gaza, and one quiet line: his hair began to grow. The Philistines throw a festival for Dagon and bring the blind man out as the entertainment, three thousand watching from the roof, which is the last mistake anyone in that building makes. He asks to be steadied against the two central pillars, prays for one more turn of strength, and takes the roof, the lords and himself together. The tally is that he killed more dying than living.
JDG 17:1-13	Micah returns the eleven hundred pieces of silver he stole from his mother, who responds to the confession with a blessing and then dedicates the money to Yahweh by having idols made out of it, an act of piety with the theology inverted. Two hundred of the eleven hundred actually reach the silversmith; the rest is unaccounted for. Micah stocks a private shrine, ordains a son, and then upgrades: a wandering Levite passes through and is hired at ten pieces of silver a year, clothes and board. Micah's conclusion is that God must now favour him, because he has acquired a credentialled priest. The narrator supplies the line that governs the rest of the book: no king in Israel, everyone doing what seemed right to him.
JDG 18:1-15	Dan, which never took its allotted land, goes looking for someone else's. The five scouts stop at Micah's shrine, recognise the Levite by his voice, and ask him for a blessing on the expedition, which he supplies without asking what it is. At Laish they find a town living quietly, unfortified, with no allies and nobody to complain to, and the report they bring home treats defencelessness as evidence of divine provision. Six hundred armed men set out. On the way past Micah's house the scouts remember the ephod, the teraphim and the two images, and remark that the brothers might want to think about that. Next time on Judges: the priest weighs up his career options.
JDG 18:16-31	The theft is conducted with six hundred armed men standing at the gate, which makes the negotiation brief. The priest asks what they think they are doing and is offered a promotion: better a tribe than one household. His heart is glad, and he carries the idols out himself. Micah's neighbours give chase and Micah gets one genuinely pitiable line, that they have taken his gods and his priest and he has nothing left, before being told to lower his voice or die. Then Dan falls on Laish, quiet and unsuspecting and with no one far or near to help, burns it, rebuilds it and names it after itself. The priest, we learn last, was a grandson of Moses, and the shrine outlasted Shiloh.
JDG 19:1-16	The book's last story opens with the same line about there being no king. A Levite goes to Bethlehem to fetch back the concubine who left him, and the visit turns into five days of a father-in-law who will not let anyone leave without one more meal. The delay matters: the Levite finally sets out too late in the day. At Jebus his servant suggests stopping, and the Levite refuses on the grounds that it is a foreign city, choosing an Israelite town instead, which will turn out to be the single worst decision in the chapter. In Gibeah of Benjamin nobody offers them a roof, and they sit in the street until an old man, himself from Ephraim, comes in from his fields. Next time on Judges: the night in Gibeah.
JDG 19:17-30	The old man takes them in and is generous about it. Then men of the city surround the house and demand the guest, and the host's counter offer is his own daughter and the concubine. The Levite settles it by putting her outside, where she is raped through the night and released at dawn. She gets as far as the door and falls with her hands on the threshold, and her master's first words in the morning are an instruction to get up. There is no answer. He carries her home, cuts her into twelve pieces, and sends the pieces around Israel, and the summons works: everyone says nothing like this has been seen since Egypt. Nowhere in this does anyone ask her a single question, and the text lets that silence stand.
JDG 20:1-25	Four hundred thousand men assemble at Mizpah and the Levite gives his account, in which the mob wanted to kill him and his concubine is simply dead, with no mention of who handed her over. The assembly demands Gibeah's guilty men, which is a lawful request, and Benjamin refuses, which turns a criminal case into a civil war. Benjamin fields twenty-six thousand plus seven hundred left-handed slingers who never miss. God is consulted only on the order of march, never on whether to fight, and the answer is Judah first. Twenty-two thousand Israelites die on day one and eighteen thousand on day two, while the tribe with the numbers, the oracle and the moral case keeps losing. Next time on Judges: the third day.
JDG 20:26-48	This time they fast, sacrifice and ask a fuller question, and get a fuller answer: go up, and tomorrow it is done. The tactic is Ai all over again, a feigned rout that pulls Benjamin onto the roads while the ambush takes the empty city, with a column of smoke as the signal. Benjamin is still shouting that this is going as it did before when it turns and sees its town on fire. Then the arithmetic of a rout: eighteen thousand, five thousand more on the highways, two thousand at Gidom, twenty-five thousand in total, and six hundred survivors sitting on a rock for four months. Israel does not stop at the army. Every Benjamite town, its people and its livestock, goes to the sword and the fire.
JDG 21:1-25	Having nearly destroyed a tribe, Israel sits down at Bethel and weeps over the tribe being destroyed, then treats its own oath as the obstacle to be engineered around rather than the vow it swore. Jabesh Gilead skipped the assembly, so twelve thousand men are sent to kill everyone there except four hundred virgins, who are handed to the survivors at Rimmon. Two hundred short, the elders devise the loophole: hide in the vineyards at the Shiloh festival, carry off the girls who come out dancing, and the technicality is that nobody gave them, so nobody broke the oath. Two towns destroyed and two sets of women taken to preserve a tribe. The book's last line is the one it has been repeating: no king, and everyone doing what was right in his own eyes.
JER 1:1-19	The job offer is retroactive: known before he was formed, sanctified before birth, appointed prophet to the nations before he had any say in it. Jeremiah's objection is that he is a child and cannot speak, which is overruled rather than answered. His mouth gets touched, and the portfolio is four verbs of demolition and only two of construction, in that order. Then two visions, and the second is the one that matters: an almond branch, which is a pun, and a cauldron tilting off the fire from the north, which is not. He is issued protective equipment in the form of metaphor, an iron pillar and bronze walls, which tells you how the reception is expected to go.
JER 2:1-19	God opens in the voice of someone recalling a wedding, the honeymoon in the wilderness, the following into unsown country. Then the questions start, and the sharpest is a market survey: go and check Kittim, check Kedar, find me one nation that has swapped its gods, because mine traded their glory for something that does not even work. The two evils are stated as one image and it is the best in the chapter: they walked away from a spring of living water in order to chisel out their own cisterns, and the cisterns leak. Meanwhile the diplomatic strategy is to go drink from the Nile, then the Euphrates, neither of which is a spring either.
JER 2:20-37	The accusation moves through images faster than it can be summarised: a noble vine gone wild, a stain that survives lye and soap, a dromedary in a hurry, a wild donkey in heat that nobody could turn back. They call a log their father and a stone their mother, and they have precisely as many gods as they have towns, which is a statistic. God notes that his discipline achieved nothing and that their own sword killed their prophets. The comparison that stings is jewellery: a bride does not forget her outfit, and these people forgot him for days without number. And through all of it, the plea in the dock: I am innocent, I have not sinned.
JER 3:1-25	The chapter opens by quoting the law against a divorced wife returning, and then telling her to return anyway, which is the whole book's argument in one verse. Israel is described waiting by the roadside with a prostitute's forehead, refusing to be embarrassed. Then the sisters: Israel got an actual divorce certificate, Judah watched the whole thing and did the same, and came back only in pretence, which is why the verdict is that the backslider looks better than the hypocrite. The promise is unexpected and specific: shepherds with sense, and a Jerusalem so fully occupied by God that nobody misses the ark or bothers building another. The last verses are the people's own words, and they are bleak.
JER 4:1-31	It begins with terms and conditions and turns into an air raid siren: trumpet, get behind walls, a lion already on the road. Jeremiah's interjection is the boldest thing in the chapter, an accusation that God let this people be promised peace while the sword was at their throat. The wind that comes is explicitly not the useful kind that winnows grain. Then his own body gives out, heart trembling at the trumpet. And then the vision, which reads like Genesis played backwards: earth waste and void, heavens unlit, mountains rocking, no people, not even birds. The last image is the city putting on scarlet and gold eye makeup for lovers who have come to kill her.
JER 5:1-31	The assignment is a search: find one person in Jerusalem doing justice and the city gets pardoned. Jeremiah gives the establishment the benefit of the doubt first, assuming the poor just never learned the law, then goes to the important people, who turn out to have broken the yoke unanimously. The comparison for their appetite is livestock, well fed stallions neighing at the neighbour's wife. Their theology is a forecast: nothing will happen, the prophets are wind. So the words become fire and the audience becomes firewood. The last verse is the one that indicts everybody: the prophets lie, the priests rule as they please, and my people love it that way. What will you do at the end of it?
JER 6:1-30	A siege told from both sides. The attackers get dialogue, arguing about whether to go up at noon or wait for dark, which is a chillingly ordinary conversation. Jerusalem produces wickedness the way a well produces water. Jeremiah is exhausted from holding wrath in, which is a job description nobody wants. Then the line the book keeps coming back to: they bandage the wound and say peace, peace, when there is no peace, and cannot even manage a blush. The invitation to stand at the crossroads and ask for the old paths gets a one-line refusal, we will not walk in it. And the metallurgy at the end is bleak: the bellows have been working, the lead is gone, and the silver came out rejected.
JER 7:1-19	The temple sermon, delivered in the temple gate, which is a choice of venue. Its target is a slogan, repeated three times in one verse so you cannot miss that it is a slogan: Yahweh's temple, Yahweh's temple, Yahweh's temple. The argument is simple. You cannot steal, murder, commit adultery and burn incense to Baal, then stand in this building and announce you are safe; that makes it a den of robbers, and he says he has seen it. Then the exhibit: go look at Shiloh. And a prohibition aimed at Jeremiah himself, do not pray for them, which is the harshest thing said to him yet. Next time on Jeremiah: a valley gets renamed.
JER 7:20-34	The argument turns to the paperwork of religion, and the claim is startling: what I commanded coming out of Egypt was not burnt offerings, it was listen to my voice. Since then, prophets sent daily, early, and necks stiffened further each generation. Jeremiah is told in advance that nobody will listen to him either, which is not encouragement. Then the valley. They built the high places of Topheth to burn their own children, and God says it never entered his mind, which is as close as this book comes to horror at something rather than anger about it. Renamed the Valley of Slaughter, filled until there is no room to bury, and every wedding voice in Judah goes quiet.
JER 8:1-22	It opens with grave robbing done as judgment: bones laid out in front of the sun and stars they worshipped, unburied, left as fertiliser, and survivors who would rather be dead. Then the natural history lesson, and it is a good one. The stork knows when to migrate. The turtledove, swallow and crane all keep the calendar. My people do not know my law. Then the false pen of the scribes, and peace, peace where there is no peace, for the second time in three chapters. The chapter ends inside Jeremiah rather than outside: heart faint, harvest past, summer over, not saved, and a question that has outlived its context by three thousand years, is there no balm in Gilead.
JER 9:1-26	It opens with a wish for a bigger tear duct and, immediately after, a wish to move out of town entirely, which is the most human sentence in the book so far. Nobody can be trusted, brother included, and tongues are bows bent for falsehood. God says he weeps too, for the burned pastures where no cattle can be heard and the birds have left. Then professional mourners are called in and told to run a training programme, teaching their daughters to wail, because death has come in through the windows. And in the middle of all this sits the most quoted verse in the chapter, telling the wise, the strong and the rich to stop boasting and boast about knowing the God who does justice instead.
JER 10:1-25	An extended piece of comedy at the expense of manufactured gods, and the funniest detail is the nail: they fasten it down so it cannot topple over. It must be carried, it cannot talk, and, crucially, it cannot even do harm, which is presented as the final humiliation. Against it, weather: the waters roar, the vapour rises, the wind comes out of storehouses. The goldsmith is disappointed by his own product. Then the tone drops without warning into the voice of somebody whose tent is down and whose children are gone. The last request is unexpectedly modest: correct me, but gently, not in anger, or there will be nothing left of me.
JER 11:1-23	Jeremiah is made to read out the terms and conditions of a contract, and his own response is a formal amen, which puts him on the side of the prosecution. The statistic is repeated for effect: as many gods as cities, as many Baal altars as streets. For the second time he is forbidden to pray for these people. Israel is a green olive tree that God himself is setting fire to. And then the chapter turns personal, and this is where the book changes: Jeremiah, who has been announcing plots against everyone else, discovers a plot against him, and it comes from Anathoth, his own hometown, with a simple ultimatum: stop prophesying or die.
JER 12:1-17	Jeremiah opens with a courtroom courtesy, you are righteous, and then asks the question anyway, why the wicked prosper. His observation is sharper than his complaint: you are near their mouths and far from their hearts. God's reply refuses the question entirely and raises the stakes, which is his usual method: if the footmen wore you out, wait until the horses, and if level ground unnerves you, wait for the Jordan thickets. Also, your brothers are in on it. Then God's own grief gets a turn, the beloved of his soul handed to enemies, the vineyard trampled by shepherds, and the line that lands hardest, the whole land is desolate because no one cares.
JER 13:1-27	The first assignment is laundry-adjacent: buy a linen belt, wear it, never wash it, then bury it in a rock cleft by the Euphrates and come back later. It is ruined, which is the entire sermon. The follow-up is a piece of deliberate banality, every bottle should be filled with wine, and the audience's response is exactly right, yes, we know, which is when the meaning lands: the bottles are people and the filling is drunkenness. Then a rhetorical question that has aged into a proverb, can the Ethiopian change his skin or the leopard his spots, applied to people who have simply had a great deal of practice at doing wrong.
JER 14:1-22	A drought chapter, and the details are observational rather than theological: children sent to the cistern coming back with empty jars, plowmen with their heads covered, a doe walking away from her own fawn, wild donkeys panting on bare rock. The prayer is well argued, asking why God is behaving like a traveller who has stopped for one night. The reply is a third prohibition on praying for them. Jeremiah's objection is the one that matters: the other prophets are promising assured peace. God's answer is that he never sent them and they will starve alongside their audience. It ends where these chapters keep ending, with the observation that no idol has ever produced rain.
JER 15:1-21	God opens by naming the two best intercessors in Israel's history and saying it would not matter if either of them showed up. Four kinds of destroyer are appointed, one of them dogs. Then a line that is hard to read past: I am weary of showing compassion. The woman who bore seven sons has her sun go down while it is still daylight. And then Jeremiah's own complaint, which is not about the nation at all: he never lent anybody money and everybody curses him anyway. He says he ate God's words and enjoyed them, and sat out every party. The reply offers no sympathy and a job instead: separate the precious from the vile and you will be my mouth, and a bronze wall.
JER 16:1-21	The prohibitions here are the strangest thing asked of any prophet, and they are all about attendance. Do not marry. Do not have children. Do not go to a funeral. Do not go to a wedding or a feast. Jeremiah's own life is being made into the sermon, which is why it works and why it is cruel. When the people ask what exactly they did, they get a straight answer and an unflattering comparison to their parents. Then the reversal, and it is quietly enormous: the oath will stop being about the exodus from Egypt and start being about the return from the north. Fishermen first, then hunters, and nothing hidden.
JER 17:1-27	Sin engraved with an iron pen and a diamond point, which is a permanent-ink metaphor before permanent ink. Then the contrast everyone remembers: the desert bush that never notices when good arrives, and the tree by the river whose leaf stays green through a drought. Then the verse that ruins self-confidence, the heart is deceitful above all things, who can know it. Ill-gotten wealth is a partridge brooding eggs she did not lay. Jeremiah's own aside is bitter and worth noticing: people keep asking him where the disaster is, as if impatient for it. And the chapter finishes, unexpectedly, on Sabbath freight regulations, with the whole future of the city hanging on what gets carried through the gates.
JER 18:1-23	A field trip to a workshop. The vessel goes wrong under the potter's hands, and the potter, without drama, makes it into something else, which is the point: the clay has no say, but the outcome is not fixed either. God states the rule in both directions, which is the interesting part, since it means the threats are conditional too. Judah's reply is quoted, and it is the flattest refusal in the book: it is useless, we will follow our own plans. Then the plot against Jeremiah, whose weapon is specified as the tongue, and Jeremiah's prayer in response, which is long, detailed, and asks for no forgiveness at all. He is not pretending to be above it.
JER 19:1-15	The prop this time is a clay bottle, and the venue is the valley where children were burned, which the text says never entered God's mind. Elders and priests are brought along as witnesses, so there is no question later about what was said. The threatened siege includes cannibalism, stated plainly and without embellishment. Then the bottle is smashed, and the detail that carries it is that a broken pot cannot be made whole again, unlike the one on the potter's wheel a chapter earlier. That contrast is the whole sermon. Afterwards he walks back into the temple court and says it all again, to a bigger audience.
JER 20:1-18	Jeremiah gets beaten and locked in the stocks by the temple's chief officer, and his response on release is to rename the man Terror On Every Side, which is either a prophecy or an excellent insult, and turns out to be both. Then comes the most exposed passage in the book. He accuses God of talking him into this, calls himself the day's entertainment, and admits that when he resolves to stop, the word turns into a fire in his bones he cannot contain. He manages a verse of praise, and then curses the day of his own birth and the messenger who delivered the good news to his father.
JER 21:1-14	The king sends a delegation to ask for a miracle, and specifically for one of the old wondrous works, which shows he knows the catalogue. The answer is that God is on the other side of the wall: he will turn their own weapons back inward and fight them himself. Then the offer, phrased as a choice and in practice not much of one: stay in the city and die, or walk out and surrender and live. It is the most unpatriotic instruction any prophet ever gave, and he gives it publicly. The house of David gets one condition attached, do justice in the morning, and the smug residents asking who could possibly come down here get their forest set on fire.
JER 22:1-30	A tour of failing kings, conducted at the palace door. The terms are simple and repeated across the book: justice, no violence to the foreigner, orphan or widow, no innocent blood. Then the review. One king is criticised specifically for his renovations, wide rooms, cedar ceiling, painted red, built on wages he never paid, and the comparison is to his father, who ate and drank and did justice, which the text says was what knowing God meant. Jehoiakim gets a donkey's funeral, dragged out past the gates. And Coniah, described as a signet ring on God's own right hand, is pulled off it and filed as childless.
JER 23:1-18	The shepherd metaphor is used as an audit: you scattered them and never once visited. God appoints himself to the job, then promises a Branch for David called Yahweh Our Righteousness. And the oath is reissued, no longer about Egypt but about the north, which quietly makes the exile the new defining rescue. Then the prophets, and Jeremiah's reaction is physical, bones shaking, staggering like a drunk. Samaria's prophets at least served Baal openly; Jerusalem's are worse, because they encourage evildoers to stay put. The last question is the sharpest in the chapter: who among you has actually stood in the council and heard anything?
JER 23:19-40	God's complaint against the false prophets is not that they are wrong but that they never turned up. I did not send them, yet they ran. He asks whether he is only a God nearby, whether anyone can hide, whether he does not fill heaven and earth. Then the dreams, quoted with visible impatience: I had a dream, I had a dream. Let the dreamer tell his dream, fine, but what is straw to wheat? His own word is fire and a hammer that breaks rock, which is offered as the test. And the ending is unusual: the phrase the message from Yahweh is banned outright, because everyone's own opinion has become his message.
JER 24:1-10	Two baskets of figs in front of the temple, one excellent and one so rotten it cannot be eaten, and a question with an obvious-looking answer. The interpretation is the interesting part, because it inverts what everyone in Jerusalem would assume. The exiles already hauled off to Babylon are the good figs, watched over, to be brought back, built, planted and given a heart to know him. The people still living in the city, king included, are the rotten ones. Surviving turns out to be no evidence of favour at all, which is the whole point and the reason it stung.
JER 25:1-18	Jeremiah opens by doing the arithmetic on his own career: twenty three years of speaking, nobody listening, plus every prophet before him, same result. Then Nebuchadnezzar is introduced with the title that must have landed like a slap, my servant. The losses listed are domestic rather than military, and better for it: no wedding voices, no millstones turning, no lamplight. Seventy years, with an expiry date attached to Babylon too. And then the prop, which is the strangest yet: a cup of wrath he has to physically carry from nation to nation until they are all staggering. He starts at home. Next time on Jeremiah: the rest of the guest list drinks.
JER 25:19-38	The cup makes its rounds, and the itinerary is the point, from Pharaoh through the Philistine cities, Edom, Moab, Ammon, Tyre, Sidon, the Arabian kings, Elam, the Medes, and eventually every kingdom on the face of the earth, with Sheshach politely last. The instruction is drink, be drunk, vomit, fall, do not get up. Refusing is not an option, and the argument for that is unnerving: judgment has already started at the city that carries God's own name, so nobody else has grounds for exemption. Then Yahweh roars, and the sound he is compared to is a man shouting while treading grapes, which keeps the cup metaphor going in a much worse direction.
JER 26:1-24	The instruction includes a warning against editing: do not omit a word. He does not, and is immediately seized by priests, prophets and the crowd, all agreed he should die. What follows is a trial, and it is the best-documented one in the book. Jeremiah's defence is short: God sent me, amend your ways, I am in your hands, and if you kill me the blood is yours. The princes acquit him, and the elders cite precedent, that Hezekiah did not kill Micah for saying much the same. Then the chapter adds the case that went the other way, Uriah, extradited from Egypt and killed, which makes the acquittal look less like justice and more like luck.
JER 27:1-22	The costume this time is a yoke, worn on his own neck, with copies sent by diplomatic courier to five neighbouring kings whose envoys happen to be in town. The message is politically outrageous and theologically simple: the earth belongs to its maker, who has handed it to Nebuchadnezzar for now, so put your neck in the yoke and keep your land. Everyone promising otherwise is lying. Then a neat challenge to the temple optimists predicting a quick return of the looted vessels: if you are real prophets, pray that the ones still here stay here. They will not. Pillars, bronze sea, bases, all of it goes, and comes back only much later.
JER 28:1-17	A rival prophet stands up in the temple with a much better message: yoke broken, everything back within two years. Jeremiah's response is a masterclass in not taking the bait. Amen, he says, I hope you are right. Then the test, which is devastatingly simple: prophets have generally predicted disaster, so anyone predicting peace only gets credit once it happens. Hananiah's rebuttal is physical, snapping the wooden yoke off Jeremiah's neck, and Jeremiah leaves without another word. The reply comes later and is better for the wait: you have broken bars of wood and replaced them with iron. Hananiah is told he will die this year, and the chapter ends by noting the month.
JER 29:1-17	A letter to exiles, delivered by diplomatic bag, and its contents are the opposite of what anyone in Babylon wanted to hear. Build houses. Plant gardens. Marry. Have children. Marry your children off. And then the line that must have been hardest of all: seek the peace of the city that took you, and pray for it, because your peace is tied to its peace. Only after all that domestic advice comes the famous verse about thoughts of peace and a future, which is a promise about seventy years from now, not about next spring. Meanwhile the people who stayed in Jerusalem are the rotten figs. Next time on Jeremiah: two prophets get named.
JER 29:18-32	The correspondence turns personal and names names. Ahab son of Kolaiah and Zedekiah son of Maaseiah are to be handed over and executed in front of the exiles, and the text notes their names will become a curse formula, which is a very specific kind of afterlife. Then Shemaiah the Nehelamite, who had written to the temple police complaining that nobody had put Jeremiah in the stocks yet, and quoting Jeremiah's own letter back as evidence, including the line about building houses. Zephaniah the priest, to his credit or otherwise, reads the complaint aloud to Jeremiah, which is how Jeremiah gets to answer it in writing, with a prophecy about Shemaiah's descendants.
JER 30:1-24	The instruction is to write it in a book, which is a sign this material is meant to outlive the crisis. The terror comes first, and the image chosen for grown men is labour pains, hands on their waists, faces white. Then the medical assessment, which is unusually honest: the wound is incurable, there is no advocate, there is no medicine, and the one who inflicted it did so deliberately, like an enemy. And then, without any bridge at all, the same speaker says he will restore health and heal the wounds. The reason given is a nickname that got back to him: it is Zion, whom no man seeks after.
JER 31:1-20	The chapter's argument is loyalty stated retroactively: I have loved you with an everlasting love, said to people currently in ruins. The return party includes the blind, the lame and women in labour, which is not how anyone would plan a triumphal procession and is exactly the point. He who scattered Israel will gather him, which the text is content to leave sitting side by side.
