Then Rachel, weeping in Ramah for children who are gone and refusing to be comforted, and God does not argue with her, he just tells her the work will be repaid. And Ephraim's repentance is quoted rather than described, which lets it sound like an actual person.
JER 31:21-40	Road signs first, which is practical: mark the route home. Then a line nobody has fully explained, a woman will encompass a man, presented as a new thing in the earth. Jeremiah mentions waking up and that his sleep had been sweet, which is the only place in the book he says anything pleasant about his own night. Then the sour grapes proverb is retired: no more inherited dental damage, everyone answers for himself. And then the new covenant, whose novelty is entirely about location, the law moved from tablets to the inside of people, with no teaching required because everyone already knows. Even the valley of corpses and ashes gets rezoned as holy.
JER 32:1-23	Jeremiah is in custody, during a siege, for saying the city will fall, and while there he is instructed to buy real estate. His cousin turns up with the offer, on cue, which is how Jeremiah knows it is genuine. The transaction is recorded with an accountant's care, seventeen shekels weighed out, deed signed and sealed, two copies, witnesses, and the whole lot handed to Baruch to be stored in a jar. The point is a single sentence: houses and fields and vineyards will be bought in this land again. Then his prayer starts confidently, nothing is too hard for you, and works steadily downhill through the history until it arrives at the siege. Next time on Jeremiah: he asks what the field was for.
JER 32:24-44	Jeremiah's prayer ends by pointing out the window: the ramps are up, the city is gone, and you had me buy a field. It is the most reasonable complaint in the book. God's reply quotes Jeremiah's own opening line back at him as a question, is there anything too hard for me, which is either an answer or a refusal to give one. Then the charge sheet, ending on children passed through fire, which he says never entered his mind. And then the reversal, phrased in the same registry-office language as the purchase: fields bought for money, deeds signed, sealed and witnessed, across Benjamin and Judah. The deed in the jar is the argument.
JER 33:1-26	Still under guard, and the second word opens with an invitation rather than a verdict: call to me and I will show you great and difficult things you do not know. The houses pulled down for siege works are going to be filled with bodies, and in the same breath he promises health, cure, peace and truth. The specific promise is a soundscape, the noise of a wedding heard again in a place currently described as having neither man nor animal in it, and shepherds counting sheep in the hill country. The guarantee offered is the sunrise: break day and night if you can, and then the covenant with David can be broken too, and not before.
JER 34:1-22	Zedekiah gets both halves of his sentence: the city burns and he goes to Babylon, but he dies in peace with a proper royal funeral, which is a strangely gentle detail to include. Two fortified cities are left. Then the incident that gives the chapter its spine. Zedekiah proclaims freedom for every Hebrew slave, everyone complies, and then everyone changes their minds and takes them back, which is the most human and worst thing in the book. God's response is a pun with teeth: you would not proclaim liberty, so I proclaim a liberty to the sword, plague and famine. The men who walked between the halves of the calf will be handed over.
JER 35:1-19	God's instruction is to organise a drinks party for a family famous for teetotalism, in a temple side room, which is a setup rather than a hospitality failure. The Rechabites decline, and their explanation is thorough: no wine, no houses, no crops, no vineyards, tents only, on the word of an ancestor named Jonadab, and they mention they are only inside the walls because of two invading armies. The sermon writes itself. One dead man's household rule has held for generations; God's instructions, delivered in person and by every prophet available, have not held at all. The Rechabites get a promise of their own, which is the only unambiguously good news anyone receives in this stretch of the book.
JER 36:1-15	The whole chapter is about a document, and the details of its production are lovingly recorded. Jeremiah dictates, Baruch writes, and Jeremiah explains that he is banned from the temple, so the reading has to be done by proxy on a fast day when the largest possible crowd is in town. It works. Baruch reads it in a scribe's room off the upper court, one man in the audience walks down to the palace where the officials are sitting, and reports. The officials, being officials, do not act on the report; they send for the document and have it read again from the beginning. Next time on Jeremiah: the king has a fire going.
JER 36:16-32	The officials react correctly, with fear, and even ask the right procedural question, how exactly was this written. They also do the decent thing and tell Baruch to disappear. Then the king, sitting by a brazier because it is the ninth month, listens to three or four columns, cuts the scroll up with a penknife and feeds it into the fire in pieces. The detail that indicts him is that nobody in the room tore their clothes. Three men try to stop him and are ignored. The reply is the neatest thing in the book: write it again. And the second edition is longer, with many similar words added, which is the worst possible outcome for a man with a penknife.
JER 37:1-21	Egypt's army marches out, the Chaldeans lift the siege, and for a moment everything looks recoverable. Jeremiah's word is that Pharaoh is going home, and that even if the besieging army were reduced to wounded men they would get up and burn the city anyway. Meanwhile he takes advantage of the lull to go see about his property, and is arrested at the gate as a defector, which is not an unreasonable charge given what he has been telling people to do. Beaten, dungeoned, then summoned for a private royal audience, where his answer is the same one that got him arrested, plus a fair question: where are the prophets who told you Babylon was not coming?
JER 38:1-14	The complaint against Jeremiah is now purely military: he is demoralising the troops. It is accurate. Zedekiah's reply to the officials asking for a death sentence is one of the saddest lines any king gets, he is in your hand, the king can do nothing against you. So Jeremiah goes down a cistern on ropes into mud. What saves him is an Ethiopian eunuch in the palace, who goes and tells the king plainly that this was evil and that the man will starve down there. And the rescue includes a detail nobody had to record: Ebedmelech finds old rags for Jeremiah to pad his armpits so the ropes do not cut him. Next time on Jeremiah: the king wants a private word.
JER 38:15-28	Jeremiah's opening position in the interview is that answering will get him killed and that the advice will be ignored regardless, which is experience rather than pessimism. Under oath, he gives terms: walk out and surrender, and you and the city both survive. Zedekiah's objection reveals what he has actually been weighing all along, not the city, not the outcome, but the possibility of being mocked by the men who defected before him. So Jeremiah shows him the alternative, the palace women led out reciting a taunt at him, feet sunk in the mire, which is the same mud Jeremiah was just pulled from. The chapter ends with a cover story agreed between the two of them, and the story holds.
JER 39:1-18	Everything Jeremiah spent forty chapters saying happens in eighteen verses, and the narration is flat, which makes it worse. Zedekiah runs at night through the garden gate and is caught on the Jericho plain, and what Nebuchadnezzar does to him is ordered with care: sons killed in front of him first, then his eyes put out, so that the last thing he ever saw was chosen for him. The city burns. And then two small mercies, recorded with the same flatness: the poorest people are given fields and vineyards, and Nebuchadnezzar personally orders that Jeremiah be looked after. Ebedmelech, who found the rags, is told he will live.
JER 40:1-16	Jeremiah is released by a Babylonian officer who delivers a short and theologically accurate sermon about why Jerusalem fell, which is a strange source for it. He is offered Babylon with a pension or the freedom to go anywhere, plus food and a gift, and he chooses to stay with the governor at Mizpah. For a few verses things go well: refugees come back from Moab, Ammon and Edom, and there is a large harvest of wine and summer fruit, which is the only prosperity in the book. Then Johanan brings intelligence about an assassination plot, names the sponsor, and offers to handle it quietly. Gedaliah's reply is the last mistake of his life: you are lying about Ishmael.
JER 41:1-18	Ishmael eats a meal with Gedaliah and then kills him at the table, along with the Jews present and the Chaldean garrison, and for a day nobody knows. What follows is worse: eighty pilgrims arrive in mourning with offerings for a temple that no longer exists, and Ishmael goes out to meet them weeping, which is the most calculated thing anyone does in this book, then kills them and fills Asa's old cistern with the bodies. Ten survive by disclosing where the food is hidden. He takes the survivors and the king's daughters toward Ammon, is intercepted at Gibeon, and the captives simply walk over to the other side. He escapes with eight men, and the rest set out for Egypt.
JER 42:1-22	The delegation's request is careful and their oath is emphatic: whatever the answer is, good or bad, we will do it. Jeremiah takes ten days, which suggests he did not have the answer in advance. The answer is stay, with a reason that is unusual in this book, that God grieves over the harm he has brought. The alternative is spelled out with a neat inversion: the sword you are running from will meet you in Egypt and the famine will follow you there. And then Jeremiah adds his own verdict, which is not a prophecy but an accusation, that they had already decided before they asked and dealt deceitfully with their own souls.
JER 43:1-13	The response to a ten-day-awaited answer is to accuse the prophet of taking dictation from his secretary. Baruch, apparently, has turned him against them. Then they do exactly what they were told not to do, and take Jeremiah and Baruch along, which means the prophet ends up in Egypt against his own preaching. There he is given another prop job: bury large stones in the brick pavement at Pharaoh's own front door, in front of witnesses, and announce that a Babylonian throne will one day stand on that exact spot. The image for the conquest is domestic and unhurried: Nebuchadnezzar will put Egypt on the way a shepherd puts on his coat, and leave in peace.
JER 44:1-16	The argument is made from the evidence outside the window, or rather from the evidence they left behind: you have seen what happened to Jerusalem, and here you are doing the same thing in a new country. The question is put in terms of self-harm, why commit great evil against your own souls, and the list of past offenders is unusually complete, including the kings' wives and their own. The threat is that the return they are dreaming about will not happen. And then the audience answers, which is rare in this book, and their answer is four words long: we will not listen. Next time on Jeremiah: they explain their reasoning.
JER 44:17-30	Their reasoning turns out to be economic and, on its own terms, evidence-based: when we burned incense to the queen of the sky we had plenty of food, and things only fell apart after we stopped. The women add a pointed procedural note, that their husbands were fully involved in making the cakes. Jeremiah's reply concedes the timeline and reverses the causation. Then he says the most cutting thing available: fine, keep your vows, perform them. And the sentence that follows is a kind of erasure, that no Judean in Egypt will ever again use God's name in an oath. The sign offered is Pharaoh Hophra, who will go the way Zedekiah went.
JER 45:1-5	Five verses, addressed to the man who did the writing. Baruch's complaint is quoted rather than described, which gives it dignity: sorrow added to pain, worn out with groaning, no rest. God does not deny any of it. What he offers instead is scale, that he is dismantling what he built across the whole land, so this is not a good moment to be seeking great things for yourself. The one promise is small, personal and, in context, enormous: wherever you go, you will escape with your life. It is the only thing anybody is offered in these chapters.
JER 46:1-28	The oracles against the nations open with a battle that had already happened, Carchemish, and the poetry is all in the preparation: harness, polish, put on the mail. Then, mid-stanza, the question, why am I watching them run. Egypt is a river in flood announcing it will cover the earth, which the text lets stand for a verse before contradicting it. The best line is a jab at a famous export: go up to Gilead and get balm, and note that all your medicines are useless. Then the second invasion, with Egypt as a beautiful heifer, her mercenaries as stall-fed calves who bolt, and the invaders as woodcutters, more numerous than locusts. Jacob gets the last word and is told not to be afraid.
JER 47:1-7	A short oracle, and its best feature is the detail about the fathers: at the sound of the chariots they do not turn back for their own children, because their hands have gone weak. That single image does more than the flood metaphor around it. Gaza gets a shaved head, Ashkelon gets nothing at all. And then the chapter does something unusual and stays with it: it addresses the sword itself, asks how long before it will be quiet, and tells it to go back in its scabbard and rest. The sword's answer, given by the prophet, is that it has orders and cannot.
JER 48:1-26	Moab's oracle works as a gazetteer, and the town names keep coming, one destroyed per clause. There is a joke buried in it, since Madmen is a real place and is told it will be brought to silence. The best image is a wine cellar: Moab has sat undisturbed on his lees for so long that his taste and smell are unchanged, and the punishment is decanting, men coming to pour him off and break the jars. Chemosh goes into captivity with his own staff, which is the recurring humiliation of these oracles. And the aside about doing God's work negligently is a chilling thing to find in the middle of it.
JER 48:27-47	It opens with a grudge stated plainly: you shook your head every time you mentioned Israel. Then, unexpectedly, the register shifts, and God announces that he is going to wail for Moab himself, weeping more than Jazer ever wept over the vine of Sibmah, and that his heart sounds like pipes for the men of Kir Heres. The specific losses are agricultural and precise: no shouting at the winepress, and the text puts it as the shouting will be no shouting. The terror, pit and snare sequence returns from Isaiah, and the closing line is one word of hope for a nation that has just been dismantled for forty seven verses.
JER 49:1-19	Ammon first, and the opening question is a property dispute: has Israel no heirs, that somebody else is living in Gad? Their god goes into exile with his own priests, which happens to every god in these chapters. Then Edom, and the argument turns on thoroughness. Grape pickers leave gleanings behind. Thieves stop once they have enough. Nobody is that restrained here, and Esau has been stripped with his hiding places opened. Tucked into the middle of that is an unexpected offer, that the orphans will be kept alive and the widows can trust him. And the pride is located precisely: you nested in the rock clefts as high as an eagle, and it will not help.
JER 49:20-39	Four short oracles run together, and each gets one good image. Edom's fall is loud enough to be heard at the Red Sea. Damascus is asked, with something like affection, how the city of praise has not been abandoned already, and then her palaces burn. Kedar and Hazor are indicted for comfort: a nation at ease, with neither gates nor bars, dwelling alone, which reads less like a charge than an epitaph, and their camels are itemised as plunder. Elam gets the four winds from four directions and a promise that no country will be without an Elamite refugee. Two of the four end with the captivity reversed, which is easy to miss and worth noticing.
JER 50:1-23	The instruction is publicity: raise a standard, publish it, do not conceal it, Babylon is taken and its gods are embarrassed. Israel's own shepherds get the blame for the sheep wandering from mountain to hill until they forgot where home was, and the predators are quoted offering a theological defence for eating them, which is the nastiest line in the chapter. Then the reversal, and the accounting is careful: Assyria devoured Israel first, Babylon broke his bones last, so Babylon gets what Assyria got. The best promise is phrased as a failed search, that Israel's guilt will be looked for and simply not be there. And Babylon, elsewhere the instrument, is now the hammer of the whole earth, broken.
JER 50:24-46	The opening image is a trap already sprung: you are caught, Babylon, and you were not aware. Then an armoury is opened, which is a startling thing to picture, and the attack orders are practical, come from the farthest border, open the storehouses, leave nothing. The middle of the chapter runs the word sword down a list, princes, wise men, boasters, soldiers, horses, chariots, mercenaries, treasures, and finishes with drought on the water, on the grounds that this is a country mad over idols. The tenants who move in afterwards are ostriches and wolves. And the last note is acoustic: the earth trembles at the noise of it, and the cry is heard among the nations.
JHN 1:1-28	No genealogy, no manger, no shepherds. John starts before Genesis does, with the Word, and only gets to a human being in verse six, and even then it is the wrong one: a witness, explicitly not the light, in case anyone was confused. The tragedy is stated flatly in two lines, that the world made through him did not recognise him and his own people did not take him in, and then the sentence the whole book turns on, that the Word became flesh. Then the interrogation, which John answers entirely by subtraction: not the Christ, not Elijah, not the prophet, just a voice. Asked why he is baptizing at all, he says the one they are looking for is already standing in the crowd. Next time on John: the Lamb of God.
JHN 1:29-51	John's line about the Lamb of God is delivered twice, and the second time two of his own disciples simply walk off after Jesus, which he seems to have intended. Jesus asks them what they are looking for, and the best they manage is asking where he lives. Then recruitment happens by word of mouth rather than by calling from boats: Andrew fetches Simon and gets him renamed on the spot, Philip is called directly, and Philip fetches Nathanael, whose opening remark about Nazareth is the most honest line in the chapter. Jesus does not argue with it. He mentions a fig tree instead, which is enough to turn Nathanael all the way round, and gets told he is impressed too easily.
JHN 2:1-25	The first sign is a catering emergency. His mother reports the problem rather than asking, he objects that it is not his hour, and she instructs the staff anyway, which settles that. Six large stone jars meant for ritual washing come back full of good wine, and the compliment goes to the bridegroom, who has no idea. Then Jerusalem, where John puts the temple clearance at the start of the story rather than the end: a whip of cords, livestock driven out, coins on the floor, tables over. Asked for his credentials, he offers a sentence about destroying and raising a temple in three days, which is a very poor answer at the time and a much better one later.
JHN 3:1-36	Nicodemus comes at night, which John mentions for a reason, and opens with a compliment. He gets an answer about being born again that he takes entirely literally, and asks whether he is expected to re-enter a womb. Jesus points out that the teacher of Israel might have managed better, and the conversation stops being a conversation: Nicodemus falls silent partway through and the speech continues without him. The wind, the serpent on the pole, the best-known verse in the book, and a definition of judgment as people preferring the dark because of what the light would show. Then John, asked to be jealous about his shrinking crowd, declines: he is the best man, not the groom, and says so.
JHN 4:1-27	The longest conversation Jesus has with anyone in this gospel is with a Samaritan woman at a well at noon, and it starts because he is thirsty and has nothing to draw with. She notices the etiquette problem immediately and says so. He talks about water that stops thirst permanently, she takes it as a plumbing improvement and asks for some, and the subject changes abruptly to her husband, then to her five husbands, which she does not deny. Her pivot to comparative theology, which mountain is the right mountain, is met with an answer that dismantles the question, and then with the thing he tells nobody else this early: I am he. The disciples come back and are astonished, but nobody asks. Next time on John: the town.
JHN 4:28-54	She abandons the water jar, which is the detail John chooses to keep, and pitches the whole town on a man who told her everything she did. While the town is walking out, the disciples are trying to get Jesus to eat, and get a speech about harvest instead. The Samaritans ask him to stay, he stays two days, and their final word to the woman is gracious and slightly rude: we believe now, but not because of you. In Galilee an official asks him to come and heal a dying boy, is told rather sharply about signs and wonders, asks again, and is sent home with one sentence. He checks the timing with the servants afterwards, which is exactly what a careful man would do.
JHN 5:1-22	A pool with five porches, a crowd of people waiting for water to move, and a man who has been ill thirty-eight years and has no one to carry him in. Jesus asks whether he wants to get well, which is not a rhetorical question in that setting. He walks, and the first thing anyone says to him is that carrying a mat on a Sabbath is illegal. He cannot even name who healed him until Jesus finds him again. What escalates the trouble is not the healing but the sentence about his Father still working, which is heard exactly as it sounds. The reply raises rather than lowers the claim: the Son copies the Father, gives life, and holds all judgment. Next time on John: the evidence.
JHN 5:23-47	The argument turns legal. He concedes the rule that self-testimony does not count, and then calls witnesses: John, described as a lamp they enjoyed while it was novel; the works themselves; and the Father. Then the charge back at them, which is sharper than anything about the Sabbath: you comb the scriptures looking for life and will not come to the one they point at, you hand each other honours, and you would welcome anyone who turned up on his own authority. The closing move is the most pointed. He declines to accuse them, on the grounds that Moses will do it, and Moses is the one they are counting on.
JHN 6:1-34	John's version of the feeding starts with a question designed as a test, and Philip fails it with arithmetic: two hundred denarii, still not enough. Andrew produces a boy with five barley loaves and two fish and does not pretend it helps. Five thousand men on the grass, as much as they want, and an instruction afterwards to collect the scraps so nothing is wasted, which is the sort of detail only John keeps. Their conclusion is to make him king by force, so he disappears up the mountain. The lake crossing at night, the figure walking on the water, and a boat that arrives. Next morning the crowd tracks him down by process of elimination, and he tells them why they came: not the sign, the lunch. Next time on John: the bread of life.
JHN 6:35-71	The bread claim gets steadily more difficult on purpose. First he is the bread of life, which is a metaphor. Then he came down from heaven, which they answer by naming his parents. Then the bread is his flesh. Then, when they ask how anyone is supposed to eat that, he does not soften it, he repeats it with blood added. This is delivered in a synagogue. The result is the only mass resignation in the gospels: many disciples decide it is unlistenable and leave. Jesus asks the twelve whether they are going too, and Peter's answer is less a declaration than an admission that there is nowhere else. And then, quietly, the note that one of them is already a problem.
JHN 7:1-26	His brothers, who John says did not believe in him, advise him on publicity: nobody hides, go up to the feast and make a splash. He declines, and then goes anyway, privately, which is not the same as changing his mind. The city is full of muttering, split between good man and fraud, all of it conducted quietly. Halfway through the festival he simply starts teaching in the temple, and the first objection is about his credentials, since he never studied. Then he goes on the offensive: none of you keeps the law, so why the death plans, and if circumcision is fine on a Sabbath, why is making a man well not. Some locals notice the authorities are saying nothing. Next time on John: the last day of the feast.
JHN 7:27-53	The argument about his origins is circular, since they know where he is from and believe the Christ's origin will be a mystery, so knowing disqualifies him. Officers are sent to arrest him and come back without him, and their explanation is the best line in the chapter: nobody ever spoke like this. The Pharisees' reply is to check whether any important person has fallen for it and to write off the crowd as ignorant. On the last day of the feast he stands and offers water to anyone thirsty, and John explains the Spirit is meant. The crowd divides over Galilee versus Bethlehem, which is a geography argument standing in for a theological one. Nicodemus asks a procedural question and is asked whether he is a Galilean too.
JHN 8:1-29	The trap is well built: a woman caught in the act, a law that prescribes stoning, and a question with no safe answer. His response is to write in the dirt, twice, and to hand the decision back with one condition attached, at which point the crowd thins from the oldest down. What he says to her is short and contains no lecture. Then the light of the world, and the Pharisees' entirely reasonable objection that a man vouching for himself proves nothing, answered by producing a second witness they cannot cross-examine. Asked where his Father is, he says they would know if they knew him. John notes nobody laid a hand on him, and gives the reason. Next time on John: Abraham comes up.
JHN 8:30-59	This starts with people who believe him and ends with them picking up rocks, which is the trajectory of the whole chapter. Freedom is the flashpoint: they hear an insult, since Abraham's children were never slaves, and he means slavery to sin. From there it escalates by paternity. They claim Abraham, he grants the bloodline and denies the resemblance. They claim God, he names the devil instead, a murderer and a liar from the start. They counter with Samaritan and demon-possessed. Then the line about never seeing death, which they take as clinching evidence, since Abraham managed to die. And then the sentence that ends the argument and the safety: before Abraham was, I AM.
JHN 9:1-41	The disciples open with the standard question, whose fault it was, and get told it is the wrong question. Then mud made with spit, a pool called Sent, and a man who comes back able to see. What follows is a courtroom farce in four rounds. The neighbours cannot agree it is him. The Pharisees split over the Sabbath. His parents, who could settle it, plead deniability because they are frightened of expulsion. And the man himself, questioned twice, gives one of the flattest testimonies anyone gives in this gospel: I do not know, I was blind, now I see. His second answer is cheekier and gets him thrown out, which is when Jesus goes looking for him.
JHN 10:1-42	The sheepfold speech goes over badly enough that he has to restart it, and the second attempt is stranger than the first, since he is now the door as well as the shepherd. The contrast is with the hired hand, whose reaction to a wolf is entirely rational and entirely useless. The line about other sheep, not of this fold, is quietly the largest claim in the chapter. Reaction splits between demon possession and the practical objection that demons do not restore eyesight. Then, at a winter festival, they ask for a straight answer, and get one: I and the Father are one. Stones come out. His defence is a piece of scriptural argument from a psalm, delivered while people are holding rocks, and then he leaves the country.
JHN 11:1-31	Word arrives that a friend is dying, and Jesus waits two days, which John reports without excusing. The disciples' objection to going back to Judea is that the last visit involved stoning, and Thomas, of all people, settles it with the gloomiest line in the gospel: let us go so we can die with him. Jesus says Lazarus is asleep, they take it as good news, and he has to say the word dead. He arrives four days late by design. Martha meets him with a sentence that is grief and reproach at once, and then, unprompted, adds that she still expects something. What she gets is a claim rather than a comfort, and she answers it head on. Next time on John: the tomb.
JHN 11:32-57	Mary says exactly what Martha said, and this time Jesus does not answer with theology. He asks where the body is, and then, in the shortest verse in the book, weeps. The bystanders are divided between how much he loved him and why he did not prevent it. At the cave, Martha makes the practical objection about four days and its consequences, out loud, in front of everyone. He prays for the benefit of the audience and says so, then shouts one name. What comes out is still tied up, and has to be unwrapped by other people. And then the coldest turn in the chapter: a committee meeting, a fear about Roman reprisals, and a high priest observing that one death is cheaper than a nation.
JHN 12:1-25	Dinner at Bethany with a man at the table who was recently dead, which John mentions without comment. Mary uses a pound of pure nard on his feet, and Judas raises the charitable objection, which John immediately undercuts by explaining that Judas ran the money box and helped himself. Meanwhile the crowds are coming to look at Lazarus, and the chief priests add him to the list, which is the only case of a miracle creating a second target. The next day, palms and Hosanna and a young donkey, and John's honest note that the disciples had no idea what they were doing at the time. Then some Greeks ask to see him, which he treats as the signal, and answers with a grain of wheat that has to be buried. Next time on John: a voice from the sky.
JHN 12:26-50	He admits his soul is troubled, considers asking to be spared, and rules it out in the same breath. The voice from the sky is heard by the crowd as weather, or possibly an angel, which is its own commentary. He says it was for their benefit. His phrase about being lifted up is explained by John as a reference to the manner of his death, and the crowd's objection is textual: the Christ stays forever, so what is this lifting. He tells them to use the daylight, and then hides, which ends his public teaching in this gospel. John accounts for the unbelief from Isaiah, and adds the detail that stings: plenty of the rulers did believe and kept quiet, because they preferred being well thought of.
JHN 13:1-38	There is no bread and cup in John. Instead the Lord and Teacher strips down, ties on a towel and washes twelve pairs of feet, including one pair belonging to a man already committed to selling him. Peter's refusal is meant as respect and is overruled, and his correction, to be washed all over, is overcorrection. The lesson afterwards is not humility in the abstract, it is an instruction to do the same thing to each other. Then the betrayal announcement, the whispered question relayed through the disciple leaning on him, and the dipped bread handed over with the words do it quickly. Judas leaves, and John ends the sentence with two words: it was night. Then the new commandment, and Peter's promise, dated.
JHN 14:1-31	The comfort speech is repeatedly derailed by the disciples, which is what makes it readable. Thomas admits they have no idea where he is going, and gets the answer the chapter is famous for. Philip asks to be shown the Father, having spent years in the room, and gets a reply that is patient and faintly incredulous. Judas, the other one, asks a good question about why the audience is so small. Around those interruptions: many rooms, greater works than his own, whatever you ask in my name, and the arrival of a Counselor whose job is described as teaching and reminding. The peace he leaves is specified as not the kind the world hands out. And the chapter ends mid-thought with an instruction to get up and leave.
JHN 15:1-27	The vine image is not sentimental: the Father runs it, unproductive branches are removed and productive ones cut back, and the point is output. Then the promotion. They are no longer servants, because a servant is not told what is going on, and they have been told everything, so they are friends, which is defined by laying down a life rather than by warmth. He also points out that the choosing went one way. Then the second half turns cold. The world's hatred is not a risk, it is a schedule, and it is inherited: they get it because he got it first. He quotes a psalm about being hated without cause, and adds that the Spirit will testify, and so will they.
JHN 16:1-33	The advance warning is specific and unpleasant: expulsion, and killers who will believe they are performing a religious duty. Then the argument that his departure is an advantage, since the Counselor cannot come otherwise, with a job description in three parts. He says there is more he cannot tell them yet, which is unusually candid. Then the riddle about a little while, which they discuss among themselves at some length without asking him, and he answers anyway with an image of labour pains forgotten. They declare, finally, that he is speaking plainly and they believe, and he replies by telling them exactly how long that will last. The last line is nonetheless cheerful: take heart, I have overcome the world.
JHN 17:1-26	The whole chapter is one prayer, out loud, with the disciples listening, which is its own kind of message. He reports the work finished before anything has visibly happened, and asks for the glory he had before the world existed. His requests are narrow and repeated: keep them, keep them together, keep them from the evil one, and specifically do not take them out of the world. The definition of eternal life offered here is not survival but knowing someone. And there is one flat parenthesis in the middle: none of them was lost, except one, named only as the son of destruction. He extends the prayer to people who do not exist yet, on the grounds that they will believe through these ones.
JHN 18:1-21	There is no agony in the garden in John, and no kiss. Jesus walks out to meet the arresting party and asks them who they are looking for, and when he answers his own question the whole detachment ends up on the ground. He asks a second time, and negotiates the release of everyone else before allowing himself to be taken. Peter's sword produces exactly one casualty, and John is the only one who names him: Malchus, right ear. Then the courtyard, where a disciple with connections gets Peter through the door, and the first person to ask him anything is the woman minding it. He says no, and goes to stand at the fire. Meanwhile the interrogation is answered with a point of procedure: ask the people who heard me. Next time on John: Pilate.
JHN 18:22-40	The reply about procedure earns him a slap, which he also queries. Then Peter's second and third denials, the last to a relative of the man he wounded, which is the detail John cannot resist, and the rooster. The delegation brings him to the Praetorium and will not go in, because entering would make them unfit to eat the Passover, and John records that without comment. What follows is the best scene in the gospel: a governor who asks a legal question, gets a philosophical answer, and ends up asking what truth is, then walking out of the room before anyone can tell him. He tries the amnesty custom as a way out. They pick a robber.
JHN 19:1-22	The flogging comes in the middle of the trial rather than at the end, as an attempt to satisfy the crowd. The soldiers do the crown and the purple and the mock salutes, and Pilate brings the result out with three words that have been quoted ever since. The charge shifts from politics to the Son of God, which makes Pilate more frightened, not less, and his question about origins gets silence. The lever that finally moves him is Caesar's friendship. From then it is procedural: the judgment seat, the presentation of their king, the answer that they have no king but Caesar. Then Golgotha, two others, and a signboard in three languages that Pilate will not change. Next time on John: the cross.
JHN 19:23-42	The soldiers split the clothes and then decline to cut up the seamless coat, gambling for it instead, which John reads as scripture and which is also just what soldiers would do. Four women stand there, three of them named Mary. From the cross he arranges his mother's housing, which is a startlingly practical thing to be doing. Then thirst, vinegar on hyssop, and two words: it is finished. The leg-breaking is a Sabbath tidiness measure that arrives too late for him, so a spear is used instead, and the writer stops the narrative to insist he saw it. Then two men who had both kept their distance, Joseph secretly and Nicodemus by night, turn up in daylight with a hundred pounds of spices.
JHN 20:1-31	The resurrection in John begins as a suspected theft. Mary's report is entirely reasonable and entirely wrong. The footrace to the tomb is recorded with the winner noted, and John keeps the domestic detail nobody would invent: the head cloth folded up on its own. Then the best recognition scene in the gospels, in which she mistakes him for the gardener and offers to carry the body away herself, and it takes one word, her name, to fix it. In the evening, locked doors, and a greeting rather than a rebuke. Thomas, who missed it, sets precise conditions and gets all of them offered back verbatim, at which point he does not use any of them. John then explains why he wrote the book at all.
JHN 21:1-25	An epilogue in which seven disciples, having seen the risen Lord twice, go fishing and catch nothing all night, which is its own comment. The man on the beach asks the question fishermen least want to hear, then tells them where to cast. Peter's response to recognition is to get dressed and then jump in the water. On shore, breakfast already cooking, and a fish count nobody can resist: a hundred and fifty-three. Then the conversation this chapter exists for. Three questions, matching three denials, three commissions, and Peter upset by the third, which is the point. He is told how he will die and told to follow anyway. Asked about someone else's future, he is told to mind his own, and John stops to correct the rumour that grew out of it.
JOB 1:1-22	Job is introduced as blameless, wealthy and slightly anxious, sacrificing on his children's behalf in case they sinned privately. Then the scene cuts to heaven, where Satan makes an audit point: piety is easy behind a hedge. God removes the hedge. What follows is the most brutal use of the word "while" in scripture, as four messengers arrive one on top of another, each the sole survivor, until the sheep, the oxen, the camels and all ten children are gone. Job shaves his head and blesses the name that took them.
JOB 2:1-13	Round two opens with the same small talk, God noting that Job has kept his integrity despite being ruined without cause, which is a remarkable thing to say out loud. Satan revises his theory to "skin for skin" and gets permission for everything short of killing him. Job ends up in the ash heap scraping his sores with a shard of pottery. His wife offers the shortest speech in the book and arguably the most practical one. Then three friends arrive, weep, and say nothing at all for a week, which is the best thing any of them do.
JOB 3:1-26	Seven days of silence end with Job cursing his own birthday in considerable detail: let the date be struck from the calendar, let no star rise on it, let the professional day-cursers come and handle it. His argument for the grave is unexpectedly democratic, since kings, prisoners, slaves and stillborn infants are all equally at rest there and nobody is anyone's taskmaster. Twice he asks why light is given to people who want the dark. The last line is the quiet one: the thing he was always afraid of has arrived.
JOB 4:1-21	Eliphaz begins with the classic opening of a man about to be unhelpful, which is asking whether you would mind if he spoke. He compliments Job on how well he used to comfort other people. Then the theory: nobody innocent ever perished, therefore. He backs it with lions who starve anyway and, more impressively, with a ghost story, a spirit sliding past his face at night while his hair stood up, delivering the news that no mortal is purer than his Maker. It is a lot of stagecraft for a point Job never disputed.
JOB 5:1-27	Eliphaz keeps going and warms to the discipline theory: God wounds and then bandages the wound he made, so being corrected is really a compliment. He throws in a numerical flourish, six troubles and then seven, and a rural retirement package including peace with wild animals, an intact fold and a grave arrived at like grain in its season. It is decent poetry and appalling timing, delivered to a man whose children are already buried. He signs off by assuring Job that they have looked into it and it checks out.
JOB 6:1-30	Job asks for his misery to be weighed on actual scales, since it would outweigh the sea's sand and that would excuse his tone. He defends complaining with livestock: nothing brays over good grass. His one request is that God finish him off. The friends get the best image in the chapter, a desert stream swollen with ice in the cold months and gone by summer, so that caravans navigate towards it, arrive, and stand there confounded. Then a fair challenge: name the sin. Teach me and I will be quiet.
JOB 7:1-21	Job's model for human life is shift work, a hired man watching the sun for the shadow that means the day is over. His nights are the assignment, his skin is breaking and closing, and his days go faster than a shuttle. He asks whether he is the sea or some monster that requires a guard, and notes that God's remedy for insomnia is worse dreams. Then he takes the reverent question about God noticing humanity and inverts it into a plea for privacy: look away, just long enough for me to swallow.
JOB 8:1-22	Bildad has no interest in Eliphaz's bedside manner. He opens by calling Job's speech wind, then makes the argument out loud that Eliphaz kept implying: your children sinned and God handled it. Having said that, he recommends prayer. His supporting authorities are the ancestors, on the grounds that we ourselves know nothing, and his supporting images are botanical and arachnid: reeds die without mud, and the godless man's confidence is a spider's web he intends to lean on. Job's future, he promises, holds laughter.
JOB 9:1-35	Job concedes the theology and then finds the flaw in it. Yes, God is unanswerable, one reply in a thousand, mountains moved before they notice, the sun told not to rise, the Bear and Orion and the Pleiades built to order. That is precisely the problem: the opposing party is also the bailiff, so if it comes to strength he wins and if it comes to justice nobody can summon him. Job's own mouth would testify against Job. Then the line the friends never recover from: he destroys the blameless and the wicked alike. What he wants is an umpire, someone who can put a hand on both of them, and there isn't one.
JOB 10:1-22	Having decided complaining is now his job, Job does it properly. He asks whether God has eyes of flesh and a mortal lifespan, since only someone short of time would need to hurry the investigation. His best argument is manufacturing: you poured me out like milk and curdled me like cheese, knit the bones in, kept me alive out of kindness, and are now dismantling it. The verdict is fixed either way, since if he is wicked there is woe and if he is righteous he still cannot lift his head. His closing request is modest and terrible: a little comfort, briefly, before the land where the light is as midnight.
JOB 11:1-20	Zophar's contribution is mostly annoyance at the word count. He would like God to answer Job personally, which is unwise of him, and offers the reassurance that God is charging Job rather less than he owes. There follows a genuinely fine passage on the unsearchable, higher than heaven and deeper than Sheol, immediately spent on the claim that he personally knows what it contains. He also lands the strangest insult in the book, about an empty-headed man becoming wise on the day a man is born as a donkey's colt. The offer is standard: clean hands, forgotten misery, brighter than noon, sleeping unafraid.
JOB 12:1-25	Job's patience gives out and the sarcasm arrives: no doubt wisdom will die with you. He points out that the man who prayed and was answered is now the neighbourhood joke, while the tents of robbers do fine. Ask the animals, ask the birds, ask the fish, they all know who did this. Then he takes their favourite genre, the hymn to God's power, and turns it against the respectable: counsellors stripped, judges made fools, priests led away, trusted men silenced, princes drenched in contempt, elders relieved of their understanding, chiefs left groping in the dark and staggering like drunks. Sing that one, he says.
JOB 13:1-28	Job informs the committee that he has read the material, thanks, and would prefer to argue with the Almighty directly. His review of their performance: forgers of lies, physicians of no value, and if they were completely silent they would be wise. He asks whether they have considered that lying on God's behalf is still lying, and that God audits character witnesses too. Their finest lines are proverbs of ashes. He will speak even if it kills him, and asks for exactly two concessions, no rod and no terror, so the questioning can go both ways. Also, a list of the charges would help, since he is a driven leaf and a moth-eaten coat.
JOB 14:1-22	The saddest chapter in the argument so far, and it is mostly botany. A tree that gets cut down smells water and starts over. A man lies down and that is the end of the transaction, no sprouting, until the heavens are no more, which is not a date anyone can plan around. Job's proposal is oddly tender: hide me in Sheol, wait out your own anger, then set a reminder and call me, and I will answer. Meanwhile his sins are itemised and sealed in a bag, which is worse than being forgotten. Water wears down stone eventually, and hope the same way.
JOB 15:1-35	Eliphaz comes back with the gloves off, which mainly means he has stopped complimenting Job first. Job is full of east wind, his own mouth convicts him, and does he think he was born before the hills or sat in on God's private meetings? Their delegation, he notes, includes men older than Job's father, a rhetorical move with no content whatsoever. His doctrine has hardened: God does not trust his own holy ones and the heavens themselves fail inspection. Then the set piece, a wicked man fat about the thighs and stiff of neck, hearing terrors, begging bread, shedding unripe grapes, living in houses that are already rubble. None of it is about Job, and all of it is about Job.
JOB 16:1-22	Job's review: miserable comforters, all of you. He observes that he could produce their speeches without effort if the chairs were swapped, complete with the head-shaking, but claims he would have used his mouth for something useful. Then the imagery turns violent and stays there, God as an animal gnashing its teeth, as a wrestler taking him by the neck, as an archery range with Job in the middle, as a giant running at him. He has sewn the sackcloth directly onto his skin, which is a detail nobody needed. His hands are clean, his prayer is pure, and he asks the earth not to cover his blood, since he still has a witness on file in heaven.
JOB 17:1-16	The grave is ready and the mockers are still in the room. Job asks for a guarantor, someone willing to strike hands and post collateral for him, and looks around a circle of three men who will not. He has become a public byword and a target for spit. His concession to orthodoxy is brief and bitter: fine, the righteous will hold their way and clean hands will strengthen, and none of you three qualifies as wise. He is particularly tired of being told the light is near while standing in the dark. If corruption is his father and the worm is his mother and sister, hope is a family member nobody has met.
JOB 18:1-21	Bildad is offended at being lumped in with the livestock, and answers with the most thoroughly booby-trapped speech in the book. The wicked man's lamp goes out, then a net takes his feet, a snare his heel, a noose waits in the ground, a trap sits on the path, and terrors chase him from all sides. The firstborn of death eats his limbs. He is escorted from his own tent to the king of terrors and sulphur is scattered over the property. Roots dry, branch cut, name gone from the street, no son and no grandson. It is a portrait, unsigned, of the man sitting in front of him who has just buried ten children.
JOB 19:1-29	Job has been counting: ten reproaches, and nobody is embarrassed yet. God, in his account, has become a besieging army, walling the road, confiscating the crown, pulling up hope by the roots and camping troops around the tent. Then the part that hurts more than the sores, a full inventory of people who have left the room: brothers, relatives, household staff, small children who insult him in the street, and a wife who cannot stand his breath. He is down to the skin of his teeth. He asks his friends for pity, notes they are not satisfied with his flesh, and then wants the whole thing carved in rock with an iron pen, because he knows his Redeemer lives and intends to see God with his own eyes, skin or no skin.
JOB 20:1-29	Zophar returns, announcing that he is answering in haste, which is at least honest. The thesis has not moved: the wicked man's good day is short. What is new is the gastroenterology. Sin tastes sweet, he tucks it under his tongue and saves it, and then it curdles in his gut into cobra venom, so the wealth he swallowed comes straight back up because God empties the stomach personally. He will never see the rivers of honey and butter. Wrath rains on him mid-meal, a bronze arrow exits through the liver, an unfanned fire finishes the tent, and both heaven and earth file statements. The word dung appears. Comfort was not the goal.
JOB 21:1-34	Job requests the floor and grants permission to mock afterwards, then aims at the load-bearing wall of their whole system. The wicked, empirically, do fine. Kids established, houses secure, bulls that never fail, cows that never miscarry, small children sent out like a flock, tambourine, harp, pipe, a comfortable life and then a quick painless drop into Sheol, all while telling God to keep his distance because prayer has no return on investment. So how often, exactly, does that lamp get put out? He also dismantles the fallback about the children paying: bill the man himself, since once his months are up he could not care less. Both men end under the same worms.
JOB 22:1-30	Eliphaz opens with a shrug of cosmic scale, since God gains nothing from anyone's virtue, and then, having run out of insinuation, produces actual charges. Seized pledges, stripped clothing off the naked, water withheld from the weary, bread from the hungry, widows sent away with nothing, the arms of orphans broken. None of it happened, but it makes the theory work, which is the point. He then accuses Job of believing God cannot see through cloud cover, warns him off the old road, and closes with terms that are almost generous: make peace, take instruction, dump your gold in the streambed, and the Almighty becomes the treasure instead, prayers answered, decrees established, light on the path.
JOB 23:1-17	Job's problem is no longer theological, it is logistical. He would very much like a hearing, case in order, mouth full of arguments, and he is confident God would not simply out-muscle him but would actually listen. The difficulty is finding the courtroom. East, west, north, south, nobody there. He falls back on what he does know: God knows his route, and tested he will come out like gold, since he has kept the way and hoarded God's words the way other men hoard bread. What terrifies him is not injustice but sovereignty, a God who answers to no one and does exactly as he pleases, and who has already written Job's portion.
JOB 24:1-25	Job's complaint becomes documentary. Boundary stones moved, flocks driven off, the orphan's donkey taken, the widow's ox held as security, and the poor sent out to forage like wild donkeys, gleaning the vineyards of the men who robbed them. They tread the presses and are thirsty; they carry the sheaves and are hungry. The night shift gets its own section: a murderer at first light, an adulterer waiting for dusk with his face covered, burglars tunnelling through walls, all of them people for whom sunrise feels like pitch dark. And the verdict on the powerful is the sharpest line in it: God keeps them secure. Then, in a little while, they die like everyone else, cut like the tops of the grain.
JOB 25:1-6	Bildad's third speech runs six verses and then he leaves the stage forever. Dominion, fear, uncountable armies, peace in the high places, and therefore nobody born of a woman can be clean. His flourish is astronomical: the moon is not bright and the stars are not pure, so what does that make you. He answers his own question with the word worm, twice, in case once was ambiguous. Notably he does not connect any of this to Job's actual case, which may be because there is nothing left to say, and this is the last anyone hears from him.
JOB 26:1-14	Job thanks them warmly for all the help rendered to the powerless and the abundant wisdom supplied to a man who had none, then asks whose spirit was doing the talking. Having established that Bildad can do a hymn, he does a better one. The dead tremble, Sheol lies open, God stretches the north over nothing and hangs the earth on nothing at all, packs water into clouds that somehow hold, rules a line between light and dark, shakes the pillars of heaven with a word and runs Rahab through. Then the point: that is the outer edge of it, a whisper at the fence. Nobody here has heard the thunder.
JOB 27:1-23	Job swears an oath by the God who has denied him justice, which is a fair summary of the whole book, and refuses on the record to agree with his friends or let go of his integrity. Then he does something cheeky: he delivers their sermon himself. Here is the portion of the wicked. Children multiplied for the sword, widows who do not bother mourning, silver heaped up like dust and then worn by better men, a house built to the standards of a moth or a night-watchman's lean-to. He goes to bed rich, opens his eyes, and is not. The east wind takes him and the neighbours clap and hiss him off the property.
JOB 28:1-28	An interlude, and the finest poem in the book so far. Humans are astonishing at extraction: shafts sunk where nobody lives, men dangling and swinging on ropes, mountains turned over by the roots, underground streams dammed, hidden things dragged into daylight down paths no falcon has seen and no lion has walked. Then the pivot. Where is wisdom mined? The deep says not here. The sea says not with me. It is not for sale in gold of Ophir, onyx, sapphire, coral, crystal, rubies or Ethiopian topaz. Destruction and Death have heard a rumour, which is the best lead available. God knows the address, having set the weight of the wind, and his advice to humans is disarmingly small: fear the Lord and stay away from evil.
JOB 29:1-25	Job spends a chapter in the past tense, and it is the most human thing in the book. God's lamp on his head, children around the tent, steps washed with butter and oil coming out of rock, which is an extravagant way of saying business was good. At the city gate the young men hid, the old men stood, the princes shut up and the nobles' tongues stuck to their palates. He is careful to say why: he pulled the poor out of trouble, made widows sing, wore justice like a robe and a diadem, was eyes to the blind and feet to the lame, and broke open the jaws of predators to get the prey back. He expected to die at home, counting his days like sand.
JOB 30:1-31	The sequel, in the present tense, and the fall is measured socially rather than financially. He is now the entertainment for men whose fathers he would not have put with the sheep dogs, outcasts living in rock holes, chewing broom roots and salt herbs, braying in the bushes. He has become their song. They spit in his face and do not hesitate. The rabble comes through the breach in him as through a hole in a wall. At night his bones are pierced and the pain does not clock off; God has dropped him in the mud and he is dust and ashes now. He cries out and God simply looks at him. Brother to jackals, companion to ostriches, skin black and peeling, harp retuned to funeral music.
JOB 31:1-40	The closing argument, structured as a series of self-curses, which is a bold form. If I looked at a young woman, if I walked with falsehood, if I waited at a neighbour's door, then let another man eat my crops and let my wife grind for someone else. His best line is about his servants: the same God made him in the womb, so what exactly would he say when God stands up? He denies withholding bread and fleece, denies trusting gold, denies even blowing a secret kiss to the sun or moon, denies gloating when an enemy fell, denies turning a traveller into the street, denies hiding sin like Adam. Then he signs his name at the bottom and demands the indictment, which he says he would wear as a crown. The words of Job are ended.
JOB 32:1-22	The three run out of speeches, and the narrator is blunt about why: Job is righteous in his own eyes. Enter Elihu son of Barachel the Buzite, who has apparently been standing there the whole time, furious at everybody, at Job for defending himself instead of God and at the friends for condemning him without ever answering him. He kept quiet out of respect for their age, on the theory that years teach wisdom, a theory he now abandons, since the breath of the Almighty and not the birthday is what gives understanding. He is very full of words. He is, by his own account, new wineskins about to burst. He also promises to use no flattering titles, twice, which is the most flattering thing said about him so far.
JOB 33:1-33	Elihu opens with reassurance nobody asked for, promising he is only clay and will not be terrifying. He then reads Job's position back to him, clean, innocent, feet in the stocks, and answers it: God is bigger and does not file reports. But he does speak, in dreams and night visions, opening the ears of sleeping men to steer them off a course that would end in the pit. And the pain in the bones is part of the curriculum, right up to the point where the bones show through the skin. Then the good part, an interpreting angel, one in a thousand, and God saying he has found a ransom, and the man's flesh coming back fresher than a child's while he sings his confession in public. God will run this twice, or three times. Now be quiet, Job.
JOB 34:1-37	Elihu opens by addressing the wise men, meaning himself and, generously, whoever agrees. He quotes Job accurately, which is new, and then improves the quotation until Job sounds like he keeps company with the wicked. The core argument is procedural: nobody appointed God over the earth, so there is no authority he could be abusing, and if he simply took his breath back all flesh would return to dust. God shows no favour to princes over the poor, watches every step, and overturns the mighty at midnight with no hand visible. His sharpest question is whether Job has ever tried the sentence "I was guilty, teach me." His verdict is that Job talks without knowledge, claps his hands and multiplies words. He also wishes Job were tried to the end, which is a thing to say to that man.
JOB 35:1-16	Elihu tackles the profit question, and his answer is oddly modern. Look up: the sky is higher than you. Your sin does nothing to God and your righteousness gives him nothing; both land on other people, who are your actual neighbours. Then a genuinely fine line about the God who gives songs in the night and teaches humans more than the birds, followed immediately by the observation that when the oppressed cry out, nobody thinks to ask where he is. God, he says, does not answer an empty cry, and certainly not from a man who complains he cannot see him while his case sits filed and waiting. The conclusion is the one Elihu always reaches: Job is talking too much.
JOB 36:1-33	Elihu asks for a little more of everyone's time, on the grounds that he still has material and that a man perfect in knowledge is in the room, referring to himself. The theology is the friends' theology with better manners: God despises nobody, gives the afflicted their due, and when he puts people in fetters it is a teaching device, ears opened, transgressions itemised, prosperity if they listen and the sword if they do not. His best line, and it is a good one, is that God delivers the afflicted by means of their affliction. Then he warns Job off bribes and off wishing for the night, and drifts into weather, water drawn up and returned as rain, clouds nobody can read, lightning held in God's hands and told where to hit. The storm is arriving while he talks.
JOB 37:1-24	Elihu's last speech is essentially a live weather broadcast, and his own heart is trembling. Thunder goes out under the whole sky, lightning to the ends of the earth, and nobody is asked to comprehend it. Snow is told to fall, rain to pour, which shuts down every man's hands and puts the animals in their dens; ice arrives on God's breath and the wide waters set hard. Clouds get loaded and steered, for correction or for kindness, depending. Then the questions Job is about to hear much more of: do you know how the lightning is made to shine, can you spread out a sky as strong as a cast metal mirror. Golden splendour out of the north, majesty unreachable, and God takes no interest in the self-declared wise. Then, mid-storm, someone else starts speaking.
JOB 38:1-41	The whirlwind arrives and God's opening move is to ask who has been darkening counsel with words without knowledge, then to tell Job to brace himself, because the questions are going the other way now. Where were you when the foundations went in and the morning stars were singing? Who put doors on the sea and told the waves how far? It runs a whole chapter, and it is the best poetry in the book: the springs of the deep, the gates of death, the address of light, the warehouses where snow and hail are kept for the day of battle, rain falling on wilderness just to make grass. Does the rain have a father? Can you bind the Pleiades, or feed a lion, or handle the ravens when the chicks are crying?
JOB 39:1-30	The interrogation moves to zoology and the tone becomes almost affectionate. Does Job supervise the birthing calendar of mountain goats? Who signed the wild donkey's release papers, the one who scorns the noise of the city and cannot hear the driver at all? Would a wild ox stand in a furrow for him, or bring the grain in? The ostrich gets a strange and honest paragraph, proud wings, eggs left in the dust, no fear because wisdom was simply not issued to her, and yet she leaves horse and rider behind. The horse is the star turn: mane quivering, snort glorious, leaping like a locust, laughing at fear, saying Aha to the trumpet and smelling the battle from miles off. Then hawk and eagle on the cliff point, eyes on distant prey. Nothing in the list is about Job.
JOB 40:1-24	God pauses long enough for an answer, and Job gives the shortest speech in the book: I am small, hand over mouth, I have said enough. This turns out not to be an exit. Round two opens with the same brace yourself, and the question underneath the whole book gets asked out loud: will you condemn me so that you can be innocent? Then a genuine offer. If Job has the arm and the thundering voice, let him dress in majesty, pour out his fury, find every proud man and put him in the dust, and God will concede that Job's own right hand saves him. And then, instead of an argument, an animal: behemoth, made the same day as Job, eating grass like an ox, bones like brass pipes, unbothered while the river climbs to his mouth.
JOB 41:1-34	A whole chapter on Leviathan, and God is clearly enjoying it. Can you land him on a hook, put a rope through his nose, keep him as a pet for your daughters, sell him by the piece at market? Try it once and you will remember the fight and decline the rematch. The description: sealed armour with no air between the plates, sneezes that flash light, eyes like the eyelids of morning, torches out of the mouth, nostril smoke like a boiling pot, a heart as hard as the lower millstone. Iron is straw, brass is rotten wood, arrows are chaff, and he laughs at a javelin. He boils the sea and leaves a white wake behind him. King over all the sons of pride, which is aimed at more than a crocodile.
JOB 42:1-17	Job's answer is six verses and concedes everything except the thing he was accused of: he spoke about matters too wonderful for him, and where he had heard of God secondhand he has now seen him, so he repents in dust and ashes. Then the twist nobody in the argument expected. God is angry at Eliphaz and the other two, because they did not speak rightly about him, as Job did. Their penance is seven bulls, seven rams, and asking the man they lectured for three cycles to pray for them. He does. Everything is restored double, the relatives who vanished reappear for dinner with a coin and a gold ring each, and there are ten more children, three of them named and given an inheritance alongside their brothers. Job dies at a hundred and forty, full of days.
LUK 1:1-35	Luke opens like a historian, with a dedication, a claim to have checked his sources, and a stated purpose. Then a childless elderly couple, both blameless, and a priest whose lot that week is the incense. Gabriel turns up beside the altar with a son, a name, a diet and a job description drawn from Elijah. Zacharias asks for evidence, given his age, and is given nine months of silence instead, which the waiting crowd outside reads correctly. Elizabeth conceives and hides for five months. Six months on, the same angel is sent much further down the social scale, to a girl in Nazareth, with a much larger promise: a throne and a kingdom without end. Mary asks the practical question. Next time on Luke: he answers it.
LUK 1:36-80	The angel's evidence is Elizabeth, six months pregnant and previously called barren, and Mary's answer is consent rather than argument. She goes to the hill country, where a baby kicks on cue and Elizabeth says the quiet part out loud. Then the Magnificat, which is not a lullaby: thrones emptied, the proud scattered, the hungry fed and the rich sent away with nothing. Three months later, John is born, and the naming turns into a family dispute settled by a writing tablet, at which point his father's voice comes back and he uses it on a prophecy. The neighbours are frightened and start talking. Luke leaves the boy in the desert until further notice.
LUK 2:1-25	An imperial paperwork exercise moves the story to Bethlehem, and Luke dates it by a governor, because that is the kind of writer he is. The birth gets one sentence and a feeding trough. The announcement, however, goes to men working nights in a field, who are frankly terrified, and the sign offered is deliberately unimpressive: a baby in a trough. Then the sky fills with an army singing. They go, they look, they tell everyone, and Luke notes that Mary says nothing and stores it. Eight days later the child is circumcised and named, and the family arrives at the temple with the cheaper offering of two birds, where an old man has been waiting. Next time on Luke: Simeon speaks.
LUK 2:26-52	Simeon had a promise that he would not die first, and now holds it in his arms, and asks to be dismissed. His song is about a light for the nations, which is a wider audience than anyone at the temple that morning. Then he turns to Mary and adds the part nobody wants: falling as well as rising, and a sword through her. Anna, a widow of eighty-four years who has not left the building, joins in. Twelve years pass in one verse, and then the family loses the boy for three days and finds him interrogating the temple teachers. His explanation, that he had to be in his Father's house, does not land, and he goes home anyway.
LUK 3:1-38	Luke fixes the date with six officials by name, then hands the wilderness prophet a microphone. Isaiah supplies the landscaping metaphor, and John supplies the insult. What is unusual here is the follow-up question: the crowds ask what to do, and get concrete answers, one coat to whoever has none, tax collectors to stop padding the bill, soldiers to stop shaking people down and live on their pay. Asked directly whether he is the Christ, he declines the promotion. Herod, criticised about his brother's wife, closes the matter by locking him up, which Luke reports before the baptism it precedes. Jesus is baptized while praying, and the sky opens. Then the genealogy, which runs the other way from Matthew's and does not stop at Abraham: it keeps going to Adam, and past him.
LUK 4:1-23	Forty days, no food, and three offers, in Luke's order: bread from a stone, then the kingdoms of the world with the devil's own claim to own them, then the temple parapet with the angel verse quoted at him. He answers with scripture each time, and the devil leaves, Luke notes, only until another time. Back in Galilee, he is popular. Then Nazareth, where he reads Isaiah on the poor, the captives, the blind and the crushed, hands the scroll back, sits down, and says the sentence out loud: today, in your hearing. The room is charmed until it does the arithmetic on whose son he is, and he tells them what they are about to say before they say it. Next time on Luke: they say it.
LUK 4:24-44	The line that empties the room is not about himself, it is about two foreigners: Elijah's widow in Sidon and Naaman the Syrian, both helped while Israel went without. The congregation carries him to the edge of a cliff, and he simply walks out through them. At Capernaum the reaction is the same word every time, authority. A demon identifies him publicly, is told to be quiet, and leaves without injuring the man. Simon's mother-in-law has her fever rebuked as though it were a spirit. At sunset the sick arrive in bulk and he touches every one of them, refusing to let demons do his advertising. By morning he has gone somewhere empty, and tells the crowd that hunts him down that other towns are owed the same.
LUK 5:1-39	The crowd on the shore forces him into a boat, and while he is there he borrows the boat's owner for the day. Simon has fished all night for nothing, and points that out before complying, then nearly sinks two boats and asks Jesus to go away. He is instead recruited, and three fishermen abandon a record catch on the beach. A leper is touched and cleansed, and Jesus goes off alone to pray, which Luke keeps mentioning. With the law's experts seated and watching, a man arrives through the roof, and forgiveness comes before mobility, which starts the argument the healing then settles. Levi is hired out of the tax office and throws a party. Asked why nobody fasts, he offers a wedding, a ruined garment, burst skins, and a note that people prefer the old wine.
LUK 6:1-26	The grain field complaint gets David and the show bread, plus a title claim. The withered hand, which Luke specifies is the right one, gets a staged confrontation: the man is called into the middle of the room first, then the question about whether the Sabbath is for saving or killing, then the healing, then rage. Before choosing the twelve, Luke has him pray on a mountain all night, which is the detail the other accounts skip. Then, not a mountain but a level place, a crowd from as far as Tyre, and a sermon that comes in matched pairs. Poor, hungry, weeping, hated, all blessed. Rich, full, laughing, well spoken of, all warned, in the same rhythm. Nobody gets to hear only half of it. Next time on Luke: enemies.
LUK 6:27-49	The instructions get harder rather than more reasonable. Love enemies, bless people who curse you, offer the second cheek, hand over the coat as well as the cloak, and do not chase what was taken. Lend expecting nothing, because loving people who love you is a standard sinners already meet. Do not judge, do not condemn, and expect your own measure back, pressed down and overflowing. Then the images: a blind man guiding a blind man into a ditch, a student who ends up resembling his teacher, and the celebrated business of the beam and the speck, which he calls hypocrisy outright. Fruit reveals the tree, and the mouth reveals the heart. Calling him Lord while ignoring him is a house someone built on dirt, and Luke lets the flood arrive.
LUK 7:1-24	The centurion never appears in person. He sends the local elders first, who vouch for him on the grounds that he paid for the synagogue, then sends friends to head Jesus off with the line about not being worthy and the argument from chain of command. The verdict is that nothing in Israel matches it. Then Nain, which is Luke's alone: a funeral coming out of the gate, a widow burying her only son, and no request from anyone. He tells her not to cry, touches the bier, and gives the man back. The crowd's conclusion, that God has visited his people, spreads. John, in prison, sends two men with a question, and gets an hour of healings and a list rather than a yes. Next time on Luke: he talks about John.
LUK 7:25-50	The question is what they went out to look at, since silk belongs in palaces: a prophet, and more, and the greatest man born of women, who is nonetheless outranked by the least in the kingdom. Luke adds the split verdict: the people and tax collectors, already baptized, agree, while the Pharisees and lawyers, who declined, do not. The generation is children who refuse every game: John fasted, so demon; he eats, so drunk. Then dinner at a Pharisee's house, interrupted by a woman with an alabaster jar who weeps on his feet and dries them with her hair. Simon objects privately, and is answered with two debtors and a quiet audit of his own hospitality: no water, no kiss, no oil.
LUK 8:1-27	Luke names the funders, which nobody else does: Mary Magdalene, Joanna, wife of Herod's steward, Susanna and others, paying for the whole operation out of their own property. Then the seed, in four conditions, and an explanation that names the cares, riches and pleasures doing the choking. A lamp is not lit for storage under furniture, and nothing hidden stays that way, so mind how you listen. His family arrive and cannot get through the crowd, and he redefines the word rather than going out. On the lake he sleeps through a storm until woken by men who think they are drowning, tells the weather to stop, and asks about faith, which nobody answers. Then the far shore, and a man with no clothes living in the tombs. Next time on Luke: his name.
LUK 8:28-56	He asks the man's name and gets an answer that is not a name. The demons negotiate: not the abyss, the pigs, and the pigs go into the lake. The town finds the man dressed and lucid, and is frightened enough to ask Jesus to go. The man asks to come along, is refused, and given a job at home instead. Then two interruptions braided together. Jairus, who runs a synagogue, is on his knees about a twelve-year-old, and on the way a woman with twelve years of bleeding and no money left touches the cloak and is cured. Jesus stops the whole procession to find out who, and Peter points out the obvious about crowds. Then the news that the girl is dead, the mourners put out, a child told to get up, and then given something to eat.
LUK 9:1-31	The twelve go out with no staff, bag, bread, money or spare coat, which leaves them entirely dependent on hospitality. Herod hears, and Luke lets him say the awkward part himself: John I beheaded, so who is this. The apostles come back with reports, the retreat to Bethsaida lasts as long as it takes the crowd to notice, and the disciples suggest catering elsewhere. Five loaves, two fish, seating in fifties, twelve baskets afterwards. Then the question, asked while he is praying, and Peter's correct answer, immediately embargoed, followed by the first plain statement of what Jerusalem means. Eight days later, up a mountain to pray, his face changes, and Moses and Elijah discuss his departure with him. Next time on Luke: the cloud arrives.
LUK 9:32-62	The three nearly sleep through it, wake into the glory, and Peter suggests building, which Luke bluntly annotates as not knowing what he said. The cloud, the voice, the silence afterwards. Next morning a father with an only son the disciples could not help, and one rebuke settles it. While the crowd is admiring the majesty of God, Jesus tells the disciples he will be handed over, and Luke says the sentence was hidden from them and they were too frightened to ask. Then they argue about rank, and get a child. Then James and John offer to burn down a Samaritan village, Elijah-style, and are told what kind of spirit that is. Three volunteers get three flat answers, ending with the man who wants to say goodbye at home and is told about plows.
LUK 10:1-21	Seventy more get sent out in pairs, and the brief is harder than the twelve's: no purse, no bag, no sandals, and no greeting anyone on the road, which rules out small talk. Say peace at the door, stay in the first house that takes you, eat whatever appears, heal, and announce the kingdom. Refused, you wipe the dust off and announce it anyway, which is not a threat so much as a delivery receipt. Chorazin, Bethsaida and Capernaum get their notice. The seventy come back thrilled about the demons, and are told, in effect, that this is the least interesting thing about them: Satan fell like lightning, scorpions are no problem, and the list their names are on matters more. Next time on Luke: a lawyer with a question.
LUK 10:22-42	A lawyer arrives to test him and ends up answering his own exam question, correctly, from the law.
