His follow-up, asking who exactly counts as a neighbour, is what Luke calls self-justification, and it gets the story everyone knows: a man beaten and left on the Jericho road, a priest and a Levite who cross to the far side, and a Samaritan, of all people, who does the whole job, including the bill and an open account with the innkeeper. Asked which of the three was the neighbour, the lawyer cannot bring himself to say Samaritan and says the one who showed mercy. Then Martha, hosting, doing all the work, and entirely right about the division of labour, and told anyway that her sister picked the better thing.
LUK 11:1-28	Asked for a prayer, he gives a short one, and then a story that is not flattering about how prayer works: a man wakes his neighbour at midnight and gets the bread not out of affection but because he will not stop knocking. Keep asking. No father hands over a stone, a snake or a scorpion instead of food. After a mute demon comes out, the crowd splits between Beelzebul and demanding a sign, and he points out that Satan running a purge of Satan is a poor business model. Luke's phrase is the finger of God. Then the strong man who thought his house was secure, and the spirit that comes back with seven friends to a place swept clean. A woman praises his mother; he redirects it. Next time on Luke: Jonah.
LUK 11:29-54	The demand for a sign gets Jonah, plus two witnesses for the prosecution: a foreign queen who travelled to hear Solomon, and a city that repented for a reluctant prophet. Then the lamp, and the warning that what you are calling light may not be. The dinner invitation that follows goes badly. He is criticised for skipping the washing and replies with cups polished on the outside, then works through the woes: herbs tithed to the leaf while justice is skipped, front-row seats, greetings in the market, and unmarked graves people walk over without knowing. A lawyer points out that this catches him too, and receives a set of his own, ending with the key of knowledge, taken away and used on nobody. From here they are watching for anything usable.
LUK 12:1-30	The crowd is large enough to be trampling itself, and the first word to the disciples is a warning about hypocrisy, on the grounds that nothing stays private: what is said in a back room ends up on the roof. Do not fear people who can only kill you. Sparrows go five for two coins and are still counted. Then the interruption Luke keeps: a man in the crowd wants help getting his share of an inheritance, and is refused the job of arbitrator and handed a warning about greed instead. The rich man's monologue is all first person, barns, grain, goods, soul, and God's reply is one question about who gets it now. Ravens keep no warehouse. Lilies out-dress Solomon. Next time on Luke: the kingdom, and lamps kept lit.
LUK 12:31-59	Sell things, give them away, and note that the heart follows the money rather than leading it. Then the waiting: belts fastened, lamps lit, a master back from a wedding who ends up serving the staff who stayed awake, and a burglar who keeps no appointment. Peter asks whether this applies to them or to everyone, which is answered sideways with the steward who assumed the boss was late, and the sliding scale that follows: knowing more means answering for more. Then the hardest lines in the chapter, spoken about himself, fire, a baptism he dreads, and division rather than peace, itemised down to in-laws. To the crowd: you can forecast rain off a west wind, so read the times, and settle with your adversary before a judge does it for you.
LUK 13:1-35	Two disasters are put to him, a massacre and a collapsed tower, and both times he refuses the arithmetic that makes the dead worse sinners, then makes it personal anyway. The fig tree gets a stay of execution and a year of manure. On a Sabbath a woman folded over for eighteen years is called out of the congregation and straightened, and the synagogue ruler complains about the day rather than the woman, so Jesus asks who among them leaves the ox thirsty on a Saturday. Then the mustard seed and the yeast, both small and both irreversible. Asked for numbers, he talks about a door that shuts. Warned Herod wants him dead, he calls him a fox and keeps his own timetable, and then grieves over Jerusalem instead of cursing it.
LUK 14:1-35	Dinner at a Pharisee's, and Luke says outright they were watching him, so he asks the question first, gets silence, heals the man anyway, and asks who leaves a son down a well until Sunday. Then he audits the seating plan: take the worst chair and let the host promote you, because being demoted in front of the room is worse than waiting. To the host: stop inviting people who can pay you back. When a guest offers a pious line about the kingdom's banquet, he gets the banquet nobody attends, three excuses involving property, livestock and a wedding, and a guest list filled from the streets. Then the terms, which Luke does not soften: family, your own life, a cross, and the arithmetic of a builder and a king who both sit down and count first.
LUK 15:1-32	The complaint that starts the chapter is about who he eats with, and the answer is three stories about looking for things. A shepherd abandons ninety-nine to find one and throws a party over it. A woman turns the house upside down for a single coin and does the same. Then two sons: one liquidates his inheritance, ends up envying pigs, and drafts a speech he never gets to finish, because his father is already running. Robe, ring, shoes, calf. The elder brother, who has done everything right, stands outside doing sums about a goat, and Luke leaves it there, with the father outside pleading and the argument unfinished. Nobody is told how it ends.
LUK 16:1-31	The strangest parable in Luke: a manager about to be sacked for waste starts forging discounts into his master's ledgers so the debtors will owe him favours, and gets praised for it. The point is not the fraud but the foresight, and the follow-up is that people who are careless with money are unlikely to be trusted with anything better. The Pharisees, whom Luke calls lovers of money, laugh. Then the second half: a man in purple, a beggar at his gate whose sores the dogs attend to, and a reversal in which the rich man, still giving orders, asks for Lazarus to be sent with water and then as a courier to his brothers. Abraham's answer is that they already have Moses, and a man back from the dead would not change that.
LUK 17:1-37	The forgiveness quota is seven times a day, which is designed to be exhausting. The apostles respond by asking for more faith, and are told a mustard seed's worth would relocate a tree, then reminded that a servant who does his job does not expect applause. Then the ten lepers, all cleansed on the way to the priests, and the only one who comes back is the Samaritan, which Luke saves for the last word of the sentence. Asked for a date on the kingdom, he says it does not arrive with a schedule and is already among them. Then Noah and Lot, both cited not for wickedness but for how normal the day looked: eating, buying, building, right up to the moment. Do not go back for your things. Remember Lot's wife.
LUK 18:1-43	The lesson on persistence rests on a judge who admits, in his own words, that he fears neither God nor man and just wants the widow to stop coming. The lesson on humility rests on a Pharisee whose prayer is a list of other people's faults, and a tax collector who manages one sentence. Babies are brought, shooed away, then let through. A ruler with a clean record and a full estate asks what he lacks, and leaves sad rather than argue. Camels and needles again, and the disciples' predictable question about who is left. Then the third passion prediction, complete with spitting and flogging, and Luke's blunt note that they understood none of it. At Jericho a blind man shouts louder than the people telling him to be quiet, which is how he gets asked what he wants.
LUK 19:1-23	A senior tax collector, rich and, Luke specifies, short, ends up in a tree because he cannot see over anyone. Jesus stops under it and invites himself to dinner, and the crowd grumbles about the company. Zacchaeus's response is arithmetic: half of everything to the poor, and four times over to anyone he cheated. Then, because Jerusalem is close and everyone expects the kingdom to arrive on schedule, a parable about a man going abroad to be made king while his own citizens petition against him. Ten servants, a mina each, and on his return an audit. Ten more, five more, and then the third servant, who has kept the coin in a handkerchief and has a theory about his employer. Next time on Luke: the reckoning.
LUK 19:24-48	The coin goes to the man who least needs it, and the citizens who objected to the king are executed in front of him, which is not a comfortable ending and is not softened. Then the entry: a colt nobody has ridden, borrowed with a line prepared in advance, cloaks on the road, and the disciples shouting about everything they have seen. Told to shut them up, he says the stones are the alternative. Then, in the middle of his own parade, he stops and cries, and what he describes is a siege: earthworks, encirclement, children on the ground, not one stone left. The temple clearance follows, and after it he simply teaches there daily, which leaves the chief priests wanting him dead and unable to act, because the crowd will not stop listening.
LUK 20:1-47	The credentials question gets a counter-question about John, and Luke lets us hear the committee reasoning it out: heaven convicts them, men gets them stoned, so they choose not knowing. The vineyard parable follows, with three beaten servants and a murdered heir, and the crowd's horrified "may it never be" only makes it worse. Then the tax trap, delivered by spies who open with compliments and are answered by a coin. Then the Sadducees' hypothetical about one woman and seven brothers, answered with angels and, more pointedly, with Moses at the burning bush. A scribe compliments him, and the questions stop. He asks his own about David, then, in everyone's hearing, describes the scribes: long robes, best seats, public greetings, long prayers, widows' houses.
LUK 21:1-38	The chapter opens on a widow and two coins, weighed against everyone else's surplus and found heavier. Then the temple's admirers get told the stonework is coming down. The timeline he gives puts the arrests first, before the cosmic part: synagogues, prisons, kings, governors, and an instruction not to rehearse anything, since the words will be supplied. Families will do the informing. Some will die. Not a hair will perish, which sits oddly beside that and is left to sit. Jerusalem surrounded means leave, and he names the pregnant and nursing women plainly rather than in passing. Then sun, moon, stars, and heads lifted rather than bowed. A fig tree budding tells you the season. Luke ends with routine: teaching all day, sleeping on the hill, crowds arriving at dawn.
LUK 22:1-36	Luke says plainly that Satan entered Judas, and then Judas goes off to negotiate. The arrangements for the meal are oddly cloak-and-dagger: follow a man with a water jar, ask the householder a set phrase, take the upper room. At the table there is a cup, then bread, then a second cup, then the flat statement that the betrayer's hand is on the table with him. The disciples begin working out who, then abandon that for an argument about rank, at a meal like this one, and are told the greatest serves. Simon gets a warning framed as sifting wheat, answers that he is ready to die, and is told how long he has. Then the strangest instruction in the chapter: sell your cloak and buy a sword. Next time on Luke: the Mount of Olives.
LUK 22:37-71	Two swords are produced, and he ends the subject with "that is enough," which is not enthusiasm. On the Mount of Olives he prays a stone's throw away, asks for the cup and defers, and Luke alone records the angel and the sweat like blood. The disciples are asleep, and Luke adds the detail that it was grief rather than laziness. Judas arrives at the head of a crowd, and the kiss is named out loud before it lands. An ear comes off and, only in Luke, goes back on. He points out that nobody needed weapons to find a man who taught in the temple daily. Then the courtyard, three denials over about an hour, the rooster, and the thing Luke has that the others do not: Jesus turns and looks at Peter.
LUK 23:1-28	The charges laid before Pilate are political, since blasphemy will not travel: tax refusal and a claim to kingship. Pilate finds nothing, and on hearing the word Galilee ships the problem to Herod, who is delighted, hoping for a performance, and gets silence, so he mocks him, sends him back in fine clothes, and Luke notes drily that the two officials became friends that day. Pilate announces twice that neither he nor Herod found a capital offence, and offers a flogging as a compromise. The crowd asks for a convicted murderer instead, and their voices prevail, which is how Luke describes the verdict: not a judgment but a volume contest. Simon of Cyrene is pulled from the road, and the women weeping are told to weep for themselves. Next time on Luke: the Skull.
LUK 23:29-56	The warning he gives the mourning women is about their own future, not his. At the Skull he is crucified between two criminals, and the first thing he says is a request that they be forgiven on the grounds that they do not understand. The mockery comes in tiers, rulers, then soldiers, then one of the men beside him, and it is the other criminal who says the only accurate thing anyone says all day: this man has done nothing wrong. He gets a promise for that same day. Then darkness, the torn veil, and a last sentence addressed to the Father. Luke's centurion says righteous rather than Son of God, and the crowd goes home beating its chest. Joseph, a councillor who had voted against all this, provides the tomb.
LUK 24:1-26	The women arrive with spices for a body that is not there, and get a question rather than an announcement: why look for the living among the dead. They are told to remember what he said in Galilee, and they do. Luke names them, and then reports that the apostles treated it as nonsense, which he does not soften. Peter runs anyway, finds folded linen, and goes home baffled. Then the road to Emmaus, where two disciples explain the crucifixion to the crucified man, at length, including their own disappointed hope and the women's report they have not believed. He calls them slow, which is fair, and starts the argument from the prophets. Next time on Luke: the bread.
LUK 24:27-53	He walks them through Moses and the prophets the whole way, then makes as if to keep going, and has to be talked into staying. The recognition happens at the table, over broken bread, and he disappears in the same moment, which they seem to find less strange than the fact that their hearts had been burning for miles. Back in Jerusalem, the eleven are already saying he has appeared to Simon. Then he is in the room, and they assume ghost, so he offers his hands and feet and then asks for food, and eats grilled fish while they watch, which settles it. The commission is short: it was all written, repentance goes to every nation starting here, you are witnesses, wait for power. At Bethany, mid-blessing, he is carried up.
MAT 1:1-25	Matthew begins with paperwork: three tidy sets of fourteen generations from Abraham through David to Joseph, and the list quietly includes Tamar, Rahab, Ruth and the woman he will only call Uriah's wife. Then the ledger stops working. Mary is pregnant and Joseph is not the reason, so he plans a quiet, decent exit. An angel interrupts the plan mid-sleep, tells him to take her as his wife anyway, and hands him the child's name.
MAT 2:1-23	Star-watchers from the east walk into Jerusalem and ask the sitting king where the new king is, which is not a tactful question. Herod is troubled, consults the professionals, extracts Bethlehem from them, and asks the visitors to report back so that he too may worship. Gold, frankincense and myrrh are handed over, and a dream sends them home by another road. Joseph is dreamed into Egypt. Herod, mocked, kills every boy in Bethlehem two years old and under. The family returns only after he dies, and only as far as Nazareth.
MAT 3:1-17	John works the wilderness in camel hair on a diet of locusts and honey, and the whole Jordan region comes out to be baptized. The religious professionals arrive and are greeted as a nest of vipers: descent from Abraham buys nothing, God can raise Abrahams out of stones, and the ax is already at the roots. He talks up someone whose shoes he is not fit to carry, and then that someone joins the queue for baptism, which John finds backwards and says so. He is talked round. The sky opens, a dove descends, and a voice settles who this is.
MAT 4:1-25	Forty days without food, and the devil arrives with three reasonable offers: stones into lunch, a jump from the temple with the angel clause quoted back, and every kingdom on earth for one bow. Each is answered with a quotation rather than an argument. The devil leaves and angels bring the meal after all. Word comes that John has been arrested, so Jesus moves to Capernaum and begins preaching John's own sentence. Four fishermen leave nets, boat and father on the spot, and crowds arrive from Decapolis to beyond the Jordan.
MAT 5:1-24	He climbs a hill, sits down, and starts congratulating the wrong people: the poor in spirit, the mourners, the gentle, the hungry for righteousness, the merciful, the pure in heart, the peacemakers, and anyone slandered on his account. Salt that has stopped being salt is gravel, and a lamp under a basket is pointless. Nothing in the law is being repealed, he insists, down to the pen strokes, and then the bar goes past the scribes entirely: contempt for a brother is filed with murder, and the gift on the altar waits until the quarrel is settled. Next time on Matthew: the standard keeps rising.
MAT 5:25-48	The escalation continues. Settle out of court while you still can. A lustful look has already done the deed, and an eye or a hand is a cheaper loss than the whole body. Divorce is narrowed to one exception. Oaths are abolished outright, since heaven, earth, Jerusalem and your own head are not yours to swear on, so say yes or no and stop. Retaliation gives way to the offered cheek, the surrendered cloak and the second mile. Love your enemies, since the sun rises on everyone regardless, and loving people who love you back is a trick tax collectors manage.
MAT 6:1-34	Three religious performances are dismantled in turn: giving with a trumpet, praying on the street corner, fasting with a carefully ruined face. All three have already been paid, in applause. Prayer gets a short model instead of volume, since the Father knew the request before it was made, with an unwelcome footnote about forgiving other people first. Treasure on earth is moth food, and the heart follows the treasure wherever it goes. Nobody serves two masters. Then the anxiety section: birds do not farm, lilies outdress Solomon, worry adds nothing to a lifespan, and tomorrow can do its own fretting.
MAT 7:1-29	The measure you use gets used on you, and the speck in a brother's eye waits behind the beam in your own. Holy things are wasted on dogs and pearls on pigs. Ask, seek, knock: no father hands over a stone when asked for bread. Do to others what you want done to you, which he says is the whole law. The gate is narrow and badly attended. False prophets wear wool and are identified by their fruit, and "Lord, Lord" plus an impressive resume of miracles gets "I never knew you." Two houses, same storm, different foundations. The crowd notices he does not sound like a scribe.
MAT 8:1-34	Coming off the mountain he touches a leper, which is the part nobody expected, then sends him to the priest quietly. A centurion asks for a long-distance cure on military logic, and is told his faith beats anything found in Israel. Peter's mother-in-law is up and serving within a verse. Two volunteers are warned: the Son of Man has no pillow, and the dead can bury each other. He sleeps through a storm, is woken by professional sailors, and calls them faithless before calming the sea. Across the water two demoniacs negotiate their exit into a pig herd, which drowns, and the town asks him to leave.
MAT 9:1-38	A paralyzed man arrives on a bed and gets forgiveness first, which the scribes silently rule blasphemy, so he heals the man to settle who has the authority. Then he hires a tax collector, eats with his colleagues, and prescribes mercy over sacrifice to the offended. Asked about fasting he answers with a wedding, an unshrunk patch and burst wineskins. On the way to a dead girl he is interrupted by a woman twelve years ill who touches his coat and is healed; the professional mourners at the house are laughing when he puts them out. Two blind men are healed and told to be quiet, and are not.
MAT 10:1-42	The twelve are named and handed authority over demons and disease, with Matthew flagging Judas at the end of the list in advance. The travel instructions are brutally light: no money, no bag, no spare coat, no staff, and permission to shake a town's dust off your sandals on the way out. Then the job description turns honest. Councils, floggings, governors, kings, brothers turning in brothers, and no need to prepare a defence, since the words will arrive when needed. Do not fear anyone who can only kill the body. Sparrows are cheap and still counted; so are the hairs on your head. He came with a sword, not peace, and a cup of cold water still counts.
MAT 11:1-30	John, in prison, sends to ask whether he backed the right man, which is a fair question from a cell. The answer is not yes but a list: blind, lame, lepers, deaf, dead, poor. Then Jesus turns to the crowd and praises the man who just doubted him as more than a prophet, and the least in the kingdom as greater still. He compares the generation to children who refuse to play either game: John fasted and was called possessed, he eats and is called a drunk. Chorazin, Bethsaida and Capernaum are told that Tyre, Sidon and even Sodom would have done better. Then, abruptly, the softest lines in the chapter: come to me, the yoke is easy.
MAT 12:1-28	The disciples snack in a wheat field on the Sabbath and the Pharisees file a complaint, so Jesus cites David eating bread he had no right to, and priests who break the Sabbath every week by working in the temple. In the synagogue the trap is baited with a withered hand and an innocent question, and he answers with the sheep everyone present would haul out of a pit on a Saturday. The hand is restored, and the Pharisees leave to discuss killing him. He withdraws, heals everyone anyway, asks for quiet, and Matthew reaches for Isaiah's servant who does not shout in the streets. A blind and mute man is freed, and the official explanation is that Satan is behind it. Next time on Matthew: he takes that theory apart.
MAT 12:29-50	You do not rob a strong man's house without tying him up first. Every sin is forgivable except one, and speaking against the Spirit is the exception. The tree is known by its fruit, the mouth reports whatever the heart is holding, and every throwaway sentence goes on the record. Asked to perform a sign, he offers Jonah instead: three days inside a fish, three days inside the earth, and notes that Nineveh managed to repent for a lesser preacher. An evicted spirit finds the house swept and empty and moves back in with seven friends. Told his family is waiting outside, he redefines the word family without going out.
MAT 13:1-30	Pushed off the beach into a boat by the size of the crowd, he starts telling stories about dirt. Seed lands on the path, on rock, in thorns, and on soil that actually works, at a hundred, sixty and thirty times return. Asked why the riddles, he says the crowd hears without hearing, and quotes Isaiah to that effect. Then he decodes it anyway: snatched, shallow, choked, or fruitful. The second story is a sabotage case. An enemy sows weeds in the wheat overnight, the staff want to go pull them, and the owner says no, you will take the wheat with them, so both grow to harvest and get sorted then. Next time on Matthew: the sorting explained, plus a very expensive pearl.
MAT 13:31-58	The comparisons keep coming and keep getting smaller: a mustard seed that ends up a tree full of birds, yeast a woman works through three measures of flour. Matthew notes he told the crowds nothing but parables. Indoors, the disciples ask for the weeds to be explained, and get an unusually literal key: sower, field, wheat, weeds, harvest, angels, furnace. Two more follow, both about someone liquidating everything for one item, a buried treasure and a single pearl, then a net that catches everything and gets sorted on the beach. Then home, where the local verdict is that they know his mother and his brothers, so how good can he be, and he does very little there.
MAT 14:1-36	Herod hears the reports and concludes, uneasily, that John is back. Matthew then fills in why that would trouble him: John had told him his marriage was unlawful, so John went to prison, and at a birthday party a dance, an oath and a request made in front of guests ended with John's head on a platter. His disciples buried him and went to tell Jesus, who left by boat for somewhere empty and was followed on foot anyway. Told to send the crowd home for dinner, the disciples are told to feed them, and five loaves and two fish cover five thousand men with twelve baskets spare. Later he walks out across the lake, is taken for a ghost, and Peter asks for a turn, manages a few steps, notices the wind, and needs fishing out.
MAT 15:1-39	A delegation arrives from Jerusalem about handwashing, and gets a lecture on loopholes instead: declaring your money devoted to God is a fine way to stop feeding your parents. Isaiah is quoted on hearts that are elsewhere while the lips are busy. The rule he gives the crowd is that the mouth is an exit, not an entrance, and the list of what comes out of the heart is not a short one. Then a Canaanite woman asks for her daughter, and gets silence, then a jurisdictional excuse, then a line about dogs, and out-argues all three with the crumbs under the table. Her daughter is healed. He heals crowds on a mountain, then feeds four thousand from seven loaves, with the disciples asking where the bread will come from as though the last time never happened.
MAT 16:1-28	Asked for a sign from heaven, he points out that they can forecast rain from a red sky but cannot read the moment they are standing in, and offers Jonah again. Then he warns about the yeast of the Pharisees and Sadducees, and the disciples conclude they are in trouble for forgetting lunch, which requires him to walk them back through both feedings before the word yeast lands. At Caesarea Philippi he asks the survey question, then the real one. Peter gets it right and is renamed the rock and handed keys. Minutes later, told the trip to Jerusalem ends in being killed, Peter corrects his teacher and is called Satan to his face. The terms follow: deny yourself, carry the cross, and note that the whole world is a poor trade for a life.
MAT 17:1-27	On a high mountain his face turns to sunlight and his clothes to white, and Moses and Elijah turn up for a conversation. Peter, present at the most extraordinary moment of his life, proposes construction. A cloud settles the matter with a voice, the three hit the ground, and when they look up it is just Jesus. Coming down: tell no one yet, and yes, Elijah already came and was badly treated, which the disciples work out means John. At the bottom, a father whose son the disciples could not help, one rebuke, one cured boy, and a note that mustard-seed faith relocates mountains. He predicts his death again, to general misery. Then the temple tax, which Peter has already promised on his behalf, arrives in the mouth of a fish.
MAT 18:1-35	The disciples ask which of them is greatest, which is its own answer, and get a child stood in the middle of the room. Become like this or forget it. Anyone who trips up one of these little ones is better off at the bottom of the sea with a millstone, and a hand, foot or eye that keeps causing trouble is expendable. A shepherd leaves ninety-nine sheep on the hills for one that wandered, and is happier about the one. Grievances get a procedure: privately, then with witnesses, then the whole assembly. Peter tries to negotiate forgiveness down to seven and is quoted seventy times seven, then handed a story about a man let off ten thousand talents who immediately throttles a colleague over a hundred denarii.
MAT 19:1-30	The test question is whether a man can divorce his wife for any reason at all, and he answers from the first chapters of Genesis rather than from the statute: Moses permitted it because hearts are hard, not because it was ever the intention. The disciples' conclusion, that marriage sounds inadvisable, is not the lesson. Children are brought over, the disciples shoo them off, and are overruled. Then a man with a clean record asks what he still lacks and is told to sell everything, which he declines, sadly and quietly, being rich. Camels and needles follow, plus the disciples' reasonable panic about who can be saved. Peter asks what is in it for those who did leave everything, and is promised twelve thrones and a warning that the order gets reversed.
MAT 20:1-34	A vineyard owner hires all day, at dawn and at the eleventh hour, and pays everyone a denarius, taking care to pay the latecomers first where the early crew can watch. They complain, and are told they received exactly what they agreed to, and that generosity is not theft. On the road up to Jerusalem he spells out the ending: handed over, condemned, mocked, flogged, crucified, raised. The immediate response is James and John's mother requesting the two best seats. He asks about the cup instead, the other ten seethe, and he explains that in this outfit greatness is a serving job. Then two blind men in Jericho shout over the crowd trying to hush them, and get asked what they want, which is the whole point.
MAT 21:1-23	The entry is staged and Matthew says so: a donkey and colt fetched on request, the prophecy quoted, cloaks and branches on the road, Hosanna in the streets, and a city asking who this actually is. Then the temple, where he clears out the market and tips the money changers' tables over along with the dove sellers' seats, pointing out that the building was advertised as a house of prayer. He heals in the wreckage. The chief priests complain about children shouting, and get a psalm quoted at them. On the way back in he finds a fig tree with leaves and no figs and ends it, then talks about mountains and prayer. Back in the temple, the authorities want his credentials. Next time on Matthew: he asks them a question instead.
MAT 21:24-46	He offers a trade: tell me where John's baptism came from and I will tell you where my authority comes from. Heaven convicts them of ignoring John, men puts them at odds with a crowd that loved him, so they retreat to not knowing, and he keeps his answer. Then two sons, one who says no and goes, one who says yes and does not, with the observation that tax collectors and prostitutes are ahead of them in the queue. Then tenants who beat, stone and kill the servants sent for the rent and then murder the heir on the theory that the estate will simply pass to them. Asked for a sentence, the priests pass it on themselves. They understand exactly who the story is about, and only the crowd keeps them from arresting him.
MAT 22:1-46	A king throws a wedding and the invited guests are too busy, then violent, so the guest list is replaced wholesale from the roads, and the one man who turns up dressed wrong is removed anyway. Then three attempts to trap him, in order. The tax question comes wrapped in flattery, and he asks whose face is on the coin. The Sadducees, who do not believe in the resurrection, arrive with an elaborate hypothetical about a woman and seven brothers, and are told they have misread both the scriptures and the point. A lawyer asks for the single greatest commandment and gets two, on the grounds that the rest hangs off them. Then he asks his own question, about David calling his own son Lord, and the questions stop for good.
MAT 23:1-39	The advice is to follow the teaching and ignore the teachers, who write heavy rules and carry none of them, wear conspicuously wide prayer boxes and long fringes, and enjoy the good seats and being greeted as Rabbi. His own people are to collect no titles at all. Then the woes arrive, seven of them, and they are specific: shutting the door and standing in it, sailing the world to produce one worse convert, a whole jurisprudence of which oaths count, tithing garden herbs while skipping justice and mercy, filtering gnats and swallowing camels, polishing the outside of the cup, beautiful tombs packed with bones, and building monuments to prophets their fathers killed. It ends not in fury but in grief: a hen, chicks that would not come, and an empty house.
MAT 24:1-27	The disciples point out the temple architecture and are told none of it will be standing. On the Mount of Olives they ask for a date and get, first, a warning about impostors. Wars, famines, plagues and earthquakes are dismissed as early labour pains rather than the finale. What follows for them is arrest, execution, universal hatred, mass defection, informers among friends, and love going cold, with the good news reaching every nation before the end. When Daniel's abomination is standing where it should not, the instruction is to leave Judea without going back indoors for anything, and there is a plain word of pity for pregnant and nursing women in that scramble. Impostors will have impressive credentials. Next time on Matthew: what the real thing looks like.
MAT 24:28-51	The real thing needs no announcement: vultures find a carcass, lightning crosses the whole sky. Sun and moon go out, stars fall, the Son of Man arrives on cloud with a trumpet and angels collecting the chosen from every direction. Then a fig tree, whose leaves tell you the season, and a flat refusal to name the date: not the angels, only the Father. Noah is the model, not for the flood but for the ordinariness before it, people eating and marrying right up to the water. Two in a field, two at a mill, one of each gone. A burglar keeps no appointment, so stay awake. The chapter closes on the servant left in charge, and the difference between one who kept feeding the household and one who assumed the boss was late.
MAT 25:1-25	Ten bridesmaids wait for a bridegroom who takes his time, and the only difference between the five who make it and the five who do not is a spare flask of oil. Everybody sleeps. At midnight the shout goes up, the unprepared five go shopping, and the door closes while they are out. Watch, he says, because nobody knows the hour. Then the accounts: three servants get five talents, two and one, sized to their ability, and the first two trade and double the money to identical praise. The third has clearly prepared his speech: you are a hard man who reaps what he never sowed, so here is your money, still in the wrapping, dug out of the garden. Next time on Matthew: how that speech lands.
MAT 25:26-46	It lands badly. If the master really is the man described, the servant is told, the money should at least have been earning interest at a bank. The talent is transferred to the one already holding ten, on the principle that having leads to more, and the servant is put out. Then the courtroom scene: the Son of Man seated in glory, every nation in front of him, and a sorting done the way a shepherd separates sheep from goats. The criteria are entirely unspiritual: food, water, welcome, clothes, a sick visit, a prison visit. Both groups are baffled, and get the same explanation from opposite ends, that the least of these was him all along. One list of omissions is enough to decide it.
MAT 26:1-39	Two days out from Passover, Jesus states plainly what is coming, and the chief priests meet at Caiaphas's house to arrange it quietly, timing it around the festival crowds. At Bethany a woman empties a jar of expensive ointment over his head, the disciples price it against charity, and he says she has prepared him for burial. Judas puts his own figure on the same event: thirty pieces of silver. At the table he says one of them will hand him over, and gives them bread and a cup with words that reframe both. He tells them all they will scatter, and tells Peter how many hours that will take. In Gethsemane he asks for the cup to pass, and does not insist. Next time on Matthew: the arrest.
MAT 26:40-75	He prays three times and comes back three times to find his closest friends asleep. Judas arrives with a crowd carrying swords and clubs, and marks him with a kiss. A sword comes out, an ear comes off, and Jesus stops it: twelve legions of angels are available and are not being called. He points out that nobody needed clubs to find a man who taught in the temple every day, and then every disciple runs. The trial cannot find usable testimony until two witnesses produce a line about the temple. Put under oath, he answers, and the high priest tears his own clothing rather than hear more. They spit on him and strike him. In the courtyard Peter denies him three times, his accent gives him away, and he weeps.
MAT 27:1-32	Morning brings the transfer to Pilate. Judas tries to give the money back, says the word innocent out loud, and is told that is his affair; he throws the silver into the sanctuary and hangs himself, and the priests, who will not put blood money in the treasury, spend it on a field for burying strangers. Before Pilate, Jesus answers one question and then stops answering, which the governor finds remarkable. Pilate offers a swap and the crowd, coached, picks Barabbas. His wife sends a warning from a dream. He washes his hands in public, which changes nothing. The soldiers put a robe on him, plait thorns into a crown, kneel, spit and strike, then march him out, pulling a bystander from Cyrene in to carry the cross. Next time on Matthew: Golgotha.
MAT 27:33-66	He refuses the drugged wine. He is crucified between two robbers, his clothes gambled for at his feet, a written charge nailed above him. Everyone within earshot says a version of the same thing: come down and we will believe you. Darkness covers the land for three hours, and then the cry asking why he has been forsaken, misheard as a call for Elijah. He cries out once more and dies. The temple curtain tears from the top down, the ground shakes, tombs open. The centurion running the execution reaches a conclusion nobody in the trial reached. The women who followed him from Galilee watch from a distance. Joseph of Arimathaea gives up his own new tomb. The priests, taking the resurrection talk more seriously than the disciples do, seal the stone.
MAT 28:1-20	Two women arrive at first light and get an earthquake, an angel who rolls the stone aside and sits down on it, and guards who go stiff as corpses. The message is short: not here, risen, as he said, come and look, now go and tell them Galilee. They leave frightened and delighted at once, and Jesus meets them on the road himself. Meanwhile the guards report to the chief priests, who pay for a better story, the one about disciples stealing the body while the guards slept, which requires the guards to admit sleeping on duty. The eleven go to a mountain in Galilee, where they worship him and, Matthew notes without embarrassment, some of them doubt. The last words are a commission to every nation, and a promise to stay to the end of the age.
PRO 1:1-33	The book states its purpose up front like a syllabus: wisdom, discernment, justice, equity, prudence for the simple and discretion for the young, plus the ability to handle a riddle. Fear of Yahweh is the entry requirement. Then a father's first practical lesson, which is about peer pressure and is startlingly bloody: if the boys invite you to lie in wait for the innocent and pool the proceeds in one purse, decline, because the ambush they are setting is their own. And then Wisdom herself takes the street corner, shouting at the city gates, asking how long the simple intend to enjoy being simple. Her closing position is not soothing: she called, nobody came, and she will laugh when the storm arrives.
PRO 2:1-22	A single long conditional sentence, the sort a teacher builds when the payoff matters more than the clauses. If you receive the words, store them, call out for discernment and dig for wisdom the way people dig for silver, then the fear of Yahweh becomes something you understand rather than recite. After that, discretion works as a bodyguard. It keeps you away from two specified hazards: men who genuinely enjoy doing evil and have gone crooked on purpose, and the smooth-talking woman whose house has a downhill exit and no return traffic. The chapter ends on real estate, since the upright stay in the land and the treacherous are pulled out of it by the roots.
PRO 3:1-35	The chapter everyone can quote half of. Trust Yahweh with all your heart, decline to lean on your own understanding, and the paths get straightened out, which sounds simple until you try it. Kindness and truth are to be worn as jewellery and written on the heart. Discipline is reframed as affection, on the grounds that a father corrects the son he delights in, a thought that lands differently depending on the father. Wisdom outperforms silver, gold and rubies, carries long life in the right hand and money in the left, and was the instrument the earth was built with. Then, abruptly, the practical part: pay the debt today rather than telling your neighbour to come back tomorrow.
PRO 4:1-27	The father cites his sources, which is a nice touch: this is what his own father told him, back when he was the tender only child. The advice is to acquire wisdom at any cost, up to and including everything you own, on the theory that she pays out in garlands and crowns. On avoiding bad company he is uncompromising: do not walk carefully on that road, do not pass by it, go around, because those men cannot get to sleep until they have made somebody fall. Then the best image in the chapter, the righteous path brightening like dawn towards full daylight while the wicked trip over obstacles they cannot see. Guard the heart, eyes front, no turning right or left.
PRO 5:1-23	The adultery chapter, and it works by aftertaste. Her lips drip honey and her mouth is smoother than oil, and the review of the experience is wormwood and a two-edged sword. Do not go near the door, because the bill is paid in honour, years and wealth, with strangers enjoying the proceeds. There is even a scripted deathbed regret provided in advance: how I hated instruction, how I ignored every teacher who tried. The counter-proposal is domestic and, for this book, unusually warm, all cisterns and springs and water that should stay on your own property, and a wife described as a loving doe. Yahweh is watching the paths regardless, and a man's own sins do the tying up.
PRO 6:1-35	A chapter of miscellaneous emergencies. Co-signing a stranger's loan is treated as a hostage situation: do not sleep until you are out of it, wriggle free like a bird out of a snare. The sluggard is then sent on a field trip to the ant, who runs no management structure whatsoever and still has the summer stores done, while the sluggard's own ruin arrives dressed as an armed robber after a little sleep and a little folding of the hands. Next, the man who winks and signals with his feet, who will simply break one day, no remedy available. Then the numbered list, six things Yahweh hates and seven that are an abomination, saving discord among brothers for last place, which is where the emphasis goes.
PRO 7:1-27	The father drops the abstractions and tells a story, from the window, through the lattice, which is a detail he did not have to include. A young man with no understanding takes the route past a particular corner at dusk. The woman who meets him has the best speech in the chapter and knows it: my vows are paid, I came out specifically looking for you, the couch has Egyptian tapestry, the bed is myrrh and aloes and cinnamon, and my husband is out of town with a bag of money until the full moon. Every detail is a reassurance and the last one is the closing argument. The boy goes along like an ox to slaughter. Her house is on a downhill street.
PRO 8:1-36	Wisdom takes the podium herself and makes a considerable case, standing where the roads meet and at the city gates. Nothing crooked in her mouth, better value than silver and gold and rubies, and a genuine boast that kings reign by her, which sounds like credentialism until you consider the alternative. Then the section that theologians never stopped arguing about: she was there before the depths, before the springs, before the mountains had been set down, before there was dust. When the sea was given its boundary she was the craftsman at his side, a delight day by day, rejoicing in the finished world and specifically in people. Whoever finds her finds life, and the rest love death.
PRO 9:1-18	Two dinner invitations, printed in the same font on purpose. Wisdom builds the house, carves seven pillars, kills the meat, mixes the wine, sets the table and sends the staff to shout for the simple. Folly sits in her doorway in the same high places of the city and uses word for word the same call, her only menu item being that stolen water is sweet and food eaten in secret is nice. The difference is not in the pitch but in the guest list, since hers are already down among the departed. Between the two invitations sits the driest advice in the book: correct a mocker and you get insulted, correct a wise man and he likes you for it.
PRO 10:1-32	The one-liners begin, and the format is relentlessly binary: righteous on the left, wicked on the right, no third option. The recurring interests are work and mouths. Lazy hands make you poor, diligent hands make you rich, and sleeping through harvest is presented as a character flaw rather than a scheduling issue. On speech it is nearly modern: a flood of words never runs short of sin, restraint is wisdom, hidden hatred produces lying lips, and love covers what hatred keeps stirring. Yahweh's blessing is described as wealth with no trouble bolted on, which is a distinction anyone who has come into money will appreciate. Best line: the sluggard is vinegar on the teeth and smoke in the eyes of whoever sent him.
PRO 11:1-31	It opens, pointedly, with weights and measures, because nothing reveals a person's theology faster than a rigged scale. From there the interest is civic: a city cheers when the righteous prosper and gets demolished by the mouths of the wicked, and a nation with no counsel falls over. Gossip is defined precisely, as betraying a confidence, which is more useful than calling it a sin. The generosity sayings run on arithmetic that should not work: scatter and you increase, water others and you get watered, hoard the grain and the neighbourhood curses you by name. Riches, it notes twice, are worthless on the day of wrath. And then the line nobody forgets, about a gold ring in a pig's snout.
PRO 12:1-28	The collection settles into its rhythm and starts calling names, since hating reproof is not unfortunate here, it is stupid. Domestic notes: a worthy wife is a crown, a bad one is rot in the bones, which is unbalanced but memorable. There is a quiet, genuinely humane line about the righteous man looking after his animal. On work, chasing fantasies is contrasted with tilling actual soil, and the lazy hunter is caught out not by failing to hunt but by not bothering to cook what he caught. On temper, the fool shows his annoyance the same day, which is the most contemporary observation in the chapter. Rash words stab like a sword and a kind word lifts a heavy heart.
PRO 13:1-25	Mouth, money, and the rod. Guard your mouth and you guard your life; open your lips wide and you are finished. There is a sharp pair about pretending, one man performing wealth on nothing and another performing poverty on a fortune, which reads like observed behaviour rather than doctrine. Dishonest money dwindles while money gathered by hand grows, an old argument about compounding. The famous one lands here: hope deferred makes the heart sick, and fulfilled longing is a tree of life, which is the most emotionally accurate sentence in the book so far. Also, walk with the wise and you become wise, which is either encouraging or damning depending on your address book.
PRO 14:1-35	This chapter keeps admitting that the system is not tidy. The heart knows its own bitterness and refuses to hand it to a stranger. Laughter can be covering sorrow. There is a way that looks entirely correct and ends in death, which undercuts every confident proverb around it. The simple believe everything, the prudent stop and check. Best agricultural line: with no oxen the stall stays clean, and also nothing gets harvested, which is a small essay on the cost of anything worth having. It is unsentimental about money, noting that the poor lose even their neighbour while the rich collect friends, and then insists that oppressing the poor is contempt for their Maker.
PRO 15:1-33	A chapter about tone of voice, which is a surprisingly narrow subject to sustain for thirty-three verses and it manages. The gentle answer turning away wrath opens it, and the theme keeps returning: the righteous weigh their answers, the wicked gush, and a word at the right time is described as a pleasure rather than a duty. Yahweh's eyes are everywhere, with Sheol and Abaddon already open for inspection, so hearts are no challenge. Then the two comparisons that carry the whole book's economics: better a little with the fear of Yahweh than a large fortune with trouble attached, and better a dinner of herbs where there is love than a fattened calf served with hatred.
PRO 16:1-33	A chapter about the gap between intending and happening. You plan the route, Yahweh moves your feet. Everyone's ways look clean to the person walking them, which is why somebody else weighs the motives. There is a block of court advice for anyone working near a king: the wrath is a death sentence and the favour is a rain cloud, so read the face carefully. Then the greatest hits, arriving practically back to back: pride before destruction, a haughty spirit before a fall, pleasant words as a honeycomb, and grey hair as a crown you can only earn by living a certain way. The best of them is the quietest: ruling your own temper beats taking a city.
PRO 17:1-28	It opens on the most persuasive argument in the book, which is that a dry crust eaten in peace beats a banquet with a fight going on. There is an admirably concrete rule about conflict: starting a quarrel is breaching a dam, so shut it before the water is moving. Two images do a lot of work, a rebuke going deeper into an understanding man than a hundred lashes into a fool, and the strong preference for meeting a bear robbed of her cubs over meeting a fool mid-folly. Mocking the poor is filed as an insult to their Maker. A friend loves at all times. And the last verse is generous to fools: keep your mouth shut and people will assume you are wise.
PRO 18:1-24	Mostly a chapter about talking, and it is unusually sharp about how arguments actually work. A fool takes no pleasure in understanding, only in expressing his opinion, which is a sentence that has aged extremely well. Answering before you have heard is named as shameful. And there is a real insight into procedure: whoever speaks first sounds convincing right up until somebody cross-examines him. Gossip goes down like dainty morsels, which is exactly why it works. The rich man's wall is unscalable in his own imagination, a phrase doing considerable damage in six words. An offended brother is harder to get into than a fortified city. And one friend can stick closer than a brother.
PRO 19:1-29	Poverty is the running subject and the treatment is unsentimental to the point of bleak. Wealth collects friends; the poor man loses his relatives too, and chases them with pleas that reach nobody. The counterweight is one line: pity the poor and you have lent to Yahweh, who settles his accounts. Elsewhere, zeal without knowledge gets its own warning, which few sermons quote. The observation about the hot-tempered man is unnervingly practical, since rescuing him from the consequences guarantees you will be doing it again. There is a continual dripping where a quarrelsome wife is concerned, and then the sluggard, who has got his hand into the dish and cannot see the point of the return trip.
PRO 20:1-30	Wine is a mocker and beer is a brawler, which is the whole hangover in six words. Honest weights come round twice, in case the first time was missed. The genuinely interesting thread is about how little a person knows himself: who can claim a pure heart, how can a man understand his own way when his steps come from elsewhere, and the spirit of man described as Yahweh's lamp going through the drawers. There is one straight piece of comedy, the buyer who says it is no good, it is no good, and then boasts about the deal once he is safely round the corner. Do not repay evil, it says, wait. Grey hair is the splendour of old men.
PRO 21:1-31	The chapter opens by describing a king's heart as plumbing, a watercourse turned wherever Yahweh wants it, which is a comforting thought for everyone except the king. Doing justice is rated above sacrifice, which is a bolder claim than it looks in a book this pious. There is a good deal of housing advice, since the corner of a roof and, failing that, actual desert both beat sharing a house with a contentious woman, a line the editor liked enough to include twice. The sluggard is killed by his own desire, his hands having declined to help. And the closing verse is the whole book in miniature: prepare the horse anyway, but the outcome is not yours.
PRO 22:1-29	A good name is worth more than money, and rich and poor share a manufacturer, which is the kind of levelling this book does without fuss. There is the verse about training up a child, and the flat economic observation that the borrower is the lender's servant, which needs no theology. The sluggard reappears with the finest excuse in scripture, a lion, outside, in the street. Then the format changes: the sentences get longer and the collection announces itself as thirty sayings. The best of them has teeth, since exploiting the poor in court is answered not by a rule but by a warning that Yahweh will take the case personally. Also, do not make friends with an angry man, in case it is catching.
PRO 23:1-35	Dining etiquette for the ambitious, and it is bleak. Watch what is put in front of you. If your host is stingy, the meal he presses on you is food you will vomit, along with your compliments. Do not exhaust yourself getting rich, because money grows eagle wings and leaves. Then the boundary stone, defended by the observation that orphans have a strong lawyer. And finally the wine passage, which is the best writing about drinking anywhere: who has woe, sorrow, bruises he cannot account for, red eyes. Do not look at it when it sparkles and goes down easily, because at the end it bites. It closes on the man who cannot feel the beating he took and is already planning the next round.
PRO 24:1-34	A house built by wisdom, rooms furnished by knowledge, wars won by having enough advisers, all reasonable. Then the chapter does something the rest of the book mostly avoids and demands action: rescue the people being led away to death, and do not try the line about not having known, because the one who weighs hearts has already considered it. Elsewhere the tone is generous. A righteous man falls seven times and stands up, which is permission rather than a standard. Do not enjoy your enemy's downfall, on the grounds that God may notice and lose interest in it. And the closing tour of the sluggard's vineyard, thorns, nettles, collapsed wall, is a whole sermon delivered as landscape.
PRO 25:1-28	A second collection, credited to Hezekiah's copyists, and the images sharpen considerably. It begins at court, with the useful advice not to seat yourself high, since being invited up beats being moved down where the prince can watch. Then the metaphors arrive in a run: a word fitly spoken as apples of gold in silver settings, a faithful messenger as snow in harvest, a boaster as cloud and wind that produce no rain, and singing cheerfully at a grieving person as removing their coat in cold weather. Feed your hungry enemy and heap coals on his head, which is either kindness or the best revenge available. A man with no self-control is a city with the walls down.
PRO 26:1-28	Twenty-eight verses on fools, and the editor has stopped being polite. Honour on a fool sits like snow in summer. Two verses in a row give flatly opposite instructions about answering him, which is either a scribal joke or an admission that neither option is good. Best of the images: sending a message by a fool amounts to cutting off your own feet, a proverb in his mouth hangs like a lame man's legs, and the fool going back to his folly is a dog returning to its vomit, a comparison nobody has improved on. The sluggard, meanwhile, has upgraded his lion, and turns on his bed like a door on hinges. Meddling in a quarrel that is not yours is grabbing a strange dog by the ears.
PRO 27:1-27	A chapter of unusually good sentences. Do not boast about tomorrow. Let someone else do the praising. A fool's provocation outweighs stone and sand, and jealousy outweighs everything. Open rebuke beats hidden love, and the wounds of a friend are called faithful, which is the kindest thing this book says about criticism. There is a completely practical note that a near neighbour beats a distant brother on the day things go wrong. And a small comic gem: bless a man loudly first thing in the morning and he will take it as a curse. Iron sharpens iron. Sheol, Abaddon and the human eye are the three things never satisfied, which is quite the company to keep. Grind a fool in a mortar and you still have a fool.
PRO 28:1-28	It opens with the wicked running from nobody at all, which is the most psychologically astute image in the collection. From there it is largely about money and how it distorts the people holding it. A rich man is wise in his own eyes while a poor man with understanding sees straight through him. Extortionate interest gets quietly reassigned to somebody who will pity the poor. There is a rare pastoral note: hiding your sins does not work, and confessing and dropping them finds mercy. And two political lines that have not dated at all, a wicked ruler over helpless people as a roaring lion and a charging bear, and men hiding themselves whenever the wicked come up in the world.
PRO 29:1-27	Effectively a chapter on governance. The people rejoice or groan depending entirely on who is in charge; a king who judges the poor fairly keeps his throne, one who takes bribes dismantles the country, and a ruler who will listen to lies ends up staffed exclusively by liars, which is presented as cause and effect rather than coincidence. Elsewhere the observations are personal and sharp: flattery is a net laid for your feet, a fool empties out all his anger while a wise man holds it in, and taking a fool to court produces rage and scoffing but no verdict. The line about no revelation and people casting off restraint is famous. The line about hasty speech being worse than folly deserves to be.
PRO 30:1-33	Agur son of Jakeh arrives, and he is the most interesting voice in the book, opening by declaring himself the most ignorant man present and asking who exactly has gathered the wind in his fists. His prayer is one of the few genuinely modest requests in scripture: no lies, and neither poverty nor riches, because full men deny God and hungry men steal. Then he turns to lists, which he clearly loves. Four things that never say enough, ending with fire. Four things too wonderful to grasp, ending with a man and a young woman. Four small creatures that are wise anyway, including the lizard who has somehow got himself into the palace. And a closing note that forcing anger produces strife the way twisting a nose produces blood.
PRO 31:1-31	King Lemuel's mother gets the last word, and she is direct. Not women, not wine, because a drinking king forgets the law and the person who loses by that is whoever is already afflicted; save the strong drink for those actually perishing. Then open your mouth for people who have none. The closing poem on the worthy woman is usually read as domestic and is mostly commercial: she evaluates a field and buys it, plants a vineyard, runs a linen business with the merchants, works past dark, and brings food in like a merchant ship. Her husband's contribution is to be well thought of at the gate. Charm is deceptive, beauty is vapour, and the request at the end is simply that she be paid and credited in public.
SIR 1:1-30	Sirach opens the way a scribe opens a career, by establishing that the material is not his: all wisdom comes from the Lord and stays with him. The unanswerable questions come first, since nobody counts the sand or the raindrops or the days of eternity. Wisdom, we learn, predates everything, was created and then counted and poured out over all God's works, which is an oddly industrial image and a good one. Then the phrase this book will not let go of, the fear of the Lord, which within twenty verses is called wisdom's beginning, its fullness, its crown and its root, apparently to leave no gardening metaphor unused. It ends on practicalities: unjust anger is never justifiable, and self-promotion gets your secrets read out in public.
SIR 2:1-18	Advice for anyone who has just decided to serve God, and the first item is that it will go badly: prepare your soul for temptation. Gold is tried in fire, and acceptable men in the furnace of humiliation, which is honest and no comfort at all. He then addresses those who fear the Lord three times in a row, wait, trust, hope, and backs it with a challenge to name one person from earlier generations who trusted and was shamed. The woes that follow are aimed at hesitation rather than wickedness: fearful hearts, faint hands, the sinner who goes two ways, and anyone who has run out of patience. The closing preference is a real one: better the hands of the Lord than the hands of men.
SIR 3:1-31	A chapter on parents that is unusually practical about what honour actually costs. The famous part is about a father's decline: help him, do not grieve him, and if his understanding fails, be patient rather than dishonouring him while you still have your strength, which is the sort of instruction that only comes from someone who has watched it happen. The reward is described beautifully, sins melting like ice in fair weather. Then a turn to humility with a line that has aged extremely well, that the greater you are the more you should humble yourself, followed by advice against seeking out what is too hard for you, which from a wisdom teacher is a strikingly modest position.
SIR 4:1-31	The first half is about poverty and it is uncompromising. Do not make hungry eyes wait. Do not add trouble to a heart already provoked. Do not turn your face away, and give nobody a reason to curse you, because the one who made him is listening. Be a father to the fatherless and stand in for the husband, and you will be treated as a son of the Most High. Then wisdom appears as an instructor of alarming methods, walking her student through crooked ways first, bringing fear and dread and tormenting him with discipline until she decides she can trust him, and only afterwards coming back by the straight road with the secrets. Also a fine distinction between shame that is sin and shame that is grace.
SIR 5:1-15	A list of sentences not to say out loud, which makes it one of the more entertaining chapters here. Do not say your goods are sufficient. Do not ask who has authority over you. And do not say the one everybody has thought, that you sinned and nothing happened, since the correct reading of that silence is patience, not permission. Do not say his compassion is great and will cover the pile, because mercy and wrath keep the same office. The imagery for indecision is agricultural and exact: do not winnow with every wind, do not walk in every path. And if you have no understanding, the instruction is simply to put your hand over your mouth.
SIR 6:1-37	The friendship chapter, and it is the most clear-eyed thing written on the subject in the ancient world. Have plenty of people at peace with you, and one counsellor in a thousand. Acquire a friend by testing him first, because the taxonomy is unforgiving: the friend who is in it for his own occasion, the friend who converts to enemy and takes the quarrel public, and the table companion who mirrors your prosperity, starts giving orders to your servants, and then cannot be found when things go wrong. Against that, a faithful friend is a medicine of life. The second half switches to wisdom and does not soften her, since she is harsh to the unlearned and lands like a testing stone, and the instruction is to put your own neck in her chain.
SIR 7:1-36	A very long list of things not to do, and the tone is that of a man who has watched people ruin themselves in every one of these ways. Do not angle for preeminence. Do not display your wisdom in front of the king. Do not volunteer to be a judge, in case you turn out to be afraid of a powerful face. Do not assume a big enough offering will be waved through on volume. Do not repeat your words in prayer, which is a shrewd note. The household section is brisker and less comfortable, since daughters are to be watched and sons to have their necks bowed from youth, but it also insists on paying a hired man properly. And the closing line is the whole book's method: remember your last end.
SIR 8:1-19	A chapter entirely about picking your fights, and its conclusion is to pick almost none. Not the mighty man, not the rich man, not the man full of tongue, because heaping wood on that fire is exactly what he wanted. Do not go to law with a judge, on the grounds that the verdict will follow his honour rather than the evidence, which is a cynical and probably accurate reading of the courts. Do not lend upwards, and if you must, treat the money as gone. Do not cross a desert with an angry man, since blood is nothing in his sight and there will be nobody to help. The kindest lines are about ageing and dying: do not mock the old or gloat over the dead, because we are all on the list.
SIR 9:1-18	The chapter every modern reader trips over, since the advice about women is relentlessly framed as hazard management: do not gaze, do not linger, do not drink with a married woman, do not keep company with a singer. The reasoning is at least stated honestly, that beauty has led many astray and love catches like fire, which locates the problem in the looker. Then it pivots, and the second half is genuinely warm. Never trade an old friend for a new one, because a new friend is new wine and only worth drinking once it has aged. Do not envy a sinner's success, since you have no idea what his collapse looks like. And keep clear of anyone with the power to kill.
SIR 10:1-31	Political theory, and the central claim is that a people takes its character from whoever is in charge: as the judge is, so are his officials, and as the ruler is, so is the whole city. An uninstructed king ruins his people. Then a long and enjoyable assault on pride, tracing empires changing hands because of injustice, violence and greed. Best pair of lines in the chapter: he is a king today and tomorrow he dies, and what he inherits is creeping things and worms. God, it says, has been taking thrones away from rulers and installing the meek for a very long time. Pride was never issued to human beings in the first place, and honour attaches to fear of the Lord, whether you are rich or poor.
SIR 11:1-34	A chapter about not judging by the surface, and it makes the case with a bee, which is small among flying things and nonetheless produces the best thing on the table. Kings have ended up sitting on the ground and total unknowns have worn diadems. There is excellent procedural advice: examine before you blame, listen before you answer, and do not interrupt. Then the parable of the saver, who scrimps for years, announces that he has found rest and will now enjoy his goods, and turns out not to know how much time is left. Call no man blessed before his death. And the best image here, the proud man as a decoy partridge in a cage, waiting to be the reason someone else gets caught.
SIR 12:1-18	Two subjects, generosity and enemies, and the first is uncomfortable. Know who you are giving to, help the godly and the lowly, and specifically withhold bread from the ungodly, on the practical grounds that he will use it against you. Then the enemy section, which is a small masterpiece of suspicion. His wickedness is rust on brass, and even if he crouches and humbles himself you will find, on wiping the mirror, that the rust never actually came off. Do not seat him beside you and certainly not on your right. He talks sweetly while surveying you for a pit, he cries real tears, and when trouble arrives he will already be standing there, offering a hand and putting a foot behind your heel.
SIR 13:1-26	The class chapter, and it is unsparing. Touch pitch and you are marked. Keep company with the powerful and rich and you are the earthen pot next to the kettle, and only one of you survives the collision. The portrait of the patron is the best thing here and reads like reportage: he trades on you while you are useful, flatters you when he needs something, humiliates you with his hospitality until he has stripped you two or three times, then laughs, and later shakes his head at you in the street. When a rich man falls there is a queue of helpers and his indiscretions get explained away; when a poor man falls he gets a lecture, and his wisdom is granted no room at all.
SIR 14:1-27	The miser's portrait, and it is a study in self-harm rather than greed. His eye is never satisfied with his own portion, he grudges the bread on his own table, and everything he saves by depriving himself is money other people will eventually enjoy. There is nobody worse off, the author says, than the man who envies himself. The counter-advice is unexpectedly generous for this book: be good to yourself according to your means, be generous to your friend while you are still alive, and do not let a good day go by, because nobody goes looking for luxury in the grave. Generations turn over like leaves on a thick tree. And wisdom, in the closing image, gets stalked: windows, doorways, a tent pitched next door.
SIR 15:1-20	Wisdom is described in domestic terms, meeting the man who keeps the law like a mother and receiving him like a bride, feeding him bread of understanding and water of wisdom, and opening his mouth in the assembly, which for a scribe is the real reward. Then the chapter turns to the argument it exists for. Do not say the Lord made you fall. Do not say he caused you to err, because he has no use for a sinful man. God made humans and left them in the hand of their own counsel, which is one of the strongest statements of free will in the whole tradition. Fire and water are set out; reach for whichever you like. Nobody was commanded to be ungodly, and nobody was issued a licence.
SIR 16:1-30	The opening is bleak about family: better to die childless than to raise ungodly children, and one good child beats a thousand of the other kind. Then the historical evidence, delivered as a docket, the giants who revolted in their strength, the neighbours Lot had to live among, and the six hundred thousand footmen who managed it through hardness of heart. The interesting part is the anticipated objection, since somebody is going to say he cannot possibly be noticed in a creation this size, and the answer is that heaven, the deep and the earth all move when God visits. The chapter finishes on the created order as a workforce that never hungers, never tires, never elbows its neighbour and has never once failed to do what it was told.
SIR 17:1-32	A compressed anthropology. Man is made of earth and returns to it, with days issued by number, authority over what lives here, and a set of instruments listed like equipment: counsel, tongue, eyes, ears, heart. He was shown good and evil, given a law of life as inheritance and an everlasting covenant, and is watched continuously, since all his works are as the sun before God. The best image is administrative and lovely: a man's almsgiving is kept like a signet and guarded as the apple of the eye. And the argument for repenting now is entirely practical rather than pious, since the dead do not give thanks and only somebody alive and in health is in a position to praise anyone.
SIR 18:1-33	It opens with a fine statement of the limits of the whole wisdom enterprise: when a man has finished, he is only at the beginning, and when he stops he will be perplexed. A hundred years at the outside, a drop from the sea, a pebble from the sand, and God's patience is presented as a direct consequence of noticing how short we are. Then the practical section, which contains one of the best lines in the book, that a word is better than a gift, and the observation that an envious man's present consumes the eyes. Advice follows on timing: learn before speaking, humble yourself before you are ill, pay the vow now, and remember hunger while you are full. Also, do not throw banquets on credit.
SIR 19:1-30	It opens with a hard economic fact, that a drunk workman does not get rich, and adds that despising small things fails you gradually rather than all at once. Then the discretion chapter proper, and it is very good. Never repeat what you were told, from friend or enemy alike, because he heard you and watched you and will get round to hating you at a convenient moment. A word can safely die with you; it will not burst you. A fool, by contrast, labours with a secret like a woman in childbirth, and it sits in him like an arrow in the thigh. There is also generous advice about correction: reprove before you threaten, since he may not have said it, and everyone has sinned with the tongue.
SIR 20:1-32	A chapter organised almost entirely around silence, and it distinguishes the kinds. One man is quiet because he has nothing to say, another because he knows his moment has not come, while the braggart talks straight through his. Then a run of paradoxes: prosperity found inside a misfortune, profit that turns into loss, a gift that repays nothing and a gift that repays double, a bargain that costs sevenfold. The fool's generosity gets a full portrait, since he gives little, scolds much, announces it like a town crier, asks for it back the next day, and then complains that he has no friends. Best line: a slip on the pavement is better than a slip of the tongue. And better to hide your folly than to hide your wisdom.
SIR 21:1-28	Sin gets the serpent treatment, with lion's teeth attached, and the point is proximity: it only bites if you draw near. There is a genuinely fine line about a poor man's prayer arriving at God's ears with the judgment following quickly. The economics is sharp too, since building your house with borrowed money is compared to stockpiling stones for the winter. But most of the chapter is a portrait gallery of the fool, and it is unusually vivid: his insides are a broken vessel that will not hold knowledge, instruction sits on him like handcuffs while it hangs on a wise man like gold, he laughs at full volume where a clever man barely smiles, and his foot is inside somebody else's house before anyone invited him. The best line closes it: the heart of fools is in their mouth.
SIR 22:1-27	The chapter opens by comparing a lazy man first to a defiled stone and then to something off a dunghill, which suggests the author had somebody specific in mind. Teaching a fool is described as gluing a potsherd back together, or waking a man from deep sleep who then asks what it was. Then the line that gets quoted: mourn the dead for seven days and a fool for his whole life, because at least the dead are resting. Sand, salt and a lump of iron are easier to carry. The second half turns to friendship and is oddly forgiving, since even a drawn sword can be recovered from, though arrogance, betraying a secret and a treacherous blow cannot. He finishes by asking for a guard on his own mouth.
SIR 23:1-28	A prayer opens it, and what he asks not to be given is precise: a proud look, greed, a shameless mind. Then the discipline of the mouth, and the target is casual swearing, on the grounds that a man who names God constantly is like a servant who is beaten constantly, always carrying a bruise. The rest concerns adultery, and the best passage is the internal monologue he writes for the adulterer: the walls hide me, it is dark, who sees me, why would I be afraid. The answer given is one of the strongest lines in the book, that God's eyes are ten thousand times brighter than the sun and look into secret places. The parallel case of an unfaithful wife is handled far more harshly, including consequences for her children.
SIR 24:1-34	The centrepiece of the book, and Wisdom delivers it herself. She came out of the mouth of the Most High, covered the earth like mist, kept her throne in the pillar of cloud, walked the circuit of heaven and the floor of the abyss, and then went looking for an address. The Creator assigned her one: Jacob, Israel, Zion. The self-description that follows is entirely botanical and aromatic, cedar, cypress, palm, roses, cinnamon, myrrh, frankincense, and it is the loveliest writing in Sirach. Then the paradox: those who eat her will be hungry again, those who drink her will be thirsty again. And then the identification that the whole book rests on, since all of this turns out to be the law of Moses.
SIR 25:1-26	It starts warmly, with three beautiful things, relatives who get along, neighbours who are friends, and a couple who actually agree, then three men he cannot stand: the arrogant poor man, the lying rich man, and the elderly adulterer with no sense. There is real praise for old age, and a happiness list of nine items plus a tenth, ending with the fear of the Lord above all. And then the second half arrives, which is the most misogynistic passage in the book: he would rather live with a lion than a wicked wife, all malice is small next to a woman's, sin and death both started with a woman, and she should be allowed no freedom of speech and cut off. There is no way to make that funny, and no reason to try.
SIR 26:1-29	The chapter opens generously, with a good wife doubling her husband's days, and produces two of the loveliest images in the book, her beauty ordering a house like sunrise and a face in ripe age like the lamp on the holy candlestick. In between sits the author's own list of terrors, which is worth noting because it is entirely social: slander of a city, a hostile assembly, a false accusation, each rated worse than death. There is also another stretch of hostile material about jealous and headstrong women, cut from the same cloth as the previous chapter.
