It ends on three things that anger him, and the first two are genuinely moving: a soldier living in poverty, and men of understanding treated as rubbish.
SIR 27:1-30	The commercial observation first, and it is memorable: sin drives itself in between buying and selling the way a nail goes between two stones. Then the assaying imagery Sirach loves, sieve and furnace, applied to conversation, so that you praise nobody before you have heard them reason. The heart of the chapter is about betrayed confidences and it is unusually wounded in tone. Break a secret and the friend is a bird already out of your hand, a gazelle already through the snare, and there is no point pursuing him. Wounds get bound and insults get patched up, but this does not. The closing physics is the standard sort, stones thrown upward landing on the thrower's head and pits swallowing their diggers, and it is still satisfying.
SIR 28:1-26	An argument about grudges, framed as a question the reader cannot answer: you are nourishing wrath against a man made of the same flesh, and you were planning to ask for mercy yourself? Remember your last end and drop it. The fire imagery is exact, since strife burns in proportion to its fuel and in proportion to the wealth of whoever is funding it, and a spark can be blown up or spat out, and both options leave by the same mouth. Then the tongue, and this is the great passage on it anywhere: it has scattered nations, pulled down fortified cities, driven women from their homes, and where a whip marks the skin the tongue breaks bones. More have fallen by it than by the sword. So fit a door and a bar.
SIR 29:1-28	On lending, and the portrait of the borrower is drawn from life: he kisses your hands until the money is in them, then the due date arrives and he discovers the times are hard, and if you ever see half of it back you should count yourself lucky and expect an enemy for free. Which is why, the author notes, people have stopped lending at all. He does not accept that as an out. Help the poor man for the commandment's sake, and treat what you gave away as treasure in storage that fights better than a shield and spear. Then the dignity section, which is the best of it: bread, water, clothing and a roof are the essentials, and a shelter of logs of your own beats good food in a house where you dare not open your mouth.
SIR 30:1-25	The child-rearing half has not aged well and there is no point pretending otherwise, since the recommended tools are stripes, a bowed neck and no liberty at all, on the theory that an unbroken horse turns stubborn. Its stated aim is at least affectionate: a well taught son continues the father, so the father dies and somehow has not died. Then the chapter changes subject entirely and becomes rather wise. Health beats all the gold there is, a poor man with a sound constitution is better off than a rich invalid, and death is preferable to a bitter life. His closing advice would pass in a modern clinic: love your own soul, comfort your heart, put sorrow away, because worry brings old age early.
SIR 31:1-31	Money first, and the observation is medical: worrying about riches eats the flesh and takes the sleep away. His test of a wealthy man is fair and hard to pass, since it is not whether he has money but whether he had the opportunity to sin and declined. Then the etiquette section, which is the most entertaining thing in Sirach. Do not reach wherever your eye goes. Do not shove into the dish. Judge your neighbour's appetite by your own. Stop first, for manners. Moderate eating means sleeping well and waking with your wits, while the insatiable man gets colic. On wine he is admirably balanced, calling it as good as life in measure and asking what life there is without it, and then flatly noting what happens when you have too much.
SIR 32:1-24	A guide to conducting yourself at a party, and it is charming. Made master of the feast? Do not get grand about it, look after the guests and sit down last. Elders may speak, but with actual knowledge, and nobody is to talk over the music, which gets two of the book's better similes, a carbuncle in gold and an emerald in gold. The advice to the young man is the best of it: speak only if you are needed, hesitate even when asked twice, pack many things into few words, and be someone who knows and holds his tongue. Then a brisk close, get up early, go home, do not loiter, do nothing without counsel and do not sit around regretting what is already done.
SIR 33:1-31	A chapter about distinction. Why is one day better than another when all the daylight comes from the same sun? Because God separated them, hallowing some and leaving others ordinary, and the author applies the same logic to people, who are clay in the potter's hand. The design principle is stated cleanly: the works of the Most High come two by two, each against its opposite. The fool gets a good image, a heart like a cartwheel and thoughts like a rolling axle. Then hard-headed advice for old age, essentially about not signing anything over early, since it is better that your children ask you than that you have to ask them. On servants he is harsh and then, unexpectedly, insists on treating one as yourself.
SIR 34:1-26	The dream chapter, and the debunking is brisk: setting your mind on a dream is grabbing at a shadow and chasing wind, and divinations are worth nothing unless they came from somewhere else entirely. What he offers instead is experience, and he cites his own, including several occasions of being nearly killed while travelling, which does more for his authority than any argument. The final third turns severe and is the best of it. A sacrifice bought with stolen money is a mockery. Bringing an offering out of the goods of the poor is like killing a son in front of his father. Withholding a labourer's wages is shedding blood. And fasting for a sin you fully intend to repeat is washing after touching a corpse and then touching it again.
SIR 35:1-20	The argument here is that ordinary conduct counts as liturgy: keeping the law multiplies offerings, returning a favour is fine flour, almsgiving is a thank offering, and simply stopping doing wrong works as atonement. He still wants the actual offering, cheerfully given, on the promise of sevenfold repayment. Then the part that matters. Do not imagine the gift buys anything, because the judge takes no bribes and will not side with anyone against a poor man. The widow's tears are described as a cry against whoever caused them, which is a legal formulation, not a sentimental one. And the prayer of the humble pierces the clouds and refuses to leave until the sceptres of the unrighteous have been broken.
SIR 36:1-26	The chapter opens on a completely different register, as a national prayer: send your fear on the nations, show new signs, crush the heads of the rulers who say there is none but us, gather the tribes and have compassion on Jerusalem. It is the closest Sirach comes to a psalm. Then, without transition, we are back to domestic advice. The belly will eat anything, but one meat is better than another, and an understanding heart tastes false speech the way a mouth tastes game, which is the sharpest thing in the chapter. Marriage gets described as property and shelter, a pillar of rest and a hedge round a possession, and the man without one is a robber skipping from city to city, trusted by nobody.
SIR 37:1-31	On advice, and it is the most cynical and useful thing in the book. Everyone claims to be your friend. Some are friends in name, one enjoys your good times and disappears in the bad, another works with you strictly for the meals. Then the counsellors, and the warning is precise: every counsellor recommends taking counsel, so establish his interest first, because he may advise in his own favour and then stand next to you to watch how it turns out. The list of people never to consult is the best part, since it includes a coward about war, a merchant about exchange rates and a sluggard about work. What he recommends instead is a godly man and your own heart, which reports better than seven watchmen on a tower.
SIR 38:1-34	It begins by defending doctors, which for an ancient religious text is refreshing: God made the physician, God made medicines out of the earth, so pray, put your life right, make the offering, and then get out of the doctor's way. The mourning advice is startlingly practical, since he prescribes real weeping and a proper burial, and then a limit of a day or two so the neighbours cannot complain, after which grief does the dead no good and the mourner harm. The last third is a magnificent portrait of skilled labour, the smith wrestling iron in the furnace heat, the engraver preserving a likeness, the potter turning the wheel with his feet, and then a snobbish conclusion that these men will not be found in council, only holding the world together.
SIR 39:1-35	The scribe's job description, and it is unmistakably a self-portrait: study the ancients, work through the dark sayings, serve among great men, travel abroad, get up early to pray, and be rewarded with a name that outlasts the world. The author then admits he is filled like the moon at the full, which is the least modest sentence here, and turns to praise. His theological position is uncompromising: nobody may ask what this is or why that is, because everything has a use and a season. The necessities list is lovely and concrete, water, fire, iron, salt, flour, honey, milk, wine, oil and clothing. And the same logic covers the unpleasant inventory, winds and hail and famine, all of it created and waiting for orders.
SIR 40:1-30	The opening is the bleakest passage in Sirach and the best written: a heavy yoke on everyone from the womb to burial in the earth, which he calls the mother of all things, and the fear of death reaching the man on the throne and the man in the hemp shirt alike. Even sleep offers nothing, since he wakes from it like a man who has just got out of the front line and is astonished the fear was about nothing. Then the mood lifts into a game of comparisons, each good thing topped by a better: a blameless wife over children and a city, the love of wisdom over wine and music, counsel over gold and silver, and the fear of the Lord over everything. It ends coldly on begging.
SIR 41:1-24	The opening is the best thing in the chapter, because it refuses to pick a side: death is bitter to a comfortable man with an appetite and welcome to a man who is old, failing and out of patience. Both are true and he says both. He then observes that there is no inquisition of life in the grave, which settles the arithmetic of ten years or a thousand. A good name outlasts a thousand treasures of gold. The rest is an inventory of shame, sorted by who is watching, and the mundane entries are the memorable ones, since alongside lying to a prince and stealing from your hosts he lists leaning your elbow on the table and saying nothing when somebody greets you.
SIR 42:1-25	The matching list of things not to be ashamed of, and it is unexpectedly commercial: the law, the covenant, exact weights, settling accounts, and putting everything in writing by number and weight, which is the most practical verse in the book. Then a father's insomnia over a daughter, worried at every stage of her life in turn, and another run of hostile remarks about women of the sort this book keeps producing. The second half changes register entirely into a hymn on God's knowledge, and the closing idea is genuinely elegant: everything is made double, each thing against its opposite, nothing left imperfect, and each half establishing the good of the other.
SIR 43:1-33	A hymn to the weather, and it is Sirach at the top of his form. The sun is a marvellous instrument that dries out the country at noon and burns three times hotter than a man working a furnace. The moon gives the month its name and marks the feasts. The stars stand in order and never faint on watch. Then a whole meteorology: the rainbow drawn as a circle of glory by the hands of the Most High, snow sprinkled down like birds flying, hail broken small, hoar frost poured out like salt and freezing into thorn points, and ice buckling a breastplate onto the water. Sailors report the sea and its monsters. And the honest conclusion: say as much as you like, the sum of it is that he is all.
SIR 44:1-23	The famous roll of honour begins, and its most striking verse is the one about the men who did not make the list, who have no memorial at all and are as though they had never been born. He includes not only rulers and prophets but composers of tunes and writers of verse, and rich men who simply lived peaceably, which is an unusually generous set of categories. Then the parade starts. Enoch pleased the Lord and left. Noah was found righteous and, in a fine phrase, taken in exchange for the world. Abraham was proved and found faithful, and got the oath about dust and stars and inheritance from sea to sea. Isaac and then Jacob receive it in turn, and the twelve portions are divided.
SIR 45:1-26	Moses first, beloved of God and men, who stopped the wonders with a word, went into the thick darkness and took his commandments face to face, all covered in five verses. Then Aaron, who gets three times the space, and almost all of it is clothing. Linen breeches, long robe, ephod, golden pomegranates, bells arranged to make a sound as he walked so the temple would hear him coming, embroidery in gold and blue and purple, the Urim and Thummim, twelve engraved stones, and a gold plate on the mitre reading HOLINESS. The revolt of Dathan, Abiram and Korah is dealt with in two verses of fire. Aaron gets the firstfruits and no land at all, because God is the portion. Phinehas takes third place, and David is mentioned.
SIR 46:1-20	Joshua gets the highlights package, with the sun going back so that one day became two, and hailstones landing on the enemy at exactly the right moment. The genuinely striking arithmetic is that of six hundred thousand men on foot, two came into the land, and the two were the ones who stood up against the murmuring. Caleb keeps his strength into old age, which the author clearly considers proof of the argument. The judges get a collective blessing conditional on their hearts, phrased carefully. Then Samuel, and the detail chosen for him is not a battle but an audit: before he died he stated publicly that he had never taken so much as a shoe from anybody, and nobody contradicted him.
SIR 47:1-25	David gets the fat separated from the peace offering, which is a compliment in this register, plus the lions and bears handled like livestock, the sling stone and Goliath's boasting, and then, tellingly for a scribe, credit for organising the temple singers and putting the festival calendar in order. Solomon is praised at length in the second person, the river of understanding, the dark parables, the gold gathered like tin, the countries marvelling, and then the sentence turns: you bowed your loins to women, you blemished your honour, and I was grieved for your folly. The division of the kingdom follows immediately as consequence, with Rehoboam and Jeroboam named as the men who finished the job.
SIR 48:1-25	Elijah arrives as fire and the account is nothing but momentum: a famine, the sky shut, fire three times, a dead man raised, kings brought down, prophets appointed, and then the tempest of fire and the chariot of fiery horses. The best line is his job description, to turn the heart of the father to the son. Elisha inherits the spirit, refuses to be intimidated by any ruler, and gets the extraordinary note that his corpse prophesied. And then, after all that, the flat verdict: none of it worked, the people did not repent, and they were carried off. Hezekiah closes the chapter with an engineering credit, cutting sheer rock with iron for water, and with Isaiah delivering the city while Sennacherib boasts.
SIR 49:1-16	Josiah gets the loveliest compliment in the book, a memory like compounded incense, sweet as honey and like music at a banquet of wine. Then the accounting on the monarchy is brutal and brief: except for David, Hezekiah and Josiah, all of them trespassed, and the kings of Judah failed. Jeremiah is mentioned as the one they treated badly, sanctified in the womb for a job description of four verbs, two of them destructive. Ezekiel gets the chariot of the cherubim in a single line. The twelve prophets are handled as a group. Then the rebuilders, Zerubbabel like a signet ring, Jesus son of Josedek, and Nehemiah credited with the walls and gates. It ends by reaching all the way back past everybody to Adam.
SIR 50:1-29	The book's climax is not a battle or a teaching but a liturgy. Simon son of Onias comes out of the sanctuary and the author simply piles up images for a full page: the morning star inside a cloud, the full moon, sun on the temple, a rainbow, roses, lilies at a waterspring, frankincense in summer, a gold vessel set with stones, a budding olive, a cypress in the clouds. Then the ritual itself, the robe, the kindred standing round him like palm trees round a young cedar, the wine poured at the altar's foot, the beaten trumpets, and everyone face down on the ground. After that the author finally signs his name, and adds a sour aside about three neighbouring peoples.
SIR 51:1-30	A closing thanksgiving, and the emergency it thanks God for is not military but reputational: a slanderous tongue, forged lies, and slander carried to the king, with nobody willing to help. That is the danger a court scribe actually faces, and it explains a good deal of the preceding fifty chapters. Then his own account of getting wisdom, which is more affectionate than anything else in the book, since he asked for her at the temple as a boy and tracked her from his youth, and the reward he names is a tongue. The last section is a recruitment pitch: come and lodge in the house of instruction, get her without money, put your neck under the yoke, and note that he worked only a little and found a great deal of rest.
SNG 1:1-17	The woman speaks first, which is worth noticing in a book this old, and she does not ease into it: kiss me, your love is better than wine. There is a chorus of friends agreeing with her, which gives the whole thing the shape of a musical. Her defence to the staring daughters of Jerusalem is practical rather than apologetic, since the sun did this while she was working the vineyards her brothers assigned her, and she notes drily that she has not kept her own. His opening compliment is that she reminds him of a horse in Pharaoh's chariots, which was flattery at the time. She prefers myrrh and henna as images. Their bed is outdoors and their roof beams are trees.
SNG 2:1-17	The two of them trade images and neither is modest about it. She is a lily; he is the one apple tree in a whole wood of ordinary timber, and she sits in its shade on purpose. The line about being faint with love and needing raisins is either romantic or a request for a snack, and the poem is happy to leave that open. Twice she turns to the audience and tells them not to stir up love before it wants to be stirred, which is the closest this book comes to caution. Then his invitation, and it is the best writing here: the winter is over, the rain has stopped, the flowers are out, the turtledove is audible, the figs are coming. Come out.
SNG 3:1-11	A night scene that reads like a dream and probably is one. She is looking for him from her bed, then in the streets, then in the squares, repeating that she sought him and did not find him. The watchmen turn up and she asks them, which is either brave or the logic of dreams. She finds him immediately afterwards and takes hold of him with no intention of letting go. Then the poem cuts abruptly to a parade: smoke, myrrh, frankincense, imported spices, and Solomon's own carriage in Lebanon cedar with silver pillars and a purple seat, guarded by sixty armed men because of fear in the night. The daughters of Zion are invited out to look at the crown.
SNG 4:1-16	The long compliment, and it is a genuinely different aesthetic. Doves for eyes is fine. Hair like a flock of goats coming down a mountain is about movement, teeth like a freshly washed flock is about completeness, and a neck like David's armoury tower hung with a thousand shields is about bearing rather than size. Then the register changes and the poem becomes a garden: a locked garden, a sealed spring, pomegranates, spikenard, saffron, cinnamon, myrrh, and living water running down from Lebanon. Her lips drip honeycomb. He invites her down out of the leopard mountains. Her reply is one line and it settles the matter: wake up, north wind, come south wind, blow the fragrance out of my garden.
SNG 5:1-16	The chapter opens with the garden visit accomplished and the friends encouraging everyone to drink abundantly, and then the mood turns. She is asleep with her heart awake when he knocks, hair damp with night dew. Her hesitation is entirely domestic, since she is undressed and her feet are washed, and by the time she gets to the door with myrrh on her hands he is gone. Calling gets no answer. The watchmen, who were merely inconvenient in chapter three, beat her and take her cloak, which the poem states and does not explain. Her answer to the daughters asking what is so special about him is a head-to-foot inventory in precious materials, ending on the line that matters most: this is my beloved, and this is my friend.
SNG 6:1-13	The daughters volunteer to join the search, and she deflects by saying he is off in his garden gathering lilies, then restates the thesis: I am his and he is mine. His praise escalates in an unusual direction, since alongside Tirzah and Jerusalem she is awesome as an army with banners, and he asks her to look away because her eyes are winning. The goats and the washed flock come back, which suggests these were set pieces. Then the arithmetic: sixty queens, eighty concubines, uncountable virgins, and his one dove who is her mother's only daughter, praised by the queens themselves. It closes on people calling her back so they can keep looking at her, and someone asking why.
SNG 7:1-13	The third and most enthusiastic inventory, and this one starts at the sandals and travels upward, which is the reverse of chapter four. Thighs like jewellery from a good workshop, a waist like a heap of wheat with lilies round it, an ivory tower for a neck, eyes like the pools of Heshbon, and, memorably, a nose like the tower of Lebanon facing Damascus, which is a compliment nobody would attempt now. He announces his intention to climb the palm tree. Her breath is apples and her mouth is good wine. Then she takes over the planning, and it is all logistics: out to the fields, overnight in the villages, up early to check the vines, and the fruit she has been keeping.
SNG 8:1-14	She opens with a workaround for public affection, wishing he were her brother so a kiss in the street would raise no eyebrows, and the charge to the daughters comes round a final time. Then the verse the whole book has been building towards, and it does not sound like love poetry so much as a verdict: set me as a seal on your heart, love is strong as death, jealousy is cruel as the grave, and its flashes are a flame of Yahweh. Floods cannot put it out, and anyone offering his entire estate for it would be laughed at. Her brothers hold a short committee meeting about their little sister; she informs them she is a wall. Last line, she sends him off over the spice mountains.
WIS 1:1-16	The book opens by addressing the judges of the earth, which tells you immediately who it thinks its readers are and what it thinks of them. Wisdom, it explains, simply will not move into a soul that is planning something, or a body held as collateral by sin. The surveillance imagery is heavy and specific: God is a true overseer of the heart and a hearer of the tongue, the spirit of the Lord fills the world, and there is an ear of jealousy that catches every murmur. So watch the backbiting. Then the line that makes the whole book worth reading: God did not make death and takes no pleasure in the living perishing. People went and befriended it themselves, and signed a covenant.
WIS 2:1-24	The author hands the microphone to the ungodly and lets them argue their case, which is the boldest move in the book, because they argue it well. Life is short, nobody comes back, we were born by accident, the breath in our nostrils is smoke and reason a spark that goes out. Therefore: wine, perfume, rosebuds crowned before they wilt, revelry everywhere. It is nearly persuasive right up to the point where the same logic turns into oppress the poor, spare no widow, respect no grey hair, and let our strength be the law. Their final proposal is to torture the righteous man to see whether God turns up. The author's verdict is short: they were blinded, and death got in through envy.
WIS 3:1-19	The rebuttal, and its central move is a change of vantage point. The souls of the righteous are in God's hand, so what looked to observers like death and ruin was in fact peace. The suffering is reclassified as assay work, gold in the furnace, and the reward is startlingly energetic: they will shine, and run about like sparks in stubble, and judge nations. Then two blessings that would have offended everybody, since a childless woman and a eunuch, both social write-offs in that world, are promised fruit and a place in the sanctuary better than wife or children. The chapter is less kind about the children of adulterers, who are dismissed at some length and with evident satisfaction.
WIS 4:1-20	An argument against measuring a life by its length or its descendants. Numerous ungodly offspring are compared to shoots with no root, which flourish for a season and then get taken out by wind, fruit still unripe. Against that, the famous formulation: honourable old age is not a matter of years, understanding is grey hair, and an unspotted life is ripe old age. Then the delicate part, which is the explanation for a good person dying young. He was pleasing to God and got taken out early, before wickedness could work on his understanding, made perfect quickly and therefore already finished. The onlookers see it, despise it, misunderstand entirely what was purposed, and are told God will laugh at them.
WIS 5:1-23	The courtroom scene, and the author clearly enjoys writing the losing side's statement. The righteous man stands there with great boldness while the people who mocked him work out what has happened. Their regret speech is genuinely good: this is the one we made a byword, we called his life madness, we went astray, the sun never rose for us. Then the images of their own vanishing, which are the best writing in the book: a ship leaving no track in the water, a bird leaving nothing in the air, an arrow whose path closes behind it. And then God gets dressed for war, jealousy as armour, judgment as a helmet, wrath sharpened into a sword, lightning aimed from clouds like a drawn bow.
WIS 6:1-25	The lecture to management continues, and its central point is one no ruler enjoys hearing: your authority was issued to you, the issuer keeps records, and the audit is proportional to the office. A man of low estate may be pardoned in mercy, while mighty men will be searched out mightily, which is stated as a principle rather than a threat. Then, having frightened them, the author changes to a courtship register. Wisdom is radiant, does not fade, and does the seeking herself, so that the man who gets up early to look finds her already sitting at his gate. The chain from desire of discipline through to nearness to God is tidy enough to be suspicious, but the closing line is unarguable: an understanding king is tranquillity for his people.
WIS 7:1-30	The speaker, generally taken to be Solomon, opens by insisting on how ordinary he is, which given the surrounding material is a real gear change. Ten months in a womb, born onto the same earth as everybody, first sound the same wail as everybody, because no king ever had a different beginning. Then he prayed and got wisdom, and preferred her to thrones, with gold rated as sand and silver as clay in her presence. The syllabus he claims is remarkable: the constitution of the world, the working of the elements, solstices, seasons, star positions, the ragings of wild beasts, the violences of winds, the thoughts of men, the virtues of roots. And the description of her is the high point, a breath of God's power and an unspotted mirror of his working.
WIS 8:1-21	A chapter of open courtship, and the author is not embarrassed about it: he sought her from youth, wanted her as his bride, and was enamoured of her beauty. The argument then becomes a sales presentation with headings. Want riches? She works all things. Want understanding? She is the architect of everything that exists. Want virtue? Her produce is soberness, righteousness and courage. Want experience? She knows the past and reads the future. The projected benefits get frankly ambitious, since he anticipates crowds, respect from elders despite his youth, silence when he pauses and hands over mouths if he keeps talking. The chapter's last move is the honest one: none of it is available unless God hands her over, so he prayed.
WIS 9:1-18	The prayer, and it is the most modest thing in the book. He asks for wisdom, the one seated beside God's own throne, and grounds the request in his limitations rather than his office: a servant, the son of a handmaid, weak, short-lived and of small power to understand judgment. Even a man perfect among men amounts to nothing without her. He mentions his commission to build the sanctuary, described as a copy of a tabernacle prepared from the beginning. Then the sentence the whole tradition kept: a corruptible body weighs down the soul, and the earthy frame lies heavy on a mind full of cares. We barely work out what is on the ground; nobody has traced the heavens.
WIS 10:1-21	A tour of Genesis and Exodus in which Wisdom does everything and nobody gets named, which is either elegant or a quiz. She guarded the first man and got him out of his own transgression. A man who left her in anger died in the rage that killed his brother. She steered a righteous man through the flood on a poor piece of wood, which is a startlingly dismissive way to describe the ark. She got one man out of the fire on Pentapolis, where a smoking waste, fruit that never ripens and a pillar of salt are still on display. She went down into the dungeon with the man who was sold and stayed until he had a sceptre. And she took the whole people across the Red Sea.
WIS 11:1-26	The author starts working a single idea very hard: the same thing that punished Egypt supplied Israel. The Egyptians got clotted blood where a river should be, which is filed as a rebuke for the decree against infants, and the Israelites got water out of flint. Since the Egyptians worshipped reptiles and vermin, they were sent an assortment of irrational creatures, on the principle that a man is punished by whatever he sinned with. There is then a wonderful digression on restraint: God could have sent bears, lions, or newly invented beasts breathing fire and flashing sparks from their eyes, and chose not to, because he arranges everything by measure and number and weight. The chapter ends surprisingly gently, with a God who overlooks sins so people can repent.
WIS 12:1-27	An extended argument about why judgment is slow, and it is more interesting than it needs to be. The old inhabitants of the land are described in the worst possible terms, enchantments, child sacrifice, cannibal banquets, and then the author insists God still spared them as men, sending hornets ahead of the army so they would go gradually and have somewhere to repent. He simultaneously maintains that their wickedness was inborn and unchangeable, which is a difficulty he does not resolve. The best line is about power: being sovereign over his own strength, God judges in gentleness. And the moral drawn is unexpectedly practical, since the lesson taught to Israel by all this is that the righteous must be a lover of people.
WIS 13:1-19	The anti-idol argument, and it opens with genuine generosity. People who mistook fire, wind, stars and water for the gods that run the world were at least looking at something real, and if they were overwhelmed by beauty they should have followed it back to whoever authored beauty. Their blame, he says, is small, since they were searching. The patience runs out with manufactured gods, and the description is merciless in its detail: the woodcutter makes a useful bowl from good timber, cooks his dinner over the offcuts, and then takes the last crooked knotty scrap, carves it in his spare time, paints it red, and bolts it to the wall so it will not fall over. Then prays to it about his marriage.
WIS 14:1-31	The wood argument continues, with a nice contrast. A man boarding a ship prays to a plank more rotten than the vessel under him, when it was providence that put a path through the sea in the first place, and a raft once carried the entire future of the species. Blessed is wood that carries righteousness; cursed is the wood somebody named a god. Then the most interesting passage, a genealogy of idolatry that is almost sociological: a grieving father makes an image of his dead child and starts honouring it, princes make statues compulsory, absent kings get flattered by portraits, and ambitious artists improve the likeness until people are worshipping a man. The vice list that follows is long, and the closing verdict is that Justice, not the idol, does the visiting.
WIS 15:1-19	A breather addressed to God, gracious and longsuffering, before the author returns to the workshop. This time it is the potter, and the observation is sharp: out of one batch of clay he makes vessels for clean uses and vessels for the opposite, and decides himself which is which, and then out of the same clay makes a god. The man doing this is earth, will shortly be earth again, and has to hand the borrowed soul back. What the author finds unbearable is not the theology but the professional pride, since the potter is competing with goldsmiths and silversmiths and considers counterfeiting a glory. His heart is ashes. And the idols themselves have no eyes, nostrils, ears, fingers, or usable feet.
WIS 16:1-29	The parallel gets worked past the point of subtlety and then produces something genuinely good. Egypt gets vermin, Israel gets quails, and the enemies are so revolted by what has been sent among them that they lose the will to eat at all. When Israel is bitten by serpents the wrath is brief, and the cure is explicitly not medical: not herb, not plaster, but a word. Then the physics goes strange in the author's favourite way, with fire burning harder inside water because creation has taken sides. The manna is the best detail: bread that tasted like whatever the eater happened to want, which survived fire and then melted under a weak sunbeam, from which he draws a lesson about getting up early to say thank you.
WIS 17:1-21	The plague of darkness, retold as a study in fear, and it is the most psychologically acute chapter in the book. The Egyptians who thought they held a holy nation captive become prisoners themselves, shut indoors and cut off from each other by a curtain of forgetfulness. Sounds rush down, phantoms arrive with unsmiling faces, no fire and no star will light the place, and the only illumination is a self-kindled glimmer that frightens them worse than the dark. The magicians who sold terror-removal services are themselves sick with a ludicrous fearfulness. Then the diagnosis, which is unimprovable: wickedness condemned by a witness within is a coward thing, and fear is nothing but the surrender of the help reason offers.
WIS 18:1-25	Light for one side, darkness for the other, in the same air. Egypt hears Israelite voices without seeing anybody and concludes those people are fortunate. The reciprocity is stated plainly: they planned to kill infants, one infant was preserved to convict them, and now their own children are gone. The Passover night is staged as a piece of music, Israel singing sacred songs in secret while the enemy's lamentation comes back in discord, and every rank from servant to king reduced to the same funeral with too many corpses to bury. Then the image the chapter is famous for: peaceful silence over everything, night at mid course, and God's word leaping off the throne as an armed warrior touching heaven and treading earth.
WIS 19:1-22	The last chapter tidies up the argument. God knew Egypt would change its mind, and it does, chasing after the people it had begged to leave, while still standing at the graves. Creation is then described as refashioned for the occasion: the cloud, dry land coming up out of the Red Sea, a grassy plain where the surge had been, and Israel crossing while roaming like horses and skipping like lambs, which is a livelier picture than the Exodus itself gives. He recalls lice instead of cattle and frogs instead of fish. The comparison with Sodom is pointed, since Sodom refused strangers while Egypt enslaved its own benefactors, and both went blind at the door. The elements, he says, changed order like notes on a lute.
1KI 19:1-21	One threatening message from Jezebel undoes the man who beat four hundred and fifty prophets the day before, which is what exhaustion does. He walks into the desert and asks to die, and God's response is not a rebuke but food and sleep, twice, with the observation that the journey is too much for him. Then Horeb, and the famous sequence in which wind, earthquake and fire all fail to contain God and a thin quiet voice does. What is striking is that Elijah answers the question identically before and after, word for word: I alone am left. He is not argued out of it. He is given work, three names to anoint, and one correction, that there are seven thousand others he did not know about. Then a young man ploughing burns his own plough and follows him.
1KI 20:1-23	Ben Hadad's opening demand is everything Ahab owns, and Ahab agrees to it immediately, which is the most revealing thing about him. It is only when the terms are raised to a house-by-house search that the elders talk him out of it. The exchange of insults is excellent, a boast about not having enough dust to go round answered by the best line Ahab ever speaks, that a man putting his armour on should not brag like a man taking it off. Then an unnamed prophet promises victory by the least likely instrument available, the provincial officials' young men, two hundred and thirty-two of them. It works while the enemy command is drunk in the tents. And Syria's postmortem is theology: their god does hills, so fight them lower down. Next time on 1 Kings: the plain.
ACT 9:1-21	Saul is introduced still breathing threats, on his way to Damascus with paperwork authorising arrests. Then the light, the fall, and a question rather than an accusation: why are you persecuting me. His escort hears the sound and sees nothing, which is Luke's way of saying it was not private. The best part is Ananias, who receives a clear divine instruction and argues with it at length, citing the man's arrest warrants, and is told to go anyway. He opens with the word brother. Next time on Acts: he leaves the city over the wall, in a basket.
ACT 9:22-43	The first consequence of the conversion is that Saul has to be smuggled out of a city in a basket, which sets the tone for the rest of his career. In Jerusalem nobody believes he is a disciple, reasonably, and Barnabas has to vouch for him, and then he argues with the Hellenists until he has to be shipped to Tarsus for his own safety. Luke notes the churches enjoyed peace immediately afterwards, and lets that sit. Then two Peter episodes, both brief. A man bedridden eight years is told to make his own bed. And in Joppa, the widows produce the clothes Tabitha made for them, which is the most affecting piece of evidence in the book, before Peter clears the room.
ACT 10:1-25	Two visions arranged to meet in the middle. Cornelius, a Roman officer with a good reputation, gets an angel, a name and an address. Peter, hungry on a roof, gets a sheet full of everything he has spent his life not eating and an instruction to help himself. He refuses, and Luke lets the argument happen three times before the sheet goes back up, which suggests it was not a quick sell. The knock at the gate lands while he is still working out what it meant, and the Spirit supplies the interpretation by telling him to go. Note how much of the chapter is travel and lodging arrangements; Luke is unhurried about it because the destination is the point. Next time on Acts: the sermon.
ACT 10:26-48	Cornelius greets him by falling at his feet, and is told to get up. Peter's opening line acknowledges out loud that he is not supposed to be in the house, which is the honest way to start. His summary of the whole story runs about six sentences and includes the detail that the risen Jesus was shown to chosen witnesses rather than to everybody, which is a strange thing to say to a room you are trying to convince. He does not get to the end of it. The Spirit arrives mid-sermon, the Jewish believers present are astonished, and Peter's question, whether anyone can refuse water to people who have already received the Spirit, is not really a question.
JER 51:1-31	Babylon's oracle is enormous and the best line in it is a piece of medical sarcasm: wail for her, fetch balm for her pain, perhaps she may be healed, immediately followed by we would have healed her and she is not healed. She was a golden cup that made the whole earth drunk, which explains the nations going mad. Then, mid-chapter, a hymn about the God who brings wind out of storehouses turns up unaltered from chapter ten, because the argument against idols has not changed. And Babylon gets called God's battle axe, with a list of everything she was used to break, including farmers and their yokes, before being handed the bill for all of it.
JER 51:32-64	Zion is finally given a voice and uses it: he swallowed me like a monster, filled his mouth with my delicacies, and threw the empty dish away. God's reply is to promise litigation, that he will plead her cause, and then to dry the fountain. The image for Babylon's end is a banquet: he will set the feast, make them drunk, and let them fall asleep permanently. And the book's last act is deliberately theatrical. Jeremiah writes it all out, hands it to a quartermaster travelling to Babylon, and gives instructions to read it aloud there, tie a stone to it and drop it in the Euphrates. The document sinks on purpose.
JER 52:1-17	An appendix, written flat, which is the right choice. Dates and measurements do the work: the tenth day of the tenth month, forts built, the siege running into the eleventh year, and on the ninth day of the fourth month no bread at all. Then the escape at night through the garden gate, the capture on the Jericho plain, and the sentence executed in the correct order so that the sons die while their father can still see. The temple, the palace and every great house burn. And the last people mentioned are the poorest, left behind to keep the vineyards. Next time on Jeremiah: the inventory is taken.
JER 52:18-34	The final chapter of a book of poetry closes with a packing list, and that is the point. Pots, shovels, snuffers, basins, spoons, then the gold and silver counted separately, then Solomon's pillars measured to the finger, hollow, with ninety six pomegranates on the sides and a hundred on the network, and bronze past weighing. Then the executions at Riblah, named by office. Then the deportation arithmetic, three totals adding to four thousand six hundred, which is a small number for the end of a kingdom. And then the strangest ending in the prophets: a king let out of prison after thirty seven years, given clean clothes, a seat above the other kings and dinner every day for the rest of his life.
1KI 20:24-43	Syria's theological fix is administrative: swap the thirty-two kings for professional captains and fight downhill. Israel camps opposite like two little flocks of goats, which is the narrator being precise rather than modest. A man of God announces that the hills-only slander will be answered by a valley. The numbers that follow are enormous and the collapsing wall of Aphek adds twenty-seven thousand of its own. Then Ahab, who has just been handed a victory on a point of doctrine, meets Ben Hadad's men in sackcloth and ropes and calls the man his brother. A prophet in a bandage traps him into sentencing himself, which Ahab only recognises once the disguise comes off. He goes home sullen and angry, a mood he will be in again shortly.
1KI 21:1-29	Ahab wants a vegetable garden and offers a fair price for it. Naboth says no, because the land is not his to sell, and Ahab responds by going to bed and refusing his dinner. Jezebel's contribution is to point out that he is supposedly the king, then to do the thing properly: letters in his name, a public fast, Naboth seated in the place of honour, two hired liars, a stoning. The legal forms are all observed, which is the horror of it. Elijah is waiting in the vineyard with a sentence that matches the crime detail for detail, dogs for dogs. Ahab puts on sackcloth and walks softly, and the verdict is deferred by one generation, which is mercy of a very literal kind.
1KI 22:1-26	Three quiet years, then Ahab wants a border town back, and Jehoshaphat agrees first and asks about God second. Four hundred prophets say yes in one voice, and one of them has made iron horns as a visual aid. Jehoshaphat's question, whether there is a real prophet anywhere, gets the most honest answer Ahab ever gives: there is one, and I hate him, because he never tells me what I want. Micaiah's first answer is a flawless parody of the four hundred, delivered until the king demands the truth and then regrets it. What follows is a scene in heaven where God asks for volunteers to deceive Ahab and a spirit puts its hand up. Micaiah gets slapped and jailed for describing it. Next time on 1 Kings: an arrow with nobody's name on it.
1KI 22:27-53	Micaiah's parting line, that if the king comes back safely then God never spoke through him, is the whole prophetic office staked on one battle. Ahab's counter-plan is a disguise, with Jehoshaphat left conspicuously in royal robes, an arrangement Jehoshaphat agrees to for reasons the text does not explore. Syria's orders are to ignore everyone but the king of Israel, so the decoy nearly works. Then an anonymous archer shoots at nothing in particular and finds the one gap in the armour. Ahab is propped upright all afternoon so the line will hold, and bleeds into the floor of his chariot until sunset. They wash the chariot, and the dogs arrive on schedule. Jehoshaphat's reign gets the standard notice, good but the high places stayed, plus a fleet that sank before it sailed.
2KI 1:1-18	Ahab's son manages to injure himself by falling through his own window lattice, and his first instinct is to send abroad for a medical opinion from Baal Zebub of Ekron. Elijah meets the messengers on the road with the obvious question, whether Israel has run out of gods, and a diagnosis: you are not getting off that bed. The king identifies the prophet from a description of his coat. Then the escalation, two captains and their fifties burned off the hillside for delivering the word come down in the wrong tone, and a third who arrives on his knees and is spared for it. Elijah then walks in and says to the king's face exactly what he had already said to the messengers. Ahaziah dies, sonless, and his brother takes the throne.
2KI 2:1-25	The last day is structured as three refusals. Three times Elijah tells Elisha to stay behind, three times Elisha answers with the same oath, and at every stop a delegation of prophets helpfully announces that his master is being taken today, as if he had not noticed. The Jordan opens for a rolled-up coat. Elisha's request, a double portion, is the inheritance share of a firstborn son, and the condition is simply that he keep watching. He does: fire, horses, whirlwind, and then nothing. The coat comes back down, the river opens a second time, and the succession is settled. Fifty men insist on searching for three days anyway. Then salt into a spring, and, at Bethel, a jeering crowd, a curse, and two bears, an episode the narrator reports without a word of comment.
2KI 3:1-27	Jehoram is graded on a curve: evil, but not like his parents, since he did at least take down the Baal pillar. Moab's tribute is quantified in livestock, a hundred thousand lambs and a hundred thousand rams' worth of wool, which explains why losing it matters. The coalition then marches seven days through desert and runs out of water, at which point Jehoram concludes God has arranged this personally. Jehoshaphat, as usual, is the one who asks for a prophet, and Elisha makes it clear he is only present out of respect for Judah. He calls for a harpist before prophesying. The trick that wins the battle is optical: dawn light on new water, read as blood by an enemy expecting corpses. It ends at a besieged wall with a child burned as an offering, and the army goes home.
2KI 4:1-24	Two women and two problems. The first is a debt about to cost a widow her sons, solved by an inventory question, what do you have in the house, and one pot of oil that keeps pouring exactly as long as there are borrowed jars to fill. The miracle stops when the containers do. The second is the Shunammite, who feeds Elisha out of nothing but courtesy and builds him a room with a bed, table, chair and lamp. Offered influence at court she declines, saying she lives among her own people. Gehazi notices the thing she has not asked for. The son arrives as promised, grows, complains of his head in a field and dies by noon. She puts him on the prophet's bed and orders the donkey driven hard. Next time on 2 Kings: it is well.
2KI 4:25-44	She tells Gehazi everything is well, which is not a lie so much as a refusal to discuss it with the assistant. Then she grabs the prophet's feet and asks the only question that matters: did I ask you for this. Elisha's first move, sending his staff ahead with instructions to talk to nobody, fails completely, and the text lets that stand. What works is unglamorous and physical, a shut door, prayer, and a man lying full length on a child twice until warmth comes back. The child sneezes seven times, which is a strange and perfect detail. Two smaller stories follow in the same register: a poisonous stew fixed with a handful of meal, and twenty loaves that feed a hundred men with leftovers, reported as flatly as a supply note.
2KI 5:1-14	The most important person here is a kidnapped girl with no name, waiting on the wife of the man whose army took her, who mentions in passing that there is a prophet who could help. The remark travels up two royal courts and arrives as a diplomatic incident: a letter, ten talents of silver, six thousand gold pieces, and a king of Israel tearing his clothes because he thinks he is being set up. Elisha's intervention: send him to me. He then declines to come outside at all, delegating a one-line instruction to a messenger. Naaman is furious, having arrived with horses and chariots and a mental image of the ceremony he was owed, and it takes his own servants to point out that he would have accepted a hard task without complaint. Next time on 2 Kings: the invoice.
2KI 5:15-27	Naaman comes back a convert and negotiates the terms of it with unusual honesty: he will worship Yahweh only, he would like two mules' worth of Israelite soil to do it on, and he asks in advance to be excused for bowing in the temple of Rimmon while his king leans on his arm. Elisha gives him the shortest possible answer, go in peace, and takes nothing. Gehazi, who has watched a fortune walk away, invents two hungry young prophets and asks for a talent. Naaman, delighted, insists on two and provides porters. The lie collapses in one exchange, where did you come from, nowhere, and Elisha's answer is that his heart went along and watched the whole thing. The disease Naaman lost is the disease Gehazi acquires, which is the tidiest and cruelest arithmetic in the book.
2KI 6:1-18	A building project, a borrowed axe head in the river, and a miracle scaled to the fact that the man who lost it did not own it. Then the register changes without warning. Syria cannot hold a war council without Israel knowing the outcome, and the king's first theory is a mole, until a servant explains that the leak is a prophet who hears what is said in the bedroom. His solution to a man who knows his every plan is a night raid, which goes as well as expected. The servant wakes to a ring of chariots and asks the reasonable question. Elisha's answer is arithmetic, ours outnumber theirs, followed by a prayer that lets someone else see the sum. Then a second prayer, and the whole army cannot see anything. Next time on 2 Kings: a guided tour.
2KI 6:19-33	Elisha walks a blinded army to the wrong city on purpose, restores their sight in the middle of Samaria, and answers the king's eager question about killing them with a rule about prisoners and an order for dinner. It works: the raids stop. Then the siege, and the tone drops away entirely. The famine is priced out first, a donkey's head at eighty shekels, and then given a voice, when a woman appeals to the king in a dispute over an agreement between two mothers to eat their children. Hers was eaten. The other has hidden her son. The king's answer is to tear his clothes, revealing sackcloth he has been wearing under them all along, then to blame the prophet and send for his head. Elisha bars the door. His last line is a man giving up on waiting.
2KI 7:1-20	Elisha's forecast is a commodity price, flour and barley at a shekel by tomorrow, which after eighty-shekel donkey heads is the least believable thing he has ever said. The officer says as much and receives a precise sentence: you will see it, you will not eat it. The relief itself is achieved by four men with leprosy, sitting at the gate because they are not allowed inside, reasoning their way to the conclusion that surrender is the only move with any upside. They find an abandoned camp, loot two tents, and then stop, on the grounds that this is a day of good news and they are hoarding it. The king assumes a trap. The road is littered with dropped equipment. The prices fall as stated, and the officer is trampled in the gate he was assigned to.
2KI 8:1-29	The Shunammite gets a property case, and the timing is absurd in the best way: she walks in to petition the king at the exact moment Gehazi is telling him the story of her son. The land comes back with seven years of back rent. Then the register darkens. Ben Hadad sends Hazael to ask a prophet for a prognosis, and Elisha answers with a sentence in two halves, tell him he lives, he dies. Then he stares at Hazael until the man is uncomfortable and weeps, because he can already see what this one will do to Israel, listed in detail. Hazael's protest that he is only a dog is not a denial. The wet cloth arrives the next morning. Judah, meanwhile, has married into Ahab's family and is losing Edom.
2KI 9:1-18	The anointing is run like a hit: one young prophet, one vial of oil, a private room, and explicit instructions to open the door and run. He delivers a commission that is really a sentence on a whole dynasty and then does exactly that. The officers outside ask what the lunatic wanted, in the tone soldiers use about prophets, and Jehu tries the modest version before telling them. Cloaks go down on the bare stairs, a trumpet sounds, and the coup exists. Jehu's first act as king is a news blackout, seal the city, nobody rides to Jezreel, which is where the wounded king is convalescing with his nephew visiting. The watchman reports a company approaching. A rider goes out to ask the standard diplomatic question and is absorbed into the column. Next time on 2 Kings: the second rider.
2KI 9:19-37	The watchman's second report is the best line in the chapter: he recognises the man by his driving. Joram, wounded, rides out to meet a coup in person and asks whether it is peace, twice, and the answer names his mother. The place where he is shot and dumped is Naboth's field, which Jehu makes sure his captain notices, because he was there when the sentence was passed. Ahaziah is chased down as an afterthought. Then Jezebel, who hears he is coming and does her eyes and her hair before appearing at the window, greets him with the name of a previous usurper, and is thrown out of it by her own household. Jehu goes inside and eats, and only afterwards remembers she was a king's daughter. Burial detail finds a skull, feet and hands.
2KI 10:1-17	Jehu's letter to Samaria is a dare he knows will be refused: you have chariots, walls, armour and seventy candidates, so pick one and fight me. The guardians read it, do the arithmetic on two dead kings, and surrender in writing. The second letter takes them at their word in the worst way, and seventy heads arrive at Jezreel in baskets. Stacking them at the gate is theatre, and so is the speech, in which Jehu concedes his own conspiracy and then invites the crowd to notice who actually did this. Everything after that is thoroughness: the household, the officials, the friends, the priests, forty-two of Ahaziah's brothers met by chance on the road. Jehonadab is offered a seat in the chariot and an invitation to come and see his zeal. Next time on 2 Kings: the great sacrifice.
2KI 10:18-36	The sting is elegant and entirely without conscience. Ahab served Baal a little, Jehu will serve him much, so bring every priest and worshipper in Israel, attendance compulsory. He fills the temple, hands out ceremonial robes so the congregation is easy to identify, and has the building checked to be sure no worshipper of Yahweh is inside, which is either scruple or crowd control. Eighty men wait outside on pain of their own lives. The offering finishes, and so does the congregation. The temple becomes a latrine, still in use at time of writing. Then the verdict, which is where the book always is: Baal gone, calves at Bethel and Dan untouched, four generations promised, and no obedience beyond the part that involved killing. Hazael starts taking land east of the Jordan.
2KI 11:1-21	The queen mother's response to her son's death is to eliminate every possible heir, which very nearly works. What defeats her is an aunt, a nurse and a bedroom, and then six years of a child living in the temple while she rules a country that does not know he exists. Jehoiada's coup is staged with unusual care: shift rotations, spears out of David's own armoury, orders to kill anyone who breaches the ring. The crowning happens before Athaliah hears anything, and she walks into a completed fact and shouts treason at it, correctly and far too late. Jehoiada's one instruction is that she not be killed inside the temple, so she is walked out to the horses' gate first. The Baal temple comes down the same day and the city is described as quiet. The king is seven.
2KI 12:1-21	A king who does right for exactly as long as his priest is telling him what right is. The interesting part is the accounting. Joash tells the priests to fund temple repairs out of the offerings, and twenty-three years later nothing has been repaired, so the money is taken off them entirely. Jehoiada's solution is a chest with a hole drilled in the lid, emptied and counted by the scribe and the high priest together, paid direct to carpenters and masons. No gold bowls are made from it and no receipts are demanded of the foremen, because they dealt honestly, which the narrator mentions as though it were remarkable. Then Hazael turns toward Jerusalem and the entire restored treasury goes out the door as a bribe. Joash is murdered by his own servants.
2KI 13:1-25	Two kings of Israel in one chapter and the same verdict on both, which by now is the point. Jehoahaz is squeezed by Syria until his army is fifty horsemen, ten chariots and ten thousand men on foot, threshed like dust. He prays, God sends relief, and nothing changes, the calves and the Asherah stay put. Then Elisha's deathbed, where the king weeps over him using Elisha's own words for Elijah. The arrow scene is a test disguised as a ritual: shoot east, that is victory, now strike the ground. He hits it three times, which is not wrong, only unenthusiastic, and the prophet is furious because the number was the answer. Elisha dies. A corpse dropped into his grave in a hurry stands up. Syria is beaten three times exactly, as specified.
2KI 14:1-29	Amaziah does one genuinely notable thing, which is to execute his father's murderers and leave their children alone, on the explicit authority of a text. Then he beats Edom and immediately writes to Israel proposing they look each other in the face. Jehoash's answer is a fable, a thistle in Lebanon asking a cedar for its daughter and getting trodden flat by a passing animal, followed by the advice to enjoy the win at home. Amaziah goes anyway. The result is a captured king, four hundred cubits of Jerusalem's wall pulled down, and the treasury moved to Samaria. He survives fifteen more years and is then assassinated at Lachish. Jeroboam the second reigns forty-one years, is evil by the usual measure, and expands Israel further than anyone since Solomon, as Jonah had said he would.
2KI 15:1-19	Judah gets one king in this stretch and Israel gets four, which tells you where the rot is. Azariah reigns fifty-two years and spends an unspecified part of them in a separate house with leprosy while his son runs the country. Then Israel, at speed: Zechariah lasts six months and is killed in front of everyone, at which point the narrator notes that the promise of four generations to Jehu is now precisely used up. Shallum lasts a month. Menahem takes Samaria, and the notice of his reign includes what he did to a town that would not open its gates, which is not something the formula can absorb. Then Assyria arrives, and the new policy is money: a thousand talents of silver for permission to keep the throne. Next time on 2 Kings: who pays for it.
2KI 15:20-38	The answer is fifty shekels a head from every rich man in Israel, and Assyria goes home, for now. After that the chapter is a list of assassinations with a formula attached to each: Pekahiah two years, killed by his own captain in the palace fortress with fifty Gileadites; Pekah twenty years, during which Tiglath Pileser simply takes the north, Naphtali and Galilee and Gilead, and deports the population; then Pekah is killed by Hoshea, who will be the last. The verdict on every one of them is the same sentence about the sins of Jeroboam, which by now reads less like a judgment than a diagnosis. Judah, in parallel, has Jotham, who is fine, builds a gate, and leaves the high places up.
2KI 16:1-20	Ahaz is the first king of Judah the formula refuses to grade at all, and the detail it gives is his son in the fire. Besieged by Syria and Israel, his solution is to buy an empire: temple silver, palace gold, and a letter to Tiglath Pileser signed your servant and your son. It works, in the sense that Damascus falls and Rezin dies. Then the strange part. Ahaz visits Damascus, likes the altar, sends the plans home by courier, and Urijah has a full-scale copy built and waiting. The old bronze altar is shifted sideways and reserved for the king's private enquiries. The temple furniture is dismantled for its metal, and the royal entrance is rerouted, the text says, because of the king of Assyria.
ACT 11:1-30	Peter gets home and is met not with congratulations but with a complaint about his dining arrangements. His defence is simply to tell the whole story again in order, sheet and all, which Luke reproduces at length rather than summarising, because the point is that the argument was won by narration. The closing line, who was I that I could withstand God, ends the discussion; the objectors go quiet and change position in a single verse. Then the scene shifts to Antioch, where the same thing is happening without anyone asking permission, and Jerusalem sends Barnabas to look into it. He fetches Saul out of Tarsus, where Saul has been parked since chapter nine. The nickname Christian is coined here, and a famine prophecy turns the new church into a relief agency almost immediately.
ACT 12:1-25	James is killed in half a verse and Peter arrested in the next, because it polled well. Herod assigns sixteen soldiers to one fisherman, who spends the night before his execution asleep. The rescue is briskly domestic: get dressed, put your sandals on, take your coat. Peter assumes he is dreaming until the angel leaves him alone in a street. Then the comedy: the prayer meeting held specifically for his release refuses to believe he is at the door, tells the servant Rhoda she is out of her mind, and settles on it being his ghost, while Peter keeps knocking. Herod meanwhile accepts being called a god and is dead by the end of the verse. Luke's next sentence is that the word of God grew.
ACT 13:1-25	The list of teachers at Antioch includes Herod's foster brother, which Luke drops in without comment. Barnabas and Saul are commissioned mid-fast and sail for Cyprus, and the first thing that happens is a magician with the working name Elymas trying to keep a Roman governor away from the faith. Saul, who becomes Paul in the same sentence and stays Paul from here on, blinds him, and the governor believes, apparently as much from the teaching as the spectacle. John Mark quits at Perga, a detail that will cost people a friendship later. Then the first of Paul's set-piece sermons, opening the way these speeches always open: Egypt, wilderness, judges, Saul, David, and only then the point. Next time on Acts: he reaches it.
ACT 13:26-52	The second half turns on a piece of close reading. David was promised his Holy One would not see decay; David demonstrably did decay; therefore the psalm was talking about someone else. It is an argument built entirely out of a text his audience already accepts, which is why it works on some of them and enrages the rest. The Gentiles ask for a repeat performance and the whole city turns up the following Sabbath, which is when the jealousy starts. The turn to the Gentiles is announced as a formal decision with a scripture attached rather than a sulk. Note the mechanics of the resulting persecution: it runs through prominent women and civic leaders, not a mob. The apostles shake the dust off and leave, and Luke ends the chapter noting that the disciples were full of joy.
ACT 14:1-28	Lystra produces the strangest scene in the book. A man lame from birth stands up, and the crowd, speaking Lycaonian so the apostles cannot immediately tell what is being decided, concludes the gods have come down and starts leading oxen to the gate. Barnabas gets to be Jupiter; Paul, being the talker, gets Mercury. The counter-sermon is short and makes no appeal to scripture at all, only rain and harvests and food, because this audience has never heard of Moses. It barely works. The same crowd is talked round by visitors from Antioch and stones Paul within a few verses, which is Luke's quietest comment on public enthusiasm. He gets up and walks back into the town. The return leg retraces every hostile city on purpose, and the summary they give at home is about a door opening.
ACT 15:1-41	The first church council convenes because some visitors from Judea told Antioch that salvation has a surgical prerequisite. Luke calls the resulting exchange no small discord, which is his way of saying it was a shouting match. Peter's argument is that God already settled it by handing out the Spirit without checking anyone's paperwork, and his line about a yoke neither our fathers nor we were able to bear is unusually candid about the law. James supplies a prophecy and a compromise: four rules, none of them the one being demanded. The letter goes out carefully worded to point out that the troublemakers had no authorisation. Then the chapter ends by undercutting itself. Having agreed on the largest question in the book, Paul and Barnabas split up permanently over whether to give John Mark a second chance.
ACT 16:1-18	Paul, fresh from a council that just ruled circumcision unnecessary, circumcises Timothy, and Luke reports this without a flicker. The next stretch is defined entirely by refusals: forbidden to speak in Asia, blocked from Bithynia, herded to the coast until a Macedonian appears in a dream and asks for help. Note the pronoun in verse ten quietly turning into we; whoever is writing has joined the party. Europe begins not with a crusade but with a Sabbath conversation by a river with a group of women, and the first convert is a businesswoman in the purple trade who wins the argument about where they will be staying. Then a fortune-telling slave girl starts advertising them accurately and loudly for days, and Paul exorcises the spirit less from compassion than from irritation. Next time on Acts: somebody notices the revenue.
ACT 16:19-40	The complaint filed against them mentions no fortune-telling and no lost income; it is dressed up as Romans being asked to accept un-Roman customs, which is what a business grievance looks like once it reaches court. They are beaten without trial and put in the stocks, and at midnight they are singing, with the rest of the prison as an audience. The earthquake opens every door and the jailer's first thought is his own execution, which tells you what the job was. His question, what must I do to be saved, is asked by a man who was holding a sword. He ends the night washing the wounds his colleagues inflicted. Told in the morning that he may leave quietly, Paul mentions for the first time that he is a Roman citizen and declines.
ACT 17:1-34	Three cities, three receptions, arranged in ascending order of civility. Thessalonica supplies a riot and the accusation that these men say there is another king, which is the sharpest summary of the charge anyone in the book manages. Beroea gets Luke's only compliment paid to an audience for checking his colleagues' claims against the text daily. Athens is a city where, he notes drily, nobody does anything but exchange novelties, and Paul is dismissed as a babbler before being invited to explain himself anyway. The Areopagus speech quotes no scripture, starts from an altar addressed to a god nobody could name, and borrows two lines of Greek poetry instead. It works until he mentions resurrection, at which point the room politely breaks up. Two converts are named and one of them is a member of the court.
ACT 18:1-28	Corinth is where the pace slows down: eighteen months, a day job making tents, and a landlord who moves house by exactly one door when the synagogue turns hostile. The vision Paul receives is not a commission but reassurance, which suggests he needed it. Gallio's ruling is the most consequential shrug in the book: he decides this is an internal argument about words and names, refuses to hear it, and thereby leaves the movement legally alone. Luke's closing detail is that a crowd then beat Sosthenes in front of the bench and the proconsul could not be bothered to look up. Paul's vow and shaved head go unexplained. Then Apollos arrives, eloquent, accurate and several years out of date, and is quietly taken aside by a couple of tentmakers and brought up to speed.
ACT 19:1-21	Paul opens at Ephesus with a diagnostic question and gets the flattest possible answer: we have not even heard there is a Holy Spirit. Twelve men are rebaptized and the lecture series moves out of the synagogue into a rented hall belonging to a man named Tyrannus, where it runs daily for two years. Then the odd stretch. Cloth carried off Paul's body is healing people, and a family of freelance exorcists decides the name of Jesus is a formula anyone can use. The evil spirit's reply, that it knows Jesus and knows Paul but has no idea who they are, is the best line in the book, and the seven leave the house naked. The result is a bonfire of magic books whose value Luke prices exactly. Next time on Acts: the silversmiths run the same arithmetic.
ACT 19:22-41	A riot organised on economic grounds and conducted in religious language. Demetrius gathers the trade, mentions the profits first and the goddess second, and within a verse the whole city is in the theatre chanting. Luke's note that most of them did not know why they had come together is the driest sentence he ever wrote. Paul wants to address the mob and is physically prevented by his friends, including several provincial officials who apparently liked him. Alexander is put forward, recognised as a Jew, and the chanting resumes for two hours. What ends it is a municipal official explaining that the courts are open, that these men have stolen nothing and insulted nobody, and that a riot with no stated cause will look very bad in the report to Rome.
ACT 20:1-38	The travelogue is thick with names and ports, and the memorable part happens because Paul will not stop talking. A young man called Eutychus goes to sleep in a third-floor window during a sermon that has already reached midnight, falls out, and is picked up dead. Paul restores him, then goes back upstairs and continues until dawn, which is either faith or an inability to take a hint. He then walks to the next port alone. The Miletus speech is his only recorded address to believers, and it is a farewell that says so twice: they will not see his face again, wolves will come, some of them from inside the room. He appeals to his own hands as evidence that he took nothing, and quotes a saying of Jesus found nowhere in the Gospels. Everybody weeps at the ship.
ACT 21:1-22	The voyage south is one long attempt to talk Paul out of it. Disciples at Tyre say through the Spirit that he must not go; the whole congregation, wives and children included, walks him to the beach to kneel in the sand. At Caesarea, Philip's four prophesying daughters are mentioned and then dropped, which is maddening. Agabus makes his point physically, tying himself up with Paul's own belt before saying whose hands it will be. Paul's answer, asking why they are breaking his heart with weeping, is the closest he comes to admitting the cost, and the party gives up with the Lord's will be done. Jerusalem listens to the report warmly, then raises the actual problem, which is not doctrine but a rumour. Next time on Acts: the public relations fix.
ACT 21:23-40	The proposed solution is a photo opportunity: sponsor four men's vows, be seen in the temple paying for them, and the rumour dies. It works exactly until it does not. Some visitors from Asia spot him, and the charge they shout has one specific detail in it, that he brought a Greek into the temple, which Luke tells us they inferred from having seen Trophimus with him elsewhere in the city. That inference nearly kills him. The temple doors are shut behind him, the beating begins, and what saves his life is the Roman garrison arriving because the city is audibly rioting. Bound with two chains and carried up the steps, Paul politely asks the commander for a word. The officer's surprise is that he speaks Greek; he had him filed as an Egyptian insurgent.
ACT 22:1-30	The defence is his conversion told a second time, and the differences from chapter nine are the point: this version is aimed at the crowd holding the rocks. He opens in Hebrew, which buys him silence, establishes his credentials at length, and takes care to describe Ananias as a devout man well spoken of by every Jew in Damascus. The crowd stays with him through the light, the voice and the blindness. The word that ends it is Gentiles, one syllable and the dust is in the air. What follows is bureaucratic comedy of the coldest sort: Paul waits until he is actually tied to the post before mentioning his citizenship, and the officer who paid a fortune for his has to hear that the prisoner was born with it.
ACT 23:1-35	Paul manages one sentence before being punched, calls the high priest a whitewashed wall, and then retracts it on a technicality that is either an apology or a very dry insult. His next move is pure tactics: he identifies the two parties in the room and announces that he is on trial for the resurrection, which sets the Sadducees and Pharisees on each other and gets him carried out by soldiers for his own safety. Forty men then swear off food and water until he is dead, a vow they will regret. The plot is undone by a nephew nobody knew he had. The commander's response is two hundred soldiers, seventy cavalry and two hundred spearmen for one prisoner, and a covering letter that quietly rearranges the timeline to make himself look better.
ACT 24:1-27	Tertullus is a professional and opens with two verses of flattery for a governor history remembers as a brute, then reaches the charges, which are grand and unsupported. Paul's reply is a lawyer's: the timeline is twelve days and checkable, nobody saw him arguing anywhere, and the men who witnessed the alleged offence have not troubled to appear. Felix, who knows more about the Way than he lets on, decides nothing. Instead the case becomes a routine. He brings his wife to hear Paul, and when the subject reaches self-control and coming judgment he gets frightened and ends the meeting, which is the most honest reaction anyone in the book has to a sermon. He keeps sending for Paul anyway, hoping for a bribe, for two years, then leaves him in chains as a parting favour.
ACT 25:1-27	A new governor, the same request, and the same road ambush waiting behind it. Festus keeps the venue in Caesarea, less from principle than from convenience, and then asks the one question that forces Paul's hand, whether he would like to be tried in Jerusalem after all. The appeal to Caesar is two words and irreversible; Festus confers with his council and grants it, and the rest of the book follows from that sentence. What comes next is Festus explaining the case to Agrippa, the most useful summary in the book because it is baffled: the accusers said nothing he expected, the dispute is about their own religion and about one Jesus who died and whom Paul insists is alive. He admits he has nothing to write to the emperor.
ACT 26:1-32	The third telling of the conversion, and the most polished, because the audience is a king and Paul knows it. He flatters Agrippa's expertise, plants the argument in the hope of the twelve tribes rather than in anything new, and asks why resurrection should be thought incredible, which lets him treat his whole case as an internal Jewish question. Only this version includes the line about kicking against the goads, a Greek proverb aimed squarely at a Greek-speaking court. Festus interrupts to shout that he has read himself insane. Paul's reply, that none of this was done in a corner, is the closest the book comes to a thesis. Agrippa's answer is famously hard to translate and sounds like a man declining politely. The verdict off-stage is that he could have walked free, had he not appealed.
ACT 27:1-21	Luke's shipwreck is the most detailed sea narrative to survive from antiquity, and he clearly enjoyed writing it. The chain of decisions is laid out precisely: the harbour is poor for wintering, the prisoner offers an opinion, the centurion prefers the captain and the owner, and the majority votes. A gentle south wind confirms them in the error, which is how these things always go.
