The portrait is of a hundred-year-old man with a barren wife choosing to believe a promise about many nations anyway, and being counted righteous for exactly that, with no work involved.
ROM 5:1-21	Peace with God, and then a chain of consequences: suffering makes perseverance, perseverance character, character hope. Paul notes that people rarely die even for good men, and that Christ died for the ungodly, which is his idea of a comparison. The second half sets Adam against Christ, one act of disobedience against one act of obedience, with death reigning in between. The law, he says almost casually, arrived to make the trespass worse.
ROM 6:1-23	Somebody has evidently asked whether more grace means more licence to sin, and Paul finds the question offensive twice, in almost identical words. His answer is baptism read as a funeral: buried with Christ, therefore dead to sin and no longer its property. Then he switches to the language of ownership, since everyone obeys somebody. He apologises for the crudeness of the slavery metaphor and uses it anyway, ending on wages: sin pays death, God gives life free.
ROM 7:1-25	Paul opens with a marriage-law analogy about a widow being free to remarry, then hurries to clarify that the law itself is not the villain. His case study is coveting: he would not have noticed the impulse if a commandment had not named it. Then the famous spiral, first person and present tense, of a man who wants the good and does the other thing, losing an argument with his own body. He calls himself wretched and thanks God in the same breath.
ROM 8:1-39	The relief after chapter seven arrives in the first sentence: no condemnation. Paul contrasts flesh with Spirit, then adoption with slavery, and has believers calling God Abba rather than master. The middle turns strange and lovely: creation itself groaning like a woman in labour, waiting, and the Spirit praying on our behalf in sounds nobody can put into words. It ends in a courtroom nobody dares prosecute in, and a list of everything that fails to separate us from God's love.
ROM 9:1-33	Paul swears he is not lying about how much this hurts, and offers to be damned himself if it would help his relatives. Then the hardest stretch in the letter: Isaac over Ishmael, Jacob over Esau before either had done anything, Pharaoh raised up specifically to be a demonstration. When the obvious objection arrives, Paul does not soften it. He asks who the clay thinks it is, questioning the potter. The chapter ends with the Gentiles arriving at righteousness they were not chasing.
ROM 10:1-21	Paul concedes his people have genuine enthusiasm and says the problem is what it is attached to. Righteousness by faith, he insists, requires no expedition: nobody has to climb to heaven or dig into the abyss, since the word is already in your mouth. Then the famous cascade of questions, running the chain of preaching backwards to whoever does the sending. It closes on Isaiah's image of God standing all day with his arms out to people who will not look.
ROM 11:1-36	Has God given up on Israel? Paul says no and offers himself as exhibit A, along with Elijah, who thought he was the last believer alive and was off by seven thousand. Then the olive tree: branches snapped off, wild shoots grafted in, and a pointed warning to the wild shoots about getting smug. Pruning shears cut both ways, and God is perfectly capable of reattaching what he removed. Paul gives up analysing and starts praising instead.
ROM 12:1-21	Eleven chapters of argument now cash out as instructions. Present your bodies as a living sacrifice, which is a deliberately awkward image, and stop letting the world set the shape of your thinking. Nobody is to overrate himself, because a body needs unlike parts. Then Paul stops building sentences and starts firing them: bless the people persecuting you, weep with the weeping, skip revenge because it is already assigned to someone better at it, and feed your enemy lunch.
ROM 13:1-14	Paul, writing to Christians living in Nero's capital, tells them to submit to the authorities and pay their taxes, which is either courage or pragmatism and probably both. The only debt he permits is love, which he says quietly absorbs the whole commandment list. Then the tone changes and the deadline arrives: the night is nearly done, so get out of bed, take off the clothes of darkness, and put on Christ instead. No provisioning for the flesh.
ROM 14:1-26	The great theological argument of Romans arrives at its practical crisis, and the crisis is dinner. Some eat anything, some eat only vegetables, some keep special days, and everyone is judging everyone. Paul refuses to rule on the menu. Instead he points out that each of them answers to God rather than to the person across the table. Nothing is unclean in itself, he says, and then tells the confident eaters not to eat if it wrecks someone. A doxology closes it.
ROM 15:1-33	The strong should carry the weak, on the grounds that Christ did not consult his own preferences either. Paul then lines up four quotations about Gentiles praising God, in case anyone missed the pattern. He describes his own policy of only preaching where nobody has preached yet, which is his stated reason for never having visited Rome. Then travel plans: Spain eventually, Rome on the way, Jerusalem first with a cash collection, and a request for prayers that Judea does not kill him.
ROM 16:1-25	A letter that spent fifteen chapters on the fate of humanity ends with Paul working through his address book. Phoebe gets a recommendation, Prisca and Aquila get credited with risking their necks, Junia is called notable among the apostles, and a striking number of the names doing the labour are women. There is one sharp aside about smooth talkers who serve their own stomachs. Tertius, who has been writing all this down by hand, slips in a greeting of his own.
TIT 1:1-16	Titus has been left in Crete with instructions to appoint elders, and the qualification list is much the same as Timothy's, which suggests Paul had one and reused it. The interesting part is who he is up against: talkers who wreck whole households for money, whose mouths, he says, need stopping. Then the line nobody expects in scripture, a Cretan poet quoted to the effect that Cretans are always liars, evil beasts and lazy gluttons, followed by Paul's four-word endorsement. This testimony is true.
TIT 2:1-15	Instructions sorted by demographic, which reads like a seating plan. Older men, older women, young wives, young men, slaves, and Titus himself, who is told to be an example so that his opponents have nothing to work with. The domestic advice is unremarkable for its period; what is not is where Paul anchors it. All of it hangs off grace having appeared, and the training it gives, while everyone waits for the appearing still to come. Then: let nobody look down on you.
TIT 3:1-15	Paul asks for civic obedience and gentleness, and grounds it in a reminder that his readers used to be foolish, gullible and hateful themselves, which is a more effective argument than most. Salvation, he insists, was not earned. Then the practical filter: keep the good works, drop the genealogies and legal squabbling, and after two warnings simply stop arguing with a divisive person. Travel plans close it, including Zenas the lawyer, who is the only lawyer in the New Testament with a good reference.
PSA 146:1-10	Opens with a note to self: soul, praise Yahweh. Then the political advice, that princes are men whose thoughts perish the same day their breath does, so invest elsewhere. The alternative resume is oddly practical for a hymn: justice for the oppressed, bread for the hungry, prisoners released, blind eyes opened, the bowed-down straightened, foreigners and orphans and widows kept. The wicked simply get their road turned upside down.
PSA 147:1-20	Grand and domestic by turns. The God who counts the stars and calls each one by name also arranges grass for the mountains and dinner for young ravens, and is unimpressed by horsepower or a good pair of legs. Winter arrives on a generous metaphor budget: snow like wool, frost like ashes, hail like pebbles, and a rhetorical question about who can stand his cold. Then he sends a word and it all thaws.
PSA 148:1-14	A roll call that starts at the top of the sky and works down, so the choir includes the sun, the moon, the waters above the heavens, great sea creatures, weather of every kind, cedars, cattle and flying things, and only then gets around to kings and judges, who are listed somewhere between the livestock and the teenagers. Everyone is present for the same reason: he commanded, and they existed.
PSA 149:1-9	New song, dancing, tambourine, harp, and saints singing for joy on their beds, which is a lovely image right up to verse six, where the same mouths full of praise are handed a two-edged sword. What follows is vengeance on the nations, kings in chains, nobles in iron, judgment carried out as written. The psalm calls this an honour belonging to all his saints, and does not soften it.
PSA 150:1-6	The Psalter signs off by abandoning content altogether and naming instruments instead. Trumpet, harp, lyre, tambourine, strings, flute, then cymbals twice, loud and afterwards resounding, in case the first pass was too subtle. Thirteen imperatives in six verses, with no complaint, no enemies and no argument left to make. The final line quietly opens the ensemble to anything at all that has breath.
1CH 13:1-14	Bringing the ark back starts as a committee decision: David puts it to the commanders and the whole assembly, notes nobody sought it under Saul, and gets a unanimous yes. Then the new cart, the music, and the oxen stumbling at Chidon's threshing floor. Uzza steadies the ark and dies for it. David, displeased and then frightened, renames the spot after the incident and parks the ark at Obed-Edom's house, where it spends three months making him rich.
1CH 14:1-17	A foreign king sends the cedar and the labour, and David works out from that what his kingdom amounts to. Thirteen more children are listed without comment. The Philistines, hearing of the coronation, come up twice. The first time David is told to go straight at them, and names the place for waters breaking out; the abandoned idols are burned rather than collected. The second time he waits behind the mulberry trees for the sound of marching in the treetops.
1CH 15:1-29	Round two on the ark, this time by the book. David pitches a tent, then rules that only Levites may carry it, and says plainly why: last time it was not done according to the ordinance. Six Levite families are counted in, told to sanctify themselves, and take it on their shoulders with poles as Moses said. A full orchestra is appointed, with Chenaniah over the singing because he was skillful. Michal watches from a window and despises him, and Chronicles leaves it there.
1CH 16:1-43	The ark reaches its tent, offerings are made, and everyone present, man and woman, goes home with bread, meat and a raisin cake. Asaph gets the ministry post and a psalm: give thanks, remember the covenant made with Abraham, the other nations' gods are idols, let the sea roar and the forest sing. The people say Amen. Then the staffing note, which is the odd part: worship now runs in two places, Asaph at the ark, Zadok at the tabernacle in Gibeon.
1CH 17:1-27	David observes that he has a house of cedar and the ark has a tent. Nathan says go ahead, and is corrected the same night. God has lived in tents since Egypt and has never once raised it with any judge of Israel. The building goes to a son instead, whose throne is promised forever, and Chronicles quietly drops the line Samuel keeps about chastening that son if he sins. David sits before Yahweh and prays a long thank-you built on the word established.
HOS 1:1-11	The book opens by handing its prophet the worst possible assignment: marry a prostitute, because the country is one. So Hosea marries Gomer and then names their three children after the sentence hanging over Israel. Jezreel for the coming reckoning, Lo-Ruhamah for no more mercy, Lo-Ammi for not my people. Three kids, three pieces of bad news, one of them raised answering to a name that means she is unwanted. Then, without warning, the chapter promises all of it will be reversed.
HOS 2:1-23	Here the metaphor stops being a metaphor and becomes a prosecution. Israel is the wife who went shopping for bread and wool and oil among her lovers, and never worked out who had been paying. The threat is thorough: stripped bare, feasts cancelled, vineyards handed to the animals. And then, mid-sentence, the whole thing softens into a courtship. Wilderness, tender speech, a valley named Trouble reopened as a door of hope, and a betrothal that lasts forever. The wronged husband proposes again.
HOS 3:1-5	Round two. Hosea is told to go love the adulteress again, and does it by purchase: fifteen pieces of silver and a homer and a half of barley, which is a price rather than a romance. His terms are exclusivity, and he offers the same back. The stated point is that Israel loves other gods and raisin cakes, and will spend a long time with no king, no sacrifice, no idol and nothing to be religious with before it comes back.
HOS 4:1-19	A lawsuit against an entire country, opening with an inventory of what the land does not contain: truth, goodness, knowledge of God. What it does contain runs to murder, theft, lying and bloodshed answering bloodshed, until the fish die of it. The priests are the real defendants, since they eat off the sin they are supposed to remove, and the ruling is the memorable one: like people, like priest. Israel gets compared to a stubborn heifer, and Ephraim gets written off with a sigh: leave him alone.
HOS 5:1-15	Everyone with authority is called into the room and told the judgement is about them. The grim part is verse four: their own deeds are what stops them turning around. They will arrive with flocks and herds to look for God and find he has left. He then compares himself, in the space of three verses, to a moth, to rot, and to a lion, which is an escalating set of things to be. Ephraim's plan is to consult Assyria.
1CH 23:1-32	David, old and full of days, hands the crown to Solomon and then does what he actually enjoys: staffing. Thirty-eight thousand Levites, sorted into twenty-four thousand supervisors, six thousand officers, four thousand doorkeepers and four thousand musicians playing instruments he mentions having invented himself. Buried in the family lists is Eleazar, who died leaving only daughters, so their cousins married them and the name survived. The retirement age drops to twenty, on the reasoning that nobody has to carry the tent any more.
1CH 24:1-31	Twenty-four priestly shifts, and the interesting part is how they are assigned. Eleazar's branch has sixteen chief houses to Ithamar's eight, which in most institutions would settle the pecking order permanently. Instead they draw lots, with a scribe writing down the results in front of the king, and the eldest house takes its turn exactly as the youngest does. Nadab and Abihu are noted as having died before their father with no children, which is how a rota becomes a memorial.
1CH 25:1-31	The temple choir is described as prophesying, with harps. Two hundred and eighty-eight trained singers under Asaph, Jeduthun and Heman, the last of whom God gave fourteen sons and three daughters, and whose last nine sons have names that read suspiciously like a sentence of prayer broken into people. Duty rosters again go by lot, small and great alike, the teacher drawing against the student, which is either radical fairness or a very tidy way to avoid an argument.
1CH 26:1-32	Gate duty allocated by lot, east, north, south, west and the storehouse, watchman facing watchman. Obed-Edom turns up with sixty-two descendants and the text explains why in four words: for God blessed him. That is the man who kept the ark for three months. Hosah promoted a son who was not the firstborn, and nobody explains it. The treasury holds spoil dedicated by Samuel, Saul, Abner and Joab, which is a list of people who did not otherwise get along.
1CH 27:1-34	The army works a rota: twelve divisions of twenty-four thousand, one month each, so the kingdom is defended year round without anyone missing a harvest. Asahel is listed as fourth captain although he was killed years earlier, with his son named after him. Then a quiet admission tucked between the tribal chiefs and the estate managers: Joab started the census, never finished it, wrath came, and the number was left out of the records. Also, somebody was in charge of the camels.
1CH 28:1-21	David gathers everyone who matters and gives the speech he has clearly rehearsed: I wanted to build it, God said no, too much blood, my son gets the job. Then he produces the plans. Not a sketch, plans, down to the weight of gold in each individual lampstand and the silver in each bowl, plus the staffing rota. He says the whole pattern came to him in writing from God's hand, which conveniently forecloses any design discussion.
1CH 29:1-14	Having spent chapters stockpiling for the temple, David announces he is adding his personal fortune on top: three thousand talents of Ophir gold, seven thousand of refined silver. Then he turns to the room and asks who else is consecrating themselves today, which is fundraising as public liturgy and works beautifully. Gold, silver, brass, iron and gemstones come in, and everyone is delighted with themselves. David prays a blessing whose punchline is that none of it was theirs to begin with.
1CH 29:15-30	The prayer keeps going, and its best line is that they are strangers here whose days are a shadow, which is a strange thing to say while handing over a hundred thousand talents of iron. A thousand bulls, a thousand rams and a thousand lambs later, Solomon is crowned for the second time, in case the first did not take. David dies old, rich and honoured after forty years, and the Chronicler cites three separate seers' histories as his sources.
1CH 18:1-17	A chapter of conquest delivered as inventory. Philistines subdued, Moab paying tribute, Hadadezer of Zobah beaten back to the Euphrates, twenty-two thousand Syrians of Damascus, Edom garrisoned. The Chronicler cannot resist one aside: the brass carried off from Hadadezer's cities is the same brass Solomon later cast into the bronze sea and the pillars. Even the plunder is building material. Tou of Hamath, an enemy of an enemy, sends his son with gifts, and the chapter closes with an orderly list of who held which office.
1CH 19:1-19	David sends condolences to Ammon's new king, whose advisers decide the sympathy card is reconnaissance. They shave the envoys and cut their robes off at the buttocks, and David, with unusual tact, tells the men to sit in Jericho until their beards grow. Ammon then spends a thousand talents of silver hiring chariots, which is an expensive way to justify a haircut. Joab, attacked from both sides, splits the army with Abishai and agrees they will rescue each other. Both halves win.
1CH 20:1-8	The verse about David staying in Jerusalem while the army besieged Rabbah is the same verse Samuel uses to introduce Bathsheba. Here it introduces nothing. No rooftop, no Uriah, no Nathan, no dead child; the narrative simply proceeds to a talent-weight crown and the sack of the city. Then three skirmishes with Philistine giants, including a man with six fingers on each hand and six toes on each foot, and Goliath's brother, killed by Elhanan.
1CH 21:1-30	In Samuel it was God who incited the census. Here it is Satan, and the whole chapter exists to make that substitution. Joab argues, loses, and takes his revenge by simply not counting Levi and Benjamin. Seventy thousand die, an angel stands over Jerusalem with a drawn sword, and David buys Ornan's threshing floor at full price, refusing to make an offering that costs him nothing. Fire falls on the altar. Samuel priced the same field at fifty silver shekels; here it is six hundred in gold.
1CH 22:1-19	Standing where the plague stopped, David announces that this is the house of God, which is a decisive way to choose a site. Then he begins procurement: stonecutters conscripted from resident foreigners, iron for the door nails, brass past weighing, cedar arriving from Tyre by the shipload. He explains to Solomon why the job is not his, in the bluntest terms the book allows, and points out that the boy's name means peace. A hundred thousand talents of gold, and instructions to add to it.
1ES 1:1-29	First Esdras begins not at the beginning but at Josiah's Passover, which tells you what this book thinks the story is about. The logistics are the same ones Chronicles gives, thirty thousand lambs from the king's own flocks, Levites doing the catering so the gatekeepers never leave their posts, and no Passover like it since Samuel. Then, with everything in perfect order, Josiah rides out to intercept an Egyptian army that does not want him. This version says outright that he ignored Jeremiah. Next time on 1 Esdras: Megiddo.
1ES 1:30-58	Josiah asks to be carried off the field, dies at home, and Jeremiah writes the lament, which becomes a standing national observance. What follows is four kings in rapid succession, each deposed or deported by whichever empire is currently in the room. The temple is defiled by the very officials meant to keep it, the messengers are mocked, and Babylon does the rest. The chapter closes on Jeremiah's arithmetic: seventy years of Sabbath owed to a land that never got any.
1ES 2:1-30	Cyrus' decree here is grander than the version in Ezra, having him announce that the Lord of Israel made him king of the whole world. The vessel count is different too, five thousand four hundred and sixty-nine rather than five thousand four hundred, which is the sort of discrepancy that happens when two scribes count the same crates. Then the Samarian officials write their letter, the king checks the archives, the archives agree, and a cavalry detachment shuts the site down.
1ES 3:1-24	This is the passage 1 Esdras exists for, and it appears in no other book. Three royal bodyguards, bored after a state banquet, invent a debating competition with a prize list that includes purple robes, a gold bed and the title of the king's cousin, and file their entries under the sleeping king's pillow. Wine, the king, and women plus truth. The first speaker's case for wine is genuinely good: it makes an orphan and a monarch identical, erases debt, and ends with drawn swords nobody remembers.
1ES 4:1-34	The second speech is the easy one: the king says kill and men kill, says build and men build, and sleeps soundly while others stand guard. Then Zerubbabel takes women, and his argument runs from the reasonable, that men leave their parents and country for a wife, to the observational, that thieves bring the loot home to impress her. His closing evidence is an eyewitness account of the king's own concubine lifting the crown off his head and slapping him while he begged to be forgiven. Next time on 1 Esdras: truth.
1ES 4:35-63	Zerubbabel pivots: heaven is high, the sun is fast, but greater is whoever made them, and truth outlasts the lot. Wine, kings, women and everyone else are unrighteous and will perish; truth takes no bribes and does not die. The hall shouts him the winner. Then, offered anything at all, he asks for none of the prizes and instead reminds Darius of a vow he made about Jerusalem, which is the smartest move in the book. He walks out with cedar, tax exemption and a building budget.
1ES 5:1-29	Ezra sends the returning exiles off with a fast and a prayer. First Esdras sends them off with a thousand cavalry, tabrets and flutes, which is a very different mood for the same journey. Then the register, and anyone holding Ezra 2 alongside it will find the family totals drifting by a few dozen here and there, the same list twice-translated. Four thousand priests, seventy-four Levites, and the ratio nobody in these books ever explains. Next time on 1 Esdras: the ones who could not prove it.
1ES 5:30-50	Temple servants, then the paragraph that appears in every version of this list: families who could not produce a genealogy, and priests struck off the register, including a man who married into a family and took their name instead of keeping his own. Barred from the holy things until a high priest with Urim and Thummim shows up. This version counts Israel from twelve years old rather than twenty, and gives them seven thousand horses. Next time on 1 Esdras: they build the altar first.
1ES 5:51-73	They start sacrificing on an altar standing on open ground, months before there is any building around it, which is the priority this whole book keeps insisting on. Cedar comes down to Joppa as it did for Solomon. Then the foundation, the trumpets, and the scene every version of this story tells: the old men who remembered the first temple weeping so loudly that nobody could hear the celebration. The neighbours offer to help, are refused, and spend two years making sure nothing gets finished.
1ES 6:1-19	Two prophets talk the builders back onto the site, and a governor arrives with the standard questions: who authorised this, and give me the names of the men in charge. Nobody is arrested. The governor writes to Darius instead, and his letter is scrupulously fair, admiring the speed of the work and quoting the Jews' own account at length, including the part where they concede that the first temple was destroyed because their ancestors deserved it. Next time on 1 Esdras: the archives.
1ES 6:20-34	The search is ordered in Babylon and the document turns up at Ekbatana, a summer palace in the hills, which is a very believable place for imperial paperwork to end up. Darius does not merely permit the work; he orders the governors who queried it to pay for it out of their own provincial tribute, supply the animals daily, and pray for him with the proceeds. Anyone obstructing gets a beam pulled out of his own house and is hanged on it.
1ES 7:1-15	The officials who came to investigate end up supervising the construction, which is the most satisfying reversal in the book. The temple is finished on a dated day in Darius' sixth year, credited jointly to the God of Israel and to three Persian kings, an attribution nobody in this book finds strange. A hundred bulls, two hundred rams, four hundred lambs, and twelve goats for twelve tribes, most of which no longer exist. Then Passover, kept by everyone who had separated themselves.
1ES 8:1-29	Ezra arrives with a genealogy sixteen deep and a commission from Artaxerxes that is startlingly generous: any Jew who wants to go may go, a hundred talents of silver on demand from the provincial treasurers, complete tax exemption for everyone employed at the temple, and authority to appoint judges across Syria and Phoenicia with sentencing powers up to death. Salt in abundance, quantity unspecified. The stated reason for all this largesse is that wrath should not come on the king's own realm. Next time on 1 Esdras: who actually signed up.
1ES 8:30-65	Ezra musters at the river and finds the flaw in his expedition: plenty of laymen, no clergy at all. So he sends eleven men to a treasury town called Casiphia to ask for some, and returns with thirty-eight Levites and two hundred and twenty temple servants. Then the reason there is no armed escort, which he admits candidly: he had already told the king that God looks after those who seek him, and could not face asking for cavalry afterwards. He weighs the treasure out, and has it weighed in again. Next time on 1 Esdras: the news that meets him.
1ES 8:66-96	The welcoming committee's news is that intermarriage is universal and the nobles led the way, which is when Ezra tears not only his clothes but his priestly vestment and pulls out his own hair. The prayer that follows never says they. It says our sins have reached above our heads, and ends by admitting he cannot stand there any longer. Then a crowd gathers, and one man proposes the remedy the book records without softening: send away the wives and the children born to them.
1ES 9:1-28	The summons comes with teeth: attend within three days or forfeit your livestock to the temple and be expelled. They come, and sit in the open in weather bad enough that the text mentions it twice, which is how the assembly wins its one concession: this cannot be settled standing in the rain, so appoint commissioners and work through it properly. Four men take the job. Three months of hearings, and the register opens, as it must, with four priests of the high priest's own family. Next time on 1 Esdras: the rest of the list, and the reading of the law.
1ES 9:29-55	The list finishes, and the sentence at the end of it is stated flatly: they sent them away with their children. Then the book changes register completely. The people ask for the law, unprompted, and Ezra reads from a wooden platform with thirteen men flanking him, from dawn to noon, while the Levites work the crowd explaining. Everybody cries. And the last instruction in the book is to go and eat something rich, share it with anyone who has nothing, and stop being sad, which is where it ends, mid-sentence.
1MA 1:1-29	Alexander beats Darius, conquers as far as there is world to conquer, divides the kingdom among his officers and dies after twelve years. His successors crown themselves and multiply evils. Antiochus Epiphanes arrives. Certain Israelites decide the covenant is holding them back, build a gymnasium in Jerusalem and surgically undo their circumcision. Antiochus beats Egypt, then strips the temple, scraping the gold off its front. Two years later a tribute collector sets out. Next time on 1 Maccabees: he arrives talking peace.
1MA 1:30-64	The collector opens with peaceable words, then sacks the city, burns it and fortifies the old city of David as a citadel for renegades. Jerusalem empties, her feasts turned to mourning. Antiochus then legislates uniformity: one kingdom, one people, one law, meaning no sabbaths, no offerings, swine on the altar and sons left uncircumcised, all on pain of death. An abomination of desolation goes up on the altar in Chislev. Scrolls are burned, mothers executed with their infants, and many choose death over eating.
1MA 2:1-34	Mattathias, a priest, relocates from Jerusalem to Modin with five sons, one of them nicknamed Maccabaeus. The king's officers offer him silver, gold and the rank of King's Friend to sacrifice first and set an example. He declines on behalf of his entire house, then watches a neighbour step up to the altar, kills him, kills the officer, and pulls the altar down. He takes to the hills with anyone zealous for the law. Others hide in the wilderness, where troops corner them on a Sabbath. Next time on 1 Maccabees: the caves give their answer.
1MA 2:35-70	The men in the caves will not fight on the Sabbath, will not even block the entrances, and about a thousand die with their families and their cattle. Mattathias draws the obvious conclusion: fight when attacked, or there will be nobody left to keep the Sabbath at all. The Hasidaeans join, altars come down, and children are circumcised whether or not anyone asked. Dying, he recites a list of ancestors who were vindicated, appoints Simon as counselor and Judas as commander, and is buried at Modin.
1MA 3:1-31	Judas takes over, introduced as a lion roaring over its prey. He kills Apollonius and keeps the man's sword for the rest of his life, which is a way of making a point. Seron comes hunting a reputation and finds Judas at the pass of Bethhoron with a hungry, outnumbered company; eight hundred Syrians stay there. Antiochus, furious, pays his army a year in advance and then discovers the treasury is empty, largely his own doing, and heads east for tribute. Next time on 1 Maccabees: he leaves a deputy behind.
1MA 3:32-60	Antiochus heads east and leaves Lysias half the army, the elephants and the crown prince, with instructions to erase Israel and resettle the land with strangers. Lysias dispatches forty thousand foot and seven thousand horse to Emmaus, and slave dealers arrive with silver and fetters to buy captives nobody has taken yet. Judas answers with a fast at Mizpeh: sackcloth, ashes, the scroll spread open, the priestly garments laid out. Then he sends home the newly betrothed, the builders, the planters and the frightened, exactly as the law requires.
1MA 4:1-33	Gorgias takes a night strike force to catch Judas asleep, guided by informers from the citadel. Judas leaves by the other road and hits the camp at Emmaus instead, so Gorgias arrives to find nobody home. At dawn three thousand badly armed men appear in the plain, hear a short speech about the Red Sea, and rout the army. Judas forbids looting while Gorgias is still loose; Gorgias sees the smoke and goes. A year later Lysias returns with sixty thousand. Next time on 1 Maccabees: they meet at Bethsura.
1MA 4:34-61	Five thousand of Lysias's men fall and he withdraws to Antioch to hire mercenaries. Judas climbs Mount Zion and finds the sanctuary derelict, the gates burned, shrubs growing in the courts. The profaned altar is dismantled and its stones stored until a prophet turns up to rule on them, a wonderfully patient piece of filing. New altar, new vessels, and on the twenty-fifth of Chislev, the anniversary of the desecration to the day, eight days of dedication with harps and cymbals.
1MA 5:1-23	The neighbours take the rebuilt altar personally and begin killing the Jews living among them. Judas works through them: the children of Esau at Akrabattine, the sons of Baean in their towers, Timotheus and the Ammonites. Letters arrive from Jews besieged at Dathema, and before the reading is finished more messengers come from Galilee with torn clothes. Judas splits the force, Simon north with three thousand, himself east with eight thousand, and leaves Joseph and Azarias holding Judea under one order: do not fight. Next time on 1 Maccabees: Judas crosses the Jordan.
1MA 5:24-44	Three days into the wilderness, friendly Nabathaeans hand Judas a list of towns holding Jews due to be killed tomorrow. He takes Bosora, marches all night, and reaches the stronghold mid-assault, hitting the besiegers from behind in three companies with trumpets; eight thousand of Timotheus's men fall. Timotheus regroups at Raphon with hired Arabians and a plan hinging on whether Judas hesitates at the brook. Judas crosses first. The survivors shelter in the temple at Carnaim, which burns. Next time on 1 Maccabees: the long walk home.
1MA 5:45-68	Judas collects every Jew in Gilead, families and livestock included, for the march to Judah. Ephron sits on the only road and refuses passage, so Ephron ceases to exist. They reach Mount Zion without losing a man. Meanwhile Joseph and Azarias, wanting a reputation of their own, attack Jamnia against orders, and Gorgias kills two thousand of them, the narrator noting they were not of the family through whom deliverance came. Judas then burns the idols of Azotus and goes home.
1MA 6:1-32	Antiochus goes temple-robbing in Elymais, finds the locals forewarned and armed, and leaves with nothing. Then the news from Judah: Lysias beaten, the abomination demolished, the sanctuary walled again. He takes to his bed and works out at last that this might be connected to having sacked Jerusalem for no reason. He hands Philip the diadem and dies. Lysias ignores the arrangement and crowns the boy Eupator himself. The citadel petitions, and the answer is a hundred thousand men and thirty-two elephants. Next time on 1 Maccabees: the elephants get their pre-match drink.
1MA 6:33-63	The elephants are given grape and mulberry juice to get them in the mood, then issued a thousand infantry, five hundred cavalry and a wooden tower apiece; the sun on the gold shields sets the mountains alight. Eleazar picks out the tallest beast, decides the king must be on it, goes underneath and kills it, and is crushed. The king was not on it. Bethsura starves out, the sanctuary starves too, and then Lysias hears Philip is going for the throne and discovers a sudden enthusiasm for religious freedom. Peace is sworn, and the wall comes down anyway.
1MA 7:1-24	Demetrius arrives from Rome with a few men, and the army obligingly hands him Antiochus and Lysias; he declines to look at them and has them killed. Alcimus wants the high priesthood and knows the route, which is to accuse Judas. Bacchides comes with him and an army, offering peace with all the sincerity you would expect. The Hasidaeans reason that a priest of Aaron's line will not hurt them, and sixty are dead by the end of the day. Bacchides fills a pit with more. Next time on 1 Maccabees: Alcimus needs another favour.
1MA 7:25-50	Alcimus returns to court with fresh grievances, and Demetrius sends Nicanor, whose opening move is a friendly visit that is actually a kidnapping. Judas notices. After losing five hundred men at Capharsalama, Nicanor stands in the temple, mocks the priests offering sacrifice for his own king, and swears to burn the place down. At Adasa on the thirteenth of Adar he is the first man in his army to die, which settles the battle at once. His head and the right hand he waved so grandly end up hanging outside Jerusalem, and the date becomes a holiday.
1MA 8:1-15	Judas receives a briefing on the Romans, and it is glowing. Gaul conquered, the Spanish mines taken, Philip and Perseus beaten, and Antiochus the Great, elephants and all, captured alive and billed for the privilege, losing India, Media and Lydia in the settlement. They install kings and remove them at will. And, the report stresses, not one of them wears a diadem or purple; three hundred and twenty men simply sit in the senate house every day, deliberating. Next time on 1 Maccabees: the fine print.
1MA 8:16-32	The briefing concludes that one man rules for a year and there is neither envy nor rivalry among them, a description of the late Republic that would have surprised the late Republic. Judas sends Eupolemus and Jason down the very long road to Rome, and the senate says yes. The treaty goes onto tablets of brass: mutual aid, no grain, arms, money or ships for each other's enemies, amendments by agreement. Rome then writes to Demetrius asking why he is leaning on their new friends, and mentions war.
1MA 9:1-24	Bacchides comes back with Alcimus and twenty thousand foot. Judas has three thousand at Elasa, and once his men get a good look at the opposition he has eight hundred. They suggest a tactical withdrawal; Judas says he would rather die than leave a reproach on his name, which turns out to be the choice actually on offer. He breaks the right wing and chases it to Mount Azotus, and the left wing closes in behind him. He falls. Israel buries him at Modin and asks how the mighty is fallen. Next time on 1 Maccabees: the collaborators surface.
1MA 9:25-50	Bacchides installs the collaborators and begins collecting Judas's friends. Jonathan is elected leader in his brother's place, which promptly makes him the next target. Then the children of Jambri seize his brother John and all his baggage, so Jonathan waits for their wedding procession: bride from Nadabath, timbrels, minstrels, ambush. The marriage is turned into mourning, in exactly those words. Cornered at the Jordan with water on both sides, Jonathan takes a swing at Bacchides, who ducks, and the whole company swims for it. Next time on 1 Maccabees: Alcimus picks up a sledgehammer.
1MA 9:51-73	Bacchides garrisons the country and takes the leading men's sons hostage. Alcimus orders the inner court wall demolished, undoing what the prophets built, and mid-demolition he is struck with a palsy, cannot speak, and dies in agony without issuing another instruction. Bacchides leaves; two quiet years. The lawless invite him back, and at Bethbasi Jonathan slips out to raid the countryside while Simon burns the siege engines. Beaten, Bacchides executes the advisers who talked him into it, makes peace, returns the prisoners, and never crosses the border again.
1MA 10:1-32	Two kings need the same man. Alexander Epiphanes seizes Ptolemais, and Demetrius, alarmed, writes to Jonathan offering permission to raise an army and a release of the hostages. Jonathan promptly rebuilds the walls. Alexander raises: high priest, King's Friend, purple robe, crown of gold, and Jonathan is vested at Tabernacles. Demetrius raises again with a document of extraordinary generosity, no tribute, no salt duty, no crown levy, a free and holy Jerusalem, and the citadel thrown in. Next time on 1 Maccabees: he keeps going.
1MA 10:33-58	The offer continues, and it is enormous: captives freed, tax holidays around every feast, thirty thousand Jews on the royal payroll under their own officers, three Samaritan districts annexed, Ptolemais signed over to the temple, and the building works billed to the crown. Jonathan and the people read it, remember who wrote it, and decline. They keep faith with Alexander, who spoke to them first. In the battle Alexander's army runs away and Alexander wins anyway, because Demetrius falls. Next time on 1 Maccabees: a wedding, then an invitation.
1MA 10:59-89	Alexander summons Jonathan to Ptolemais, dresses him in purple, seats him beside the throne and has a herald announce that complaints about this man are no longer permitted. The complainants leave. Then Demetrius's son lands from Crete, and his general Apollonius sends Jonathan a letter of pure schoolyard: come down from your mountains and fight in the plain. Jonathan takes Joppa, then stands perfectly still at Azotus while the cavalry throws darts until the horses are spent, and Simon walks through the phalanx. Eight thousand dead, and Ekron as a gift.
1MA 11:1-26	Ptolemy arrives in Syria talking peace and quietly garrisoning every city he passes, which is how you annex a kingdom while remaining someone's father-in-law. At Azotus the locals lay out the burned temple of Dagon and the corpses in his path as a complaint about Jonathan; he says nothing. Then he takes his daughter back, hands her to Demetrius, and wears two crowns at once. Alexander loses, flees to Arabia, and is beheaded by his host. Ptolemy dies three days later. Next time on 1 Maccabees: Jonathan brings presents.
1MA 11:27-48	Demetrius confirms the high priesthood and sells tax exemption for three hundred talents, in writing, to be posted on the holy mount so nobody can pretend it did not happen. Then, feeling safe, he dismisses the army that got him there, and it never forgives him. Tryphon starts shopping for Alexander's small son as a replacement king. Jonathan asks for the citadel to be cleared and gets a request for soldiers instead, and his three thousand men rescue Demetrius when a hundred and twenty thousand Antiochenes come to kill him. Next time on 1 Maccabees: the thank-you note.
1MA 11:49-74	Antioch surrenders and the Jews go home loaded with spoil and royal gratitude. Demetrius, safe again, breaks every promise he ever made to Jonathan, the narrator noting simply that he lied in all that he spoke. Tryphon arrives with the child Antiochus, collects the soldiers Demetrius dismissed, and takes Antioch and the elephants. The boy king confirms Jonathan and gives Simon the coast. Then at Hazor an ambush empties Jonathan's army except for two captains; he throws earth on his head, prays, turns around, and wins anyway.
1MA 12:1-28	Jonathan writes to Rome to renew the alliance and, while he is at it, to Sparta, on the strength of an old letter claiming the Spartans and the Jews are relatives, both of the stock of Abraham. The letter is a small diplomatic masterpiece: we need nothing from you, we have the holy books, but we would hate to drift apart, and we do mention you at sacrifices. Then Demetrius's generals return with a bigger army, spies report a night attack, and Jonathan keeps everyone armed and awake. Next time on 1 Maccabees: the campfires.
1MA 12:29-53	The enemy leave their campfires burning and slip away, and Jonathan is fooled until daylight; he chases them, loses them, and takes it out on the Zabadaean Arabs. Simon grabs Joppa first. They raise the walls and build a mound to cut the citadel off from the market. Then Tryphon, who wants the throne and finds Jonathan in the way, greets him with gifts, suggests forty thousand men are unnecessary between friends, invites him to Ptolemais with a thousand, and shuts the gates. Israel mourns, and the neighbours begin circling.
1MA 13:1-29	Simon stands up in Jerusalem and points out that his entire family is dead and he is what remains, then volunteers to be next. The people make him leader on the spot. Tryphon offers to sell Jonathan back for a hundred talents and two of his sons, and Simon, who knows perfectly well it is a lie, pays anyway so that nobody can say he refused. Tryphon takes both and keeps Jonathan. A snowstorm turns him back from the citadel, and he kills Jonathan in Gilead instead. Next time on 1 Maccabees: Tryphon promotes himself.
1MA 13:30-53	Tryphon kills the boy he had been ruling for and puts the crown on himself. Simon, meanwhile, sends Demetrius a golden crown and a palm branch and gets back tax immunity, an amnesty for everything to date, and the strongholds he built. The yoke of the heathen is lifted, and people begin dating their contracts by Simon's first year, which is the real measure of independence. Gazara is stormed and cleansed. The citadel starves until it surrenders, and Simon marches in with harps, cymbals and palm branches, then makes the date a holiday.
1MA 14:1-27	Demetrius goes to Media for reinforcements and is arrested on arrival, which concludes his contribution. Then the book stops fighting for a page and describes peace instead: Joppa opened as a harbour, the land yielding, old men gossiping in the streets, young men in splendid armour, everyone under his own vine and fig tree with nobody to frighten him. Rome and Sparta write to renew the treaty, on brass. Simon sends Rome a gold shield weighing a thousand pounds. Next time on 1 Maccabees: the people put it in writing.
1MA 14:28-49	The full text of the honours decree, read at Asaramel and set up on brass. It rehearses the family record, notes that Simon armed the troops out of his own pocket, and then does the important part: leader and high priest forever, or until a faithful prophet turns up to say otherwise, which is a carefully hedged forever. Nobody may contradict him, call an assembly without him, or wear purple. The copies go into the treasury for Simon and his sons.
1MA 15:1-22	Antiochus, son of Demetrius, writes from offshore offering Simon everything he already has, plus the right to mint his own coinage, which is the one item on the list that is actually new. He lands, the armies change sides, Tryphon runs out of men, and is bottled up at Dor by sea and land. Meanwhile Numenius comes back from Rome with a circular letter to every king in the region: leave the Jews alone, return their fugitives, and yes, we kept the shield. Next time on 1 Maccabees: the offer expires.
1MA 15:23-41	The Roman letter goes out to everywhere with a harbour: Sparta, Delos, Rhodes, Cyprus, Cyrene, a whole page of them. Simon sends Antiochus two thousand men and a fortune in silver, and Antiochus refuses the gift and tears up every agreement instead. His envoy Athenobius demands Joppa, Gazara and the citadel or a thousand talents, then stands in Simon's house counting the gold plate and losing his composure. Simon replies that they took nothing that was not their fathers', offers a hundred talents, and Athenobius storms off. Cendebaeus begins raiding from Kidron.
1MA 16:1-24	John reports the raids, and Simon, too old for another campaign, hands the army to his sons with a short speech about the family business. At the brook the men hesitate; John crosses first and they follow, and Cendebaeus is broken. Then the murder: Ptolemy son of Abubus, captain of Jericho and Simon's own son in law, invites him and two of his sons to a banquet at Dok, waits until they have drunk freely, and kills them. Assassins sent to Gazara for John arrive too late. The book ends pointing at a chronicle nobody has.
2CH 1:1-17	Kings has Solomon simply dreaming at Gibeon and leaves the awkwardness there. The Chronicler explains it: the Tent of Meeting and Bezalel's bronze altar were at Gibeon, so this is not a dubious high place, it is the correct address. A thousand burnt offerings later, God offers a blank cheque and Solomon asks for wisdom to judge, which earns him wisdom plus everything he politely declined. He goes home and starts buying chariots, which will not end well but not today.
2CH 2:1-18	Solomon's letter to Huram of Tyre is a small masterpiece: the house will be great because our God is great, but of course no house can contain him, so really this is just somewhere to burn incense. Then the order, in detail, with payment in wheat, barley, wine and oil. Huram writes back blessing Israel's God, which is diplomacy of a very high order, and sends a craftsman with a Danite mother. The labour force is a hundred and fifty-three thousand resident foreigners, carefully not Israelites.
2CH 3:1-17	One word here that Kings never uses: Moriah. The temple site is not merely Ornan's threshing floor but the mountain, and the Chronicler drops it in without further comment and moves straight to measurements. What follows is gold applied at an implausible rate, six hundred talents in the inner room alone, and a note that the nails came to fifty shekels of gold, which is the sort of detail an accountant includes to prove he was there. Two pillars, named Jachin and Boaz.
2CH 4:1-22	Furniture, cast at scale. A bronze altar twenty cubits square, which Kings somehow never mentions installing, and a metal sea standing on twelve oxen arranged three to each compass point with their backsides discreetly inward. It holds three thousand baths here; Kings said two thousand, and neither book pauses over it. Ten lampstands, ten tables, a hundred gold basins. Huram cast the bronze in the clay flats of the Jordan, in quantities the text declines to weigh.
2CH 5:1-14	The ark goes into the dark room under the cherubim's wings, poles left long enough to be seen from outside, and the narrator notes it contained nothing but the two stone tablets, which after all this gold is a striking sentence. Then the Chronicler adds what Kings leaves out: the full choir, Asaph, Heman and Jeduthun in linen, and a hundred and twenty trumpeters, all landing on one note. At that moment the cloud arrives and the priests cannot stand up in it.
2CH 6:1-22	Solomon opens by pointing out that God said he would live in thick darkness, and that he has therefore built him a house, which is either faith or a spectacular non sequitur. Chronicles adds a detail Kings omits: a bronze platform five cubits square, built specially so the king could kneel where everyone could see him do it. The prayer's central admission is that the heavens cannot contain God and this building certainly cannot, delivered by the man who just spent seven years on it.
2CH 6:23-42	What follows is a list of disasters filed in advance: defeat, drought, famine, blight, locusts, siege, and whatever sickness has not yet been invented. Solomon asks that the foreigner who comes from far away be answered in full, on the grounds that it is good publicity for the name. Then the case he lingers over longest, exile, repentance in a far country, prayer turned toward home, which reads very differently once you notice who the Chronicler's first readers were.
2CH 7:1-22	Kings has Solomon finish praying and the cloud arrive. Chronicles has fire fall from heaven first, consuming the offering while the whole nation watches from the pavement. Then the numbers: twenty-two thousand cattle, a hundred and twenty thousand sheep, so many that the new bronze altar cannot cope and the courtyard has to be consecrated as overflow. God's night reply contains the verse everybody quotes about humbling and healing, and immediately after it the threat that this very impressive building can become a byword.
2CH 8:1-18	A quiet correction to Kings, which said Solomon handed Hiram twenty towns as payment. Here Huram gives cities to Solomon, who rebuilds them and moves Israelites in. Everything else is administration: fortresses, store cities, chariot towns, and conscript labour drawn strictly from the surviving Canaanites, with the text insisting no Israelite was enslaved. Pharaoh's daughter is relocated out of David's old house on holiness grounds, which is the closest this book comes to mentioning Solomon's marriages.
2CH 9:1-31	The queen of Sheba comes to test him, runs out of breath somewhere between the food and the staff uniforms, and concedes the reports undersold him. Then an inventory of wealth so relentless it stops meaning anything: six hundred and sixty-six talents a year, gold shields, an ivory throne with fourteen lions on it, silver counted as worthless. Solomon then dies. No foreign wives, no high places, no adversaries raised up: the Chronicler simply declines to mention that any of it went wrong.
2CH 10:1-19	The north asks for a tax cut. Rehoboam consults the men who actually ran the country under his father, who advise being nice, then consults his friends, who suggest the line about his little finger being thicker than his father's waist. He goes with his friends. Ten tribes leave on the spot, and the man he sends to negotiate happens to be the head of forced labour, which the crowd stones to death. Rehoboam departs by chariot at speed.
2CH 11:1-23	Rehoboam raises a hundred and eighty thousand men to take the north back and a prophet tells him to stand down, which, surprisingly, he does. Instead he fortifies fifteen towns and stocks them properly. The interesting migration is the priests and Levites, who abandon their own land and walk south because Jeroboam has sacked them and staffed his calf shrines himself. Then eighteen wives, sixty concubines and eighty-eight children, which the text presents as prudent regional administration.
2CH 12:1-16	Rehoboam gets comfortable and immediately abandons the law, and Chronicles connects the invoice to the purchase without pausing. Shishak arrives with twelve hundred chariots, Shemaiah delivers the one line explanation, and king and princes concede that God is righteous. The verdict is downgraded from destruction to servitude, so they can compare working for God with working for somebody else. Shishak carries off Solomon's gold shields; Rehoboam has bronze ones made and guards them carefully, which is the whole reign in one object.
2CH 13:1-22	Outnumbered two to one, Abijah delivers a sermon from a hilltop to the enemy army, covering the covenant of salt, Jeroboam's ingratitude, and the northern ordination policy of consecrating anyone who turns up with a bull and seven rams. It is a fine speech, and Jeroboam responds by putting an ambush behind him. Judah, surrounded, shouts, the priests blow the trumpets, and the northern army loses half a million men. Abijah takes Bethel, then celebrates with fourteen wives.
2CH 14:1-15	Ten years of quiet, which Asa spends demolishing shrines and building walls, on the stated theory that peace is a construction window rather than a holiday. Then Zerah the Ethiopian arrives with a million men and three hundred chariots, and Asa's prayer contains the only line that matters: to you there is no difference between the mighty and the man with no strength. The Ethiopians are broken and chased to Gerar, and Judah goes home with sheep, camels and a great deal else.
2CH 15:1-19	A prophet meets Asa on the road with the sentence that runs through this whole book: seek him and he will be found, forsake him and he will forsake you. Asa takes it seriously enough to hold a national covenant renewal, sworn with trumpets, and attaching the death penalty to not seeking God, which is an unusual way to guarantee enthusiasm. He also removes his own grandmother from the queen mother's post and burns her idol, which is not a family dinner anybody enjoyed.
2CH 16:1-14	Thirty-five years of relying on God, and then Baasha builds a fort and Asa immediately hires Syria with the temple silver. It works, which is the problem. Hanani points out that a man who beat a million Ethiopians has just paid to lose a smaller war, and Asa puts him in the stocks, the reformer having become the kind of king who jails a prophet. Three years later his feet fail and he consults only physicians. The funeral spices were magnificent.
2CH 17:1-19	Jehoshaphat begins with garrisons and then does something no other king in this book thinks of: he sends a travelling faculty of princes, Levites and priests round the towns of Judah with the law book, to actually teach it. The results, as the Chronicler files them, are tribute from the Philistines, seven thousand seven hundred rams from the Arabians, and neighbours too nervous to attack. Then the muster roll, over a million men, including one commander described as having simply volunteered.
2CH 18:1-16	Kings tells this story about Ahab. Chronicles tells it about Jehoshaphat, the good king who married into the wrong family and then accepted dinner. Four hundred prophets say go, in perfect unison, and one of them has made himself iron horns as a visual aid. Jehoshaphat asks, with great delicacy, whether there is anyone else. There is: Micaiah, whom Ahab hates on the grounds that he is always right. Next time on 2 Chronicles: the prophet finally says what he actually saw.
2CH 18:17-34	Micaiah's second answer is a scene in the heavenly court, where God asks for volunteers to entice Ahab to his death and a spirit offers to lie through four hundred mouths. Zedekiah slaps him, Ahab jails him on prison rations until his safe return, and Micaiah simply notes the condition. Ahab then goes into battle in disguise while letting his ally wear the royal robes, which is not friendship. An arrow shot by nobody in particular finds the one gap in his armour.
2CH 19:1-11	A seer meets Jehoshaphat at the gate to ask whether helping the wicked was really the plan, then concedes there are good things in him, which is as close as this book gets to a mixed review. Jehoshaphat's response is administrative reform. Judges in every fortified city, told that they sit for God and not for men, and a court in Jerusalem split between the chief priest for religious matters and a royal officer for the king's. Israel's first documented judiciary, and it comes with an ethics policy.
2CH 20:1-19	Three armies converging on Judah, and Jehoshaphat's opening move is to call a fast and then quote Solomon's dedication prayer back to God, which is either great faith or excellent lawyering. His closing line is the honest one: we have no strength, we do not know what to do, our eyes are on you. The answer comes through a Levite from Asaph's choir, and it is essentially that they should turn up and watch. Next time on 2 Chronicles: the choir goes first.
2CH 20:20-37	Jehoshaphat sends the worship team out ahead of the infantry, which is either the boldest tactical decision in the book or a refusal to make one. As the singing begins, the coalition turns on itself, Ammon and Moab wiping out Seir and then each other, and Judah arrives to find nothing left to fight. The plunder takes three days to carry. Later he goes into shipbuilding with the king of Israel, is told it is doomed, and the ships duly break up in port.
2CH 21:1-20	Jehoshaphat divides the estate sensibly, and Jehoram simplifies the arrangement by killing all six brothers. Married into Ahab's family, he imports the family religion, and Edom and Libnah both leave. Then a letter arrives from Elijah, who in Kings is a northern prophet with nothing to do with Judah, promising a disease of the bowels that takes two years. Raiders carry off his wives and all but his youngest son. The obituary is four words long: he departed without being missed.
2CH 22:1-12	Ahaziah inherits a throne only because raiders killed everyone ahead of him, and inherits Athaliah as his chief adviser, which the text says plainly was the problem. He visits his wounded cousin in Jezreel on precisely the day Jehu arrives to exterminate Ahab's house, and is killed in the sweep. He gets a burial anyway, on the strength of being Jehoshaphat's grandson. Athaliah then kills the entire royal family, except one baby smuggled out by his aunt and raised in the temple for six years.
2CH 23:1-21	The coup is planned like a liturgy. Jehoiada recruits five captains, brings in Levites from every town, splits them into three shifts by gate, and arms them from the museum: David's own spears and shields, still in the temple. The boy king is crowned in the middle of it. Athaliah walks in, sees the crowd and the trumpets, and gets out one word, twice, before she is walked outside to be killed, because the priest will not have blood in the house.
2CH 24:1-27	Joash is a good king for exactly as long as his guardian lives, which is the whole point of the chapter. The repair fund works beautifully once they stop asking the Levites and put a collection box by the gate. Then Jehoiada dies at a hundred and thirty, the princes flatter the king, and the shrines come back. When Jehoiada's own son says so publicly, Joash has him stoned in the temple court. A small Syrian raiding party then defeats his army, and his servants finish the job in his bed.
2CH 25:1-14	Amaziah executes his father's killers but spares their children, quoting Deuteronomy at the executioners, which is genuinely admirable. Then he hires a hundred thousand northern mercenaries, is told to send them home, and asks the only question on his mind: what about my hundred talents. The answer, that God can afford more, does not console the mercenaries, who sack Judean towns on the way out. He wins in Edom, throws ten thousand prisoners off a cliff, and brings the defeated gods home to worship. Next time on 2 Chronicles: somebody points out the obvious.
2CH 25:15-28	The prophet's question is unanswerable: why worship gods that could not save their own people from you? Amaziah's reply is to ask who appointed him royal counsellor. Then, flushed with Edom, he picks a fight with Israel, and Joash sends back a fable about a thistle asking a cedar for its daughter and being flattened by passing wildlife. Amaziah goes anyway, is captured, and watches four hundred cubits of his own city wall come down. His subjects eventually chase him to Lachish and finish it.
2CH 26:1-23	Uzziah is the most competent king in the book and the chapter cannot stop listing things: Philistine walls demolished, towers, cisterns, farms he ran because the text says he loved the soil, three hundred thousand troops, and siege engines mounted on the battlements to throw stones. Then the sentence that undoes it all: when he was strong, his heart was lifted up. He walks into the sanctuary with a censer, eighty priests block him, and the leprosy surfaces on his forehead while he is still arguing.
2CH 27:1-9	Sixteen years, and the most interesting thing about Jotham is a single clause: he did right, but he did not enter the temple. After his father, that is not piety, it is a man who has learned exactly one lesson and learned it thoroughly. Gates, walls, forts in the forests, and three consecutive years of Ammonite tribute in silver, wheat and barley. The verdict on him is the whole theology of Chronicles in a line: he became mighty because he ordered his ways.
2CH 28:1-27	Ahaz collects catastrophes. Syria takes captives, Pekah kills a hundred and twenty thousand Judeans in a day, and two hundred thousand women and children are marched to Samaria. Then the one bright scene in the chapter, and it happens in the north: a prophet and four Ephraimite chiefs stop the column, and the victors clothe, feed and anoint the prisoners and carry the weak home on donkeys. Ahaz meanwhile pays Assyria for help that never comes, then sacrifices to the gods that beat him and locks the temple.
2CH 29:1-18	First month of the first year of the reign, and Hezekiah's opening act as king is to unlock the building his father nailed shut. His speech to the Levites is unusually direct about cause and effect: the lamps went out, the offerings stopped, and that is why your relatives are in captivity. Then sixteen days of hauling rubbish out of the holy place and down to the Kidron, which is the least glamorous work in Chronicles. Next time on 2 Chronicles: they find out what Ahaz threw away.
2CH 29:19-36	The vessels Ahaz discarded turn up, cleaned and laid out by the altar, which is the detail that makes the restoration real. Then a sin offering explicitly made for all Israel, not just Judah, from a king who governs none of the north. The music is staged precisely: the song starts when the offering starts. And a quiet embarrassment in the accounting, that there were not enough consecrated priests to skin the animals, so the Levites did it, being, the text says, more upright about consecrating themselves.
2CH 30:1-27	Hezekiah invites the northern tribes to Passover, in the second month, because nobody was ready in the first, which is a bold approach to a fixed festival. Most of Ephraim laughs at the couriers. Some come anyway. Then the real problem: half the congregation is ritually unclean and eats anyway, and Hezekiah simply prays that God pardon anyone who sets his heart to seek him, purified or not. God agrees. They enjoy it so much they run the whole thing twice, and nothing like it has happened since Solomon.
2CH 31:1-21	The reform continues by demolition, out into Ephraim and Manasseh where Hezekiah has no jurisdiction at all. Then the administration, which is where this chapter lives: divisions restored, the king funding the daily offerings personally, and a tithe so large it sits in heaps from the third month to the seventh. Hezekiah asks the priests about the heaps and is told, in effect, that they have eaten their fill and this is what is left. So they build storerooms and appoint a clerk.
2CH 32:1-17	Sennacherib arrives, and Hezekiah's response is admirably practical: block every spring outside the city so the besiegers arrive thirsty, rebuild the wall, add a second one, make weapons. Then the speech, whose good line is that Assyria has an arm of flesh and Judah has something else. Sennacherib's reply, delivered by envoys and by letter, is a survey of every god who failed to save his people, and includes the clever accusation that Hezekiah weakened Judah by demolishing its own altars. Next time on 2 Chronicles: the letters get answered.
2CH 32:18-33	The propaganda is shouted in Hebrew at the men on the wall, which is a detail about siege warfare worth noticing. Hezekiah and Isaiah pray, an angel goes through the Assyrian camp, and Sennacherib goes home to be murdered by his sons in his own temple. Then Chronicles compresses everything Kings tells at length: the illness, the sign, the pride, the humbling, and the Babylonian envoys, whom God uses to find out what is in Hezekiah's heart. Also, he rerouted the Gihon spring.
2CH 33:1-25	Fifty-five years of the worst king Judah produced: altars in the temple courts, an idol in the sanctuary itself, his own children through the fire, mediums and sorcery. Kings leaves it there. Chronicles adds what Kings has no room for: Assyrian officers hook him and haul him to Babylon in fetters, and in a foreign prison he prays, and is heard, and comes back and dismantles his own life's work. His son Amon copies the sins and skips the repentance entirely.
2CH 34:1-17	Josiah is crowned at eight, starts seeking God at sixteen and starts demolishing at twenty, which is a precise timetable for a conversion. The purge is unusually physical: idols ground to powder and the powder scattered over the graves of the people who worshipped them, and the bones of their priests burned on their own altars. Then, four years later, during a routine building fund audit, the high priest mentions that he has found the book of the law lying in the temple. Next time on 2 Chronicles: somebody reads it out loud.
2CH 34:18-33	A king hears the law read aloud for the first time and tears his clothes, which tells you how long it had been. His officials take the question to Huldah, a prophetess living in the second quarter of Jerusalem, and her answer is in two halves: the curses stand and will not be quenched, and Josiah personally will die before he has to watch. He responds by reading the whole book to the nation himself and swearing to keep it anyway.
2CH 35:1-27	The Passover is enormous and meticulously staged: thirty thousand lambs from the king's own flocks, the Levites doing the flaying because the priests are working until nightfall, the singers never leaving their posts because their colleagues cover the catering. Nothing like it since Samuel. Then, with the temple in order and the law restored, Josiah rides out to intercept an Egyptian army that has no interest in him, is warned off in God's name by a pharaoh, ignores it, and dies of an arrow at Megiddo.
2CH 36:1-23	Four kings in twenty-three years, three of them removed by foreign powers, one renamed on the way out. The chapter is not really interested in them; it is interested in the pattern. God sends messengers early and often, they are mocked, and eventually there is no remedy. The temple is stripped, burned and the walls broken. Then the strangest note in Chronicles: the land finally gets the Sabbath rests it was owed, seventy years of them, and the book ends mid-sentence with Cyrus saying go up.
2JN 1:1-13	Thirteen verses, and most of them are a door policy. The elder greets a chosen lady, is pleased some of her children are walking in truth, and restates the commandment to love one another while noting it is not new. Then the actual business: people are going around denying that Jesus came in the flesh, and if one of them turns up, do not let him in and do not even greet him, because saying hello counts as joining in. He would rather finish this conversation in person.
2MA 1:1-18	The book opens with mail. Jerusalem writes to the Jews in Egypt, prays for them, remembers the trouble Jason started, and gets to the point, which is a request that Egypt observe the Chislev festival. A second letter adds the news: Antiochus went to the temple of Nanaea in Persia proposing marriage to the goddess in order to collect her treasure as a dowry, and the priests locked the doors and dropped rocks on him through the ceiling. Next time on 2 Maccabees: the fire that was not fire.
2MA 1:19-36	An origin story for the festival. The priests hid the altar fire in a dry well before the exile; Nehemiah later sends their descendants to fetch it, and they come back with thick water instead. He pours it over the sacrifice, the sun comes out, and the whole pile goes up. Prayers, hymns, and the leftovers set fire to some stones for good measure. The Persian king fences the spot off and starts handing the water out as a royal favour. They call it Nephthar, meaning cleansing, though everyone says Nephthai.
2MA 2:1-17	Jeremiah, it turns out, hid the ark. Warned by God, he carried the tabernacle, the ark and the incense altar up the mountain Moses climbed, sealed them in a chamber in the rock, and told the men trying to note the route that the place would stay lost until God gathered his people. The letter then lists precedents for heavenly fire, mentions that Nehemiah founded a library, and adds that Judas collected the books scattered by the war. If you want copies, they say, send someone. Next time on 2 Maccabees: the author explains himself.
2MA 2:18-32	The letters close, and the actual author steps forward to explain that everything following is a one-volume condensation of five books by Jason of Cyrene. He wants it readable. He compares the historian to the man who builds a house and himself to the one who paints it, notes that abridging is sweaty thankless work rather like catering a banquet, and finally observes that a long preface to a short history is foolish, then stops writing the preface.
2MA 3:1-19	Under Onias, Jerusalem is so well run that the king pays for the sacrifices out of his own revenue. Then Simon, the temple's own guardian, loses an argument about market regulation and takes his revenge by telling the governor there is untold money in the treasury. Heliodorus is sent to collect it. Onias explains that it is deposits, widows and orphans and one very well connected Hyrcanus, and gives exact figures. Heliodorus is unmoved and sets a date. The whole city goes into mourning. Next time on 2 Maccabees: a horse.
2MA 3:20-40	Heliodorus reaches the treasury and is met by a horse. Its rider wears golden armour and the animal strikes him with its forefeet, while two remarkably handsome young men appear on either side and flog him at length. He goes down speechless and leaves on a stretcher, having arrived with an honour guard. His friends beg Onias to pray for him, and Onias, mainly worried the king will suspect foul play, obliges. Heliodorus recovers, testifies, and advises the king that any enemy he wants scourged should be sent to Jerusalem.
2MA 4:1-25	Simon, having started the whole affair, now accuses Onias of it, men begin turning up dead, and Onias takes the matter to the king. Seleucus dies, Antiochus Epiphanes succeeds, and Jason buys his own brother's high priesthood for three hundred and sixty talents, with a surcharge for permission to open a gymnasium and register Jerusalem as citizens of Antioch. The priests start skipping sacrifices to make the discus. Then Menelaus, sent to deliver Jason's payment, outbids him by three hundred talents. Next time on 2 Maccabees: he does not pay either.
2MA 4:26-50	Jason, who supplanted his brother, is supplanted in turn and flees to the Ammonites. Menelaus pays the king nothing at all, which is noticed. He bribes the deputy Andronicus with stolen temple gold, and when Onias says so publicly, Andronicus swears friendship, coaxes him out of sanctuary and kills him. Even the Greeks are appalled. Antiochus weeps, strips Andronicus and executes him where he did it. Lysimachus then loots the temple until a crowd armed with stones, logs and handfuls of ash kills him by the treasury. Menelaus bribes his way to acquittal; his accusers are executed.
2MA 5:1-14	For forty days the sky above Jerusalem shows cavalry in gold-worked robes charging, wheeling and hurling spears, and everyone hopes it is a good sign. It is not. On a false report that Antiochus is dead, Jason storms the city with a thousand men and butchers his own citizens, the narrator observing that victory over kinsmen is the worst kind of defeat. He gains nothing and dies a fugitive, unburied, abroad. Antiochus, assuming revolt, takes the city and gives three days to killing. Eighty thousand dead, as many again sold. Next time on 2 Maccabees: he goes inside the temple.
2MA 5:15-27	Menelaus personally shows Antiochus around the temple so he can take the vessels and every gift previous kings had dedicated, eighteen hundred talents' worth. The narrator's explanation is careful: God looked away for a while because of the nation's sins, and the place exists for the nation, not the nation for the place. Then Apollonius arrives with twenty-two thousand men, plays the peaceful visitor, waits for the Sabbath, parades his troops, and kills the people who came out to watch. Judas and nine others go into the hills and live on wild plants.
2MA 6:1-17	An elderly Athenian arrives with a brief to abolish the law and rename the temple after Jupiter Olympius, and the sanctuary is turned into a party. Sabbath, feasts and even admitting to being a Jew are outlawed; every month there is compulsory sacrificial meat for the king's birthday, and an ivy-wreathed procession for Bacchus. Two women who had their sons circumcised are thrown from the wall with the infants at their breasts. Others burn in caves rather than break the Sabbath by defending themselves. The author stops to tell his readers this is chastening, not abandonment. Next time on 2 Maccabees: Eleazar refuses.
2MA 6:18-31	Eleazar, ninety years old and a leading scribe, is forced to eat pork. He spits it out and walks to the rack himself. Sympathetic officials, old friends of his, offer a way out: bring your own lawful meat and let us pretend. He refuses, on the grounds that the young would hear Eleazar had converted at ninety, and that pretending buys nothing from God. Under the scourge he says he could have escaped this and is enduring it willingly. The text presents his death as the example he meant it to be.
2MA 7:1-21	Seven brothers and their mother are arrested and scourged to make them eat pork. The eldest answers for all of them, and is mutilated and burned alive in a pan while the others watch. The second is scalped, and says the King of the world will raise them again. The third holds out his tongue and his hands and says he received them from heaven and expects them back. One after another the brothers die refusing, and their mother watches all of it in a single day. There is nothing wry to say about this chapter. Next time on 2 Maccabees: the youngest son.
2MA 7:22-42	The mother tells her sons that she did not give them their breath and cannot take the credit, and the Creator who did will give it back. Antiochus, sure he is being mocked, offers the youngest money, office and friendship, then asks the mother to talk sense into him. She agrees, leans in, and in their own language tells him to look at heaven and earth, understand that God made them out of nothing, and not to fear the butcher. The boy refuses, tells the king he will not escape God, and dies. The mother dies last.
2MA 8:1-19	Judas collects six thousand men from the villages and starts working at night, burning towns and taking back positions until his name is everywhere. Philip writes for help, and Nicanor arrives with twenty thousand and a business plan: settle the king's two thousand talent debt to Rome by selling Jews, ninety slaves to the talent, with the dealers invited in advance. The nervous desert. The rest sell what they own and pray for those already sold. Judas points out that the other side trusts weapons. Next time on 2 Maccabees: the buyers' money changes hands.
2MA 8:20-36	The watchword is The Help of God, the army is divided into four under Judas and his brothers, and the sacred book is read aloud first. Nine thousand of Nicanor's men die, the rest run, and the Jews collect the money the slave dealers had brought to buy them, which is the most satisfying accounting in the book. They break off early because the Sabbath is starting, then share the spoil with the maimed, the widows and the orphans. Nicanor sneaks home dressed as a fugitive, telling everyone the Jews have someone fighting for them.
2MA 9:1-13	Antiochus, having just been chased out of Persepolis for trying to rob a temple, hears about Nicanor and decides to take it out on Jerusalem, which he announces he will convert into a mass grave. The sentence is barely finished before the pain starts. He orders the driver to go faster, falls out of the chariot, and is carried the rest of the way in a litter while worms swarm out of him and the smell empties the road. The man who thought he could weigh mountains concludes that mortals should not be arrogant. Next time on 2 Maccabees: the apology letter.
2MA 9:14-29	The vows begin. Jerusalem free, the Jews made equal to citizens of Athens, the sanctuary refurnished at his own expense, and, at the top of the bidding, he will become a Jew himself and tour the world publicising God's power. None of it helps.
