He then writes to the Jews as fellow citizens, in the cheerful tone of a man with a mild cold, and gets to the real business, which is naming his son as successor before the neighbours notice the vacancy. He dies in the mountains abroad.
2MA 10:1-19	Judas takes back the temple, clears the marketplace of foreign altars, and strikes fire out of stones for a new one. The sacrifices resume after a two-year gap on the twenty-fifth of Chislev, the anniversary of the desecration to the day, and run for eight days with palms, in the manner of tabernacles, since they spent the last tabernacles hiding in caves. Then the political summary: Eupator succeeds, Lysias runs the government, and Ptolemy Macron is called a traitor for treating Jews justly and poisons himself. Next time on 2 Maccabees: the two towers.
2MA 10:20-38	Some of Simon's men accept seventy thousand drachmas to let prisoners slip out of the towers, and Judas executes them for selling their own kindred, then takes the towers anyway. Timotheus comes back with cavalry from all over Asia. After prayer in sackcloth, five horsemen with golden bridles appear from the sky, two riding either side of Judas as armour while shooting arrows and thunderbolts at the enemy. Twenty thousand five hundred fall. Timotheus shuts himself in Gazara, where on the twenty-fifth day the garrison's blasphemy provokes a breach, and he is found in a cistern.
2MA 11:1-18	Lysias arrives with eighty thousand foot and eighty elephants, intending to settle Greeks in Jerusalem, tax the temple like any other shrine, and auction the high priesthood annually, which is an unusually frank business model. The Jews pray for a good angel and get a horseman in white with golden weapons at the head of the column. Eleven thousand of Lysias's men die and he leaves in disgrace. Being, the text says, not a man void of understanding, he concludes the Hebrews cannot be beaten and opens negotiations. Next time on 2 Maccabees: the correspondence.
2MA 11:19-38	The chapter is four letters. Lysias will do what he can. The king writes to Lysias granting the temple back and permission to live by ancestral custom, on the grounds that undisturbed subjects are less trouble. The king writes to the nation offering an amnesty for anyone home by the thirtieth of Xanthicus, with their own food and laws, and adds that he is sending Menelaus to encourage them, which is a choice. The Roman envoys write to agree, and to ask for someone quickly, since they are on their way to Antioch.
2MA 12:1-22	The treaty holds for about a paragraph. The men of Joppa invite their Jewish neighbours out on boats with their wives and children, then drown two hundred of them at sea. Judas burns the harbour and the fleet by night, and does Jamnia's too, a fire visible from Jerusalem. An Arab force attacks, loses, and immediately proposes friendship and cattle, which is accepted. Caspin insults him from its walls, is stormed, and the lake beside it runs red. Then Timotheus's hundred and twenty thousand simply panic and run. Next time on 2 Maccabees: Timotheus negotiates.
2MA 12:23-45	Thirty thousand die in the pursuit. Timotheus is caught and talks his way out by mentioning that he holds his captors' relatives, and they let him go. Carnion and Ephron fall; Scythopolis, which had been decent to its Jews, is thanked and left alone. Gorgias escapes because a Thracian horseman disables the man dragging him off by the cloak. Then the discovery: every one of their own dead is wearing an idol of Jamnia under his clothes. Judas raises two thousand drachmas for a sin offering on their behalf, on the grounds that they will rise again.
2MA 13:1-26	Eupator and Lysias arrive with a hundred and ten thousand men, twenty-two elephants and three hundred scythed chariots. Menelaus tags along hoping to be handed the government, and is instead dropped into a fifty cubit tower of ashes at Beroea, the local penalty for sacrilege, which the narrator finds fitting given what he did to a holy altar. Judas raids the camp at night under the watchword Victory is God's and brings down the largest elephant. Then Philip revolts back in Antioch, and the king abruptly discovers a taste for peace and goes home.
2MA 14:1-25	Demetrius lands at Tripolis and disposes of Antiochus and Lysias. Alcimus, a disgraced former high priest with no way back to the altar, arrives with a gold chaplet and a palm branch and explains to the council that Judas is the sole obstacle to peace. Nicanor, previously master of the elephants, is sent to kill him. Instead, having heard how these people fight, Nicanor opens negotiations, and the two get on so well that he keeps Judas at his side and advises him to marry and settle down. Judas does. Next time on 2 Maccabees: Alcimus reads the treaty.
2MA 14:26-46	Alcimus shows Demetrius the treaty and calls it treason, and Nicanor is ordered to send Judas to Antioch in chains. He turns cold; Judas notices and vanishes. Nicanor then stands in the temple demanding the priests produce him, and swears that otherwise he will flatten the place and put up a temple to Bacchus. Then Razis, an elder they called Father of the Jews, is surrounded by five hundred soldiers and kills himself rather than be taken, in a death the text describes without sparing anything. Nothing in this passage is funny.
2MA 15:1-19	Nicanor decides to attack on the Sabbath, and when the Jews with him object, he asks whether there is really a Sovereign in heaven who commands that, and announces that he is a sovereign on earth. He does not manage it. He also plans his victory monument in advance. Judas meanwhile encourages his men out of the law and the prophets and recounts a dream: Onias the dead high priest praying for the people, and beside him Jeremiah, who hands Judas a golden sword. They resolve on a straight fight. Next time on 2 Maccabees: the sword is used.
2MA 15:20-39	The elephants are posted, the cavalry drawn up, and Judas prays for another angel of the sort that handled Sennacherib. Nicanor advances with trumpets and paeans, Judas advances praying, thirty-five thousand of Nicanor's men die, and Nicanor himself is found dead in his armour. His head and the hand he waved at the temple go up on the citadel, and the thirteenth of Adar becomes a fixed holiday. The author then signs off, hoping the book was good, and comparing writing to mixing wine with water, since neither is drinkable alone.
3JN 1:1-14	A letter about a man who will not answer letters. Gaius is praised for putting up travelling missionaries who refuse to take money from outsiders. Diotrephes, who the elder notes loves to be first, ignores what he writes, badmouths him, won't host the brothers, and throws out anyone who does. The elder's response is essentially that he will bring it up when he arrives. Demetrius gets a good word, presumably because he is the one carrying the letter.
3MA 1:1-29	Philopater fights Antiochus at Raphia. An assassin reaches his tent and kills the wrong man, because Dositheus, a Jew who had left the religion, had swapped in a stand-in. Arsinoe rallies the line in tears with her hair down and an offer of two minas of gold a head, which works. Ptolemy then tours the temples, reaches Jerusalem, sacrifices, and decides he would like to see inside the sanctuary. Told that only the high priest goes in, once a year, he argues that the rule cannot possibly be meant to apply to him. The city empties into the streets.
3MA 2:1-17	Simon the high priest kneels and prays, and it is a very carefully organised prayer. He starts with precedent: the giants drowned, the Sodomites burned, Pharaoh rolled under the sea while Israel walked across. He moves to the promise that prayer made in this house would be heard. He concedes freely that the nation's sins are why they are in this position. Then the actual request, which is less about rescue than reputation: do not let this place be defiled and give the arrogant something to boast about.
3MA 2:18-33	The prayer ends, God hears it, and the king is shaken like a reed in the wind and dropped on the pavement, paralysed and unable to speak. His bodyguards carry him out. He recovers, repents of nothing, and leaves making threats. Back in Egypt he puts up a pillar announcing that anyone refusing to sacrifice is barred from their own temple, that Jews are to be registered among the common people and branded with the ivy leaf of Dionysus, and that resisters die. Join the rites and get Alexandrian citizenship. Most pay bribes instead.
3MA 3:1-16	The rage widens from Alexandria to the countryside, with orders to collect the Jews and kill them. The charge circulating is that they keep others from the ordinances of the law, which mostly means they eat separately and worship separately and are therefore unsociable and disloyal. Greek neighbours who know better cannot help, though some promise quietly to try. Then the royal letter, which opens with the news that the king is very well, that his affairs are very well, and that he was generous to the temples, including that of these wretched people at Jerusalem. Next time on 3 Maccabees: the rest of the letter.
3MA 3:17-30	The letter continues in the wounded tone of a man who was refused entry to a building. They welcomed him only in appearance, they alone among the nations refuse to bow to kings, and when he generously offered them Alexandrian citizenship and initiation into the rites they turned it down, which he takes as proof of future treason. So: every Jew, with wife and children, in iron chains, for a cruel death. Shelter one and your whole household is tortured. Inform on one and you get his property, two thousand drachmas, your freedom and a crown.
3MA 4:1-21	The decree is greeted with street parties. The deportation is grim enough that some of their enemies weep watching it: old men driven along at speed, brides hauled out unveiled with dust in their perfumed hair, bridegrooms wearing halters instead of garlands, everyone chained below decks where the planks shut out the daylight. They are penned in the hippodrome where every visitor to the city can see them. Then the registration for execution runs forty days, dawn to dusk, and fails, because there are too many Jews and the clerks have run out of paper and pens.
AMO 1:1-15	Amos is a herdsman from Tekoa and dates himself two years before the earthquake, which tells you how this book was remembered. Then he works his way round the compass. Damascus, Gaza, Tyre, Edom, Ammon, each introduced with the same formula about three transgressions and then four, each charged with something specific and brutal, each promised fire on its palaces. It is a very satisfying sermon if you are standing in the audience, because so far none of these are you.
AMO 2:1-16	Two more stops, Moab for burning a king's bones into lime and Judah for its own paperwork, and then the trap closes on Israel. The charge sheet turns economic: the righteous sold for silver, the poor for a pair of shoes, garments taken in pledge used as bedding beside the altar, court fines drunk as wine in the temple. God adds that he supplied prophets and Nazirites and was answered with wine and an order to stop prophesying. The last image is the strongest man running away naked.
AMO 3:1-15	The chosen-people line gets turned over: you only have I known, therefore I will punish you for everything. Then a string of questions with one answer each, about lions and snares and trumpets, arriving at the point that disaster in a city is never an accident. The rescue image is bleak comedy, a shepherd recovering two legs and a piece of an ear from the lion's mouth. The property inventory follows: winter house, summer house, houses of ivory, all struck.
AMO 4:1-13	It opens by calling the wealthy women of Samaria cows of Bashan, an image that does not soften on rereading, and promises they will be led off with fish hooks. Then the invitation, delivered perfectly straight: go to Bethel and sin, go to Gilgal and sin more, bring the tithes, and do brag about them, since that is the part you enjoy. The rest is a list of disasters, each closing on the same flat refrain, yet you have not returned to me.
AMO 5:1-27	The chapter is a funeral song sung over someone not yet dead: the virgin of Israel has fallen, and the arithmetic is a thousand marching out and a hundred coming back. Seek me and live, God says, and do not go to Bethel. The day of Yahweh arrives here as a warning rather than a hope: a man escapes a lion, meets a bear, gets home and is bitten by a snake on his own wall. No feasts, no harps, only justice rolling like a river.
AMO 6:1-14	A woe on the comfortable, and the detail is what makes it: ivory beds, choice lambs, improvised music in the style of David, wine drunk out of bowls rather than cups, the very best oils. The charge is not the luxury itself but the last clause, that none of them are grieved over the ruin of Joseph. So they go into exile first, at the head of the queue. A grim aside follows about a survivor telling a relative not to say Yahweh's name out loud.
AMO 7:1-17	Two visions, locusts and fire, and both times Amos intervenes with the same plea, that Jacob is small, and both times God calls it off. The third is a plumb line, which cannot be argued with, and the pleading stops. Then the politics arrive: Amaziah, the priest at Bethel, reports him to the king and suggests he go be a prophet somewhere he can get paid. Amos replies that he is not a prophet at all, he is a fig farmer, and then ruins Amaziah's life in one verse.
AMO 8:1-14	The vision is a basket of summer fruit, chosen for a pun rather than for horror, and the meaning is that Israel is ripe. Then the traders, checked here in their own words: when will the Sabbath be over so we can sell, with a small measure, a heavy weight and a crooked balance, and the sweepings mixed into the wheat. The sentence includes the strangest famine in the prophets, not of bread but of hearing God, with people searching sea to sea and finding nothing.
AMO 9:1-15	The last vision has God standing at the altar telling someone to bring the roof down, followed by a list of hiding places that do not work: the grave, the sky, the top of Carmel, the sea floor, where a serpent is on standby. Israel is also told, flatly, that its exodus is not unique, since the Philistines and Syrians got one too. Then the sieve, which keeps every kernel, and an ending of rebuilt cities and mountains dripping wine.
ESG 1:1-5	The Greek edition cannot bear to start where the Hebrew does, so it opens two years earlier with a dream. Thunder, earthquake, two serpents squaring up, every nation massed against the just, and then a tiny spring swelling into a river while the light comes up. Mordecai sits on it all night and cannot read it. He then foils an assassination plot by two door-keeping eunuchs, gets promoted, and acquires an enemy in Haman, which is the Greek text quietly supplying the motive the Hebrew never gives. Next time on Greek Esther: the party.
ESG 1:6-22	The Greek describes the decor in loving detail: purple on marble, couches of gold and silver, a floor of emerald and pearl, and one jewelled cup valued at thirty thousand talents, which is an absurd figure and clearly meant to be. Drinking is by nobody's rule but the king's. Then Vashti declines to be paraded, and the empire's response is legislation. Memucan's argument is not that the queen was wrong but that wives everywhere will hear about it, so the decree goes out in every language.
ESG 2:1-23	The king's temper cools and his staff, sensing danger, propose a nationwide search for a replacement rather than letting him brood. Esther, an orphan raised by her cousin, arrives at the women's quarters and immediately charms the man in charge, which is the first sign that she is good at this. Twelve months of preparation, six of myrrh and six of spices, and then one evening. She keeps her family secret. Mordecai, meanwhile, uncovers another plot, reports it, and is duly written down and forgotten.
ESG 3:1-15	Mordecai will not bow, is asked about it daily for some time, and finally explains that he is a Jew, which is when the personal becomes genocidal. Haman's pitch to the king is a masterclass in the passive threat: a certain people, scattered everywhere, laws unlike anyone's, disobedient. The Greek text prints the decree in full, and it is worse read whole, a document that opens by praising the writer's own moderation and gentleness before ordering the killing of women and children.
ESG 4:1-16	Mordecai's protest is deliberately public: sackcloth, ashes, and shouting through the open street that a people that has done no wrong is being destroyed. He goes as far as the gate and no further, because mourning clothes are not permitted inside. Esther sends him clothes, which he refuses, then messages, and the exchange sharpens until Mordecai says the thing the whole book turns on: who knows whether you came to the throne for exactly this. She asks for three days of fasting. Next time on Greek Esther: the prayers.
ESG 4:17-17	This is the material the Hebrew Esther does not have, and it changes the book's temperature entirely. Mordecai explains that his refusal to bow was never pride, and that he would have kissed Haman's feet gladly if that were all it cost. Esther takes off the royal clothes, fills her hair with ashes, and prays for words to use before the lion. The line that stays with you is that she calls the crown she wears loathsome, and wears it only when she must.
ESG 5:1-14	The Hebrew gives this scene two verses. The Greek gives it a whole set piece: Esther swapping ashes for splendour, walking in on two maids because she is too frightened to walk alone, and fainting outright when the king looks up in anger. The text says plainly that God changed his mood, at which point he leaps off the throne and calls her sister. She faints again. Then two dinners, and Haman going home so pleased that his wife suggests building a seventy-five foot gallows before breakfast.
ESG 6:1-14	The Greek attributes the insomnia directly to the Lord, which removes any doubt about who is running the plot. The king reads his own daily records as a sleep aid, discovers he never thanked Mordecai, and asks the first man in the courtyard for advice. Haman, arriving to request a hanging, hears what should be done for a man the king wishes to honour and answers as any vain man would. He then leads the horse himself. His wife's verdict is the sharpest line in the book.
ESG 7:1-10	Esther's answer at the second dinner is precisely worded: my life for my petition, my people for my request, and we have been sold. Asked who dared, she names him. The king walks out to the garden to think, and Haman makes the worst decision of a career full of them by throwing himself onto the queen's couch. The king comes back in. A chamberlain helpfully mentions the gallows Haman built, including its height, and the matter resolves itself immediately.
ESG 8:1-12	Esther gets Haman's estate, Mordecai gets Haman's ring, and then the constitutional problem arrives: an order sealed with the royal ring cannot be revoked, not even by the king who sealed it. The empire's answer is not to repeal the decree but to publish a second one on top of it, saying whatever they like. Which is how a bureaucracy that cannot admit error still saves a nation. Next time on Greek Esther: the second letter, in full.
ESG 8:13-17	The Greek prints the counter-decree, and it is a small masterpiece of face-saving. Haman is retrospectively demoted to a Macedonian, an alien to Persian blood, whose real aim was to hand the empire to Macedon, so the king was the intended victim all along. The Jews are described as sons of the living God living by the justest laws. Provinces that decline to comply are threatened with fire, spear, and permanent uninhabitability. Mordecai leaves the palace in purple and gold.
ESG 9:1-32	The reckoning, and the Greek is markedly more restrained than the Hebrew about the numbers: fifteen thousand in the provinces rather than seventy-five. The ten sons of Haman are named individually, and it is noted twice that no plunder was taken, which for this genre is the moral point. Esther asks for a second day in Susa, which is not a comfortable request and the book does not pretend it is. Then the festival is founded and named after the lots that were meant to destroy them.
ESG 10:1-3	Three verses of ordinary epilogue, taxes and annals and Mordecai promoted, and then the Greek does something the Hebrew never can: it goes back and explains the dream from chapter one. The little spring that became a river was Esther. The two serpents were Mordecai and Haman. Even the closing note is scrupulous, naming the year in Egypt and the men who carried the letter, which is the only footnote in the Bible about the Bible's own delivery.
EZR 1:1-11	A Persian king issues a building permit and the book treats it as prophecy fulfilled, which it is. The proclamation is careful: anyone may go, and anyone staying behind is expected to fund a neighbour who goes, which is how you finance a migration without a budget. Then the receipt. Cyrus takes the temple vessels out of his own gods' house, has his treasurer count them out to Sheshbazzar item by item, and the total comes to five thousand four hundred. Next time on Ezra: everyone who signed up.
EZR 2:1-44	The register of the return, and the thing to notice is how it is organised: some families by ancestor, some by hometown, because after seventy years half the people knew where they came from and half only knew whose they were. Adonikam's house comes in at exactly six hundred and sixty-six. And the numbers that matter are at the end: over four thousand priests, and seventy-four Levites. Next time on Ezra: the ones who could not prove it.
EZR 2:45-70	Buried in this list of temple servants, one of whom is descended from somebody named Sisera, is the harshest paragraph in the book. Several families could not produce their genealogy, and three priestly houses, one of them named for the wife's family rather than the husband's, were struck off the priesthood and told they could not eat the holy food until a priest with Urim and Thummim ruled on them. No such priest is ever mentioned again.
EZR 3:1-13	They build the altar before the temple, on its old base, and start the daily offerings while the site is still bare, which the text says plainly they did because they were frightened of the neighbours. Cedar comes down from Lebanon to Joppa exactly as it did for Solomon. Then the foundation is laid and the noise is the point: the young men shout, the old men who remembered the first house weep, and from a distance nobody could tell which sound was which.
EZR 4:1-24	The neighbours open with an offer to help, on the grounds that they have been sacrificing to the same God since an Assyrian king relocated them. Refused, they spend the next several reigns funding lobbyists. The letter they eventually get to Artaxerxes is a small masterpiece of civil service prose: rebellious city, tax revenue at risk, and a helpful suggestion that His Majesty check the archives. He does. The archives agree. Work stops at swordpoint until Darius.
EZR 5:1-17	Two prophets talk the builders into starting again, and the local governor turns up almost immediately with the two questions a bureaucracy always asks: who authorised this, and what are your names. What follows is remarkable restraint on both sides. Tattenai lets the work continue while he writes to Darius, and his letter is scrupulously fair, quoting the Jews' own account in full and asking only that the archives be checked. Occasionally the empire works exactly as designed.
EZR 6:1-22	The scroll turns up, filed not in Babylon but in a summer palace in Media, which is the most realistic detail in the book. Darius rules for the builders, and then goes further than anyone asked: fund it from provincial taxes, supply the sacrifices daily, and anyone interfering gets a beam pulled from his own house to be impaled on. The temple is finished, dedicated with twelve goats for twelve tribes, and a Passover kept by people who had every reason not to expect one.
EZR 7:1-28	Ezra is introduced by genealogy back to Aaron and then by the only line that matters: he had set his heart to study the law, to do it, and to teach it, in that order. Artaxerxes' letter is extraordinarily generous, up to a hundred talents of silver on demand, tax exemption for every temple worker, and authority to appoint judges with powers up to execution. The salt is granted without limit, which is the sort of clause only a real civil service writes.
EZR 8:1-21	Ezra takes a headcount at the river Ahava and discovers a problem: about fifteen hundred men, and not one Levite among them. Nobody wanted the job. So he sends eleven men to a place called Casiphia to ask Iddo, politely, for some ministers, and comes away with thirty-eight Levites and two hundred and twenty temple servants, all listed by name. Then he calls a fast, because the next part is the road. Next time on Ezra: why they travelled without an escort.
EZR 8:22-36	Ezra admits the reason he has no armed escort: he had already told the king that God protects those who seek him, and was too embarrassed to ask for soldiers afterwards. So he fasts instead, and then weighs twenty-five tonnes of silver and gold into the hands of twelve priests, with instructions to have it weighed again at the other end. It arrives intact, is weighed, and the figures are written down. Faith, followed immediately by double-entry bookkeeping.
EZR 9:1-15	The report Ezra receives is that intermarriage is widespread and that the officials have been the worst offenders, which is the detail that undoes him. His reaction is physical: torn clothes, hair pulled from his head and beard, then sitting on the ground unable to speak until the evening offering. The prayer that follows never once says they, only we. He does not ask for anything. He simply says that God has punished them less than they deserved and that nobody can stand here.
EZR 10:1-44	Shecaniah proposes the solution, which is to send away the foreign wives and their children, and the book records it without softening what that means. The assembly agrees, then points out reasonably that it is pouring with rain and this cannot be done in a day, so commissioners are appointed to work through it city by city. Four men are named as objecting. The process takes three months, and the register at the end lists the men, priests included, one family at a time.
HAB 1:1-17	The book opens as a complaint rather than an oracle: how long do I shout violence at you before you do something. God's answer is worse than the silence. He is raising the Chaldeans, a nation whose horses outrun leopards and who laugh at fortresses while building ramps up them. So Habakkuk asks the obvious follow-up, which is how a God too pure to look at evil can appoint a worse party to punish a bad one, while people are hauled in like fish and the fisherman worships his own net.
HAB 2:1-20	Habakkuk does something unusual for a prophet: he climbs the watchtower and waits to be answered, and says so. The answer is to write it large enough to read at a run, and that the vision will keep its appointment even if it takes its time. Then the reply proper, five woes aimed at the plunderer, including the stone crying out of the wall and the beam answering it, and a closing sneer at a man shouting wake up at a plank of gold-plated wood.
HAB 3:1-19	The argument ends in a hymn, complete with musical directions. God arrives from Teman like a sunrise with plague walking in front of him, the old mountains collapse, and the sun and moon stop where they are. Habakkuk admits the vision leaves his bones rotting and his lips shaking. Then the ending, which earns the whole book: no figs, no grapes, no olives, no crops, no flocks, nothing left in the stalls at all, and he says he will rejoice anyway.
HAG 1:1-15	The complaint is a stalled building project. The public position is that the time is not right for rebuilding the temple, and God's reply is to ask how the panelling in everyone's own houses is coming along. Then the audit: you sow a lot and harvest little, you eat and are not full, and your wages go straight into a bag with holes in it. Remarkably for this genre, it works. The governor, the high priest and the people all turn up and start work three weeks later.
HAG 2:1-23	The old men who remember the first temple are visibly unimpressed by the replacement, so Haggai tells everyone to be strong and keep building, on the grounds that the silver and the gold were never theirs anyway. Then a small masterclass in priestly law: holiness does not transfer by touch, but corpse-uncleanness does, and the point is made without anyone having to say it twice. The dates are precise, the harvest figures are dismal, and blessing is scheduled to start today.
HEB 5:1-14	A high priest is appointed, not self-appointed, and is meant to be gentle with the ignorant because he shares their weakness. Christ qualifies by appointment, and by learning obedience through suffering, and gets the odd title priest after the order of Melchizedek. The author starts to explain Melchizedek, then breaks off to complain that his readers have gone dull of hearing: they ought to be teachers by now and instead need milk, being babies.
HEB 6:1-20	Enough foundations, says the author, and then delivers the most frightening paragraph in the letter: people who have tasted the heavenly gift and then fall away cannot be renewed. Land that drinks the rain and grows thistles gets burned. Having said it, he hurries to reassure them that he expects better of them, since God has not forgotten their work. Abraham waited and got the promise, sworn by God on the only name available, his own. Hope as anchor.
HEB 7:1-28	Now the Melchizedek argument, built on what the text does not say. No father, no mother, no genealogy, no recorded death, therefore a priest continually. Abraham tithed to him, and Levi, still unborn in Abraham's body, tithed along with him, which makes the lesser priesthood junior by ancestry. Since a new priesthood requires a new law, the old commandment is annulled for weakness. Jesus, from Judah, a tribe with no altar rights, holds an unchangeable priesthood by oath.
HEB 8:1-13	The main point, finally stated: this high priest is seated in heaven, serving the real tabernacle rather than the copy Moses was told to build to pattern. If the first covenant had been faultless, nobody would have gone looking for a second. The author then lets Jeremiah do the arguing, quoting the new covenant written on minds and hearts at length, and draws the conclusion that interests him: calling one covenant new ages the other.
HEB 9:1-28	A tour of the old tabernacle furniture, lamp stand, show bread, the golden pot of manna and Aaron's budded rod, with the mercy seat behind the second veil where the high priest went once a year, never without blood. All of it, the author says, was a standing sign that the way in was not yet open. Christ entered once with his own blood. Aside on wills requiring a death. Nearly everything is cleansed with blood, and men die once.
HEB 10:1-39	The argument lands: an annual sacrifice is an annual reminder that the problem is still there, and bull's blood was never going to do it. The priests stand, day after day; this one sat down, which is the whole point. Then the pastoral turn, draw near, keep meeting, some of you have stopped, and a second terrifying warning about wilful sin. He reminds them they once had their property looted and took it cheerfully.
HEB 11:1-17	A definition of faith, then the author starts naming names, and the roll call is doing something sly: every person on it acted on something they could not see. Abel dead and still talking, Enoch simply not found, Noah building a ship over weather that had not happened. Abraham left without an address, lived in tents in the land he was promised, and waited for a city instead. Next time on Hebrews: the list keeps going, and it stops being cheerful.
HEB 11:18-40	The parade continues through Isaac, Jacob blessing on his staff, Joseph fussing about his bones, Moses hidden in a basket and later declining Egypt, then the Red Sea, Jericho and Rahab. The author announces he is running out of time, dumps five more names, and the tone changes: from stopping lions' mouths to being sawn apart, wandering in caves in animal skins. The world was not worthy of them, and none of them got the thing they were promised.
HEB 12:1-29	The witnesses become spectators, and the readers are told to run. Their suffering is reframed as discipline, on the ground that fathers who ignore their children are not showing kindness, so nobody wants the exemption. Esau appears as the cautionary example, a man who traded his birthright for one meal and cried about it afterwards to no effect. And the closing contrast: not Sinai, burning and terrifying, but Zion. The last line is that God is a consuming fire.
HEB 13:1-25	The letter ends with a list of ordinary instructions, one of which slips in that hospitality has occasionally meant hosting angels without noticing. Honour marriage, stop loving money, ignore the strange food teachings. Then a last flourish of the letter's own logic: the sin offering was burned outside the camp, Jesus died outside the gate, so go out there. He calls this thirteen chapters few words, mentions Timothy is out, and passes on greetings from the Italians.
HOS 6:1-11	A tidy little repentance song opens the chapter: he tore us, he will heal us, back on our feet by the third day, reliable as sunrise. God's reply is that their devotion has the staying power of morning dew. Then the famous line, mercy rather than sacrifice and knowledge of God rather than burnt offerings, followed immediately by the detail that the priests are running an ambush on the Shechem road.
HOS 7:1-16	Every attempt to heal Israel only uncovers more of what needs healing. The picture is an oven left overnight by a sleeping baker and roaring by morning, which is what the court's intrigue does to its own kings: they eat their judges and go through monarchs with nobody thinking to pray. Then two of the better insults in the prophets. Ephraim is a cake not turned over, and a witless dove flapping between Egypt and Assyria.
HOS 8:1-14	Blow the trumpet, there is an eagle over the temple. The charges are all self-service: kings appointed without asking, idols cast from their own savings, a calf that Israel is reminded a workman made, so it can hardly be a god. The line everyone knows is here, sow the wind and reap the whirlwind, followed by Ephraim as a wild donkey wandering off to Assyria to pay for company. More altars, more sinning.
HOS 9:1-17	No harvest festival this year. The threshing floor will not feed them, the new wine fails, and the venue changes to Assyria, with unclean food and a grave in Memphis. Their offerings will taste like the bread of mourners. Note verse seven, where the country decides the prophet is a fool and the inspired man insane, which is a review rather than a rebuttal. God remembers finding Israel like grapes in the desert, and ends on empty wombs.
HOS 10:1-15	The complaint is that prosperity went straight into religious decoration: the richer the vine, the finer the altars, all of which are coming down. The people work out that they have no king and did not much want one. Samaria's calf is shipped to Assyria as a gift, its king drifts off like a twig, and the survivors ask the hills to fall on them. The advice is agricultural: break up your fallow ground, since what you plowed was wickedness.
HOS 11:1-12	The tone changes completely. God talks like a parent, remembering teaching Ephraim to walk, holding him by the arms, bending down to feed him, and being repaid with incense to idols. The sentence still stands: Assyria as king, the sword on the cities. But then verse eight breaks off mid-judgement, how can I give you up, how can I make you like Admah, and compassion wins, on the stated grounds that he is God and not a man.
HOS 12:1-14	Ephraim's diet is wind, which is a fair description of a foreign policy that signs with Assyria and ships oil to Egypt at the same time. The argument then reaches back to Jacob, grabbing heels before he was born and wrestling an angel to a draw, as if to say the family has always been like this. Ephraim's own line is the giveaway: I have become rich, so nobody will find any sin in me. The scales are crooked.
HOS 13:1-16	Ephraim used to command silence in a room; then Baal, and then nothing. Their idols are craftsmen's work and their future is a list of things that do not last: mist, dew, chaff, smoke. The diagnosis is that being well fed made them forget him, and the response is a short bestiary of lion, leopard, and bear robbed of her cubs. He asks where the king is now, having given them one in anger. The last verse is unrelieved: Samaria falls, and nobody is spared.
HOS 14:1-9	The ending is a script. Israel is told what to say, right down to the words: Assyria cannot save us, and we will stop calling the work of our hands our gods. God accepts it and switches entirely to horticulture, dew and lily and deep roots and olive trees and the smell of Lebanon. Then a last line addressed over the reader's shoulder, that these ways are right, and the righteous walk in them while the rebellious trip over them.
JOL 1:1-20	The book opens by insisting this be remembered for generations, then describes what happened: locusts, in four waves, each finishing what the last one left. What follows is a list of people told to wail, organised by profession. Drunkards, because the wine is gone. Priests, because there is nothing left to offer. Farmers, because every tree in the inventory is withered. Even the livestock are described as perplexed, standing in a field with no pasture and no explanation.
JOL 2:1-32	The locusts get promoted. Now they are an army with cavalry, scaling walls, climbing through windows, marching in ranks nobody breaks, with Eden ahead of them and wilderness behind. Then the whole thing halts on two words, yet even now, and the demand is a fast and a torn heart rather than a torn shirt. God relents, sends grain and wine and oil, and offers the best line in the book: I will restore the years the locusts have eaten.
JOL 3:1-21	The complaint gets specific and mercenary. The nations cast lots for Judah's people and traded a boy for a prostitute and a girl for a drink, so the sentence is the same trade run backwards. Tyre, Sidon and Philistia are named for shipping the children to the Greeks. Then the famous line inverted: beat your plowshares into swords, everyone report to the valley of decision. It closes with hills flowing milk and a fountain coming out of the temple.
JON 1:1-17	Sent east to Nineveh, Jonah buys a ticket for the furthest known point west. The storm arrives, the sailors jettison the cargo and pray to every god they have, and the prophet is asleep in the hold, which the captain finds remarkable. The lots point at Jonah, who cheerfully explains that he made the sea and dry land and is currently running from the one who did. The pagan sailors then try to row him back to shore rather than drown him, which is the joke.
JON 2:1-10	A psalm delivered from inside a fish, and it never mentions the fish. It is a proper thanksgiving, complete with waves and weeds and the bottom of the mountains, thanking God for a rescue that has not yet finished. Note verse eight, where the man who ran from a direct instruction pauses to criticise people who cling to worthless things. Salvation belongs to Yahweh, he concludes, and the fish is instructed to put him back on the beach.
JON 3:1-10	Second attempt. Jonah walks one day into a three-day city and delivers the shortest sermon in scripture, eight words in Hebrew and no mention of repentance. It works catastrophically well. The whole city believes, the king gets off his throne and into ashes, and the fast is extended to the livestock, who are also issued sackcloth and told to cry mightily to God. God sees it and calls the whole thing off.
JON 4:1-11	The prophet whose sermon worked is furious, and admits the reason he ran: he knew God was merciful and would go soft. He asks to die, sets up a shelter outside town, and waits to see if the city gets destroyed anyway. A vine grows, he is delighted, a worm kills it overnight, and he asks to die again. God's answer is the last line of the book, a question about a hundred and twenty thousand people who cannot tell left from right, and also the cattle.
JUD 1:1-25	Jude sat down to write something encouraging, then wrote this instead. Certain men have crept in and turned grace into a licence, and Jude reaches for every cautionary tale available: the wilderness dead, Sodom, and the angels who abandoned their own address and are chained in the dark awaiting the day. Michael himself, arguing with the devil over the corpse of Moses, declined to be rude about it. The intruders are rainless clouds, fruitless trees, wandering stars. Enoch called it.
MAL 1:1-14	The whole book is structured as an argument with a heckler. God says I have loved you, and the immediate reply is: how, exactly? The answer involves Jacob and Esau and a promise that whatever Edom rebuilds will come down again. Then the priests, who ask how they have despised his name and are shown the blind, lame and sick animals on their own altar. The killer line is a suggestion: try presenting that to the governor and see whether he is delighted with you.
MAL 2:1-17	The priests get the worst image in the twelve prophets, which involves the dung of their own festivals and their own faces. The comparison is Levi, whose covenant was life and peace and whose lips kept knowledge, against a priesthood that has made people trip over the law. Then the domestic charge: an altar covered in tears, and God explaining unsentimentally that he is not accepting the offering because of what the man did to the wife of his youth. The exchanges close on the accusation of wearying him.
MAL 3:1-18	A messenger is coming to clear the road, and the Lord will arrive at his own temple, which sounds like good news until the follow-up question: who can actually stand there. The images are domestic and unpleasant, a refiner's fire and launderers' soap, and the first people scrubbed are the priests. The list of defendants runs from sorcerers to men who short their workers' wages. Then the boldest line in the book, an outright dare: bring the whole tithe in and test me.
MAL 4:1-6	Four verses of furnace and two of homecoming. The proud burn like stubble, root and branch, while everyone else gets the sun of righteousness with healing in its wings and goes out skipping like calves let out of a shed in spring. Then the last instruction of the prophets: remember Moses, and expect Elijah. The very final note is not cosmic at all, it is about fathers and children turning back toward each other.
MIC 1:1-16	The book opens with God stepping out of his temple and walking on the mountains, which melt like wax under him. Samaria is to be tipped into the valley stone by stone, and the trouble does not stop at the border: it reaches Jerusalem's gate. Micah announces he will mourn stripped and howling like a jackal, then delivers a passage of relentless wordplay, town by town, in which every name is turned against its inhabitants. The instruction at the end is to shave your head for the children who are gone.
MIC 2:1-13	The crime here is insomnia with a purpose: they lie in bed devising schemes and execute them at dawn, because they can. Fields coveted, fields seized, houses taken, families removed from their inheritance. The reply is that they will one day sing their own funeral song about their land going to strangers. Told to stop prophesying, Micah observes what kind of prophet this audience would actually pay for: one preaching wine and strong drink. Then, without warning, a flock is gathered and led out through a broken gate.
MIC 3:1-12	The address to the rulers begins politely and then turns into butchery imagery held for three straight verses: skin off, bones broken, flesh in the cooking pot. The professional prophets are next, priced per prophecy, peace for whoever feeds them and war for whoever does not, and their punishment is a permanent night with nothing to see in it. Bribes on the bench, fees in the pulpit, and yet everyone insists God is with them. The verdict is Zion plowed like a field.
MIC 4:1-13	The threat stops and the famous vision arrives: the temple mountain lifted above the hills, nations walking up it on their own initiative, swords beaten into plowshares and nobody bothering to learn war. The detail that makes it is domestic rather than grand, every man sitting under his own vine and fig tree with nothing to be afraid of. Then the lame and the outcast are collected into a nation, and Zion is told, with no softening, that the route there runs through Babylon.
MIC 5:1-15	The chapter opens with a siege and a judge being slapped with a rod, then pivots to the smallest town on the list. Bethlehem Ephrathah, too minor to matter, supplies the ruler whose origins are described as ancient. He will be their peace when Assyria arrives, backed by seven shepherds and, for good measure, eight. The remnant is given two incompatible images in consecutive verses, dew on the grass and a lion loose among sheep, and both are meant. Then the horses, chariots and idols are all removed.
MIC 6:1-16	God takes his people to court with the mountains as jury, and his opening question is almost hurt: what have I done to you, answer me. The defence escalates absurdly, offering calves, then thousands of rams, then rivers of oil, then a firstborn child, each bid larger and more useless than the last. The answer is the famous one, and it costs nothing: act justly, love mercy, walk humbly. Then back to the short measure and the crooked weights, and a curse of harvests nobody gets to eat.
MIC 7:1-20	Micah opens as a man walking a stripped vineyard looking for one grape. Nobody honest is left, judges are for sale, and the advice is to trust no neighbour, no friend and nothing said in front of your own wife, since your enemies are your household. Then he stops and simply waits, telling his enemy not to celebrate too early. The book closes on a pun on his own name, who is a God like you, and on sins thrown into the sea.
NAM 2:1-13	The siege arrives, and Nahum narrates it like a man watching from a rooftop: scarlet uniforms, chariots flashing about the streets like lightning, troops tripping over themselves getting to the wall. The river gates open, the palace melts, and the ruling household is led out with its servants moaning like doves. Then a cheerful invitation to loot, since the silver and gold are apparently endless. The chapter finishes by asking where the lions' den has got to, which is not a kind question to ask an empire.
NAM 3:1-19	The charge is a city built on plunder, with the streets described corpse by corpse. The punishment is public humiliation, skirts lifted, filth thrown, a spectacle nobody will mourn. Then the argument that lands hardest: are you better than Thebes, which had the sea for a wall, allies on every side, and went into captivity anyway, its children killed in the streets. Nineveh's fortresses are ripe figs that fall into an open mouth, and its officials are locusts that leave the moment the sun comes out.
NEH 1:1-11	Nehemiah asks a visiting relative for news from home and gets the worst possible answer: the survivors are in disgrace and the wall is rubble. He sits down and weeps, then fasts for days. The prayer is standard confession until the last line, where he asks for mercy in the sight of this man, without saying who. The narrator then supplies the missing information in five words at the very end of the chapter: he was the king's cupbearer.
NEH 2:1-20	A cupbearer with a visibly unhappy face in front of a Persian king is a man in real danger, and Nehemiah says he was terrified. He gets asked anyway, prays between one sentence and the next, and comes away with leave, letters of safe conduct and a requisition for timber, including timber for his own house, which is the sort of thing you only remember to ask for if you have thought it through. Then he inspects the ruins at night, telling nobody.
NEH 3:1-32	A wall built by whoever was standing next to whom, and the list is the point. Goldsmiths and a perfumer doing masonry. District rulers repairing their own stretch. Priest after priest working directly opposite his own front door, which is an excellent way to guarantee quality. Shallum builds with his daughters. And one small permanent embarrassment: the men of Tekoa took two sections, but their nobles would not put their necks to the work, and somebody made sure that was written down.
NEH 4:1-23	The opposition begins with mockery, Tobiah offering the line that a fox climbing this wall would knock it over, which is the sort of remark that gets people building faster. The wall reaches half height. Then the mockery turns into a plot, and Nehemiah's answer is to arm the workforce: half building, half standing guard, everyone carrying a sword while hauling stone, a trumpeter posted beside him to call reinforcements to whichever stretch is attacked. Nobody takes their clothes off for the duration.
NEH 5:1-19	The wall is going up and meanwhile the workforce is going bankrupt: mortgaged fields, borrowed tribute money, children sold into slavery to fellow Jews. Nehemiah's response is a public assembly at which the creditors are given no room to negotiate, and they agree so quickly they never manage a reply. He seals it by shaking out his lap as a curse. Then a quiet boast worth keeping: twelve years as governor, a hundred and fifty at his table daily, and he never once claimed the food allowance.
NEH 6:1-19	Four invitations to a friendly meeting in the plain of Ono, and four identical refusals: I am doing a great work and cannot come down. The fifth arrives as an open letter, deliberately unsealed so everyone reads the accusation that he means to crown himself. Then a prophet advises him to hide in the temple, and Nehemiah spots the trap in the advice itself. Fifty-two days to build. And a closing detail: half the Judean nobility was writing to Tobiah the whole time, being his in-laws.
NEH 7:1-43	The wall is up, so Nehemiah turns to the problem nobody solves with masonry: the city is enormous and almost empty. He starts by finding the old register of the first return, and copies it out. Anyone comparing it with the list in Ezra will notice the totals do not all match, family by family, though both books arrive at the same grand total, which is the sort of thing that happens to a document copied twice over a century. Next time on Nehemiah: the ones who could not prove it.
NEH 7:44-73	The register finishes with singers, gatekeepers, temple servants and the same painful paragraph Ezra records: families who could not produce their genealogy, and three priestly houses struck off and barred from the holy food until a priest with Urim and Thummim could rule on them. Then the livestock, down to six thousand seven hundred and twenty donkeys, and the building fund, itemised by who gave what. Everyone goes home to their own towns, and the seventh month arrives.
NEH 8:1-18	The people ask for the book, which is a reversal worth noticing; nobody made them. Ezra reads from a wooden platform for five or six hours while Levites work the crowd giving the sense, since the language has drifted. Then everybody bursts into tears, and the leadership's response is essentially not today: go and eat something rich, drink something sweet, and send a share to anyone with nothing. The next day they read further, discover the rule about booths, and camp on their own roofs for a week.
NEH 9:1-21	A day divided into quarters, a quarter reading and a quarter confessing, in sackcloth and dust. The prayer that follows is the whole national history recited back to God as evidence, and it is careful to give both sides. Creation, Abram, the sea, Sinai, the manna. Then the golden calf, and the point of the prayer arrives early: even then the pillar did not leave. The detail that lands hardest is the smallest one, that for forty years their feet did not swell.
NEH 9:22-38	The history continues, and settles into a loop that the prayer does not try to hide: they were comfortable, they rebelled, they were conquered, they cried out, they were rescued, repeat. Prophets warned them and were killed for it. The conclusion is not a plea for rescue but a verdict on themselves: you have been just, we have been wicked, and we are servants in our own country while the harvest goes to foreign kings. Then they sign something.
NEH 10:1-39	The covenant, with the signatures listed first, Nehemiah at the top, and eighty-odd priests, Levites and chiefs after him. The clauses are revealing about what actually worried them: no intermarriage, no shopping on the Sabbath even when the neighbours bring goods to the gate, debts cancelled every seventh year, and a self-imposed annual temple tax of a third of a shekel. They even cast lots to decide whose turn it was to supply firewood, which is the least glamorous promise anyone ever sealed.
NEH 11:1-36	The city is finished and nobody wants to live in it, so they draw lots: one household in ten relocates to Jerusalem, and the text notes that the people blessed anyone who volunteered instead of being drafted, which tells you how popular the posting was. Then the residency roll, and the imbalance is striking: over a thousand priests inside the walls, and two hundred and eighty-four Levites. The singers, meanwhile, had quietly built themselves villages just outside town.
NEH 12:1-30	A roster of priests and Levites reaching back to the first return, followed by six generations of high priests, which is the only place the book lets you feel how much time has passed. The Levites are described as praising watch next to watch, on David's old rota, still running. Then, for the dedication, they have to go and find the Levites, because they had scattered to wherever the food was. Next time on Nehemiah: two choirs on top of a wall.
NEH 12:31-47	The dedication is staged as a circuit: two enormous choirs put up onto the wall itself and sent round the city in opposite directions, Ezra leading one and Nehemiah walking behind the other, past every gate they had rebuilt. They meet at the temple and the noise carries for miles. Then, characteristically, the chapter ends with storeroom appointments and a note that the tithes were actually paid, which for this book is the happy ending.
NEH 13:1-15	Nehemiah goes back to Persia for a while, and the whole thing comes apart while he is away. He returns to find that the priest in charge of the storerooms has cleared one out and given it to Tobiah, the man who spent six chapters trying to stop the wall. Nehemiah throws the furniture into the street personally. Then he discovers the Levites have not been paid in so long they have gone back to farming. Next time on Nehemiah: the Sabbath market.
NEH 13:16-31	The complaints keep coming. Winepresses working on the Sabbath, Tyrian fishmongers trading in the city, so Nehemiah has the gates locked from dusk and threatens to lay hands on the merchants who camp outside waiting. Then the intermarriage problem, and his response is not a dignified one: he curses them, hits some of them and pulls out their hair, citing Solomon as the precedent. The high priest's grandson, married to Sanballat's daughter, is simply run off. Remember me for good, he says, and the book stops.
OBA 1:1-21	One chapter, one target, no digressions. Edom lives up a cliff and asks who could possibly bring it down, which in this genre is a question that answers itself. Even thieves leave something, the argument runs, and Edom will be left nothing, betrayed by the allies who ate at its table. The actual charge is small and damning: on the day Jerusalem fell, Edom stood on the other side and watched, then joined in. Whatever you did comes back on your own head.
REV 1:1-20	John is on Patmos, which he mentions without complaint, and hears a trumpet voice behind him telling him to write. He turns and gets the full description: robe to the feet, hair white as snow, eyes of fire, feet like brass out of a furnace, a voice like many waters, seven stars in one hand and a sword coming out of the mouth. John responds by falling over like a corpse, which seems reasonable. He is told not to be afraid.
REV 2:1-29	The letters begin, and each opens with I know your works, which is never entirely comforting. Ephesus works hard, sniffs out fake apostles, and has stopped loving anybody. Smyrna gets no rebuke, only a warning about prison. Pergamum lives next to Satan's throne, kept the faith when Antipas was killed, and still has Balaam enthusiasts in the pews. Thyatira is improving, apart from tolerating a self-declared prophetess the letter simply calls Jezebel. Prizes: hidden manna, a white stone, a private name.
REV 3:1-22	Three more assessments, and the compliments run out. Sardis has a reputation for being alive, which the letter dismantles in one clause. Philadelphia has little strength and is handed a door nobody can shut, plus the offer of becoming architecture. Laodicea is the famous one: neither hot nor cold, so it gets spat out. It considers itself rich and self-sufficient, and is told it is blind and naked and should buy some eye salve. Then the knock at the door.
REV 4:1-11	A door opens in heaven and John is invited up to look. Throne, gemstone light, an emerald rainbow, twenty-four elders in white with gold crowns, thunder coming out of the furniture, and a floor of glass like a frozen sea. Four creatures covered in eyes front and back, shaped as lion, calf, man and eagle, six wings each, saying holy holy holy without pause, ever. The elders' one action is to throw their crowns down. Nobody in the room is impressed with themselves.
REV 5:1-14	A scroll with seven seals, and a challenge nobody answers. John, who has just seen the throne room of heaven, bursts into tears because the document cannot be opened. An elder tells him to look at the Lion of Judah, and the thing John turns to see is a Lamb that appears to have been killed, with seven horns and seven eyes. It takes the scroll. The harps come out, and the singing widens until every created thing is doing it.
REV 6:1-17	The seals open and the horses ride: conquest, slaughter, and famine with a set of scales and a voice quoting grain prices while carefully protecting the luxury goods. The fourth rider is simply named Death, with Hades trailing behind him like an assistant. The martyrs under the altar want to know how long, and are handed robes and told to be patient. Then the sky rolls up like a scroll and the powerful of the earth ask the mountains to fall on them.
REV 7:1-17	An interruption before the seventh seal. Four angels hold the winds still so nothing moves, while the servants of God get stamped on the forehead, and the count comes in tribe by tribe at a hundred and forty-four thousand. Then John looks again and sees an uncountable crowd from every nation in white with palm branches, which is a different sort of number entirely. An elder asks who they are, John declines to guess, and is told they came out of the great tribulation.
REV 8:1-13	The seventh seal opens and nothing happens for half an hour, which after six seals of catastrophe is the loudest silence in the book. An angel mixes incense with the prayers of the saints, then loads the same censer with fire and throws it at the earth. Four trumpets follow, each taking exactly a third: earth, sea, rivers and sky. The poisoned star is named Wormwood. An eagle flies overhead announcing that the remaining three trumpets are worse.
REV 9:1-21	A fallen star unlocks the abyss and the smoke turns into locusts, which are described in detail and get stranger with every clause: horse bodies, gold crowns, human faces, women's hair, lion teeth, scorpion tails. They are forbidden to touch grass and permitted to torment people for five months without killing them, in a period when death goes conspicuously missing. Their king has a name in two languages. Then two hundred million cavalry, and the survivors change nothing.
REV 10:1-11	An angel comes down dressed in a cloud with a rainbow on his head, plants one foot in the sea and one on land, and roars. Seven thunders answer intelligibly, and John, reaching for his pen, is told to seal it up and write none of it, which is the one thing in this book kept back from the reader. The angel swears time is up. Then John eats a book, on instructions, and finds it sweet going down and bitter afterwards.
REV 11:1-19	John is handed a measuring reed and told which parts not to measure. Two witnesses in sackcloth spend 1,260 days breathing fire at anyone who bothers them and shutting off the rain, which makes them extremely unpopular. The beast kills them, and the world throws a party and sends each other gifts over the corpses in the street. Three and a half days later they stand up and the mood changes considerably. Seventh trumpet: heaven announces the management has changed.
REV 12:1-17	A pregnant woman dressed in the sun, and a seven-headed red dragon waiting under her to eat the baby the moment it arrives. The child is snatched up to the throne, the woman runs for the desert, and Michael settles the matter upstairs by throwing the dragon out of heaven altogether. Down here he is furious and short of time. He chases her, spits a river after her, and the ground drinks it. So he goes after her other children instead.
REV 13:1-18	A beast walks out of the sea assembled from spare parts, leopard, bear, lion, with seven heads and a fatal wound that healed, which is precisely what the world finds impressive. The dragon hands over his authority and everyone asks who could possibly fight this. Then a second beast, horns like a lamb and a voice like a dragon, does the marketing: fire from the sky, a statue that talks, and a mark without which nobody buys anything. The number, we are told, is a man's. 666.
REV 14:1-20	Relief first: the Lamb on Zion with the sealed crowd singing a song nobody else is able to learn. Then three angels fly past in turn with an eternal announcement, a funeral notice for Babylon, and a very specific warning about the mark. A voice interrupts to bless the dead. Then the harvest imagery, which starts pastoral and does not stay that way: the earth is reaped, the grapes are trodden, and the blood comes out to the height of a horse's bridle.
REV 15:1-8	A short chapter that is mostly staging. Seven angels appear with the last seven plagues, and the people who beat the beast are standing on a sea of glass shot through with fire, playing harps and singing Moses' song about the nations coming to worship, which is a generous thing to sing at that moment. The temple opens, the angels come out dressed for a ceremony, and a living creature hands them the bowls. Then smoke, and nobody may go in.
REV 16:1-21	The bowls empty out in sequence, and the trumpets' careful one third gives way to all of it. Sores, then a sea like a dead man's blood, then rivers, and the angel of the waters pauses to say the punishment fits. The fourth turns the sun up, and people respond by cursing God rather than repenting, which becomes the chapter's refrain. Frogs come out of three mouths to recruit kings for Megiddo. The seventh brings hailstones weighing a talent apiece.
REV 17:1-18	A woman on a scarlet seven-headed beast, dressed in purple, gold and pearls, holding a beautiful cup full of filth, with her own identity written across her forehead in case anyone missed it. John admits he stared. The angel then delivers the most puzzle-like passage in the book: seven heads are mountains and also kings, five down, one standing, the beast is an eighth and also one of the seven. The ending is the twist: the horns turn on the woman and eat her.
REV 18:1-24	Babylon's funeral, sung by the people who did business with her. The kings weep from a safe distance, the sailors throw dust on their heads, and the merchants mourn hardest of all, over an inventory that begins with gold and silk and cinnamon and ends, without changing tone, with bodies and human souls. She had said in her heart that she was a queen and no widow. An angel drops a millstone into the sea to demonstrate the manner of her going.
REV 19:1-21	Heaven celebrates, at length and loudly, and announces a wedding. John, overwhelmed, tries to worship the angel and is told off for it briskly. Then the sky opens: a rider named Faithful and True, eyes on fire, wearing many crowns and a name he alone knows, robe already bloodstained before the battle, followed by cavalry in white linen. An angel calls the birds to dinner in advance. The fight itself takes two verses. The sword is his mouth.
REV 20:1-15	An angel arrives with a key and a chain and files the dragon away for a thousand years, with the odd detail that he must afterwards be released for a while. The martyrs reign in the interval. Then Satan is let out, does exactly what he was locked up for, gathers Gog and Magog, and is finished off by fire without a battle. The great white throne follows: books opened, the sea handing back its dead, and Death itself thrown into the lake of fire.
REV 21:1-27	Everything is replaced, including the sea, and the city comes down dressed as a bride. The promise is domestic rather than cosmic: God moving in, and tears wiped off faces. Then the angel produces a golden measuring reed and the vision turns into a surveyor's report, a cube twelve thousand stadia on a side, jasper walls, twelve foundations in twelve named stones, gates cut from single pearls. No temple, and no sun, both for the same reason.
REV 22:1-21	The book ends where the story started, with a tree and a river, except the tree is now public and its leaves are medicine for whole nations. John, having learned nothing from chapter nineteen, tries to worship the angel again and is corrected again. Daniel was told to seal his book; John is told not to, because the time is at hand. Then come quickly three times, an invitation to anyone thirsty, and a stern note against editing.
ZEC 1:1-21	It opens with a warning not to repeat the family history, followed by a pointed question: your fathers, where are they, and the prophets, do they live forever. Then the visions start. A man on a red horse standing in a ravine among myrtle trees, with more horses behind him, all reporting that the world is calm, which is precisely the problem for a ruined city. An angel asks how long is seventy years supposed to be. Four horns follow, and four craftsmen arriving to knock them off.
ZEC 2:1-13	A surveyor sets out to measure Jerusalem, and an angel is dispatched at a run to tell him not to bother. The city is going to outgrow walls entirely, so God offers to be the wall himself, made of fire, and the glory inside it as well. Then a call to everyone still comfortable in Babylon to get out. The phrase that lands is that whoever touches them is touching the apple of his eye. It ends by telling all flesh to be quiet, because God has just got up.
ZEC 3:1-10	A courtroom scene, and the defendant is the high priest, standing there in filthy clothes with an accuser at his elbow. The verdict skips the argument entirely: the accuser is rebuked and the clothes are simply taken away and replaced. Zechariah, who has been silent all book, interrupts to request a clean turban as well, and gets it. Then a stone with seven eyes on it, a servant called the Branch, and the promise that a land's guilt can be removed in a single day.
ZEC 4:1-14	The angel has to wake him for this one. A gold lampstand, seven lamps, seven pipes each, two olive trees flanking it, and twice the angel asks whether he knows what it means and twice he admits he does not, which is refreshingly honest for a visionary. The message inside it is the famous one, not by might nor by power but by my Spirit, along with a line for anyone unimpressed by a small rebuilding project: who despises the day of small things.
ZEC 5:1-11	Two of the strangest visions in the book, back to back. First a scroll the size of a room flying over the country, itemised as a curse on thieves down one side and perjurers down the other, which moves into the guilty man's house and stays there until the house is gone. Then a grain basket with a woman called Wickedness inside it, shoved back down and capped with a lead weight, and flown off to Shinar by two women with stork wings.
ZEC 6:1-15	Four chariots come out between two bronze mountains, with colour-coded horses, and are identified as the four winds sent to patrol the world. The ones heading north are reported to have settled God's spirit up there, which is a very restrained way to describe whatever they did. Then the vision stops and the practical instruction arrives: take the gold that three returning exiles brought back, make crowns, and put them on the high priest's head, with a speech about the Branch who will build the temple.
ZEC 7:1-14	A delegation arrives with a simple administrative question: are we still doing the fifth-month fast? The answer never addresses it. In seventy years of fasting, God asks, was any of that for me, and when you eat, is that not also for yourselves. Then the actual requirement, quoted from the old prophets: true judgement, kindness, and leaving the widow, the orphan, the foreigner and the poor alone. Their fathers stopped their ears, so when they called, nobody answered. The question about the fast is not returned to.
ZEC 8:1-23	The vision of restored Jerusalem is refreshingly small in scale. Not thrones or armies, but old men and old women sitting out in the street with walking sticks, and children playing in it. God notes that if the remnant find this marvellous, he does not. The reversal is stated flatly: I meant to do harm and did not relent, and now I mean to do good. The four fasts become feasts, and the chapter ends with ten foreigners grabbing one Jew's coat to be taken along.
ZEC 9:1-17	The oracle works down the coast. Tyre, praised for its cleverness, piled up silver like dust and gold like street mud, and burns anyway. The Philistine cities lose their kings, and Ekron is quietly annexed. Then the tone changes completely for the arrival of a king who is deliberately unimpressive: righteous, bringing salvation, and riding a donkey, with the chariots and the battle bow abolished rather than deployed. The prisoners come up out of a pit with no water in it and are promised double.
ZEC 10:1-12	The advice is to ask God for rain rather than the household idols, whose speciality is comforting lies, which has left everyone drifting about like sheep with nobody in charge. God's complaint is aimed squarely at the shepherds and the male goats. Judah is then promoted from flock to warhorse, and supplies its own cornerstone, tent peg and battle bow. The gathering runs from Egypt to Assyria and ends with the pleasant problem of a country too small for the number of people coming home.
ZEC 11:1-17	This is the bleakest chapter in the book, and it is acted out rather than seen. Zechariah is told to shepherd a flock already sold for slaughter, whose traders thank God for the margins. He carries two staffs named Favor and Union, disposes of three shepherds in a month, then resigns. Asked for his wages, he is weighed thirty pieces of silver, which God describes as a handsome price, in the tone of someone who means the opposite, and it gets thrown to the potter.
ZEC 12:1-14	Jerusalem becomes two things at once: a cup that makes everyone around it stagger, and a stone so heavy that whoever tries to lift it does himself an injury. Horses are struck with terror, riders with madness, and the weakest man in the city is rated as good as David. Then the chapter drops abruptly into mourning, family by family, wives kept separately, over the one they pierced. The public triumph and the private grief are placed side by side without explanation.
ZEC 13:1-9	Idols are abolished, and so, remarkably, is prophecy: the profession becomes so disgraced that a prophet's own parents threaten him and he denies ever having been one, claiming to be a farmer and blaming his scars on friends. Read that alongside the hairy mantle he is no longer willing to wear and it is a whole small comedy of embarrassment. Then the sword against the shepherd, the sheep scattering, and two thirds of the land cut off while the last third goes into the fire to be refined.
ZEC 14:1-21	The final vision goes for maximum scale. All nations besiege Jerusalem, the city falls, and then God arrives and stands on the Mount of Olives, which obligingly splits in half and opens an escape route. The day is described as neither day nor night, with the light arriving in the evening. The plague on the attackers is genuinely horrible, and the ending is oddly domestic: cooking pots and horse bells all stamped holy, and no trader left in the temple.
ZEP 1:1-18	Zephaniah opens at maximum volume and does not come down: everything swept off the earth, people and cattle and birds and even the fish, which is a detail nobody asks for. The day of Yahweh is described as a sacrifice with the guests already invited, which is one way to phrase it. The best image is God going through Jerusalem with lamps, looking for the men settled comfortably on their dregs who have concluded he does nothing either way. Their silver will not help.
ZEP 2:1-15	A brief window is offered first: seek righteousness and humility, and you may be hidden. Then the tour. The Philistine coast becomes sheep pasture, Moab and Ammon go the way of Sodom and Gomorrah for the crime of insulting the neighbours, and Assyria is left dry. The finish is the pleasure of the chapter: Nineveh, the city that said I am, and there is no one besides me, ends up with birds calling through its windows and passers-by shaking their fists at the ruins.
ZEP 3:1-20	The city's leadership is described in animals: princes as roaring lions, judges as evening wolves who leave nothing for the morning, and prophets and priests who have simply given up pretending. God notes that his own justice turns up every single morning and nobody is embarrassed by the contrast. Then everything turns. The proud are removed and what is left is a poor, afflicted people who tell no lies. The last image is unexpected: God rejoicing over them with singing.
3MA 5:1-28	Hermon, master of the elephants, is ordered to drug five hundred of them with neat wine and frankincense and turn them loose on the Jews at dawn. The king goes off to dinner with the people who hate Jews most. Hermon does his job. The king, however, is given sleep, and does not surface until mid afternoon, at which point he demands to know why the Jews are still alive, blames Hermon, and reschedules. The crowd gathers again before dawn, and the king arrives having entirely forgotten what the elephants are for. Next time on 3 Maccabees: Hermon explains.
3MA 5:29-51	Hermon reports that the elephants are ready exactly as ordered, and the king, whose memory has been rearranged, turns on him and says his own parents and children would have made better food for the beasts than these loyal Jews. Everyone leaves quietly. At the next banquet he orders it again, and his own relatives ask how long he intends to keep doing this, since the city is close to riot. He swears to go through with it, and to burn Judea too. At dawn the elephants go out, and the Jews say goodbye to each other.
3MA 6:1-20	Eleazar, an old and much respected priest, quiets the elders and prays a list of precedents: Pharaoh drowned, Sennacherib demolished, the three friends cooled inside the furnace, Daniel brought back up from the lions, Jonah recovered from the sea monster. His one condition is striking: if we must die, kill us yourself, but do not let the heathen say their god could not deliver them. He finishes exactly as the king arrives with the elephants, whereupon two terrifying angels come down, visible to everybody except the Jews. Next time on 3 Maccabees: the elephants turn around.
3MA 6:21-41	The elephants turn around and trample the army behind them. The king bursts into tears, rounds on his own friends, accuses them of governing badly and of plotting against his throne, and orders the prisoners released as sons of the living God. He then pays for a seven day feast on the very ground meant to be their grave, and they turn it into banqueting booths with dancing and psalms. The men who had cheerfully registered them for death go home in disgrace. The days become an annual commemoration, and the king writes them a letter.
3MA 7:1-23	The king writes to his commanders explaining that certain friends had urged him to destroy the Jews, that these friends were the problem all along, and that he himself never really meant it. He notes that the God of heaven shields them as a father shields his sons, and warns that anyone troubling them will be dealing with him, not with the palace. Before leaving, the Jews ask permission to deal with the members of their own community who had abandoned the law, and kill three hundred. Then a seven day feast at Ptolemais, a pillar, and home with their goods returned.
4MA 1:1-35	The author opens by announcing his thesis, which is that religious reasoning is the absolute master of the passions, and promises to prove it from the deaths of Eleazar, seven brothers and their mother. First, definitions. Reasoning, wisdom, prudence, justice, manliness, temperance. Pleasure and pain are the two roots: lust comes before pleasure and joy after, fear before pain and sorrow after. Reasoning is a gardener who prunes rather than uproots. His first proof is that a hungry man can look at forbidden food and decline it.
4MA 2:1-24	Now the worked examples. Joseph, young and available, talked himself out of it. A miser under the law lends without interest and writes off debts at the Sabbath, which is reasoning beating avarice on its own ground. A grasping farmer leaves his gleanings anyway. Law overrules love of parents, of spouse and of friends when they go wrong, and even hatred, since you may not cut down an enemy's crops. Moses was angry with Dathan and Abiram and did nothing about it. Jacob cursed the anger of Simeon and Levi, which proves anger can be governed.
4MA 3:1-21	The clarification: reasoning does not uproot the passions, it opposes them. You cannot delete desire, only refuse to obey it. The illustration is David, who after a day of fighting developed an irrational craving specifically for water from the enemy's camp. Two of his men broke through the lines and fetched a pitcher of it. David, parched, reckoned that a drink worth a man's blood was too dangerous to his soul, and poured it out to God. Then the author turns to history: peace, royal funding, and then some men with new ideas.
2ES 1:1-20	Sixteen generations of genealogy before anyone says anything, which establishes that Ezra is a priest and that the author has a list habit. Then God gives him the assignment: go tell the people what they have done, and their children too. What follows is a grievance ledger, sea parted, Moses supplied, pillar of fire provided, quails delivered, manna served, rock split for water, each item paired with the murmuring it received. Next time on 2 Esdras: the patience runs out.
2ES 1:21-40	The ledger closes and the verdict arrives.
